### [1] [DevicesWorld: Benchmarking Cross-Device Agents in Heterogeneous Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13465)
*Huatao Li,Xinwei Geng,Yuheng Wang,Yutong Li,Runde Yang,Hantao Chen,Shu Yao,Jingru Fan,Xuhui Ren,Yuanyuan Zhao,Fei Huang,Chen Qian*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: DevicesWorld is a large-scale benchmark (6,140 tasks) for evaluating LLM agents on cross-device collaborative tasks spanning mobile, desktop, and IoT environments. Frontier agents achieve only 12.5% success, revealing significant challenges in cross-device information acquisition, interface manipulation, and multi-condition task completion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world user goals frequently span multiple heterogeneous devices (phone, desktop, IoT), yet existing agent benchmarks focus on single-device environments. There is a critical need for a unified, executable benchmark that can evaluate whether LLM agents can acquire, integrate, and act on information distributed across devices to complete end-to-end cross-device tasks.

Method: The authors introduce DevicesWorld, a benchmark with 6,140 tasks integrating mobile, desktop, and IoT environments into a unified cross-device interaction and evaluation framework. Each task specifies a natural-language goal, participating devices with initial states, executable actions, rule-based verifiers, and cleanup procedures. A multi-stage construction and quality-control pipeline ensures tasks reflect realistic user needs while enabling automatic outcome verification from device states and generated files. Five frontier LLM-agent systems are evaluated on a fixed evaluation set.

Result: All evaluated methods achieve low success rates, with the best reaching only 12.5%. Among failures, approximately 28.7% satisfy at least one scoring condition but fail the full task. Trajectory analysis reveals agents get stuck acquiring cross-device information, confuse source and output devices, manipulate interfaces incorrectly, or terminate prematurely before all conditions are jointly satisfied.

Conclusion: DevicesWorld establishes cross-device collaborative operation as an executable, reproducible, and diagnostically useful evaluation problem. The substantial performance gap (best 12.5%) underscores that reliable cross-device agent collaboration remains an open research challenge, with clear failure modes identified for future work.

Abstract: LLM-based agents have rapidly improved at operating individual digital environments such as mobile applications, desktop systems, and smart homes. However, real-world user goals often span multiple devices: information may come from a phone, be processed on a desktop, and the result may need to appear on another device. Most existing benchmarks center on a single dominant execution environment, making it difficult to evaluate whether agents can acquire and integrate information across heterogeneous devices and complete end-to-end tasks with cross-device dependencies. We introduce DevicesWorld, a large-scale executable benchmark for cross-device collaborative operation. DevicesWorld contains 6,140 tasks and integrates three classes of device environments -- mobile, desktop, and IoT -- into a unified cross-device interaction and evaluation framework. Each task defines a natural-language user goal, participating devices and initial states, executable actions, rule-based verifiers, and a cleanup procedure. A multi-stage construction and quality-control pipeline keeps tasks close to realistic user needs while allowing final outcomes to be automatically verified from device states and generated files. We evaluate five frontier LLM-agent systems on a fixed evaluation set. All methods achieve low success rates, with the best reaching only 12.5%. Among failed runs, about 28.7% satisfy at least one scoring condition yet still fail the full task. Trajectories show that agents become stuck acquiring information or manipulating interfaces, confuse source and output devices, or terminate before all conditions are jointly satisfied. DevicesWorld turns cross-device collaborative operation into an executable, reproducible, and diagnostically useful evaluation problem for research on reliable cross-device agents.

</details>


### [2] [FixItFlow: Automated Troubleshooting Guide Generation from Cloud Incidents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13035)
*Srihari Unnikrishnan,Jaskaran Singh Walia,Drishti Goel,Supriyo Ghosh*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: FixItFlow is an automated system using LLMs to generate troubleshooting guides from historical cloud incident data. It extracts diagnostic patterns, synthesizes structured guides with verified commands, and enforces strict validation against hallucination. In evaluation with 26 engineers, generated guides achieved 61.5% positive clarity ratings and a 2.3x reduction in incident mitigation time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cloud services face frequent incidents requiring rapid, consistent diagnosis and resolution. Manually creating troubleshooting guides is labor-intensive, leading to incomplete coverage and outdated documentation. There is a need for automated, reliable guide generation to reduce documentation burden while improving incident response.

Method: FixItFlow is an automated pipeline that: (1) extracts diagnostic patterns from historical incident data and engineer actions; (2) uses large language models to synthesize structured troubleshooting guides with verified commands; and (3) enforces strict validation mechanisms to prevent hallucinated or fabricated content in the generated guides.

Result: In an evaluation with 26 engineers, FixItFlow-generated guides received 61.5% positive ratings for clarity. Incidents with associated generated guides saw a 2.3x reduction in mitigation time compared to those without.

Conclusion: Automated troubleshooting guide generation via systems like FixItFlow can meaningfully improve incident response speed and consistency while significantly reducing the documentation burden on engineering teams.

Abstract: Cloud services experience frequent incidents that require rapid diagnosis and resolution. Troubleshooting guides help engineers respond consistently, but creating them manually is labor-intensive, resulting in incomplete coverage and outdated documentation. We present FixItFlow, an automated system that generates troubleshooting guides from historical incident data using large language models. The system extracts diagnostic patterns from engineer actions, synthesizes structured guides with verified commands, and enforces strict validation to prevent fabricated content. In our evaluation with 26 engineers, generated guides achieved 61.5\% positive ratings for clarity and demonstrated a 2.3x reduction in mitigation time for incidents with associated guides. These results indicate that automated guide generation can improve incident response while reducing documentation burden on engineering teams.

</details>


### [3] [Ask Before You Diagnose: Safe-Psych, a Sequential Evaluation Benchmark for LLMs in Psychiatry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13036)
*Oriana Presacan,Andreea Grama,Larisa Irimină,Alireza Nik,Jaya Ojha,Vajira Thambawita,Ciprian I. Băcilă,Bogdan Ionescu,Michael A. Riegler*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Safe-Psych is a benchmark of 1,000+ psychiatric clinical notes with incremental evidence disclosure and per-stage action labels (DIAGNOSE, CLARIFY, ABSTAIN). Evaluation shows even strong LLMs fail to recognize incomplete information, with >60% under-abstention, premature diagnoses, and poor calibration. Safety prompting shifts errors toward excessive abstention. The benchmark highlights LLMs' inability to recognize when they need more clinical evidence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly deployed for clinical decision support, but existing medical benchmarks assume complete information is available upfront. In real healthcare settings, clinical evidence is often incomplete or evolving, and models should request clarification or abstain rather than generate unsupported answers. No existing benchmark evaluates how LLMs handle sequential, incrementally disclosed clinical information with explicit abstention/clarification expectations.

Method: The authors introduce Safe-Psych, comprising over 1,000 real-world psychiatric clinical notes segmented to simulate incremental evidence disclosure. Each stage includes psychiatrist-derived action labels: DIAGNOSE, CLARIFY, or ABSTAIN. Multiple state-of-the-art LLMs are evaluated in both full-information (all evidence provided at once) and sequential settings (evidence disclosed incrementally), measuring under-abstention, over-abstention, premature diagnosis rates, and diagnostic accuracy.

Result: Under-abstention exceeds 60% for most models — they provide diagnoses when they should abstain or clarify. Safety-aware prompting reduces premature commitment but shifts errors toward excessive abstention. In sequential evaluation, models frequently diagnose before sufficient evidence is available and rarely seek clarification unless explicitly prompted. Premature diagnoses are less accurate than diagnoses made when sufficient evidence is present.

Conclusion: Capability does not ensure safe calibration: even strong LLMs struggle to recognize when clinical information is incomplete. The core limitation is metacognitive — models fail to know when they need more information. Safe-Psych is released to support research on improving LLM safety in healthcare.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for decision support in healthcare, but clinical evidence is often incomplete or evolving. When the available information is insufficient to support a reliable answer, models should request clarification or abstain rather than provide unsupported responses. Existing medical benchmarks, however, typically assume that complete information is available upfront. We introduce Safe-Psych, a sequential benchmark for evaluating how LLMs handle evolving diagnostic uncertainty in clinical psychiatry. Safe-Psych contains over 1,000 real-world psychiatric clinical notes segmented to simulate incremental evidence disclosure, with psychiatrist-derived action labels at each stage: DIAGNOSE, CLARIFY, or ABSTAIN. We evaluate multiple state-of-the-art LLMs in full-information and sequential settings. Our findings show that capability does not ensure calibration: even strong models struggle under incomplete clinical information, with under-abstention exceeding 60% for most models and safety-aware prompting reducing premature commitment only by shifting errors toward excessive abstention. In sequential evaluation, models frequently diagnose before sufficient evidence is available and rarely seek clarification unless explicitly prompted; these premature diagnoses are less accurate than on-time diagnoses. Overall, Safe-Psych reveals a limitation across the evaluated models: recognizing when clinical evidence is incomplete and additional information is needed. We release Safe-Psych to support research on improving LLM safety in healthcare.

</details>


### [4] [The Perplexity Trap: When Patent Law Makes Human Writing Look Like AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13044)
*Anubhab Banerjee*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Zero-shot AI text detectors catastrophically fail on patent claims, with false-positive rates exceeding 60% across all tested methods, because human-drafted claims share the low-perplexity, low-burstiness characteristics of LLM text. A lightweight linguistic-complexity regressor improves accuracy by 13 percentage points without likelihood-based inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The EPO's 2026 Guidelines hold applicants responsible for LLM-assisted content, creating a need to detect AI-generated patent text. However, realistic prosecution uses consumer GPUs (~8GB VRAM), and Article 84's clarity requirements drive human drafting onto the same low-perplexity, low-burstiness manifold as LLM output, making detection fundamentally difficult.

Method: Benchmarked three open-source zero-shot detectors (Binoculars, Fast-DetectGPT, DetectGPT) on 500 granted EPO H04 telecom patents vs. 500 LLM-generated counterparts under consumer hardware constraints, using five prompting strategies. Tested robustness via Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct regeneration, LoRA-adapted Pythia-2.8B scoring heads, cross-IPC replication (A61K, C07D, F03D), and H100 re-evaluation. Proposed a seven-feature linguistic-complexity logistic regression as an alternative detector.

Result: All zero-shot detectors exceeded 60% false-positive rate at claim level: Binoculars 78.3%, Fast-DetectGPT 61.3%, DetectGPT 80.5%. Mean FPR across cross-IPC replication was 84.6%. The linguistic-complexity logistic regression achieved 74.0% accuracy at 28.1% FPR, a 13 percentage-point gain over a perplexity-only baseline.

Conclusion: The failure of zero-shot detectors on patent claims is structural, not due to insufficient substitute-model capacity. Linguistic-complexity features offer a viable, hardware-efficient alternative that does not rely on likelihood at inference time.

Abstract: The European Patent Office (EPO) reported record filings in 2025, and the 2026 EPO Guidelines hold applicants strictly responsible for LLM-assisted content under Article 83 and Rule 42, creating pressure to triage suspected AI-generated patent text. Two constraints make this hard. First, realistic prosecution settings often have only consumer GPUs with about 8 GB VRAM, not datacenter-class scoring stacks. Second, Article 84 of the European Patent Convention requires claims to be clear and concise, pushing human drafting onto the same low-perplexity, low-burstiness manifold that LLMs occupy. We benchmark three open-source zero-shot detectors on 500 granted EPO H04 telecom patents versus 500 LLM-generated counterparts using five prompting strategies, all under the consumer hardware envelope. At claim level, all detectors exceed 60 percent false-positive rate: Binoculars 78.3 percent, Fast-DetectGPT 61.3 percent, DetectGPT 80.5 percent. The failure persists under Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct regeneration, LoRA-adapted Pythia-2.8B scoring heads, cross-IPC replication on A61K, C07D, and F03D (mean FPR 84.6 percent), and H100 re-evaluation with published Falcon-7B and GPT-J-6B heads, arguing the issue is structural rather than substitute-model capacity. A seven-feature linguistic-complexity logistic regression reaches 74.0 percent accuracy at 28.1 percent FPR, a 13 percentage-point gain over a perplexity-only baseline at a comparable operating point, without using likelihood at inference and within the same hardware budget.

</details>


### [5] [Do LLMs Need Architectural Changes for Simultaneous Speech Translation? A Prefix-to-Prefix Data Driven Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13158)
*Junkun Chen,Jian Xue,Ming Tang,Abdel Heba,Hoda Gholami,Ruchao Fan,Jinyu Li*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: CSSEL-P2P is a data-driven approach for simultaneous speech translation using decoder-only LLMs. It combines fixed-length chunked streaming decoding with a rewind-based committed prefix strategy, and uses teacher-labeled prefix-to-prefix (P2P) targets with bounded waiting for fine-tuning, achieving +1.54 COMETKiwi improvement over the streaming baseline without architectural changes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Simultaneous speech translation (SimulST) with decoder-only LLMs struggles due to limited context and cross-lingual reordering under strict latency constraints. Existing solutions rely on architectural modifications or explicit read/write policies, which are brittle in conversational speech where segmentation boundaries are ambiguous. A simpler, data-driven alternative is needed.

Method: The authors propose CSSEL-P2P, built on a chunked streaming speech encoder LLM (CSSEL). The method uses: (1) fixed-length chunks for cumulative streaming decoding with a rewind-based committed prefix mechanism, and (2) teacher-labeled prefix-to-prefix (P2P) targets with bounded waiting for fine-tuning. This avoids architectural changes or explicit read/write policies.

Result: On an in-house conversational speech evaluation, CSSEL-P2P improves streaming translation quality by +1.54 COMETKiwi over the CSSEL streaming baseline, with only a marginal increase in latency (+0.15s Average Lagging).

Conclusion: Effective simultaneous speech translation can be achieved without architectural modifications by leveraging P2P supervision within a chunked streaming decoder-only LLM framework, offering a simple yet robust data-driven alternative to policy-based approaches.

Abstract: Simultaneous speech translation (SimulST) requires incremental translation under strict latency constraints, yet remains challenging for decoder-only LLM systems due to limited context and cross-lingual reordering. Recent approaches often introduce architectural changes or explicit read/write policies to control output timing, which can be brittle in conversational speech where segmentation boundaries are ambiguous. We present a simple data-driven alternative: fixed-length chunks for cumulative streaming decoding with a rewind-based committed prefix, and teacher-labeled prefix-to-prefix (P2P) targets with bounded waiting for fine-tuning, yielding CSSEL-P2P, where CSSEL is our proposed chunked streaming speech encoder LLM. In our in-house conversational speech evaluation, CSSEL-P2P improves streaming quality by +1.54 COMETKiwi over the CSSEL streaming baseline at comparable latency (+0.15s Average Lagging), suggesting effective SimulST without architectural changes via P2P supervision.

</details>


### [6] [What Models Express, Suppress, and Resist: Auditing Open-Weight LLMs with Persona Vectors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13162)
*Winston Zeng,Ali Emami,Jinho Choi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper systematically applies persona vectors across 53 traits in two open-weight models, classifying each trait as natural (expressed at baseline), steerable (latent but amplifiable), or intractable (resistant to extraction). Key findings include an asymmetry where default traits resist compositional collapse, while steerable traits (hyperbole, hallucination, sycophancy) show largest steering gains. The work also demonstrates that vectors transferred from fine-tuned variants can recover otherwise intractable traits like "evil," revealing refusals within chain-of-thought reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work on persona vectors covers only a handful of traits. The authors aim to systematically map the behavioral organization latent in language model activation space at scale — understanding not just what models do, but what they could express, hide, or resist — and to distinguish traits that are natural defaults from those that are steerable or intractable.

Method: The authors compile a 53-trait inventory spanning four behaviorally distinct domains and label each trait in two open-weight models. They extract persona vectors (behavioral directions in activation space) for each trait and classify them as natural, steerable, or intractable. They evaluate compositional properties by pairing traits (171 generic-trait pairs), test transfer of vectors from fine-tuned variants for intractable traits, and validate default clinician behavior against a board-certified psychologist's independent desirability judgments.

Result: Both models default to helpful, task-oriented behavior: all nine agentic traits are natural, and clinician defaults match a psychologist's judgments on 16/17 traits. Steering produces largest gains on traits defaults exclude (hyperbole, hallucination, sycophancy). Compositionally, two steerable traits can collapse, but pairs involving a default never do. A vector transferred from a fine-tuned variant recovers "evil" where standard extraction fails, with residual refusals surfacing in chain-of-thought.

Conclusion: Persona vectors are most informative not as steering controls but as probes of behavioral organization. The asymmetry between natural defaults and steerable traits reveals a structured latent behavioral landscape, and transfer from fine-tuned variants offers a path to recovering otherwise intractable traits.

Abstract: What a language model will and will not do is largely set during post-training, but which behaviors it expresses, hides, or resists is not revealed by prompting alone. Persona vectors, behavioral directions in activation space, can probe this organization, but prior work covers only a handful of traits. We present the first systematic application of persona vectors at this scale, compiling a 53-trait inventory across four behaviorally distinct domains and labeling every trait in two open-weight models as natural (expressed at baseline), steerable latent but amplifiable, or intractable (resistant to standard extraction). Both models default to helpful, task-oriented behavior: all nine agentic traits are natural, and their default clinician behavior matches a board-certified psychologist's independent desirability judgments on 16 of 17 traits. Steering produces its largest gains on traits these defaults exclude: hyperbole, hallucination, and sycophancy. The same asymmetry holds across all 171 generic-trait pairs: two steerable traits can collapse the composition, but pairs involving a default never do. Where standard extraction fails on a trait like "evil," a vector transferred from a fine-tuned variant still recovers it, with the residual refusals appearing inside the model's chain-of-thought. Persona vectors are most informative not as a set of controls but as a probe of behavioral organization.

</details>


### [7] [Text2Sign: A Single-GPU Diffusion Baseline for Text-to-Sign Language Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13164)
*Ruize Xia*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Text2Sign is a lightweight text-to-sign-language video diffusion model that runs on a single NVIDIA L4 GPU, using frozen vision-language text encoders and factorized spatiotemporal attention to generate short, low-resolution sign language clips efficiently.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Text-to-signer video generation using full video diffusion models is prohibitively expensive to train and evaluate. The authors aim to create a practical, low-cost alternative that operates on a single commodity GPU, serving as a research baseline.

Method: Text2Sign combines a frozen vision-language text encoder with a 3D encoder-decoder architecture and factorized spatiotemporal attention (reducing full-video attention costs). It ablates convolution-only vs. transformer backbones, frozen pretrained vs. task-specific text encoders, and factorized vs. full attention. A held-out denoising audit tests prompt sensitivity by comparing correct, removed, and shuffled text conditions.

Result: The best short-run achieves validation loss of 0.0648; a longer-run checkpoint reaches 0.00999. On an evaluation slice: SSIM 0.2403±0.0238, PSNR 15.11±0.42 dB, temporal consistency 1.0000±0.0000 (8-step DDIM, guidance scale 5.0). Generates 32-frame 64×64 clips in 12.60s (2.54 FPS) with 3.12 GB peak memory. The denoising audit reveals weak prompt sensitivity: text removal minimally increases loss (0.9875→0.9891), and shuffled prompts perform similarly to correct ones.

Conclusion: Frozen text conditioning improves short-budget validation loss but yields limited prompt-specific separation. The system is restricted to low-resolution, short clips and lacks expert linguistic evaluation, positioning it as a single-GPU research baseline rather than a production-ready sign-language system.

Abstract: Sign language is a primary communication channel for millions of Deaf and hard-of-hearing people, yet text-to-signer video generation remains costly because video diffusion models are expensive to train and evaluate. This paper presents Text2Sign, a text-conditioned diffusion model for short sign-language clips that runs on a single NVIDIA L4 GPU. It combines a frozen vision-language text encoder with a 3D encoder-decoder and factorized spatiotemporal attention to reduce the cost of full-video attention while preserving motion coherence.
  We compare convolution-only and transformer-style backbones, frozen pretrained and task-specific text encoders, and factorized versus full attention. On a signer-disjoint How2Sign split, the best short-run ablation reaches a validation loss of 0.0648, while a longer-run checkpoint reaches 0.00999. On a compact evaluation slice, the latter achieves an SSIM of $0.2403 \pm 0.0238$, a PSNR of $15.11 \pm 0.42$ dB, and temporal consistency of $1.0000 \pm 0.0000$ using 8-step DDIM sampling with a guidance scale of 5.0. It generates a 32-frame, $64 \times 64$ clip in 12.60 seconds, or 2.54 frames per second, with peak inference memory of 3.12 GB.
  A held-out denoising audit shows only weak prompt sensitivity: removing text increases late-timestep loss from 0.9875 to 0.9891, while shuffled prompts perform similarly to correct prompts. Frozen text conditioning therefore improves short-budget validation loss, but prompt-specific separation remains limited. The system is restricted to low-resolution, short clips and lacks expert linguistic evaluation, so it should be viewed as a single-GPU research baseline rather than a complete sign-language production system. Code is available at https://github.com/xiaruize0911/text2sign.

</details>


### [8] [RAGthoven at SemEval-2026 Task 1: A Multi-Stage Pipeline Walks Into a Benchmark and Barely Clears the Bar](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13189)
*Marek Šuppa,Viktória Ondrejová,Lucia Ganajová,Gregor Karetka,Daniel Skala*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: RAGthoven is a multi-stage LLM pipeline for multilingual humor generation that combines retrieval-augmented generation from a joke corpus with computational humor theory. It achieved a statistical tie for Rank 1 across English, Spanish, and Chinese in SemEval-2026 Task 1A, with agentic variants showing no improvement over the simpler non-agentic pipeline despite higher computational cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors aim to tackle the challenging task of multilingual constrained humor generation, which requires both creative fluency and adherence to formal constraints across languages. They investigate whether multi-stage pipeline architectures, retrieval augmentation, theoretical grounding in humor theories, and agentic scaffolding can improve over strong single-model baselines.

Method: The system decomposes humor generation into a four-stage LLM pipeline: (1) a Planner augmented with RAG from a curated joke corpus, (2) a Best-of-N Writer, (3) a Reflector for self-critique, and (4) an LLM-as-a-judge Judge. The pipeline is grounded in Benign Violation Theory and Script-based Semantic Theory of Humor. Two agentic variants are also evaluated: ReAct-style sequential tool-calling (Exp09) and autonomous multi-branch orchestration (Exp10), both with a ConstraintAudit checker. Ten experiments were conducted across four frontier models, with final evaluation on a 12-instance held-out English sample and official SemEval test sets.

Result: RAGthoven tied for Rank 1 with the Gemini 2.5 Flash baseline in all three languages (overlapping confidence intervals). Spanish showed the largest gain (+42 Elo, 1182 vs. 1140), while English (1045 vs. 1081) and Chinese (1045 vs. 1053) slightly favored the baseline. Agentic variants produced no outputs judged superior to the non-agentic pipeline despite substantially higher tool-call budgets.

Conclusion: The results indicate language-dependent diminishing returns from elaborate multi-stage prompt engineering and agentic scaffolding when a strong frontier model is already in the loop. The authors suggest that simpler pipelines may suffice, particularly when the base model is highly capable.

Abstract: We present RAGthoven, our system for SemEval-2026 Task 1 (MWAHAHA), Subtask A (multilingual constrained humor generation in English, Spanish, and Chinese). RAGthoven decomposes creative text generation into a multi-stage large language model (LLM) pipeline (Planner, Best-of-N Writer, Reflector for self-critique, LLM-as-a-judge Judge) grounded in computational humor theory (Benign Violation Theory, Script-based Semantic Theory of Humor) and refined across ten experiments.
  In our final configuration, we augment the Planner with retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) from a curated joke corpus, seeding generation with diverse joke mechanisms. We also evaluate two agentic variants -- ReAct-style sequential tool-calling (Exp09) and autonomous multi-branch orchestration (Exp10) -- that expose the same four stages with a deterministic ConstraintAudit checker. Across four frontier models on a held-out 12-instance English sample, neither agentic variant produced outputs we judged superior to the non-agentic pipeline despite substantially higher tool-call budgets.
  RAGthoven shares Rank 1 with the Gemini 2.5 Flash baseline in all three languages, with overlapping organizer-reported confidence intervals. In Spanish, it leads the baseline by 42 raw Elo points (1182 vs. 1140), while in English (1045 vs. 1081) and Chinese (1045 vs. 1053) the baseline holds the higher raw rating within the same statistical tie.
  Together, these results suggest language-dependent diminishing returns from elaborate multi-stage prompt engineering and agentic scaffolding once a strong frontier model is in the loop.

</details>


### [9] [Adaptive Filtering of the KV Cache: Diagnosing and Correcting Structural-Role Bias in LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13205)
*Soumil Mandal*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper critiques attention-based KV cache eviction methods (H2O family), showing they systematically retain noise (delimiters, KEY tokens) over content (VALUE tokens) on structured inputs like JSON, collapsing accuracy. A role-conditional allocation strategy using SnapKV's windowed score with a single hyperparameter recovers most lost accuracy without retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing attention-based KV cache eviction methods like H2O use accumulated attention mass as a signal energy proxy to retain "important" tokens. However, on schema-dense structured inputs (e.g., nested JSON), this metric is a non-stationary filter that disproportionately retains non-content tokens (delimiters, whitespace, structural KEY tokens) while evicting answer-carrying VALUE tokens, causing catastrophic accuracy collapse under tight memory budgets.

Method: The authors (1) analyze the role-dependent retention bias of H2O on JSON inputs, quantifying that sink roles carry ~10x more attention energy than content roles and KEY tokens are over-retained at ~1.8x the rate of VALUE tokens; (2) conduct a counterfactual experiment identifying KEY suppression as the most effective deployable filter; (3) propose a retraining-free, role-conditional allocation mechanism over SnapKV's windowed score, using a single tuned hyperparameter to reweight token retention by role; (4) employ a 15 MB linear role probe for token labeling at inference time.

Result: H2O collapses exact-match accuracy from 88% to 0% at a 5% KV cache budget on JSON tasks. The proposed role-conditional method closes 63–98% of this H2O gap at sub-20% budgets. At higher budgets, it modestly matches or exceeds full-cache accuracy, showing a small denoising effect (borderline significant at B=0.50, indistinguishable from zero at B=0.30 over four seeds).

Conclusion: Attention mass is a flawed proxy for token utility on structured inputs. Role-conditional KV cache allocation, informed by a lightweight linear probe, substantially mitigates this failure mode without retraining. However, matching parser-level downstream accuracy remains an open problem.

Abstract: Attention-based KV cache eviction (H2O and its descendants) compresses the memory-constrained state of a long-context model by ranking tokens on accumulated attention mass, treated here as signal energy, and keeping the heaviest. On schema-dense input streams such as nested JSON, this score acts as a non-stationary filter that disproportionately retains noise: a non-content sink role (delimiters or whitespace) carries an order of magnitude more energy than any content role, and structural KEY tokens are over-retained at roughly 1.8x the rate of the answer-carrying VALUE tokens, collapsing exact-match accuracy from 88% to 0% at a 5% budget as the signal-to-noise ratio of the retained state degrades. A counterfactual experiment establishes that suppressing KEY tokens is the best deployable filter. Our retraining-free, role-conditional allocation over SnapKV's windowed score, governed by a single tuned hyperparameter, closes 63-98% of the H2O gap at sub-20% budgets and, at higher budgets, modestly matches or exceeds full-cache accuracy -- a small, seed-sensitive denoising effect (borderline significant at B=0.50; not distinguishable from zero at B=0.30 over four seeds). A 15 MB linear role probe supplies these labels at negligible inference cost, though matching parser-level downstream accuracy remains open.

</details>


### [10] [GSM-Plus-BN: A Perturbation-Based Benchmark for Bangla Mathematical Reasoning in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13248)
*Bidyarthi Paul,Nahida Jannat Mayouree,Md. Asif Karim,Sagar Chandra Nath,Swastika Kundu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces GSM-Plus-BN, the first perturbated Bengali mathematical reasoning benchmark, evaluating six open-source LLMs on 9,000 samples. GPT-OSS-20B achieves the highest seed accuracy (96.08%), while larger models show better perturbation robustness. CoT prompting improves performance, but a gap remains compared to English benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mathematical reasoning evaluation for LLMs has been largely restricted to English, creating inequity for linguistically diverse communities. With over 230 million Bengali speakers and no existing perturbated Bengali math benchmark, there is a critical void in assessing model robustness and genuine comprehension beyond pattern recognition in Bengali.

Method: The authors construct GSM-Plus-BN by translating the English GSM-Plus benchmark into Bengali with human verification. The dataset comprises 1,000 seed questions and 8,000 perturbed variants. Six open-source LLMs (Qwen3-32B, Llama-3.1-8B-Instant, Llama-3.3-70B-Versatile, Llama-4-Scout-17B-16E-Instruct, GPT-OSS-120B, GPT-OSS-20B) are evaluated under both Standard Prompting and Chain-of-Thought (CoT) Prompting across the full 9,000-sample benchmark.

Result: GPT-OSS-20B achieves the highest seed question accuracy of 96.08% under Standard Prompting. Larger models (Llama-3.3-70B, GPT-OSS-120B) exhibit superior robustness across perturbation types. CoT prompting consistently improves reasoning performance over Standard Prompting for most models. However, all models show a notable performance gap compared to their English benchmarks, highlighting the difficulty of perturbed Bengali mathematical text.

Conclusion: GSM-Plus-BN provides a foundational resource and baseline for Bengali mathematical reasoning research. The persistent performance gap relative to English underscores the need for continued work on low-resource language mathematical reasoning and model robustness to linguistic perturbations.

Abstract: The evaluation of mathematical reasoning in large language models (LLMs) has predominantly focused on high-resource languages like English. This has created a significant barrier to the equitable development and deployment of AI in linguistically diverse regions such as Bangladesh, where over 230 million people speak Bengali. Despite this global significance, there has been minimal prior work on mathematical reasoning in Bengali and no existing research that systematically benchmarks a perturbated Bengali mathematical dataset, leaving a critical void in assessing model robustness and true comprehension beyond pattern recognition. This study addresses this gap by introducing GSM-Plus-BN, a novel perturbated Bengali mathematical dataset derived from the English GSM-Plus benchmark and verified by human translators. We evaluate six open-source LLMs Qwen3-32B, Llama-3.1-8B-Instant, Llama-3.3-70B-Versatile, Llama-4-Scout-17B-16E-Instruct, GPT-OSS-120B, and GPT-OSS-20B using a benchmark of 9,000 evaluation samples comprising 1,000 seed questions and 8,000 perturbed variants under both Standard Prompting and Chain-of-Thought (CoT) Prompting. Experimental results show that GPT-OSS-20B achieves the highest seed question accuracy of 96.08% under Standard Prompting, while larger models such as Llama-3.3-70B and GPT-OSS-120B demonstrate superior robustness across perturbation types. Furthermore, CoT prompting substantially improves reasoning for most models compared to Standard Prompting, yet a notable performance gap persists across all models relative to their English benchmarks, underscoring the inherent difficulty of perturbed Bengali text. This research makes a foundational contribution by providing GSM-PLUS-BN as a new resource and baseline for future Bengali mathematical reasoning research.

</details>


### [11] [Discourse-Aware Policy Analysis with Argumentation: A Hybrid LLM-Symbolic Framework for Disaster Governance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13260)
*Stylianos Loukas Vasileiou,Olga Derendiaeva*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Apaf is a hybrid LLM-symbolic pipeline that transforms policy documents into quantitative bipolar argumentation graphs by classifying arguments into deliberative/managerial frames and identifying four frame-mediated relation subtypes, enabling structured, inspectable analysis of implicit tensions in governance texts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Policy documents contain implicit tensions between participatory and managerial governance commitments that are not explicitly stated. Existing NLP methods (e.g., end-to-end summarization) produce fluent but unstructured output that domain experts cannot inspect or contest, and current computational discourse approaches cannot capture frame-mediated relations where one argument narrows or instrumentalizes another.

Method: Apaf operationalizes critical discourse analysis as a quantitative bipolar argumentation framework. The pipeline first classifies arguments into deliberative vs. managerial frames using an LLM, then applies deterministic rules over LLM-extracted features to identify four relation subtypes: agency reduction, agenda shift, instrumental support, and normative support. The output is a structured argument graph. A novel dataset of 100 sub-documents from disaster-risk-reduction policy across the USA, UK, Canada, and Australia was created for evaluation.

Result: The resulting argument graphs are accurate, interpretable, and stable across jurisdictions, demonstrating the pipeline's effectiveness on the novel multi-jurisdiction disaster-risk-reduction policy dataset.

Conclusion: Hybrid LLM-symbolic approaches like Apaf can bridge the gap between fluent generation and structured, contestable analysis, offering a practical tool for making implicit policy tensions visible and auditable to domain experts.

Abstract: Policy documents shape governance outcomes, but their reasoning is often implicit. Participatory commitments and managerial control routinely coexist in the same text, and the tensions between them are rarely stated directly. Existing computational approaches to policy discourse cannot express the frame-mediated relations that drive these tensions, where one argument narrows or instrumentalizes another rather than rejecting it. End-to-end summarization by large language models produces fluent text but offers little structure that domain experts can inspect or contest. We present Apaf, a hybrid LLM--symbolic pipeline that operationalizes critical discourse analysis as a quantitative bipolar argumentation framework over policy text. Arguments are first classified into deliberative or managerial frames. Four frame-mediated relation subtypes (agency reduction, agenda shift, instrumental support, and normative support) are then produced by deterministic rules over LLM-extracted features. We release a novel dataset of 100 sub-documents of disaster-risk-reduction policy from the USA, UK, Canada, and Australia, and show that the resulting argument graphs are accurate, interpretable, and stable across jurisdictions.

</details>


### [12] [Finding the Right Tables and Columns: A Benchmark and Corpus-Adaptive Embeddings for SQL Schema Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13311)
*Qingcheng Zeng,Puxuan Yu,Aman Mehta,Fuheng Zhao,Rajhans Samdani*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper reframes text-to-SQL schema linking as a standalone retrieval task, benchmarks off-the-shelf embedders (which perform poorly), and proposes corpus-adaptive fine-tuning using synthetic queries and hard negative mining, boosting a 305M embedder to match 4–8B models while demonstrating transferability rather than memorization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Schema retrieval — identifying relevant tables and columns from databases with thousands of schema elements — is a critical pre-generation step for enterprise-scale text-to-SQL, yet it lacks first-class evaluation as a standalone retrieval task. Existing embedders transfer poorly to this setting.

Method: The authors recast five text-to-SQL benchmarks (Spider, BIRD, BEAVER, LiveSQLBench) into table- and column-level retrieval tasks under two document representations. They propose corpus-adaptive fine-tuning: (1) natural-language queries are synthesized directly from the target schema corpus, (2) granularity-aware hard negatives are mined, and (3) a 305M-parameter embedder is contrastively fine-tuned. The same recipe is also applied to an 8B embedder. Leave-one-corpus-out experiments and leakage audits validate transferability.

Result: The 305M model's average recall@10 rises from 60.4 to 75.6 (nDCG@10 from 51.9 to 68.0), making it the strongest sub-1B retriever and competitive with 4–8B state-of-the-art embedders. The 8B embedder improves from 77.8 to 78.4 recall@10. Gains reflect genuine schema-retrieval ability, not memorization.

Conclusion: Schema linking is established as a standalone retrieval task. Lightweight, label-free corpus adaptation is a practical, backbone-agnostic route to deploying schema retrieval at enterprise scale.

Abstract: Retrieval in the SQL setting has largely been studied as the task of finding, within a large collection of SQL statements, the statement that answers a natural-language question. At scale, however, a more fundamental retrieval problem precedes generation: schema retrieval, identifying the tables and columns a question requires in a database that may contain thousands of them, far more than fit in a model's context. We argue that this step warrants first-class evaluation. To this end, we recast five text-to-SQL datasets (Spider, BIRD, BEAVER, and two LiveSQLBench variants) as retrieval tasks at both table and column granularity, covering realistic and enterprise-scale schemas under two document representations, and we show that off-the-shelf text and code embedders transfer poorly to this setting. We then propose corpus-adaptive fine-tuning: natural-language queries are synthesized directly from the target schema corpus, granularity-aware hard negatives are mined, and a 305M-parameter embedder is fine-tuned contrastively. This procedure raises average recall@10 from 60.4 to 75.6 (nDCG@10 from 51.9 to 68.0), making the 305M model the strongest retriever under one billion parameters and competitive with state-of-the-art embedders of 4-8B parameters, more than an order of magnitude larger. The same recipe improves an 8B state-of-the-art embedder from 77.8 to 78.4 recall@10, matching the best result on the benchmark and indicating that the adaptation is backbone-agnostic. Leave-one-corpus-out experiments and a leakage audit show that these gains reflect a transferable schema-retrieval ability rather than memorization of the evaluation data. Our results establish schema linking as a standalone retrieval task and lightweight, label-free corpus adaptation as a practical route to deploying it at enterprise scale.

</details>


### [13] [Meta-Learning Preferences for Multilingual LLM Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13315)
*Jiaying Lin,Seongho Son,Nam Phuong Tran,Long Tran-thanh,Ilija Bogunovic,Debmalya Mandal*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A meta-learning framework for RLHF and DPO enables low-resource language alignment by transferring knowledge from high-resource languages using as few as 100 target-language preference samples, achieving up to 28% win-rate improvement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Human preference data for aligning LLMs is unequally distributed across languages, creating a severe scarcity in low-resource languages. Existing alignment methods (RLHF, DPO) require substantial preference data that is simply unavailable for most languages, motivating a transfer learning approach.

Method: A meta-learning framework applied to both RLHF and DPO settings. The method learns a transferable initialization from preference data in high-resource languages (meta-training), which can then be rapidly adapted to a target low-resource language using minimal preference samples. Theoretical guarantees are provided for both meta-reward modeling and meta-policy optimization.

Result: In extremely low-resource settings with only 100 target-language samples, the approach achieves up to 28% win-rate improvements over baselines. Consistent outperformance is demonstrated across multiple target languages, model scales, different meta-training language combinations, and varying linguistic distances from target languages.

Conclusion: Meta-learning provides an effective strategy for cross-lingual transfer of alignment, enabling robust multilingual LLM alignment even under severe data scarcity, with both empirical and theoretical support.

Abstract: Unequal availability of human preference data across languages poses a significant challenge for aligning large language models in multilingual settings. To address the lack of sufficient data in low-resource language alignment, we propose a meta-learning framework for Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback and Direct Preference Optimization. By leveraging preference data from other languages, our framework learns a transferable initialization that enables effective adaptation to a target language with minimal data. We provide theoretical guarantees for both the meta-reward modeling and meta-policy optimization settings, and empirically demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach on multilingual benchmarks. In an extremely low-resource setting with only 100 target-language preference samples, our approach achieves up to $28\%$ win-rate improvements over baseline methods, and consistently outperforms baselines across multiple target languages and model scales. Our approaches retain these advantages across different combinations of meta-training languages and varying linguistic distances from the target languages.

</details>


### [14] [Evaluation Ability Does Not Imply Optimization Utility: LLM-as-a-Judge Signals in Closed-Loop Table Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13347)
*Donghwan Kim*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper investigates LLM-as-a-judge in iterative refinement for table recognition, finding that judge scores are weak (ties, irreproducible rankings, failure to identify better candidates), severe performance losses occur under unconstrained regeneration, and a structure-preserving constraint reduces catastrophic failures but not without tradeoffs—demonstrating that evaluation ability does not guarantee optimization utility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-as-a-judge is widely deployed for feedback and selection in closed-loop regeneration pipelines, yet its effectiveness remains insufficiently validated. The authors aim to rigorously test this paradigm using table recognition as a controlled testbed where deterministic TEDS (Tree-Edit-Distance-based Similarity) evaluation provides ground-truth comparison.

Method: The study employs FinTabNet and OmniDocBench datasets with TEDS as the deterministic metric. Three experimental dimensions are examined: (1) judge signal quality assessed through score ties, ranking reproducibility, and selection policy performance (comparing judge-guided selection against random baselines, including an earliest-iteration tie rule); (2) the effect of a structure-preserving instruction on severe-loss rates under unconstrained regeneration; and (3) an exploratory 2x2 analysis examining the interaction between the structure-preservation constraint and judge feedback.

Result: Three key findings: (1) Judge signals are weak—frequent score ties, non-reproducible rankings, and the only policy outperforming random relied on an earliest-iteration tie rule, meaning the advantage cannot be attributed to judge scores. Iteration generates better candidates but the judge fails to identify them. (2) Severe losses occur even without specific judge feedback; a structure-preserving instruction significantly reduces severe-loss rates on FinTabNet with directional consistency on OmniDocBench, implicating target-preservation failure under unconstrained regeneration as the proximate mechanism. (3) The structure-preservation constraint reduces the severe-loss tail but yields no overall improvement; in a 2x2 analysis, this protective effect becomes unstable when judge feedback is retained.

Conclusion: Evaluation ability does not imply optimization utility. Iterative refinement requires, at minimum, a verification signal that deterministically detects structural change rather than relying on judge scores alone. The results do not dispute the value of LLMs as evaluators but highlight fundamental limitations in using them as optimization signals for closed-loop regeneration.

Abstract: LLM-as-a-judge is widely used to provide feedback and selection signals in closedloop regeneration, but this use remains insufficiently validated. We study it in table recognition, where deterministic TEDS evaluation provides a controlled testbed, using FinTabNet and OmniDocBench. Three findings emerge. First, judge signals were weak on both datasets: scores frequently tied, rankings were not reproducible, and the only selection policy that beat random on both datasets depended on an earliest-iteration tie rule, so its advantage cannot be attributed to the judge scores alone. Iteration produced better candidates, but the judge failed to recover them. Second, severe losses occurred even without specific judge feedback. A structurepreserving instruction significantly reduced the severe-loss rate on FinTabNet and was directionally consistent on OmniDocBench. The contrasts support target-preservation failure under unconstrained regeneration as a proximate mechanism of the observed severe losses. Third, the structure-preservation constraint reduced the severe-loss tail but produced no improvement. In an exploratory 2x2 analysis, the same protection was not stably observed when judge feedback was retained. These results do not dispute the value of LLMs as evaluators. Instead, they show that evaluation ability does not imply optimization utility. Iterative refinement requires, at minimum, a verification signal that deterministically detects structural change, rather than judge scores alone.

</details>


### [15] [A POS Tier Is the Key to Automated Annotation for Low-Resource Language Documentation: Neural Interlinear Glossing for Irabu, a Southern Ryukyuan Language](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13372)
*Michinori Shimoji*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A neural annotation pipeline (BiLSTM-CRF) for Irabu Ryukyuan demonstrates that including a POS tier significantly improves glossing accuracy, especially under extreme low-resource constraints (~1 hour of annotated discourse). While gold-standard POS tags yield substantial gains (+4.4 points, growing as data shrinks), automatic POS tagging errors currently prevent full realization of this benefit in a pipeline, though the latent value recovers as tagger accuracy approaches 92-96%. The paper recommends quadrilateral annotation (text, POS, gloss, translation) for documentary practice.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Interlinearized text production for field linguistics is prohibitively expensive (~1 hour per minute of recording), and for endangered languages like Irabu Ryukyuan, time with native speakers is critically limited. Automating parts of this workflow has direct documentary value, but the challenge is achieving usable accuracy under realistic extreme low-resource conditions.

Method: A full neural annotation pipeline (morpheme segmentation, POS tagging, glossing) is implemented using small, transparent BiLSTM-CRF models on Irabu Ryukyuan, constrained to ~1 hour of fully annotated discourse. Two factors are manipulated: annotation richness (with/without POS tier) and training data quantity (6-47 minutes). Controlled noise experiments systematically degrade gold POS tags to measure the relationship between tagger accuracy and downstream glossing gain.

Result: Gold POS tags improve glossing by +4.4 (SD 0.7) points across all seeds, with gains growing as data shrink (+11.6 points at 25% data). A POS tier more than halves the glossed data needed for a given accuracy. In the automatic pipeline, the tagger's 12% error rate means incorrect POS misleads glossing more than no POS, so the gain is latent. Controlled degradation shows the gain returns as tagger accuracy rises, with break-even at ~88% and +1.6 to +3.2 points recovered at 92-96%.

Conclusion: The paper recommends quadrilateral annotation (text, POS, gloss, translation) as standard documentary practice, as the POS tier provides substantial latent value for downstream tasks that materializes as automatic tagger accuracy improves — a trajectory the authors expect to continue.

Abstract: Discourse data are the primary empirical basis of grammar writing in field linguistics, but producing interlinearized text is notoriously expensive - on the order of one hour of work per minute of recording. For endangered languages, where the time remaining to verify analyses with native speakers is itself limited, automating parts of the interlinearization workflow has direct documentary value. We implement a full neural annotation pipeline (morpheme segmentation, POS tagging, glossing) for Irabu Ryukyuan using deliberately small, transparent BiLSTM-CRF models, and evaluate it under a realistic hard constraint: approximately one hour of fully annotated discourse as the entire supervised resource. Two factors of the annotation itself are manipulated: its richness (with or without a POS tier) and its quantity (training budgets from 6 to 47 minutes). Gold POS improves grammatical glossing by +4.4 (SD 0.7) points (significant in all 5 seeds), and the gain grows as data shrink (+11.6 points at a quarter of the data); a POS tier more than halves the amount of glossed data needed to reach a given accuracy. In a fully automatic pipeline this gain is not yet realized: the tagger still errs on 12% of morphemes, and an incorrect POS misleads the glossing model more than no POS at all. The value is latent rather than lost: degrading gold POS with controlled noise shows the gain returning as tagger accuracy rises, with break-even near our tagger's current 88% and +1.6 to +3.2 points recovered at 92-96%. We conclude with a concrete recommendation for documentation practice: annotate quadrilinearly - text, POS, gloss, translation.

</details>


### [16] [GFlowRL: Scaling Distribution-Matching RL to Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13394)
*Xiaodong Liu,Michael Xu,Jack W. Stokes,Paul Smolensky,Doug Burger,Jianfeng Gao*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: GFlowRL removes the learned partition function from GFlowNet-style RL, replacing it with an in-batch Monte Carlo estimate, and adds importance-sampling correction and asymmetric flow-gap clipping, achieving stable scaling up to 235B parameters and SOTA results on math, code, and adversarial benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scaling GFlowNet-style RL to modern post-training pipelines is challenging because the learned prompt-conditional partition function introduces gradient instability and engineering overhead as model size, rollout horizon, reward noise, and distributed complexity increase. The authors aim to demonstrate that this partition function is unnecessary and can be replaced with a simpler alternative.

Method: GFlowRL replaces the auxiliary partition network with an in-batch Monte Carlo estimate from the rollout group. It incorporates two stabilizers: (1) importance-sampling correction to address rollout/trainer drift, and (2) asymmetric flow-gap clipping to handle outlier residuals. This streamlined algorithm preserves the reward-distribution-matching objective of GFlowNets.

Result: GFlowRL achieves a Codeforces rating of 2048 at 14B scale (within 25 Elo of o3-mini), the highest average ASR@1 on AdvBench and HarmBench, and outperforms prior SOTA multi-turn attackers. It scales stably across both dense and MoE architectures up to 235B parameters, where prior GFlowNet-style methods (FlowRL) diverge.

Conclusion: The learned partition function in GFlowNet-style RL is replaceable by a simpler in-batch Monte Carlo estimate. With appropriate stabilization, GFlowRL is the first GFlowNet-style algorithm to scale stably across both dense and sparse architectures, matching or exceeding strong baselines on math, code, and safety benchmarks.

Abstract: Generative Flow Networks (GFlowNets) offer a promising alternative to reward-maximizing reinforcement learning (RL) for large reasoning models, encouraging diverse reasoning paths by matching reward distributions rather than collapsing to dominant modes. Recent work shows promise on math and code, but scaling GFlowNet-style RL to modern post-training pipelines remains difficult: as model size, rollout horizon, reward noise, and distributed-systems complexity grow together, a learned prompt-conditional partition function becomes a source of gradient instability and engineering overhead rather than a useful normalizer. Through systematic analysis, we find that the learned partition function, previously treated as essential, can be replaced by an in-batch Monte Carlo estimate computed from the rollout group already required for training. We propose GFlowRL, a streamlined GFlowNet-style RL algorithm that removes the auxiliary partition network entirely while preserving the reward-distribution-matching objective, completed by two stabilizers: importance-sampling correction for rollout/trainer drift and asymmetric flow-gap clipping for outlier residuals. GFlowRL exceeds all counterparts on math, code, and adversarial red-teaming benchmarks, reaching a Codeforces rating of 2048 at the 14B scale (within 25 Elo of o3-mini) and attaining the highest average ASR@1 on AdvBench and HarmBench, outperforming the previous SOTA multi-turn attacker in a regime where FlowRL, a prior GFlowNet-style method, diverges. The same recipe transfers to all evaluated MoE configurations up to 235B parameters, where FlowRL again fails to converge. To our knowledge, GFlowRL is the first GFlowNet-style RL algorithm to scale stably across both dense and sparse architectures. Code will be at: https://github.com/microsoft/gflowrl

</details>


### [17] [Demystifying On-Policy Distillation: Roles, Pathologies, and Regulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13399)
*Rui Wang,Hongru Wang,Yi Chen,Boyang Xue,Tianqing Fang,Wenhao Yu,Kam-Fai Wong*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: On-policy distillation (OPD) acts as an exploration catalyst in LLM post-training by providing dense token-level guidance, but its effectiveness depends entirely on signal quality. Two pathologies — Student-Teacher Mismatch and Length Exploitation — derail exploration. Lightweight signal regulations (advantage clipping, log-scale compression) mitigate these issues and outperform existing baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite OPD becoming a key paradigm in LLM post-training, its training dynamics are poorly understood. The authors aim to systematically clarify OPD's role, identify failure modes (pathologies), and propose practical regulations to ensure effective distillation.

Method: The authors conduct a systematic study of OPD dynamics: (1) they establish OPD's role as an exploration catalyst via dense token-level guidance, using controlled experiments varying prompt diversity vs. per-problem sampling; (2) they identify two pathologies — Student-Teacher Mismatch (misalignment from large distributional gaps) and Length Exploitation (length-dependent shortcuts from aggregated token-level objectives); (3) they propose lightweight signal regulations — advantage clipping and log-scale compression — to correct the guiding signal. Experiments span seven benchmarks against OPD variants and RLVR baselines.

Result: Prompt diversity matters more than per-problem sampling numbers for exploration. OPD's effectiveness hinges entirely on guiding signal quality. The two identified pathologies demonstrably derail exploration. Advantage clipping and log-scale compression successfully alleviate length exploitation, enabling stable and effective distillation that surpasses OPD variants and RLVR baselines across seven benchmarks.

Conclusion: Well-regulated signal quality, rather than mere teacher scale, governs successful exploration in OPD. Lightweight regulations like advantage clipping and log-scale compression are sufficient to tame pathologies and achieve state-of-the-art distillation performance.

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) has become a key paradigm in LLM post-training, yet its training dynamics remain poorly understood. We present a systematic study examining the role, pathologies, and regulations of OPD. We first clarify the role of OPD as an exploration catalyst: it steers the student toward correct reasoning paths via dense token-level guidance, without expanding capability ceiling. We confirm this by showing that prompt diversity matters more than per-problem sampling numbers, and critically, that the effectiveness of OPD hinges entirely on the quality of its guiding signal. This dependency exposes two pathologies that derail exploration. The Student-Teacher Mismatch occurs when a large teacher-student distributional gap causes the guiding signal to misalign with task correctness, steering exploration in counterproductive directions. Length Exploitation arises when the aggregated token-level objective creates length-dependent shortcuts, allowing the student to game the reward landscape through response truncation or redundant padding, exploring degenerate length modes rather than reasoning strategies. To tame these pathologies, we investigate lightweight signal regulations: advantage clipping and log-scale compression, ensuring exploration is guided by faithful signals. Experiments across seven benchmarks demonstrate that these regulations alleviate length exploitation and enable effective distillation, stably surpassing OPD variants and RLVR baselines, thereby confirming that well-regulated signal quality, rather than mere teacher scale, governs successful exploration in OPD.

</details>


### [18] [Exploring Post-Training Alignment of Small Language Models for Biomedical Data-to-Text Generation: A Case Study of Medication Leaflet](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13430)
*Xi Yang,Guodong Liu,Chuqin Li,Fan Wu,Ergin Soysal,Min Jiang,Xing He,Jiang Bian,Yi Guo,Shams Zaman,Thomas Fuchs,Todd Sanger,Yonghui Wu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A comparative study of post-training alignment methods (SFT, DPO, ORPO, GRPO) applied to small language models for biomedical text generation, showing SLMs can outperform GPT-5 on medicine package leaflet generation and cross-dataset generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Biomedical informatics requires translating complex data into patient-friendly narratives. While large proprietary models exist, there is a need to evaluate whether small language models (SLMs) with appropriate post-training can achieve competitive or superior performance in specialized biomedical data-to-text tasks, offering more efficient and deployable solutions.

Method: The study trains Qwen-based small language models using four post-training methods: SFT, DPO, ORPO, and GRPO. Evaluation is conducted on a medicine package leaflets dataset using lexical overlap metrics (ROUGE) and semantic similarity measures. Cross-dataset generalizability is assessed using curated drug label data from openFDA. Comparisons are made against the proprietary GPT-5 model.

Result: Three key findings: (1) aligned SLMs outperform proprietary GPT-5; (2) ORPO surpasses SFT baselines; (3) GRPO achieves the most robust cross-dataset generalization among all alignment methods tested and GPT-5.

Conclusion: Small language models, when properly aligned with techniques like ORPO and GRPO, can match or exceed large proprietary models in specialized biomedical text generation. GRPO in particular offers superior cross-dataset robustness, suggesting SLMs are a viable, efficient alternative for biomedical data-to-text applications.

Abstract: Translating complex biomedical data into patient-friendly narratives is central to modern biomedical informatics. This study presents a comparative analysis of training small language models (SLMs) in specialized biomedical datato-text generation tasks. We explore widely adopted post-training methods including supervised fine-tuning (SFT), direct preference optimization (DPO), odds ratio preference optimization (ORPO), and group relative policy optimization (GRPO) with Qwen-based SLMs on a medicine package leaflets dataset. To assess cross-dataset generalizability, we also curated drug label data from openFDA. We evaluate models using both standard lexical overlap metrics like ROUGE as well as semantic similarity measures. Across our experiments, the results show that (1) the aligned SLMs outperform proprietary models like GPT-5; (2) ORPO outperforms the SFTbaselines; (3) GRPO yields the most robust cross-dataset performance among the alignment methods tested as well as GPT-5.

</details>


### [19] [When Rubrics Change: Cross-Rubric Generalization for Critical Thinking Essay Scoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13433)
*Nischal Ashok Kumar,Payu Wittawatolarn,Sana Kang,Marisa C. Peczuh,Blair Lehman,Ryan Baker,Caitlin Mills,Sherry Lachman,Ruochen Sun,Andrew Lan*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper introduces cross-rubric generalization for automated essay scoring, where models are trained on essays scored under one rubric and evaluated on unseen rubrics targeting different essay aspects. An LLM fine-tuning framework using rubric-agnostic intermediate representations ("traits") and target-essay supervision improves macro F1 by 5.0% over baselines, with an open-source Llama model approaching GPT-4 performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior AES research focuses on cross-prompt generalization, assuming fixed scoring rubrics. In real educational settings, educators frequently revise or create new rubrics to assess different essay dimensions. This paper addresses the underexplored problem of cross-rubric generalization — training under one rubric set and evaluating on entirely new rubrics targeting different aspects of writing.

Method: The authors propose an LLM fine-tuning framework with two key components: (1) rubric-agnostic intermediate representations called "traits" that abstract away from specific rubric formulations, and (2) target-essay supervision, where the model receives labels for seen rubrics during training to learn transferable scoring capabilities. Experiments use an AES dataset augmented with multiple rubric-defined labels of student critical thinking skills. The approach is evaluated across settings varying the visibility of target rubrics and target essays during training, with comparisons against GPT-4-mini prompting and GPT-4.

Result: Traits improve macro F1 by 5.0% over a no-trait baseline in the hardest setting (both target rubrics and target essays unseen). Increasing target-essay supervision consistently improves performance. The best fine-tuned open-source Llama-based model outperforms GPT-4-mini prompting by 2.1% macro F1 and trails GPT-4 by only 1.9%.

Conclusion: Trait-based intermediate representations combined with controlled target-essay supervision effectively enable generalization to unseen rubrics. This demonstrates that structured abstraction and supervision design are key to building AES systems that can adapt to evolving educator scoring criteria, narrowing the gap between open-source models and frontier proprietary LLMs.

Abstract: Automated essay scoring (AES) research has largely focused on cross-prompt generalization, where essays from unseen prompts are scored while the scoring criteria are typically held constant. In practice, however, educators may revise or even introduce new rubrics in their scoring task, to evaluate different aspects of essays. We study cross-rubric generalization: training on essays labeled under one set of rubrics and evaluating on previously unseen rubrics, which target different aspects of the essay. We use a Large Language Model (LLM) fine-tuning framework with two components: rubric-agnostic intermediate representations, called traits, and target-essay supervision under seen rubrics during training. On an AES dataset augmented with multiple rubric-defined labels of student critical thinking skills, we find that traits improve macro F1 by 5.0% over a baseline without traits in the hardest setting, where both target rubrics and target essays are unseen during training. We further find that increasing target-essay supervision improves performance, with our best fine-tuned open-source Llama-based model outperforming GPT-5-mini prompting by 2.1% macro F1 and trailing GPT-5 by 1.9%. These results show that trait-based intermediate structure and controlled supervision improve generalization to unseen rubrics.

</details>


### [20] [Live Gurbani Tracking: A Benchmark and Reference System for Captioning Sikh Kirtan](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13457)
*Karanbir Singh*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A benchmark for live captioning of Sikh Kirtan, formalized as predicting exact (shabad_id, line_idx) pairs from canonical scripture. The paper releases 12 evaluation cases, a frame-accuracy scorer, and a reference system achieving 57.9% frame accuracy on the hardest live×blind variant.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard ASR and open-vocabulary lyrics transcription are unsuitable for Sikh Kirtan captioning because any misspelled Gurmukhi text is religiously inappropriate. The task requires exact, word-for-word lines from the Sri Guru Granth Sahib Ji (SGGS), making it a closed-vocabulary retrieval problem rather than a transcription problem. Existing metrics (WER/CER) measure transcription accuracy, not the display accuracy this task demands.

Method: The task is formalized as predicting (shabad_id, line_idx) or null at each time t. The problem space is organized as a 2×2 matrix: live vs. offline (causal vs. full-audio) and blind vs. oracle (shabad identity discovered vs. given). The reference system uses a fine-tuned 120M IndicConformer → fuzzy matcher → state machine pipeline, quantized to INT8 ONNX with RTF ~0.05 on one Apple Silicon core. The benchmark includes 4 hand-annotated recordings × 3 cold-start offsets = 12 evaluation cases (~57 min), scored at 1s resolution with a 1s collar and gap-tolerant boundary scoring.

Result: The reference system achieves 57.9% overall frame accuracy across all 12 cases on the live×blind variant, with 10/12 correct shabad locks. Three trivial baselines are provided: empty, shifted-5s, and perfect.

Conclusion: The benchmark, reference system, and live deployment are released under permissive licenses, establishing a foundation for further work on closed-vocabulary, display-accuracy-oriented captioning of sacred sung recitation where transcription errors are unacceptable.

Abstract: We present a benchmark and reference system for live captioning of Sikh Kirtan - the continuous, sung recitation of verses from the Sri Guru Granth Sahib Ji (SGGS). Unlike open-vocabulary lyrics transcription, Kirtan captioning is a closed-vocabulary problem: every displayed line must be an exact, word-for-word line from the canonical scripture, because displaying misspelled Gurmukhi is considered religiously inappropriate. We formalize the task as predicting, at every time t, a pair (shabad_id, line_idx) or null, and organize the problem space into a 2x2 matrix along two orthogonal axes: live vs. offline (causal vs. full-audio access) and blind vs. oracle (shabad identity discovered vs. given). We release v1 of the benchmark - 4 hand-annotated Kirtan recordings x 3 cold-start offsets = 12 evaluation cases, ~57 minutes of scored audio - together with a scorer that computes frame accuracy at 1s resolution over a scored region, with a 1s collar and gap-tolerant scoring at segment boundaries. We describe a reference system (fine-tuned 120M IndicConformer -> fuzzy matcher -> state machine; INT8 ONNX; RTF ~0.05 on one Apple Silicon core) that achieves 57.9% overall frame accuracy across all 12 cases (10/12 correct shabad locks) on the hardest variant (live x blind). We compare against three trivial baselines (empty, shifted-5s, perfect) and discuss why standard ASR metrics (WER/CER) measure transcription accuracy rather than the display accuracy this task requires. The benchmark, reference system, and a live deployment are released under permissive licenses to facilitate further improvements.

</details>


### [21] [MyAG: A Graph-Based Framework for Designing and Analyzing Composable LLM Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13474)
*Zhisong Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: MyAG is a graph-based framework for building composable LLM agent systems using three graph abstractions (component, workflow, search) that enable flexible reuse, hierarchical composition, and performance analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM agent frameworks often tightly couple component definitions with execution strategies, limiting flexibility and reuse. There is a need for a principled separation of concerns that allows the same components to be composed with different strategies, analyzed for tradeoffs, and hierarchically nested.

Method: MyAG introduces three graph abstractions: (1) a **component graph** defining agents, environments, and modules as nodes; (2) a **workflow graph** specifying execution control flow; and (3) a **search graph** capturing runtime execution traces. It supports recursive system nodes for hierarchical composition and includes monitoring/visualization tools.

Result: Experiments on representative agent applications demonstrate that MyAG supports flexible agent system design and enables analysis of performance-efficiency tradeoffs through its graph-based abstractions.

Conclusion: MyAG provides a modular, open-source framework that decouples component definition from execution strategy, facilitating flexible and analyzable LLM agent system construction.

Abstract: We present MyAG, a graph-based framework for designing and analyzing composable LLM agent systems. Our framework separates agent system construction into three graph abstractions: a component graph for agents, environments, and modules; a workflow graph for execution control; and a search graph for runtime execution. This separation allows users to flexibly reuse the same components with different strategies. We further support hierarchical composition through recursive system nodes and provide monitoring and visualization tools for inspecting agent execution. Experiments on representative agent applications show that our framework supports flexible agent system design and helps analyze performance-efficiency tradeoffs. Our framework is publicly available and fully open-source.

</details>


### [22] [Cost-Pragmatic Quality Gating and Selection-Fusion Multi-Model Combiners for BioASQ Phases A+ and B](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13551)
*Dima Galat,Marian-Andrei Rizoiu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper describes a BioASQ Task 14B 2026 system featuring two key innovations: (1) a dual retrieval pipeline with a hybrid first stage (dense + BM25 + RRF) and an agent-driven pipeline, using a BGE cross-encoder quality gate for selective re-retrieval, and (2) a decomposition of multi-model ensemble lift into selection and fusion components to guide answer aggregation strategy. The system achieved top rankings on multiple leaderboard metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Improve biomedical question answering by addressing two core challenges: when and how aggressively to re-retrieve documents when first-stage retrieval is weak, and how to optimally combine answers from multiple language models in Phase B answering, moving beyond ad-hoc ensembling toward principled aggregation.

Method: (1) Retrieval: A union of two parallel pipelines — a hybrid first stage (dense BGE + BM25 + Reciprocal Rank Fusion, R@200 = 99.3%) and an agent-driven pipeline that decomposes questions across PubMed, Europe PMC, and iCite. A BGE cross-encoder quality gate identifies weakly-supported questions for cost-pragmatic selective re-retrieval. (2) Phase B Answering: Multi-model ensemble lift is formally decomposed into a selection component (bounded by per-question oracle performance) and a fusion component (where aggregators can exceed individual models). This decomposition predicts that LLM-as-judge excels on selection-dominated metrics (yes/no, multi-reference ROUGE) while being structurally limited on fusion-friendly metrics (factoid rank-1, list recall). A synonym-union resolver is employed for list recall.

Result: On Task 12B 2024 validation, the cost-pragmatic re-retrieval policy significantly outperforms a skill-strict baseline on list F1 and list precision at 12% lower cost. On Task 13B test, list F1 rises by +0.132 absolute, indicating retrieval headroom. On Task 13B 2025, the synonym-union resolver wins list recall on every head; GPT-5.5 solo retains the list-F1 lead. On Task 14B 2026 preliminary leaderboard, the team ranks first on the combined-exact aggregate for three of eight leaderboards, wins four individual question-type cells, and takes #1 on Phase B b3 ideal.

Conclusion: Principled retrieval re-retrieval policies and formal ensemble decomposition provide measurable gains. The decomposition framework predicts aggregation strategy effectiveness per metric type without running experiments, and the synonym-union resolver excels at recall while precision remains a trade-off best served by single strong models like GPT-5.5.

Abstract: We describe our BioASQ Task 14B 2026 system. The work centers on two design decisions: how aggressively to re-retrieve when first-stage retrieval is weak, and how to combine multiple language-model answers.
