Retrieval unions two parallel pipelines - a hybrid first stage (dense BGE + BM25 + RRF, reaching R@200 = 99.3% on the BioASQ-13b historical archive) and an agent-driven pipeline that decomposes the question over PubMed, Europe PMC, and iCite - with a BGE cross-encoder quality gate flagging weakly-supported questions for selective re-retrieval. On Task 12B 2024 validation, a cost-pragmatic re-retrieval policy beats a skill-strict baseline significantly on list F1 and list precision, at 12% lower re-retrieval cost. Holding prompt and model fixed across val and test 13B (different question sets), list F1 rises by +0.132 absolute on the BioASQ-released gold-input pool, consistent with substantial retrieval-side headroom. For Phase B answering we decompose multi-model ensemble lift into a selection component bounded by the per-question oracle and a fusion component that aggregators can exceed. The decomposition predicts before any experiment that LLM-as-judge wins on selection-dominated metrics (yes/no, multi-reference ROUGE) but is structurally insufficient on the recall component of fusion-friendly metrics (factoid rank-1, list recall). On Task 13B 2025 our synonym-union resolver wins list recall on every head, while GPT-5.5 solo retains the list-F1 lead because the resolver's wider item set costs precision. On the Task 14B 2026 preliminary leaderboard our team places first on the combined-exact aggregate on three of the eight (phase x batch) leaderboards, wins four individual question-type cells, and takes #1 on Phase B b3 ideal.

</details>


### [23] [Graded Entity-Familiarity Readouts in Language Models: Polish Adaptation, Cross-Language Robustness, and Refusal Steering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13568)
*Grzegorz Brzezinka*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Probing the final prompt token's activations in 12 instruction-tuned LLMs reveals a graded entity-familiarity signal that separates real from fabricated entities, tracks popularity in Polish-adapted models robust to prompt language, can be steered to modulate refusal rates, and serves as a competitive pre-generation abstention gate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether language models possess an internal, pre-generation estimate of entity familiarity, and whether this representational signal can be disentangled from the policy that governs refusal behavior.

Method: The authors curated a dataset of 1,440 Polish entities (across four domains and ten Wikipedia-pageview popularity deciles) plus fabricated controls. They trained linear probes on the final prompt-token activations from twelve instruction-tuned models (Bielik, PLLuM, Gemma-4, Qwen3 families) to predict entity familiarity. They further conducted prompt-language robustness tests (Polish vs. English stems), activation steering experiments (adding a familiarity direction to modulate refusal in Gemma-4-12B), and compared familiarity probes against post-generation detectors as abstention gates.

Result: Familiarity-probe scores consistently separated real from fabricated entities across all model families. In Polish-adapted families (Bielik, PLLuM), scores also tracked entity popularity (Spearman ρ 0.28–0.57), while non-Polish-adapted models showed at most 0.11. Prompt-language manipulation retained 96–101% of within-language AUROC. Steering with a one-dimensional familiarity direction in Gemma-4-12B monotonically shifted refusal rates (0.24→1.00 for well-known entities; 0.73→0.00 for unknown). Familiarity probes proved competitive as pre-generation abstention gates, though post-generation detectors better predicted behavioral error on average.

Conclusion: Language models harbor a graded, pre-generation entity-familiarity readout that is linearly accessible from activations. This representational familiarity signal is dissociable from the downstream refusal policy, suggesting a two-stage process: familiarity encoding followed by a behavioral decision to abstain or answer.

Abstract: Can a language model estimate its familiarity with an entity before generating an answer? We study activations at the final prompt token in twelve instruction-tuned models from the Bielik, PLLuM, Gemma-4, and Qwen3 families, using a new dataset of 1,440 Polish entities spanning four domains and ten Wikipedia-pageview deciles, plus fabricated controls. Familiarity-probe scores separate real from fabricated entities in every family; in the Polish-adapted Bielik and PLLuM families they additionally track entity popularity (model-mean Spearman $ρ$ 0.28-0.57, versus at most 0.11 in Gemma-4 and Qwen3), a pattern more strongly associated with Polish adaptation than with parameter count in this model sample. In a paired experiment on two families, probes retain 96-101% of within-language AUROC when the Polish question stem is replaced with an English one around unchanged entity names, showing robustness to prompt language in this setting. In Gemma-4-12B, the only model that natively refuses, adding a one-dimensional familiarity direction at a single layer moves refusal rates monotonically in both directions (0.24 to 1.00 on well-known entities; 0.73 to 0.00 on unknown ones). Finally, a calibrated familiarity probe is competitive among pre-generation abstention gates, although post-generation detectors better predict behavioral error on average. These results support a graded pre-generation entity-familiarity readout, and a separation between representational familiarity and the policy that converts it into abstention.

</details>


### [24] [Memory as a Controlled Process: Learned Adaptive Memory Management for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13591)
*Eric Hanchen Jiang,Zhi Zhang,Yuchen Wu,Levina Li,Dong Liu,Xiao Liang,Rui Sun,Yubei Li,Edward Sun,Haozheng Luo,Zhaolu Kang,Aylin Caliskan,Kai-Wei Chang,Ying Nian Wu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: MemCon frames memory operations in LLM agents as a Markov Decision Process, learning an adaptive online policy (contextual bandit with UCB) that decides when/what/how much to retrieve, inject, consolidate, or forget—outperforming static heuristics by up to 15.2 points while reducing token use by 5–20%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM agent memory systems use fixed, hand-designed retrieval heuristics, but optimal memory behavior is inherently context-dependent: early tasks need minimal retrieval, recurring goals benefit from plan reuse, stuck agents need alternative queries, and long task streams require consolidation/pruning. This static approach is a core bottleneck for agentic learning.

Method: MemCon models memory operations as a Markov Decision Process and learns an online policy via a lightweight tabular contextual bandit with UCB exploration. It is backend-agnostic, wrapping any existing memory implementation, and uses only task-by-task binary feedback—no pretraining, no extra LLM calls. It adaptively controls retrieval timing/quantity, distilled plan injection, and memory consolidation/forgetting.

Result: Across 6 benchmarks, 3 agent frameworks, and 3 LLM backbones, MemCon consistently outperforms multiple memory baselines by up to 15.2 points in task success rate while reducing token consumption by 5–20%.

Conclusion: Treating memory as an adaptive controlled process rather than a static store yields significant gains in both task performance and efficiency. The lightweight, feedback-driven, backend-agnostic design makes MemCon practical for real-world deployment across diverse agent architectures.

Abstract: Large Language Model (LLM) agents increasingly rely on external memory systems to accumulate experience across tasks. Yet nearly all existing approaches, from graph-structured memories to reflective insight stores, access memory through fixed, hand-designed heuristics. We argue that this static view of memory is a core bottleneck for agentic learning because optimal memory behavior is fundamentally context-dependent. The early stages of the tasks, benefit from minimal retrieval because memory is sparse; recurring goal types benefit from plan reuse rather than generic nearest-neighbor lookup; stuck agents benefit from re-retrieval with alternative queries; and across long task streams, the memory store itself must be consolidated and pruned to remain useful. We present Memory as a Controlled Process (MemCon), a framework that models memory operations as a Markov Decision Process and learns an online policy that adaptively decides when, what, and how much to retrieve, when to inject a distilled plan, and when to consolidate or forget. MemCon is backend-agnostic: it wraps any existing memory implementation, learns from task-by-task binary feedback with no pretraining and no additional LLM calls, and uses a lightweight tabular contextual bandit with UCB exploration that converges within tens of tasks. Across 6 benchmarks, 3 agent frameworks, and 3 LLM backbones, MemCon consistently outperforms multiple memory baselines by up to 15.2 points in task success while reducing token consumption by 5--20%.

</details>


### [25] [Analogical Deep Research: Retrieving and Integrating Historical Analogies for Foresight Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13602)
*Yongqiang Chen,Guangyi Chen,Yuewen Sun,Kun Zhang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces Analogical Deep Research (ADR) as a new task for LLM agents, creates the ADR-bench benchmark, identifies that LLMs fail at historical analogy because they match surface features rather than underlying causal mechanisms, and proposes CANA — a causal agentic framework using structural decomposition and reflective feedback — which improves analogy generation by up to 10% and outperforms state-of-the-art deep research agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Historical analogies — systematic comparisons between current and past structurally similar events — are powerful for foresight analysis, yet LLM agents struggle with this task. The authors identify that the core obstacle is LLMs' tendency to match on surface-level features rather than underlying causal mechanisms. They argue ADR is inherently a causal reasoning problem requiring understanding why events occurred, motivating the need for a principled framework and benchmark.

Method: The authors construct ADR-bench, the first benchmark for Analogical Deep Research. They then propose two theoretical principles for ADR: (1) mechanism alignment — analogies must align at the causal mechanism level, not surface features; and (2) cross-analogy confirmation — multiple analogies should corroborate each other. Based on these principles, they design CANA (Causal Analogical Researcher), an agentic framework that uses structural decomposition to represent events causally and incorporates structural feedback loops to iteratively refine both the identification and integration of historical analogies.

Result: CANA achieves up to 10% improvement in historical analogy generation over baseline LLM agents and surpasses state-of-the-art deep research agents on ADR-bench. Case studies on ongoing real-world events further validate CANA's effectiveness in leveraging historical analogies for foresight analysis.

Conclusion: ADR is fundamentally a causal reasoning task. The two principles of mechanism alignment and cross-analogy confirmation are essential for effective analogical reasoning. CANA demonstrates that guiding LLMs with structural causal representations and reflective feedback significantly improves their ability to find and integrate historical analogies for foresight.

Abstract: Systematic comparisons between current situations and structurally similar past events in the historical, i.e., historical analogies, is among the most powerful tools for foresight analysis. In this work, we present a new task called Analogical Deep Research (ADR) to Large Language Model (LLM) agents and construct the first ADR benchmark ADR-bench to study whether LLM agents are able to find and leverage historical analogies when doing foresight analysis. Our investigation reveals a key obstacle: LLM agents are poor at finding analogies because they match on surface features rather than underlying mechanisms. We argue that ADR is inherently a causal question as it requires understanding why the event occurred. Based on our theoretical analysis, we propose two principles required for ADR, including the mechanism alignment and cross-analogy confirmation. Built upon our theoretical results, we propose a new agentic framework called Causal Analogical Researcher (CANA) that guides LLMs to find and integrate historical analogies. CANA incorporates a simple yet effective structural decomposition representation, and integrates structural feedback for reflective improvements of historical analogy identification and integration. We show that CANA brings up to 10% improvements in historical analogy generation, and surpasses the state-of-the-art deep research agents in the ADR-bench. Case studies with the ongoing events confirm the effectiveness of CANA in leveraging historical analogies.

</details>


### [26] [Self-Evolving Agent Harnesses via Gated Semantic Quality-Diversity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13683)
*Xiaotian Luo,Fengxingyu Wang,Chuanrui Hu,Dizhan Xue,Yafeng Deng*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A self-evolving agent-harness framework that uses deterministic code to credit genuine improvements, achieving +9 to +15.5pp gains across seven domains with a frozen model. Gains retain 86-147% of training improvement on sealed tests, demonstrating generalization rather than overfitting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deployed LLM agents depend heavily on their harness (prompts, injected knowledge, runtime control, configuration), which is often the only lever available. Manually improving it is unreliable because self-generated feedback is noisy and apparent gains may be artifacts or overfitting. The core challenge is trustworthy attribution: knowing which change truly helped.

Method: A self-evolving framework that decouples proposal from credit assignment. An LM diagnoses failures and proposes harness patches, while deterministic code handles all sampling, measurement, and significance testing, ensuring every credited improvement is trustworthy by construction. Patches are stored in a gated, categorical quality-diversity archive (GSME) keyed on (WHERE × WHY) pathology rather than specific tasks — an anti-overfitting inductive bias. Generalization is measured on a sealed test set scored only after evolution completes.

Result: Across seven domains with a frozen open-weight model, the harness achieves +9 to +15.5pp gains on a sealed test set, retaining 86-147% of training gain — strong evidence of generalization. The winning patch tracks the model's dominant pathology, not its size or family; changing the model changes the pathology and patch, while the same pathology-to-patch correspondence recurs across two model families. What transfers is the diagnose-and-credit loop, not any specific harness.

Conclusion: Trustworthy automatic harness improvement is achievable by separating proposal generation from credit assignment and organizing patches by pathology rather than task. The framework generalizes across domains and model families, with the core transferable component being the diagnose-and-credit loop itself.

Abstract: An LLM agent's real-task performance is shaped as much by the harness around its model as by the frozen model itself: its prompts, injected knowledge, runtime control, and configuration. In deployment the harness is often the only lever available, so improving it automatically is the natural way to raise performance without touching the weights. The hard part is not generating changes but knowing which one truly helped. Self-generated feedback is noisy, and an apparent gain can be a measurement artifact or an edit that merely overfits the tasks it was tuned on. We present a self-evolving agent-harness framework that separates proposing changes from crediting them: a language model diagnoses failures and proposes patches, while all sampling, measurement, and significance testing are owned by deterministic code, so every credited improvement is trustworthy by construction. Patches populate a gated, categorical quality-diversity archive (GSME) keyed on the (WHERE x WHY) pathology an edit addresses rather than the tasks it fixes, an anti-overfitting inductive bias; generalization is measured on a sealed test scored only after evolution. Across seven domains with a frozen open-weight model, the harness is train-selected and scored once on a sealed test; its credited gains there are +9 to +15.5pp and retain 86-147% of the training gain, evidence they generalize rather than overfit. The winning patch tracks the model's dominant pathology, not its size or family: changing the model can change the pathology and the patch, while the same pathology-to-patch match recurs across two model families. What transfers is the diagnose-and-credit loop, not any specific harness.

</details>


### [27] [The Test Oracle Problem in Synthetic LLM-as-Judge Corpora: Disappearance, Distortion and a Validation Protocol](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13707)
*Serkan Ballı*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: A structural flaw in synthetic corpus generation for LLM-as-judge bias studies — a shared decoding parameter truncating hallucinated answers — produced a spurious 32-point cross-lingual accuracy collapse. Only manual inspection caught it; aggregate statistics didn't. The paper connects this to the test oracle problem and proposes a validation protocol for oracle-less corpora.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-as-judge bias research relies on synthetic corpora where an LLM generates hallucinated "negative" answers paired with factual ones. The authors discovered that a silent, structural failure mode in this pipeline can fabricate statistically robust but entirely spurious results, and that conventional aggregate checks fail to detect such faults — motivating a deeper examination of the test oracle problem in this context.

Method: The authors analyzed a multilingual (Turkish/English) faithfulness-judgment corpus where a shared decoding-budget parameter accidentally truncated hallucinated answers. They diagnosed the fault through manual reading of raw generations, conducted a controlled producer-swap experiment to confirm causation, replicated the spurious effect across sample sizes (N=50 to N=500), and provided a mechanistic three-layer account of the failure. They also compared this oracle-less corpus paradigm against deterministic perturbation-based corpora, injecting an analogous fault into the latter to demonstrate automatic detection via gold-to-negative string comparison. A positive control experiment validated the oracle argument.

Result: The truncated hallucinated answers caused a 32-point cross-lingual collapse in judge selection accuracy — a large, statistically robust effect that vanished to ceiling once the shared parameter was corrected. A second measured bias (Markdown-formatting preference) was distorted in magnitude and sign depending on stimulus length. Aggregate statistical checks failed to expose either issue. The deterministic perturbation corpus caught an analogous injected fault with 100% accuracy via zero-cost string comparison, confirming the oracle advantage.

Conclusion: Corpora built via LLM generation of negative examples lack an item-level integrity oracle, making them structurally vulnerable to silent failures. The authors propose a validation protocol for analysts working in this oracle-less regime, recommending manual inspection of raw generations and caution against relying solely on aggregate metrics.

Abstract: Studies of bias in LLM-as-judge systems typically build synthetic corpora by prompting an LLM to generate a hallucinated answer to pair with a factual one, then presenting both to a judge. We report a case in which this generation step silently failed, and use it to argue that the failure mode is structural rather than incidental. In a multilingual (Turkish/English) faithfulness-judgment corpus, a decoding-budget parameter shared between judging and generation calls truncated one producer's hallucinated answers to a few words. The resulting items produced a large, statistically robust effect: a 32-point cross-lingual collapse in one judge's selection accuracy, replicated from N=50 to N=500, explained by a three-layer mechanistic account, and confirmed by a controlled producer-swap experiment, none of which was real. The effect vanished to ceiling once the shared parameter was corrected, and only manual reading of the raw generations, not any aggregate statistical check, exposed the fault. A second measured bias (Markdown-formatting preference) was not fabricated but distorted by the same fault, its magnitude and in one case its sign shifting with stimulus length, a mode aggregate metrics cannot distinguish from the first. We frame the underlying vulnerability using the test oracle problem: corpora whose negative examples are LLM-generated carry no mechanical way to verify item integrity, while corpora built by deterministic perturbation of a gold answer carry an item-level oracle for free. A positive control supports this claim directly: an analogous fault injected into a minimal perturbation-based corpus is caught with 100% accuracy by a zero-cost, zero-human gold-to-negative string comparison. We close with a validation protocol, derived from our own case, for analysts working in the oracle-less regime that we argue describes most contemporary multilingual LLM-as-judge corpora.

</details>


### [28] [Self-supervised Speech Comparison for L2 Phone, Rhythm, and Intonation Scoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13721)
*Stephen McIntosh,Reuben Smit,Daisuke Saito,Nobuaki Minematsu,Herman Kamper*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper proposes a text-free L2 pronunciation assessment framework using DTW over WavLM self-supervised representations, achieving super-human performance on phonetic scoring and near-human performance on rhythm assessment, while intonation scoring remains more challenging.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: L2 speech assessment has focused heavily on phonetics while neglecting suprasegmental features (rhythm, intonation). Existing methods typically require labeled L2 data, limiting applicability in low-resource settings. The authors aim to develop a text-free, multi-aspect pronunciation assessment framework that works without transcriptions or labeled L2 data.

Method: The authors use Dynamic Time Warping (DTW) over self-supervised WavLM representations to compare learner speech against native templates. For phonetic scoring, they compute DTW distance between learner and native embeddings. For rhythm assessment, they introduce novel methods that quantify the degree of warping in the DTW alignment path. For intonation, they combine DTW distance computed over prosodic residuals (after subtracting segmental information) with pitch and intensity features.

Result: The basic DTW approach exceeds human inter-rater agreement on holistic and sentence-level phonetic scoring for both English and Japanese L2 speech. The rhythm assessment method approaches human-level performance. Intonation assessment combining prosodic residual DTW with pitch and intensity features yields more modest results, suggesting room for improvement in this dimension.

Conclusion: Self-supervised representations (specifically WavLM) serve as a strong, text-free foundation for multi-aspect L2 pronunciation assessment, particularly for phonetic and rhythm dimensions. The framework is well-suited for low-resource scenarios since it requires only native reference templates rather than labeled L2 training data.

Abstract: L2 speech assessment has traditionally focused on phonetic assessment, leaving the scoring of suprasegmental features such as rhythm and intonation underexplored. Moreover, assessment methods often require training with labeled L2 speech data, making them difficult to apply in low-resource settings. We investigate whether DTW over self-supervised WavLM representations can provide a text-free framework for assessing phonetic accuracy, rhythm, and intonation in English and Japanese L2 speech. Results show that a basic DTW-based approach that compares learner speech to native templates exceeds human agreement on holistic and sentence-level phonetic scoring. For rhythm, we introduce methods that measure the degree of warping in the DTW alignment path; our best method approaches human-level performance. For intonation, we combine DTW distance over prosodic residuals with pitch and intensity features, but performance remains more modest on some tasks. Our results point to self-supervised representations as a promising, text-free basis for multi-aspect pronunciation assessment.

</details>


### [29] [Post-Training Shifts Confidence: A Three-Stage Analysis of How SFT, RL, and OPD Shape Pre-, Intra-, and Post-CoT Calibration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13753)
*Shuhao Li,Guodong Du,Anhao Zhao,Wanyu Lin,Tianyu Yuan,Xiaoyu Shen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper investigates how different post-training methods (SFT, RL, OPD) affect model confidence calibration during reasoning, introduces a three-stage framework to evaluate confidence at pre-, mid-, and post-reasoning stages, and proposes PosConf—a position-aware strategy that selectively uses confidence from reliable relative-position intervals, yielding significant gains in answer aggregation and early stopping.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Post-training methods (supervised fine-tuning, reinforcement learning, and on-policy distillation) are typically evaluated only by final-answer accuracy, overlooking how they reshape model confidence during chain-of-thought reasoning. Understanding confidence calibration across reasoning stages can enable better answer aggregation, early termination, and difficulty estimation.

Method: The authors introduce a three-stage calibration framework evaluating confidence (1) before reasoning (difficulty estimation), (2) during reasoning (early termination), and (3) after reasoning (answer aggregation). They conduct controlled comparisons of SFT, RL, and OPD on math reasoning benchmarks, analyze how confidence reliability varies with reasoning position, and propose PosConf, which applies confidence only from reliable relative-position intervals (e.g., avoiding OPD's later inverse-calibration region, leveraging RL's post-path-commitment signal).

Result: Key findings: OPD yields the best pre-reasoning confidence; SFT provides the strongest online signal for early stopping; RL gives the most reliable trace-level signal for aggregation. Confidence reliability is position-dependent—RL confidence becomes informative after a path-commitment phase, while OPD confidence is useful early but inversely calibrated later. PosConf improves RL answer aggregation by 6.1 points over majority voting and improves OPD early stopping by up to 4.3 points under tight token budgets.

Conclusion: Confidence in reasoning models should be used both stage-wise and position-awarely. Different post-training methods confer different calibration strengths at different reasoning stages, and leveraging confidence only from reliable relative-position intervals yields consistent improvements across aggregation and early-stopping tasks.

Abstract: Large language models have made strong reasoning gains through supervised fine-tuning, reinforcement learning, and on-policy distillation, yet these post-training methods are usually evaluated only by final-answer accuracy. We study how they reshape confidence during reasoning. We introduce a three-stage calibration framework that evaluates confidence before, during, and after chain-of-thought generation, corresponding to difficulty estimation, early termination, and answer aggregation. Through a controlled comparison on mathematical reasoning benchmarks, we find that OPD provides the most useful pre-reasoning confidence, SFT gives the strongest online signal for early stopping, and RL produces the most reliable trace-level signal for aggregation. We further show that confidence reliability is position-dependent: RL confidence becomes informative after a path-commitment phase, while OPD confidence is useful early but can become inversely calibrated later. Based on this observation, we propose PosConf, a position-aware confidence strategy that uses confidence only from reliable relative-position intervals. PosConf improves RL answer aggregation by 6.1 points over majority voting and consistently improves OPD early stopping under tight token budgets, with gains up to 4.3 points by avoiding its later inverse-calibration region, showing that \emph{confidence in reasoning models should be used both stage-wise and position-awarely}. Our code is available at https://github.com/EIT-NLP/Post-Training-Calibration.

</details>


### [30] [SPyCE: Skill-Policy Co-evolution for Multimodal Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13854)
*Ru Zhang,Weijie Qiu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: SPyCE proposes a framework where multimodal agent reasoning trajectories are distilled into a hierarchical skill library that co-evolves with the policy during reinforcement learning, creating a closed loop where better policies produce better skills and vice versa, outperforming both RL and memory-based baselines across eight benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RL methods for multimodal agents reduce trajectories to scalar rewards, forcing the policy to rediscover reusable tool-use patterns from scratch on every new task. Memory-based alternatives retain past experience but only perform test-time retrieval without updating the policy to internalize reusable patterns. Neither approach enables the policy to truly learn and absorb reusable skills from experience.

Method: SPyCE (Skill-Policy Co-evolution) introduces a hierarchical skill library with two levels: execution skills (local visual operations) and workflow skills (high-level priors for orchestrating tool use). During training, the policy conditions on retrieved skills to guide rollouts, while the skill library is continuously updated using valuable rollouts generated by the policy. This forms a co-evolutionary closed loop — improved policies yield better skills, and the evolving skill library provides stronger priors for subsequent policy rollouts.

Result: Experiments across eight benchmarks show SPyCE consistently outperforms both RL-based and memory-based baselines. Ablation analysis confirms that both the hierarchical skill design and the co-evolution mechanism are critical components of the framework.

Conclusion: Joint skill-policy optimization represents a promising paradigm for building capable multimodal agents. By enabling skills and policies to co-evolve rather than treating trajectories as disposable rewards or static memories, agents can more effectively accumulate and leverage reusable tool-use patterns.

Abstract: Multimodal agents that think with images iteratively manipulate visual evidence and invoke tools across many steps. Existing reinforcement learning methods reduce trajectories to scalar rewards, forcing the policy to discover reusable tool-use patterns from scratch on every new task; memory-based alternatives retain past experience, yet they rely on test-time retrieval, without updating the policy to absorb reusable patterns from that experience. Our key insight is that multimodal reasoning trajectories should be distilled into reusable skills that co-evolve with the policy during training, rather than being consumed as rewards or retrieved from a static store. To this end, we propose SPyCE (Skill-Policy Co-evolution), a framework that distills trajectories into a hierarchical skill library and updates it throughout reinforcement learning. Execution skills capture local visual operations, while workflow skills encode high-level priors that orchestrate tool use. During training, the policy model conditions on retrieved skills to guide its rollouts, while the skill library evolves using valuable rollouts generated by the policy. This creates a closed loop in which improved policies yield better skills, and the evolving skill library, in turn, provides stronger priors for policy rollouts. Experiments across eight benchmarks demonstrate that SPyCE consistently outperforms both RL-based and memory-based baselines. Further analysis reveals that both the hierarchical skill design and the co-evolution mechanism are critical to our design. These results suggest joint skill-policy optimization as a promising paradigm for building capable multimodal agents.

</details>


### [31] [High-Order Question Generation in a Multilingual Educational Context](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13901)
*Suna-Şeyma Uçar,Itziar Aldabe,Nora Aranberri,Orphée De Clercq*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This study explores using LLMs to generate high-order questions via prompts based on Claim-Evidence-Reasoning and Divergent Questioning frameworks (alternatives to Bloom's Taxonomy) across Basque, Spanish, and English. While models generate questions effectively in all three languages, only ~50% of answerable questions are rated as high-order by teachers. The alternative frameworks yield structurally and conceptually diverse questions, suggesting they can complement each other and Bloom's Taxonomy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Critical thinking is fostered by high-order questioning, yet educators struggle to craft such questions and default to low-order ones. Existing LLM-based question generation research relies predominantly on Bloom's Taxonomy and focuses exclusively on English, leaving gaps in exploring alternative pedagogical frameworks and multilingual contexts.

Method: The authors designed prompts grounded in two alternative frameworks—Claim-Evidence-Reasoning (CER) and Divergent Questioning—and applied them to both an open-source and a proprietary LLM to generate questions in Basque, Spanish, and English. Generated questions were then evaluated by teachers to determine whether they qualified as high-order and to assess structural/conceptual variety.

Result: Both LLMs generated questions effectively across all three languages. However, only about half of the answerable questions were recognized by teachers as genuinely high-order. Positively, the CER and Divergent Questioning frameworks produced questions with structural and conceptual variety, indicating their potential as complementary alternatives to Bloom's Taxonomy.

Conclusion: CER and Divergent Questioning are viable, complementary alternatives to Bloom's Taxonomy for guiding LLM-based high-order question generation in multilingual settings. Future work should focus on improving the proportion of questions that teachers recognize as truly high-order.

Abstract: Critical thinking is a fundamental skill that helps learners move beyond simple memorization. One way to develop this skill is through high-order questioning. However, crafting such questions remains a challenge for educators, and classroom practices tend to rely on low-order questions. Large Language Models have demonstrated strong capabilities in generating high-order questions, especially when guided by prompts based on Bloom's Taxonomy. Yet, existing research has largely centered on this framework and focused only on English. This study addresses these gaps by introducing prompts grounded in two alternative frameworks: Claim-Evidence-Reasoning and Divergent Questioning within a multilingual context using Basque, Spanish, and English. Results indicate that while both an open-source and a proprietary model rather effectively generate questions in all three languages, only about half of the answerable questions are recognized by teachers as high-order. A positive finding is that the alternative frameworks produce structurally and conceptually varied questions, suggesting they could complement each other and provide viable alternatives to Bloom's Taxonomy.

</details>


### [32] [DeepStress: Stress-Testing Deep Search Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13920)
*Ismael Rousseau,Geraldine Damnati,Frederic Bechet*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: DeepStress is a stress testing framework that evaluates search agents' robustness to poor-quality evidence by replacing retrieval modules with a controlled synthetic environment, manipulating trustworthiness, relevance, and factuality. Results on HotpotQA and BrowseCompPlus reveal significant variation in agent resilience and motivate new evaluation metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While search agents excel at multi-step QA, their robustness to low-quality evidence is under-explored because realistic benchmarks rarely include such noise — yet real-world applications frequently encounter it, leading to catastrophic failures. There is a need for systematic stress testing under controlled conditions.

Method: The authors propose DeepStress, a stress testing framework that replaces the retrieval module of search agents with a controlled synthetic environment. This allows precise manipulation of three document reliability dimensions: trustworthiness, relevance, and factuality. The framework enables controlled experiments measuring agent behavior under varying evidence quality.

Result: Testing multiple search agents on HotpotQA and BrowseCompPlus revealed substantial differences in their ability to handle unreliable information. The paper also proposes new metrics that better capture system outcomes and the interactions between conflicting parametric (model-internal) and retrieved knowledge.

Conclusion: Search agents vary dramatically in their robustness to poor-quality evidence, highlighting a critical gap between benchmark performance and real-world reliability. Controlled stress testing and improved metrics are essential for developing more resilient systems.

Abstract: While search agents demonstrate impressive capabilities in multi-step question answering, their robustness to poor-quality evidence remains under-explored. This phenomenon occurs rarely in realistic benchmarks but can lead to dramatic failure in real life applications. Therefore in this study we propose DeepStress, a stress testing framework that controls the frequency of challenging evidence by replacing the retrieval module of search agents with a controlled synthetic environment. We use this framework to control three dimensions that can affect document reliability: trustworthiness, relevance, and factuality. Testing several search agents on HotpotQA and BrowseCompPlus, we demonstrate that agents exhibit substantial differences in their ability to handle unreliable information and propose new metrics that better document systems outcomes as well as the interactions between conflicting parametric and retrieved knowledge.

</details>


### [33] [DeltaMerge-LowRes: Composing Language and Task Deltas for Low-Resource Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13967)
*Son Ha Xuan,Xuan-Bach Le,Phat T. Tran-Truong*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper proposes ΔMergeLowRes, a method that separately fine-tunes language and task deltas for low-resource NLP, then composes them at inference using novel merging rules—most notably cross-axis TIES—achieving significant gains over task-only baselines across summarization, QA, and classification in African languages.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard low-resource NLP pipelines fuse language adaptation and task adaptation into a single expensive fine-tuning run. The authors ask whether these two axes can instead be trained independently and recombined cheaply in weight space, avoiding costly joint training while preserving or improving performance.

Method: ΔMergeLowRes learns a language delta (Δ_L) from unlabeled monolingual text and a task delta (Δ_T) from labeled English data. At inference, the deltas are composed via one of four rules: (1) additive merging, (2) activation-guided merging, (3) sparsity-aware merging, and (4) a novel cross-axis TIES rule that adapts TIES-Merging's three steps—trimming, sign election, and merging—to operate across language and task axes rather than two task axes. Evaluation spans 158 cells across four task families and four African languages, with 10,000-sample paired bootstrap per cell.

Result: Cross-axis TIES wins summarization on 3/4 languages by +4 to +7 chrF (18.59 vs. 13.80 task-only). It improves QA by +2.32 F1 and +2.91 EM. Sparsity-aware merging reduces classification expected calibration error (ECE) by 36% at parity macro-F1. The choice of composition rule materially affects what the merged model preserves, suppresses, and calibrates.

Conclusion: Language and task adaptation can be effectively decoupled and recombined in weight space. The cross-axis TIES rule provides the strongest overall performance, and the composition strategy significantly influences model behavior across preservation, suppression, and calibration dimensions. All artifacts are released for reproducibility.

Abstract: Adapting a multilingual encoder to a new language \emph{and} a new task with only a few hundred gold examples is a common low-resource NLP setting, yet the two axes are usually fused via an expensive language--task fine-tuning run. We ask whether they can instead be trained separately and recombined in weight space. \DeltaMergeLowRes{} learns a language delta $Δ_L$ from unlabeled monolingual text and a task delta $Δ_T$ from labeled English data, then composes them at inference under one of four rules: additive, activation-guided, sparsity-aware, and a novel \emph{cross-axis TIES}. The new rule adapts the TIES-Merging steps of trimming, sign election, and merging to the language and task axes rather than to two task axes. Holding $(Δ_L,Δ_T)$ fixed across rules on four task families and four African languages ($158$ evaluated cells, $10{,}000$-sample paired bootstrap per cell), we find: (i) cross-axis TIES wins summarisation on $3/4$ languages by $+4$ to $+7$ chrF (chrF $18.59$ vs.\ $13.80$ task-only); (ii) it improves QA F1 by $+2.32$ and EM by $+2.91$; and (iii) sparsity-aware merging cuts classification ECE by $36\%$ at parity macro-F1. The composition rule materially changes what the merged model preserves, suppresses, and calibrates. We release all JSON traces and a claim ledger.

</details>


### [34] [Constraint-Aware Counterfactual Editing for Aspect-Based Sentiment Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13977)
*S M Rafiuddin,Vamsi Krishna Pavuluri,Atriya Sen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: CAVE-ABSA is a constraint-aware framework for generating and validating aspect-level counterfactuals in ABSA. It localizes target-aspect opinion spans, performs controlled rewriting, repairs candidates, and filters them through multi-dimensional validation (aspect-level verification, semantic similarity, AMR-guided structural preservation, edit minimality, fluency, contradiction detection). The framework separates generation from validation to produce meaningful counterfactuals for robustness evaluation and data augmentation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing counterfactual generation for ABSA is challenging because valid counterfactuals must flip only the target aspect's sentiment while preserving non-target sentiments, semantics, fluency, and factual consistency. Current methods focus on sentence-level label flipping, producing edits that are fluent but often aspect-invalid, semantically drifting, or contradictory. A principled, validated approach for aspect-level counterfactual generation is lacking.

Method: CAVE-ABSA consists of five stages: (1) opinion span localization for the target aspect, (2) controlled counterfactual rewriting to flip sentiment, (3) a repair module that refines candidate edits, (4) multi-dimensional filtering using aspect-level verification, semantic similarity checks, AMR-guided structural preservation, edit minimality scoring, fluency assessment, and contradiction detection, and (5) dataset construction from validated counterfactuals. Generation and validation are explicitly separated.

Result: The paper presents the CAVE-ABSA framework design; specific quantitative results are not detailed in the abstract. The framework is positioned as a means to construct validated counterfactual ABSA datasets for downstream robustness evaluation and data augmentation.

Conclusion: CAVE-ABSA provides a principled, validated approach to aspect-level counterfactual generation, enabling more reliable robustness testing of ABSA models by ensuring counterfactuals are aspect-grounded, semantically preserved, and free of contradictions.

Abstract: Aspect-Based Sentiment Analysis (ABSA) requires models to identify sentiment toward specific aspects rather than relying on the global polarity of a sentence. This makes counterfactual evaluation especially challenging: a valid counterfactual should flip the sentiment of one target aspect while preserving the sentiment of all non-target aspects, semantic meaning, fluency, and factual consistency. Existing counterfactual generation methods often focus on sentence-level label flipping and may produce edits that are fluent but aspect-invalid, semantically drifting, or contradictory. To address this limitation, we propose CAVE-ABSA, a Constraint-Aware Validated Editing framework for generating and validating aspect-level counterfactuals. CAVE-ABSA localizes the opinion span associated with the target aspect, performs controlled counterfactual rewriting, refines candidates through a repair module, and filters them using aspect-level verification, semantic similarity, AMR-guided structural preservation, edit minimality, fluency, and contradiction detection. The framework is designed to construct validated counterfactual ABSA datasets for robustness evaluation and data augmentation. By explicitly separating generation from validation, CAVE-ABSA provides a principled approach for producing meaningful aspect-local counterfactuals and for testing whether ABSA models truly rely on aspect-grounded sentiment reasoning.

</details>


### [35] [Can an Old Dog Be Taught New Tricks? Taking LLMs Beyond Sentence Level Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14040)
*Alaina Brandt*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper presents PAT, a RAG-based translation system that moves beyond sentence-by-sentence MT by using a comparable corpus and user specifications to generate whole-document translations with pragmatic reformulation. Evaluation shows LLMs can shift toward discourse-level reformulation, though effectiveness remains inconsistent.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing automatic translation systems (CAT tools, MT) overwhelmingly treat translation as sentence-by-sentence, ignoring discourse organization, rhetorical style, and pragmatic norms that differ across languages. The authors aim to move LLM-based translation toward whole-document, corpus-informed generation that reformulates target texts to fit the target language's communicative context.

Method: The authors built PAT (Pragmatic Auto-Translator), a Retrieval-Augmented Generation system combining: (1) user-configured specifications, (2) a comparable corpus of authentic longform U.S. English and Latin American Spanish texts, and (3) retrieved paragraph-, section-, and document-level examples fed to an LLM for whole-document generation. Six automatic translations of essays on generative AI were evaluated across three projects using a customized Multidimensional Quality Metrics (MQM) typology by two trained evaluators for U.S. English → LATAM/Mexican Spanish.

Result: A limited prompt produced no meaningful reformulation. Specifications and corpus-informed translations showed substantial reformulation at times, but not consistently effective. The paradigm shift from sentence-level to document-level translation is feasible with LLMs, but reformulation effectiveness needs improvement.

Conclusion: LLMs can be moved toward pragmatic reformulation and away from sentence-by-sentence translation, but further work is required to improve reformulation quality. The paper contributes insights on automatic translation system design, comparable corpus construction, and translation quality evaluation methodology.

Abstract: Automatic translation systems, from CAT tools to MT, overwhelmingly treat translation as a sentence-by-sentence act. This paper asks whether LLMs can be moved beyond that paradigm through whole-document, corpus-informed translation. We present PAT (Pragmatic Auto-Translator), a RAG-based system that pairs user-configured specifications with context from a comparable corpus of authentic longform texts in U.S. English and Latin American Spanish, passing retrieved paragraph-, section-, and document-level examples to an LLM for whole-document generation. The goal is draft translation for professional verification: target texts reformulated to fit their Spanish-language context, where discourse organization, rhetorical style, and pragmatic norms differ meaningfully from English. We evaluated six automatic translations of essays on generative AI across three projects using a customized MQM typology, assessed by two trained evaluators working from U.S. English into LATAM and Mexican Spanish. Results show that a limited prompt produced no meaningful reformulation, and specifications and corpus-informed translations at times showed substantial reformulation, though not always to effect. We find that LLMs can be moved toward reformulation and away from the sentence-by-sentence paradigm, though more work is needed to improve the effectiveness of those reformulations. In this paper, we discuss considerations related to automatic translation system design, corpus construction, and translation quality evaluation methodology and results.

</details>


### [36] [Hindcast: Replaying Prediction Markets to Evaluate LLM Forecasters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14051)
*Xiao Ye,Jacob Dineen,Evan Zhu,Shijie Lu,Kevin Song,Ben Zhou*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Hindcast is a new backtesting framework that prevents LLM forecasters from cheating via retrieval of post-event reports or training-data contamination. By freezing a Reddit snapshot at a past date t₀ and scoring against both outcomes and contemporaneous market prices, it ensures evaluation truly measures foresight rather than recall.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard backtesting of forecasting systems suffers from two information leaks: (1) retrieval-augmented models can surface post-event reports, and (2) newer models may have the outcome in their training data. Both leaks cause the evaluation to measure recall rather than genuine forecasting ability. A rigorous, leak-proof evaluation framework is needed.

Method: Hindcast replays resolved Polymarket prediction markets against a frozen snapshot of public Reddit. For each market, a cutoff date t₀ is set before the outcome was known. Models can only read posts written before t₀. Forecasts are scored against both the actual outcome and the market's own price at t₀ (a human crowd forecast from identical information). The frozen snapshot enables fair re-evaluation of future models without staleness.

Result: Once both information leaks are closed, retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) still improves most models, but only on markets where Reddit contained relevant pre-event discussion. On markets where the archive held only speculation without substantive signal, retrieval actually degraded forecasting performance.

Conclusion: Hindcast provides a principled, leak-proof evaluation methodology for LLM forecasters. Retrieval's value is conditional on the quality of pre-event information: it helps when discourse is informative and hurts when the archive contains only noise.

Abstract: Forecasters are evaluated by backtesting, which replays resolved questions and grades the probability the system would have assigned before the outcome was known. For LLMs, two channels leak the answer into this test. A model that retrieves can surface reports written after the event, turning forecasting into a lookup, and each new model is trained on data closer to the event, so a question that lay in the future for last year's models sits inside this year's training data. Either way, the test grades recall while claiming to grade foresight. We introduce Hindcast, which closes both leaks by grading a model as if it stood at a chosen past date $t_0$, before the outcome existed in either channel. Hindcast replays resolved Polymarket prediction markets against a frozen snapshot of public Reddit, lets the model read only posts written before $t_0$, and scores each forecast against both what happened and the market's own price at $t_0$, itself a human forecast made from the same past information. Because the cutoff is set per market and the snapshot never changes, the evaluation re-runs on new markets as models improve, without going stale. Once the leak is closed, retrieval still helps most models, but only where Reddit discussed the event beforehand. Where the archive carried only speculation, retrieval hurts.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [37] [Networked Intelligence: Active Shared Context Graphs for Human-AI Team Science](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13220)
*Sutanay Choudhury,Jeffrey J. Czajka,Lummy M. O. Monteiro,Erin Bredeweg,Jason McDermott,Katherine Wolf,Alex Beliaev,Josh Elmore,Paul Piehowski,Kylee Tate,Yuqian Gao,Aivett Bilbao,Kelly Stratton,Scott Baker,Jaydeep P. Bardhan,Kristin Burnum Johnson,Chris Oehmen,Robert Rallo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Mycelium is a multi-user, multi-agent shared workspace that routes observations and hypotheses between human researchers and AI agents. It was empirically tested in a multi-omics campaign where routed context turned a local finding into a cross-expert mechanistic constraint and experimental design. The paper also provides a computational account of "networked intelligence" as sparse conditional computation over distributed scientific contexts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Challenging scientific problems are solved by teams with diverse priors, tacit knowledge, and domain-trained intuitions — not by a single reasoner. Current AI-for-science systems focus on scaling individual reasoning (models, context windows, agentic execution) but neglect the problem of connecting intelligence across humans and AI agents, i.e., cultivating "networked intelligence."

Method: The authors built Mycelium, an active shared workspace that (1) captures observations and hypotheses from both human users and AI agents, (2) tracks how they relate to the team's evolving model, and (3) routes information to the person or agent whose next decision it can inform. They also formalize networked intelligence as sparse conditional computation over distributed scientific contexts.

Result: In a biological multi-omics campaign, Mycelium successfully routed shared context that converted a local analytical finding into a cross-expert mechanistic constraint, which in turn informed an experimental design. The computational account distinguishes conditions under which a scaled standalone agent can match a network versus when independent expertise and non-mergeable contexts make the network irreducible.

Conclusion: Scaling scientific discovery requires not just better individual models but infrastructure for networked intelligence — connecting humans and AI agents so insights flow to where they can be acted upon. Mycelium demonstrates this principle in a real biological workflow and provides a formal grounding for when networks outperform standalone systems.

Abstract: Most AI-for-science systems focus on scaling a single reasoning process through better models, larger context windows, long-horizon agentic execution, or digital co-scientists working with one principal user. However, challenging scientific problems are rarely solved by one reasoner alone. They are solved by teams whose members bring different priors, experimental backgrounds, tacit knowledge, and domain-trained intuitions. The open problem is therefore not only how to scale models, but how to cultivate networked intelligence: scaling the connections between humans and AI systems so that a result or hypothesis produced in one context reaches another person, agent, instrument, or robot that can act on it. We introduce Mycelium, an active shared workspace that automatically connects researchers and AI agents as a multi-user co-scientist. As human users and agents work, the system captures important observations and hypotheses, tracks how they relate to the team's evolving model, and routes them to the person or agent whose next decision they can inform. We evaluate Mycelium in its first empirical test, a biological multi-omics campaign in which routed shared context turned a local analytical finding into a cross-expert mechanistic constraint and ultimately into an experimental design. We also give networked intelligence a computational account as sparse conditional computation over distributed scientific contexts. This account distinguishes when a scaled standalone agent can match the network from when independent expertise and non-mergeable contexts make the network irreducible.

</details>


### [38] [AI advice suppresses people's willingness to say "I don't know", even when the advice is wrong and accuracy is incentivized](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13562)
*Chiara Marcoccia,Walter Quattrociocchi,Valerio Capraro*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Access to AI advice nearly eliminates people's willingness to say "I don't know," leading them to answer more questions but with dramatically lower accuracy and inflated confidence—even when the AI is reliably wrong. Incentives mitigate but do not reverse this effect.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Human judgment relies on knowing when to suspend judgment, yet AI assistants rarely decline to answer. The authors investigate whether mere access to AI—regardless of its accuracy—alters people's metacognitive threshold for deciding when they "know enough" to answer a question.

Method: Five experiments (N = 3,132; four preregistered) in which participants answered difficult questions engineered so that AI advice was always wrong. Participants could always decline to answer. The design cleanly separates AI access/use from AI accuracy. Conditions varied whether AI advice was actively requested or unsolicited, and whether accuracy was incentivized or inaccuracy penalized.

Result: Access to AI nearly eliminated participants' willingness to suspend judgment—this occurred regardless of whether advice was solicited or unsolicited. Participants answered more questions but were correct only one-third as often as without AI, while their confidence nearly doubled. Incentivizing accuracy and penalizing errors reduced AI reliance and improved accuracy, but suspension of judgment remained far below the no-AI baseline.

Conclusion: Ubiquitous, unsolicited AI suggestions do not merely affect answer accuracy; they fundamentally alter the metacognitive threshold at which people decide whether they know enough to answer, undermining the crucial human capacity to say "I don't know."

Abstract: Knowing when to say "I don't know" is fundamental to human judgment, yet AI assistants offer a fluent answer to almost any question. In five experiments (N = 3,132; four preregistered, one direct replication), participants answered difficult questions and could always decline to respond. We engineered the questions so that AI advice was wrong, separating AI use from its accuracy. Merely having access to AI nearly eliminated participants' willingness to suspend judgment, and this held whether the advice was actively requested or simply displayed. Consequently, participants answered more questions but were correct about a third as often as when AI was unavailable-yet their confidence nearly doubled. Incentivizing accuracy and penalizing inaccuracy led participants to seek and follow AI advice less, answer more accurately, and suspend judgment more often, though still far less than when AI was unavailable. As AI suggestions grow ubiquitous and unsolicited, they may not simply affect answer accuracy; they may even alter the metacognitive threshold at which people decide whether they know enough to answer.

</details>


### [39] [When Bots Join the Team: Bot Adoption and the Institutional Fabric of Open-Source Software Projects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13679)
*Yongren Shi,Wenyi Gong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Analyzing 2,991 GitHub projects, the study finds that bot adoption is associated with strengthened group organization — more repeated collaboration, greater recognition of bots, fewer conflict cascades, and more distinctive outputs — suggesting rule-based agents can become part of a community's social infrastructure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As AI agents increasingly join human teams, it is fundamentally unclear whether adding an automated participant strengthens or weakens group organization. Prior work treats bots primarily as tools; this paper reframes them as social participants to investigate their effect on durable coordination within collaborative communities.

Method: The authors conduct a longitudinal observational study of 2,991 open-source GitHub projects, examining two years before and after each project's first bot adoption. Using institutional theory, they measure three organizational capabilities (repeated engagement, social memory via bot-specific mentions, and role differentiation) and two outcomes (conflict cascades in pull request discussions and output distinctiveness via topic modeling). Analyses use fixed-effects panel models to identify precisely timed associations around adoption.

Result: Bot adoption is followed by: increased repeated collaboration, greater social memory (more bot-specific mentions in discussions), fewer conflict cascades, and more distinctive project outputs. These changes cluster around the adoption event rather than accumulating gradually. Critically, capabilities predict outcomes by their function (coordination vs. differentiation) regardless of whether humans or bots provide them; human-side capabilities account for the bot-conflict reduction but not the bot-distinctiveness increase.

Conclusion: The findings are consistent with the interpretation that predictable, rule-based agents can integrate into a community's social infrastructure. The bot serves as the occasion for organizational change, but social organization itself is the mechanism driving improved coordination and differentiation outcomes. The authors caution that results are associational, not causal, due to the lack of an untreated comparison group.

Abstract: AI agents are joining human teams, raising a basic question: when an automated agent becomes a regular participant, does group organization strengthen or weaken? We study this question in open-source software, where bots open pull requests, review code, and merge changes alongside people, leaving a public record of every interaction. Treating bots as participants rather than tools, we examine 2,991 GitHub projects for two years before and after each adopted its first bot. We measure three capabilities that institutional theory links to durable coordination - repeated engagement, social memory, and role differentiation - and two outcomes: conflict cascades and output distinctiveness. Bot adoption is followed by more repeated collaboration, greater recognition of specific bots in discussion, fewer conflict cascades, and more distinctive outputs. These changes cluster around adoption rather than accumulating gradually. Because we lack an untreated comparison group, we interpret the results as precisely timed associations, not causal effects. Two patterns are difficult for alternative explanations to account for: capabilities predict outcomes according to their function - coordination versus differentiation - rather than whether humans or bots provide them, and human-side capabilities account for the bot-conflict association but not the bot-distinctiveness association. The findings are consistent with a specific interpretation: predictable, rule-based agents can become part of a community's social infrastructure. The bot is the occasion; social organization is the mechanism.

</details>


### [40] [Harness Handbook: Making Evolving Agent Harnesses Readable,Navigable, and Editable](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13285)
*Ruhan Wang,Yucheng Shi,Zongxia Li,Zhongzhi Li,Yue Yu,Junyao Yang,Kishan Panaganti,Haitao Mi,Dongruo Zhou,Leoweiliang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces the Harness Handbook—a behavior-centric representation automatically synthesized from agent harness codebases—and Behavior-Guided Progressive Disclosure (BGPD), which together improve behavior localization for AI agent harness evolution. The approach reduces the bottleneck of mapping modification requests to relevant source code locations, improving edit-plan quality while using fewer tokens.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern AI agent harnesses are large, tightly coupled, and behaviorally distributed across codebases. When modification requests describe desired behaviors, developers must manually map these to relevant code locations—a process called behavior localization—which remains a central bottleneck in harness evolution despite advances in code search and long-context processing.

Method: The authors propose two complementary techniques: (1) the Harness Handbook, synthesized automatically via static analysis and LLM-assisted structuring to link each behavior to its corresponding source code, and (2) Behavior-Guided Progressive Disclosure (BGPD), which guides agents from high-level behavior descriptions to relevant implementation details and verifies candidate locations against current source.

Result: On diverse modification requests from two open-source harnesses, Handbook-Assisted planning improved behavior localization and edit-plan quality while using fewer planner tokens, with the largest gains observed on scattered code sites, rarely executed paths, and cross-module interactions.

Conclusion: Evolving complex agentic systems depends not only on generating edits but also on determining where those edits should be made. The Harness Handbook and BGPD address this behavior localization challenge, demonstrating that behavior-centric representations can substantially improve the efficiency and accuracy of harness modification.

Abstract: The capability of a modern AI agent depends not only on its foundation model but also on its harness, which constructs prompts, manages state, invokes tools, and coordinates execution. As models, APIs, environments, and requirements evolve, the harness must be continually modified. Before such a change can be made, a developer or coding agent must identify all code locations that implement the target behavior. This is difficult because production harnesses are large, tightly coupled, and behaviorally distributed, while modification requests describe what the system should do and repositories are organized by files and modules. Code search, repository indexing, and long-context processing ease inspection, but still leave this behavior-to-code mapping to be recovered by hand. Behavior localization is therefore a central bottleneck in harness evolution. We introduce the Harness Handbook, a behavior-centric representation synthesized automatically from a harness codebase via static analysis and LLM-assisted structuring, linking each behavior to its corresponding source. We also introduce Behavior-Guided Progressive Disclosure (BGPD), which guides agents from high-level behaviors to relevant implementation details and verifies candidate locations against the current source. On diverse modification requests from two open-source harnesses, Handbook-Assisted planning improves behavior localization and edit-plan quality while using fewer planner tokens, with the largest gains on scattered sites, rarely executed paths, and cross-module interactions. Evolving complex agentic systems thus depends not only on generating edits, but also on determining where those edits should be made.

</details>


### [41] [Set-shifting Behavioral Test for Harnessed Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13396)
*Ziwei Ye*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a benchmark to evaluate how LLM agents adapt when the reliability of tools silently shifts mid-session. Using a set-shifting paradigm from cognitive psychology, it finds that agents default to rigid routines, concentrate call shares on few tools, and exhibit distinct failure modes. Toolset framing (competing vs. complementary) significantly alters routing behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents in real-world deployments face silent reliability shifts in their tool ecosystems (e.g., APIs deprecating, services degrading). Understanding whether agents can flexibly adapt—akin to cognitive set-shifting in humans—is critical for robust agent design. Prior work largely assumes static tool reliability.

Method: The authors construct a benchmark with redundant tool-skill libraries where multiple tools solve the same task but differ in hidden reliability. A branched evaluation schedule shifts the reliable tool group at predefined, hidden boundaries, with matched no-shift controls. Agent trajectories are scored using set-shifting accuracy: the joint probability of routing to the target tool group across all post-shift windows. Experiments test open-weight LLMs in an open-source agentic harness, and manipulate how tools are framed (competing vs. complementary).

Result: Agents settle into a small recurring routine within a few turns after each boundary. Call shares concentrate on a few discrete values following reliability shifts. Qualitatively distinct failure modes emerge across agents using the same routines. Set framing—whether alternatives are presented as competing or complementary—shifts routing dynamics, indicating that presentation format meaningfully influences adaptive tool selection.

Conclusion: LLM agents exhibit cognitive rigidity analogous to poor set-shifting in humans, defaulting to entrenched routines rather than exploring when tool reliability silently changes. The finding that set framing modulates routing behavior suggests a practical lever for improving agent adaptability. The benchmark provides a foundation for studying and mitigating tool-selection brittleness in agentic systems.

Abstract: What happens to an LLM agent's tool choice when the reliable tool silently changes within an ongoing session? We borrow set-shifting from cognitive psychology to study how well agents adapt to hidden reliability shifts. Our benchmark mounts tool-skill libraries with redundancies, where many tools solve the same task but differ in hidden reliability. In our evaluation framework, a branched schedule shifts the reliable tool group at hidden boundaries and pairs every shift with a no-shift control. We find that agents, by default, settle on a small recurring routine within a few turns of each boundary, with call shares concentrating on a few discrete values after each reliability shift. We score the set-shifting accuracy for each agent trajectory: the joint probability of routing to the target tool group in every post-shift window. We test open-weight LLMs in an open-source agentic harness and find qualitatively distinct failure modes across the same set of routines. We also find that set framing, how the toolset presents the alternatives as competing or complementary, shifts the routing dynamics.

</details>


### [42] [AgentCompass: A Unified Evaluation Infrastructure for Agent Capabilities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13705)
*Zichen Ding,Jiaye Ge,Shufan Jiang,Kai Chen,Mo Li,Qingqiu Li,Zehao Li,Zonglin Li,Tiaohao Liang,Shudong Liu,Zerun Ma,Zixing Shang,Wenhui Tian,Zun Wang,Liwei Wu,Zhenyu Wu,Jun Xu,Bowen Yang,Dingbo Yuan,Qi Zhang,Songyang Zhang,Peiheng Zhou,Dongsheng Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AgentCompass is an open-source, modular evaluation infrastructure for LLM-based agents that decouples benchmarks, harnesses, and environments, supporting 20+ benchmarks across five capability dimensions with fault-tolerant async execution and trajectory analysis tools.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM agent evaluation pipelines are highly fragmented and tightly coupled, which hinders reproducibility and leads to redundant engineering effort across different projects. A unified, extensible infrastructure is critically needed as LLMs increasingly evolve into autonomous agents.

Method: AgentCompass decomposes the evaluation process into three independent, pluggable components — Benchmark (defines tasks and metrics), Harness (orchestrates agent-task interaction), and Environment (provides execution context and tools) — enabling flexible configuration without reimplementing core logic. It also includes a fault-tolerant asynchronous runtime for robust execution and comprehensive trajectory analysis tools to diagnose subtle failure modes such as reward-hacking.

Result: AgentCompass natively supports over 20 benchmarks spanning five capability dimensions, demonstrating broad coverage. The trajectory analysis tools enable transparent diagnosis of nuanced failure modes, and the asynchronous runtime ensures fault tolerance during evaluation.

Conclusion: AgentCompass provides the research community with a scalable, reproducible, and lightweight infrastructure that reduces engineering overhead and promotes standardized, transparent evaluation of LLM-based agents.

Abstract: As Large Language Models (LLMs) evolve into autonomous agents, the need for unified evaluation infrastructure becomes critical. However, current evaluation pipelines remain highly fragmented and tightly coupled, hindering reproducibility and causing redundant engineering. To address this, we introduce AgentCompass, an open-source, lightweight, and extensible infrastructure for evaluating LLM-based agents. AgentCompass organizes the evaluation process around three independent components, namely Benchmark, Harness, and Environment, thereby enabling flexible configurations without requiring the reimplementation of complex execution logic. Furthermore, it features a fault-tolerant asynchronous runtime and comprehensive trajectory analysis tools to transparently diagnose nuanced failure modes like reward-hacking. Natively supporting over 20 benchmarks across five capability dimensions, AgentCompass provides the community with a scalable and reproducible infrastructure for advancing agent research.

</details>


### [43] [OriginBlame: Record- and Token-Level Data Provenance for AI Training Datasets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13037)
*Haolin Xue*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ob is a record- and token-level data provenance system that tracks author identity through data pipelines, enabling precise forget-set generation for machine unlearning. It reduces over-deletion from 101× to 1.3× compared to dataset-level approaches, adds minimal throughput overhead (1.3–19%), and improves unlearning quality by 42% on a 1.7B model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: When individuals request data removal under regulations like GDPR, model trainers face a critical gap: existing unlearning algorithms need a precise "forget set" (exactly which training records belong to a given author), but no tool can locate these records. Current provenance systems operate at file or dataset granularity, forcing catastrophic over-deletion — deleting far more data than necessary, degrading model utility.

Method: The authors present ob, a record- and token-level provenance system that propagates author identity through data processing pipelines. It instruments data loading and preprocessing to maintain fine-grained lineage metadata — from original source documents through transformations to final training tokens. When a revocation request arrives, the system resolves it into a precise forget set using deterministic queries over the provenance graph, without requiring full pipeline re-execution.

Result: On 219,555 Wikipedia pages: (1) record-level provenance reduces dataset-level over-deletion from 101× to just 1.3× excess data removed; (2) integration overhead is 1.3–4.0% throughput loss on HuggingFace datasets and 2.1–19.0% on Datatrove pipelines for wiki data; (3) on a 1.7B parameter model, provenance-based forget sets improve unlearning performance by 42% over random baseline forget sets.

Conclusion: Fine-grained data provenance at the record and token level bridges a practical gap between machine unlearning algorithms and real-world deletion requests. By enabling precise forget-set construction with minimal overhead, ob makes compliance-driven data removal both practical and less destructive to model utility than coarse dataset-level approaches.

Abstract: When a data contributor requests removal, model trainers face a practical gap: unlearning algorithms require a forget set, yet no tool can locate which training records belong to a given author. Existing provenance systems operate at file or dataset level, forcing catastrophic over-deletion. We present ob, a record- and token-level data provenance system that propagates author identity through data processing pipelines and resolves revocation requests into precise forget sets via deterministic queries. Evaluation on 219,555 Wikipedia pages demonstrates that record-level provenance eliminates dataset-level over-deletion (from 101x to 1.3x), while integration adds 1.3-4.0% throughput overhead (HuggingFace) and 2.1-19.0% (Datatrove) on wiki data. On a 1.7B model, provenance-based forget sets improve unlearning by 42% over random baselines.

</details>


### [44] [SPINE: Bridging the Cyber-Physical Gap with Agentic AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13049)
*Minkyu Ham,Dongho Kim,Chan Lee,Jiayi Wang,Min Jun Kim,Yixi Zhang,Guo Ye,Jihai Zhao,Soyeon Park,Han Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SPINE is an agentic framework that uses two multi-agent workflows (profile builder and debugger) to enable non-experts to calibrate and deploy bimanual robots, outperforming expert baselines in both success rate and time-to-teleoperation across two distinct robotic platforms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deploying foundation model intelligence onto physical robots requires tedious, expert-driven calibration — termed the "spinal cord" bottleneck. This deployment gap is a primary obstacle to scalable Embodied AI, motivating a solution that reduces dependence on specialized robotics expertise.

Method: SPINE employs an agentic framework with two orchestrated multi-agent workflows: (1) a profile builder that constructs robot-specific context, and (2) a debugger that iterates through diagnosis, repair, and validation cycles until teleoperation is achieved. The system is evaluated on two bimanual platforms — DOBOT X-Trainer and AgileX PiPER (ROS/CAN-based) — across multiple debugging scenarios with both novice and expert human baselines.

Result: On DOBOT X-Trainer (7 scenarios), a novice using SPINE achieved 100% operationalization success (vs. 75% for Claude Code baseline) and reduced mean time-to-teleoperation from 16 min 45 s to 13 min 47 s.
