On AgileX PiPER, SPINE resolved all 10 implanted bugs versus 9/10 for the expert baseline in comparable time, demonstrating cross-platform transferability.

Conclusion: SPINE demonstrates that structured multi-agent workflows can bridge the deployment gap in Embodied AI, enabling cross-platform transfer, reducing reliance on expert calibration, and advancing scalable real-world robot deployment.

Abstract: Foundation models have given robots a sophisticated brain for complex decision-making, yet deploying that intelligence into a physical platform still demands tedious, expert-driven calibration. This deployment gap, the robot's spinal cord, remains a primary bottleneck to scalable Embodied AI. Hence, we propose SPINE (Scalable Physical Integration with ageNtic Expertise): an agentic framework for systematically debugging and deploying bimanual robots with minimal robotics expertise. SPINE's harness comprises two orchestrated multi-agent workflows: a profile builder that creates robot-specific context, and a debugger that cycles through diagnosis, repair, and validation until teleoperation works. Across seven DOBOT X-Trainer debugging scenarios, a robotics novice using SPINE outperformed human operators using Claude Code with the same reference materials, but without SPINE's structured workflow, improving operationalization success from 75% to 100% and reducing mean time-to-teleoperation from 16 min 45 s to 13 min 47 s. On AgileX PiPER, a distinct ROS/CAN bimanual arm, SPINE resolved all 10 implanted bugs, versus 9 out of 10 for the expert baseline, in nearly the same amount of time. Together, these results show that SPINE can transfer across bimanual platforms, reduce dependence on expert calibration, and move embodied AI closer to scalable real-world deployment.

</details>


### [45] [Interventional Grounding Audits: Black-Box Premise-Dependency Tests for LLM Chain-of-Thought via Predicate Substitution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13069)
*Hironao Nakamura*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces interventional grounding audits, a black-box method that tests whether each reasoning step in a chain-of-thought genuinely depends on its stated premises by intervening with predicate substitutions. On the ProntoQA benchmark with GPT-4o, the method achieves high F1 in detecting premise dependencies and uncovers "right answer, wrong reasoning" cases invisible to passive evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Chain-of-thought reasoning from LLMs can appear logically valid while lacking genuine dependence on stated premises—a phenomenon known as "post-hoc rationalization." Existing evaluation methods are largely passive (e.g., self-consistency) and cannot verify whether each individual reasoning step actually relies on its premises. The authors aim to provide a step-level, black-box audit that causally tests premise dependency.

Method: The proposed method is called interventional grounding audits. It operates as follows: (1) For a given CoT reasoning trace, identify individual premises containing target predicates. (2) Intervene by substituting the target predicate with a fresh, semantically neutral symbol. (3) Re-run the model with the intervened premise. (4) Check whether the normalized conclusion (canonical predicate form) of each reasoning step changes. A change indicates genuine premise dependency; no change signals that the step was insensitive to the premise. The method is evaluated on ProntoQA, a synthetic multi-hop deductive reasoning benchmark with gold proof trees, where ground-truth step-level premise dependencies are known.

Result: On 50 ProntoQA problems using GPT-4o, interventional grounding audits achieved F1=0.806 for detecting proof-tree dependencies and F1=0.885 for predicate-determining dependencies, with 100% recall. This significantly outperforms a self-consistency baseline (F1=0.343; 95% bootstrap CIs non-overlapping). Additionally, 66% of correctly-solved problems contained at least one aligned step insensitive to a direct proof-tree dependency under consistent substitution—predominantly involving entity-introduction premises—revealing "right answer, wrong reasoning" signals invisible to passive methods.

Conclusion: Interventional grounding audits provide a robust, black-box, step-level method for detecting whether CoT reasoning genuinely depends on its premises. The method reveals systematic reasoning failures (e.g., entity-introduction blind spots) that passive evaluation misses. However, the approach is currently limited to formal, parsable benchmarks and may not directly generalize to less structured domains.

Abstract: Large language models produce chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning that appears logically sound yet may not genuinely depend on its stated premises. We introduce interventional grounding audits, a black-box, step-level test of premise dependency: we intervene on a single premise by substituting its target predicate with a fresh symbol, re-run the model, and check whether each reasoning step's normalized conclusion (canonical predicate form) changes. We evaluate on ProntoQA, a synthetic multi-hop deductive reasoning benchmark with gold proof trees, where step-level premise dependencies are known. Applied to 50 ProntoQA problems with GPT-4o, our method achieves F1 = 0.806 on detecting proof-tree dependencies (F1 = 0.885 on predicate-determining dependencies; Recall = 100%), significantly outperforming a self-consistency baseline (F1 = 0.343; 95% bootstrap CIs non-overlapping). We further identify that 66% of correctly-solved problems contain at least one aligned step insensitive to a direct proof-tree dependency under consistent substitution -- all involving entity-introduction premises, a documented blind spot of the consistent-substitution evaluator -- a "right answer, wrong reasoning" signal invisible to passive methods. All audit certificates, raw outputs, and reproduction scripts are available in a public GitHub repository, and we discuss scope limits beyond formal, parsable benchmarks.

</details>


### [46] [Probabilistic Extension of Neuro-Symbolic AGI Robots based on Belnap's Typed Intensional FOL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13073)
*Zoran Majkic*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper extends the neuro-symbolic AI framework IFOL_B by incorporating Nilsson's probability structures and Shannon's maximum entropy principle. It introduces global symmetry transformations for knowledge preservation and local symmetry transformations for real-time subproblem decisions, with neural networks computing the probability density function KI for unknown sentences.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome limitations of purely neural systems (lack of interpretability and logical structure) by expanding the cognitive power of IFOL_B, a neuro-symbolic AI framework. The authors aim to enable probabilistic reasoning over unknown sentences while preserving logical soundness through symmetry transformations.

Method: The approach builds on IFOL_B, integrating Nilsson's probability structures to compute probabilities for unknown sentences. It introduces two classes of symmetry transformations: global transformations that preserve the knowledge database and logical deduction, and local transformations for real-time decisions on constrained subproblems. The probability density function KI is derived using Shannon's maximum information entropy principle, implemented via neural networks within a probabilistic neuro-symbolic AGI architecture.

Result: The paper presents a formal framework combining logical self-reference with probabilistic inference. The global and local symmetry transformations provide theoretically-grounded mechanisms for handling uncertainty in neuro-symbolic systems, while the KI probability density function enables principled computation of probabilities for unknown sentences under maximum entropy constraints.

Conclusion: The expanded IFOL_B framework, augmented with Nilsson's probability structures and symmetry transformations, enhances the cognitive capabilities of neuro-symbolic AGI by enabling principled probabilistic reasoning over incomplete knowledge. The neural network-based computation of KI under maximum entropy provides a bridge between symbolic logical deduction and sub-symbolic probabilistic inference.

Abstract: Neuro-symbolic AI based on $IFOL_B$ is a way to combine neural learning and symbolic reasoning to overcome limitations of purely neural systems (like lack of interpretability and logical structure) with formal logical machinery for self-reference. In this paper we expand the cognitive power of $IFOL_B$ by using the probability computation for the currently unknown sentences, based on Nilsson's probability structure for the $IFOL_B$. We introduce the global symmetry transformation that preserves the current knowledge database and logical deduction, and the local one used for real-time decisions about concrete (sub)problems that involve only a very strict subset of $IFOL_B$ predicates. The computation of probability density function $KI$ in both cases, based on the Shannon's maximum information entropy, is provided by neural networks of this probabilistic neuro-symbolic AGI.

</details>


### [47] [Self-Improvements in Modern Agentic Systems: A Survey](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13104)
*Zhe Ren,Yimeng Chen,Dandan Guo,Guowei Rong,Tonghui Li,R. B. Xiong,Qingfeng Lan,Wenyi Wang,Li Nanbo,Yibo Yang,Mingchen Zhuge,Jürgen Schmidhuber*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This survey formalizes self-improving AI agents as adaptive systems that update either model parameters or scaffold components (prompts, memory, tools, control logic) through self-induced update operators, organizing prior work by update targets and driving signals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Self-improving autonomous agents are transitioning from research to deployment, creating a need for a unified framework to understand and categorize how these systems achieve controllable evolution from experience with minimal human input.

Method: The authors propose a system-level framework that models an agent as a configuration coupling a foundation model with an operational scaffold (prompts, memory, tools, control logic). Self-improvement is formalized as a self-induced update operator that obtains and commits updates to either model parameters or scaffold components. The survey organizes prior work along two axes: the target of the update and the signals that drive the change.

Result: The survey provides a structured taxonomy of self-improving agent approaches, reviews applications, discusses evaluation methodologies, and identifies open problems and future directions. A curated repository of technical resources is maintained at the provided GitHub link.

Conclusion: Self-improving agents represent a significant frontier in AI, with a coherent design space emerging around update targets and driving signals. Key open problems remain in evaluation, safety, and ensuring truly controllable evolution.

Abstract: Self-improving autonomous agents are moving from research prototypes to deployed systems. The primary goal is controllable evolution, or adaptation, from experience with minimal or even no human input. This survey frames modern self-improving agents as adaptive systems that convert experience into accumulated capability gains. We offer a system-level framework that represents a modern agent as a configuration coupling a foundation model with an operational scaffold of prompts, memory, tools, and control logic. Within this framework, self-improvement is formalized as a self-induced update operator that obtains and commits updates to model parameters or scaffold components. We organize prior work by update target and by the signals that drive change, then review applications and discuss evaluation, before closing with open problems and future directions. For convenience, we track technical updates on https://github.com/selfimproving-agent/awesome-Self-Improving-Agents.

</details>


### [48] [Improving Molecular Property Prediction in Small Language Models Using Graph-based Tools](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13115)
*Konstantinos Bougiatiotis,Dimitrios Kelesis,Georgios Paliouras*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Small language models for molecular property prediction are augmented with GNN-derived graph context (predictive hints, explanatory subgraphs, and text descriptions) via a modular prompting framework, yielding up to 74% relative improvement on Tox21, though a gap to specialized GNNs persists.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: SLMs operating on SMILES strings suffer from "structural blindness" because linear sequence representations inadequately capture molecular graph topology, limiting their zero-shot predictive performance.

Method: A Context-Augmented Prompting (CAP) framework where, at inference time, a trained GNN provides a predictive hint with confidence, and a GNN-based explainer extracts an instance-specific subgraph (as subgraph SMILES and an explanatory paragraph). Three SLMs are evaluated on MUTAG and Tox21 across five prompting configurations spanning SMILES-only to full tool use.

Result: Graph-enriched prompts yield substantial accuracy gains, often exceeding 25% relative improvement and reaching up to 74% on Tox21. Functional relevance of extracted motifs is validated via necessity-based edge-drop intervention. A persistent gap to specialized GNN models remains.

Conclusion: Augmenting SLMs with graph-derived structural context significantly boosts molecular property prediction, but text-conditioned reasoning alone cannot yet match dedicated GNN architectures, underscoring both the value and limitations of this hybrid approach.

Abstract: Small language models (SLMs) have shown promise for zero-shot molecular property prediction from SMILES strings, yet they often suffer from structural blindness because sequence representations under-specify key graph-topological cues. We propose a modular Context-Augmented Prompting framework that enables agentic tool use at inference time: a trained GNN expert model provides a predictive hint with confidence, and a GNN extracts an instance-specific explanatory subgraph (e.g., a subgraph SMILES and an accompanying explanatory paragraph). We evaluate three commonly used SLMs on MUTAG and Tox21 under five prompting configurations ranging from SMILES-only to using all available tools at hand. Across two datasets, enriching prompts with graph-derived context yields substantial accuracy gains, often exceeding 25% relative improvement and up to 74% on Tox21. We further validate the functional relevance of the extracted motifs via a necessity-based edge-drop intervention. Despite the observed gains, a persistent gap remains to specialized GNN models, highlighting both the value and limits of text-conditioned reasoning for molecular structure.

</details>


### [49] [Oracle Agent Memory as an Enterprise Memory Substrate for Long-Horizon AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13157)
*Richmond Alake,Cesare Bernardis,Paul Cayet,Luca Engel,Damien Hilloulin,Sungpack Hong,Allen Hosler,Nickolas Kavantzas,Ingo Kossyk,Son Le,Rhicheek Patra,Kartik Talamadupula,Valentin Venzin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Oracle Agent Memory is a database-native memory layer for long-horizon agents that manages the full memory lifecycle (ingestion, extraction, consolidation, retrieval, summarization, revision) with layered architecture separating active memory from passive storage. It achieves 93.8% accuracy on LongMemEval using ~10.7x fewer tokens than flat-history baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Practical agent deployments need memory beyond simple document retrieval — they must retain task state across long conversations, recover user-specific facts across sessions, and accumulate procedural knowledge from prior outcomes. Existing approaches lack systematic handling of what becomes durable state, how it is scoped, retrieved under latency constraints, and revised over time.

Method: The system is built directly on Oracle Database as a native memory substrate. It employs a layered architecture separating an active memory core from a passive memory-store interface, with explicit scope control across users, agents, and threads. The memory lifecycle covers ingestion, extraction, consolidation, retrieval, summarization, and revision/removal. Evaluation uses both downstream task accuracy and memory-centric metrics: evidence retrieval, recall, latency, and estimated token consumption.

Result: The system achieves 93.8% accuracy on the LongMemEval benchmark. Compared to flat-history baselines, it uses approximately 10.7x fewer tokens. Results are contextualized against published or reported external baselines where available.

Conclusion: A database-native approach to agent memory, with explicit lifecycle management and layered architecture, can dramatically improve both accuracy and token efficiency for long-horizon agents, as demonstrated by strong LongMemEval results and substantial token savings over flat-history methods.

Abstract: Agent memory is a systems problem for long-horizon agents. Practical deployments require retention of task state across extended conversations, recovery of user-specific facts and preferences across sessions, and accumulation of procedural knowledge from prior outcomes. These requirements extend beyond document retrieval: a memory layer must determine which interactions become durable state, how that state is scoped, how it is retrieved under latency constraints, and how it is revised or removed over time. This report studies Oracle Agent Memory as a database-native memory substrate built on Oracle Database. Three themes organize the discussion: memory as a lifecycle spanning ingestion, extraction, consolidation, retrieval, summarization, and revision or removal; a layered architecture that separates an active memory core from a passive memory-store interface with explicit scope control across users, agents, and threads; and evaluation methodology in which downstream task accuracy is complemented by memory-centric measures such as evidence retrieval, recall, latency, and estimated token use. The report summarizes LongMemEval results, reaching 93.8% accuracy, compares Oracle Agent Memory against flat-history baselines, using about 10.7x fewer tokens, and published or reported external baselines where available, and closes with implementation-oriented appendix material covering setup, thread lifecycle, and search semantics.

</details>


### [50] [Learning Safe Agent Behaviour from Human Preferences and Justifications via World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13172)
*Ilias Kazantzidis,Timothy J. Norman,Yali Du,Christopher T. Freeman*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DROPJ is a human-centered RL method that learns a world model from offline data, uses human gameplay in the simulator to generate trajectories, elicits preference comparisons with safety justifications, trains a reward model, and deploys via model predictive control — reducing training cost and improving safety.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In safety-critical settings, unknown environment dynamics and lack of reward functions make traditional RL impractical. The authors aim to leverage human input for both safe training and deployment of agent policies without real-world risk.

Method: DROPJ proceeds in four stages: (1) learn a world model from offline real-world trajectory data; (2) have a human play in the learned simulator to generate informative simulated trajectories; (3) sample pairs of trajectory segments, elicit human preferences and safety justifications, then train a reward model from these justified preferences; (4) deploy the agent via model predictive control using the learned reward model and world model.

Result: Human-generated informative simulated trajectories significantly reduce training computational cost compared to other strategies and improve deployment performance. Preference-based feedback substantially outperforms other feedback types in deployment performance. Safety justifications accompanying preferences significantly enhance safety and allow prioritization of user-specified safety aspects during deployment.

Conclusion: DROPJ demonstrates that combining human gameplay in learned simulators with justified preference elicitation yields a practical, safe, and computationally efficient approach for training and deploying agents in safety-critical environments where traditional RL fails.

Abstract: We address the problem of safely training an agent policy and deploying a good and safe policy, in settings where the environment dynamics are unknown and no suitable reward function is available. In the context of safety-critical environments, we consider traditional reinforcement learning impractical and resort to the resource of human input. We introduce DROPJ, a human-centred method for both safe training and deployment. We first learn a world model (a learned simulator) from a dataset of prior real-world trajectories. A human then plays the game in this learned simulator to extract several informative simulated trajectories. From these, we sample pairs of simulated trajectory segments and elicit from a human their preference over these segments, as well as a reason (justification) for their choice. We then train a reward model from these justified preferences and use it, together with the world model, to directly deploy the agent using model predictive control. Running real-user experiments, we find that generating informative simulated trajectories from a user significantly reduces the computational cost during training compared to other strategies, and can also improve the performance during deployment. In the context of training within a learned simulator, we show that the use of preferences rather than other types of feedback substantially improves the performance during deployment. We further demonstrate that safety justifications accompanying preferences can significantly enhance safety or prioritise user-prescribed aspects of safety associated with them during deployment.

</details>


### [51] [CayleyR: Solving the TopSpin puzzle via cycle intersection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13219)
*Yuri Baramykov*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: cayleyR is an R package that solves permutation puzzles like TopSpin(n,k) using iterative bidirectional search on Cayley graphs, detecting cycle intersections between random walks from start and target states, with C++ and optional Vulkan GPU acceleration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide an efficient, publicly available software tool for solving permutation puzzles by leveraging the algebraic structure of Cayley graphs of the symmetric group, specifically targeting the TopSpin(n,k) puzzle class.

Method: Iterative bidirectional search on the Cayley graph of Sn: random operation sequences generate cycles from both initial and target states; when their intersection is non-empty, a connecting path is found. If no direct intersection exists, a distance-guided bridge selection narrows the gap and the process repeats. Implementation uses a C++ hash-indexed state store with optional Vulkan GPU acceleration.

Result: The package is implemented, publicly available on CRAN, and provides a working solver for the TopSpin(n,k) puzzle whose state space forms a Cayley graph of Sn generated by a cyclic shift and a prefix reversal.

Conclusion: cayleyR successfully demonstrates that cycle-intersection-based bidirectional search on Cayley graphs is a viable method for solving structured permutation puzzles, with the package offering both CPU and GPU-accelerated backends for practical use.

Abstract: We present cayleyR, an R package for solving permutation puzzles by detecting cycle intersections in Cayley graphs. The core algorithm performs an iterative bidirectional search: from both the initial and target permutation states, random operation sequences generate cycles in the Cayley graph of the symmetric group Sn; their intersection yields a connecting path. When no direct intersection is found, a distance-guided bridge selection narrows the gap, and the process repeats. The package targets the TopSpin(n,k) puzzle, whose state space is a Cayley graph of Sn generated by a cyclic shift and a prefix reversal. We describe the mathematical framework, the algorithm, and its implementation, which combines a C++ hash-indexed state store with optional Vulkan GPU acceleration. The software is publicly available on CRAN.

</details>


### [52] [AI-Native Insurance for Agentic AI: Pricing, Underwriting, and End-to-End Automation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13230)
*Quanyan Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents a mathematical framework for insuring agentic AI systems, modeling risk via a multidimensional risk state and formulating insurance contract design as a constrained optimization problem. It characterizes insurability conditions and demonstrates the approach through a healthcare case study.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic AI systems—capable of autonomous decisions, tool invocation, and external environment modification—pose novel insurance challenges that traditional actuarial frameworks cannot adequately address. There is a need for an AI-native underwriting and pricing framework tailored to the unique risk profile of autonomous AI deployments.

Method: A deployment is modeled through a risk state vector capturing five dimensions: autonomy level, operational authority, permission exposure, governance maturity, and dependency concentration. This risk state is mapped to event probabilities, loss severities, governance costs, premiums, deductibles, coverage allocation, and policy covenants. An optimization problem is then formulated for insurance contract design subject to participation, profitability, and incentive compatibility constraints. Structural properties of insurability are derived, including an insurability region, monotonic feasibility deterioration with exposure, and governance certification thresholds.

Result: The framework yields a characterization of the insurability region, proof of monotone deterioration of feasibility with increasing exposure, and identification of governance certification thresholds required for coverage eligibility. Insurance is shown to function dually as an operational cost and a regulatory mechanism for AI deployments.

Conclusion: The proposed framework provides a principled, mathematically rigorous foundation for underwriting and pricing agentic AI insurance. A healthcare case study demonstrates practical applicability, including contract optimization, sensitivity analysis, and automated claims processing, positioning insurance as both a cost and a governance instrument for autonomous AI systems.

Abstract: Agentic AI introduces new insurance challenges because autonomous AI systems can make decisions, invoke tools, modify external environments, and interact with third-party services. This paper develops an AI-native mathematical framework for underwriting, pricing, and contract design for agentic AI deployments. A deployment is represented by a risk state that captures autonomy level, operational authority, permission exposure, governance maturity, and dependency concentration. The framework maps the risk state to event probabilities, loss severities, governance costs, premiums, deductibles, coverage allocation, and policy covenants, and formulates an optimization problem for insurance contract design under participation, profitability, and incentive compatibility constraints. The paper establishes structural properties of insurability, including characterization of an insurability region, monotone deterioration of feasibility with increasing exposure, and governance certification thresholds. Insurance is further interpreted as both an operational cost and a regulatory mechanism for AI deployment. A healthcare case study illustrates contract optimization, sensitivity analysis, and automated claims processing for agentic AI systems.

</details>


### [53] [Cost-Optimal Foundation Model Deployment Portfolio for Transportation Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13239)
*Xi Cheng,Ke Liu,Siyuan Feng,Jane Lin,H. Oliver Gao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper formulates the Foundation Model Deployment Portfolio (FMDP) problem — a mixed-integer program that selects which (model, deployment mode) pairs should serve each TMC function to minimize total cost of ownership under quality, latency, safety, and shared GPU constraints. The problem is proven NP-hard, a greedy heuristic is proposed, and a case study demonstrates 97% cost savings by strategically mixing open-source and closed-API models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As foundation models (LLMs, VLMs) proliferate across transportation management center tasks, practitioners face a portfolio allocation problem: multiple models with different cost, quality, and latency profiles must be assigned to multiple functions under a shared hardware budget. There is no existing framework for making these joint model selection and deployment-mode decisions optimally.

Method: The authors formalize FMDP as a mixed-integer program minimizing TCO subject to per-function quality, latency, and safety constraints with shared GPU capacity. NP-hardness is proved via reduction from 0-1 knapsack. A polynomial-time greedy heuristic is proposed for practical solution. An illustrative case study with 5 TMC functions and 19 candidate (model, mode) pairs evaluates the approach, supplemented by break-even analysis for on-premise GPU investment.

Result: The FMDP solution yields a mixed portfolio at $34/month — 97% below the cheapest feasible all-closed-API baseline. Four functions are routed to open-source APIs; only one function (whose quality floor no open-source model meets) uses a closed API. Break-even analysis reveals on-premise GPU investment is only justified above ~309 vision queries/hour or if API prices double.

Conclusion: Strategic mixing of open-source and closed-API foundation models can dramatically reduce TCO for TMC deployments. The FMDP framework provides a principled optimization approach for this portfolio decision, and the greedy heuristic offers a practical solution method for real-world instances.

Abstract: Foundation models, including large language models (LLMs) and vision-language models (VLMs), are increasingly used for transportation management center (TMC) tasks such as anomaly detection, incident reporting, and traveler information. Deploying multiple such models across TMC functions raises a portfolio question: which model should serve each function, in which deployment mode, and under what shared hardware budget? We formulate this as the Foundation Model Deployment Portfolio (FMDP) problem, a mixed-integer program minimizing total cost of ownership (TCO) subject to per-function quality, latency, and safety constraints over shared GPU capacity. We prove the problem NP-hard by reduction from the 0-1 knapsack problem and propose a polynomial-time greedy heuristic. In an illustrative case study with five TMC functions and 19 candidate (model, mode) pairs, FMDP identifies a mixed portfolio costing $34/mo (97% below the cheapest feasible all-closed-API baseline) by routing four functions to open-source APIs and the one function whose quality floor no open-source model meets to a closed API. Break-even analysis shows that on-premise GPU investment becomes reasonable only above approximately 309 vision queries/hour or if API prices double.

</details>


### [54] [Theory-Level Autoformalization: From Isolated Statements to Unified Formal Knowledge Bases](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13292)
*Marcus J. Min,Mike He,Zhaoyu Li,Zixuan Yi,Sharad Malik,Aarti Gupta,Xujie Si,Osbert Bastani*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Argues for a shift from statement-level to theory-level autoformalization, where entire mathematical theories (axioms, definitions, lemmas, and their interdependencies) are formalized as structured libraries, and outlines challenges and future directions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current autoformalization research focuses narrowly on translating individual statements, ignoring that real formalization requires an entire web of axioms, definitions, and lemmas before target theorems can even be stated. This gap motivates a paradigm shift to theory-level formalization.

Method: This is a position paper that surveys existing autoformalization approaches, examines the significance and challenges of theory-level formalization, addresses alternative viewpoints, and proposes three promising research directions for advancing the field.

Result: The paper identifies key open challenges including: (1) handling complex inter-dependencies among theory components, (2) maintaining consistency across large formal libraries, and (3) scaling beyond isolated statements. Three paths forward are proposed as promising directions.

Conclusion: Theory-level autoformalization represents a necessary evolution of the field, requiring structured libraries that capture complete theories rather than isolated statements. The paper advocates for community attention to this broader framing and provides a curated survey resource.

Abstract: Autoformalization translates informal natural language into formal, machine-verifiable languages. While most work focuses on individual statements, real formalization efforts are inherently theory-level: they require an entire web of axioms, definitions, and lemmas before target theorems can even be stated. In this position paper, we argue for theory-level autoformalization: formalizing complete theories, including all their inter-dependencies, as structured libraries. We examine the significance of this shift, address alternative views, identify open challenges, and propose three promising paths forward. Our survey of autoformalization is available at https://github.com/marcusm117/Awesome-Autoformalization.

</details>


### [55] [EZSMT Version 3, Matured](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13344)
*Yuliya Lierler*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EZSMTV3 is an extensible SMT-based Constraint Answer Set Programming framework that improves upon EZSMT+ with a richer input language, weak constraint optimization, and easier integration of new constraint types, using off-the-shelf SMT solvers (CVC5, YICES, Z3) for reasoning. It benchmarks competitively against CLINGCON, CLINGO[DL], and CLINGO[LP] and supports mixed integer-real constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing CASP systems either implement custom constraint solvers or lack extensibility and expressive input languages. The authors aim to advance the translational approach by building a framework that (1) leverages mature, state-of-the-art SMT solvers rather than reinventing search, (2) supports richer constraint domains including mixed integer-real reasoning, and (3) provides a modular architecture for easily integrating new constraint types and future theoretical exploration.

Method: EZSMTV3 adopts a translational approach: CASP programs are compiled into SMT-LIB formulas, which are then solved by off-the-shelf SMT solvers (CVC5, YICES, Z3). The system extends EZSMT+ with a more expressive input language, introduces weak constraints for optimization (e.g., minimize/maximize), and defines clean extension interfaces for adding new constraint theories. No custom search procedures are implemented.

Result: Benchmarking shows EZSMTV3 is competitive with existing CASP systems (CLINGCON, CLINGO[DL], CLINGO[LP]). The system successfully handles mixed-domain problems combining integer and real constraints, demonstrating the flexibility gained by targeting general SMT solvers.

Conclusion: EZSMTV3 provides a robust, extensible platform for CASP that capitalizes on advances in SMT solving. Its translational architecture and support for weak constraints and mixed domains position it well for both practical applications and future theoretical work in the CASP paradigm.

Abstract: Constraint Answer Set Programming (CASP) is a hybrid reasoning paradigm that combines Answer Set Programming (ASP) with Constraint Processing and Satisfiability Modulo Theories (SMT), enabling powerful declarative encodings of complex combinatorial search problems. This paper presents the design and implementation of EZSMTV3, an extensible SMT-based CASP framework that advances the translational approach to CASP solving. Building upon the foundation of the EZSMT+ system, EZSMTV3 introduces a more expressive input language, supports optimization via weak constraints, and offers foundations for streamlined integration of new constraint types. Rather than implementing custom search procedures, EZSMTV3 leverages state-of-the-art SMT solvers, such as CVC5, YICES, and Z3 to perform reasoning. The paper provides benchmarking results comparing EZSMTV3 with its CASP peers such as CLINGCON, CLINGO[DL], and CLINGO[LP], while showcasing its ability to handle mixed-domain constraints involving both integers and reals. The system provides a robust platform for future extensions and theoretical exploration within the CASP domain.

</details>


### [56] [LAPO: Leave-One-Turn Attribution for Self-Generated Process Rewards in Multi-Turn Search Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13501)
*Qiang Zhu,Jiajun Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LAPO introduces a self-generated process-supervision method for multi-turn search reinforcement learning that uses backward leave-one-turn attribution — replacing a turn with a [DELETE] placeholder and measuring the resulting change in answer likelihood — combined with sign-consistency gating, achieving strong gains over baselines without any external reward models or judges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard terminal-outcome rewards in multi-turn search RL cannot differentiate between useful, redundant, and harmful intermediate search interactions, limiting the agent's ability to learn effective retrieval strategies. The authors aim to provide fine-grained turn-level process supervision without requiring external reward models, teachers, verifiers, or LLM judges.

Method: LAPO (Likelihood-based Attribution for Process-Oriented supervision) operates in two stages: (1) Backward Leave-One-Turn Attribution: For each search turn, the turn and its retrieval observation are replaced with a fixed [DELETE] placeholder, and the change in the current policy's mean log-likelihood of the gold answer (Answer-Likelihood Gain) is measured, capturing each turn's marginal contribution while preserving downstream interactions. (2) Sign-Consistency Gating: Only normalized process advantages whose directional sign agrees with the raw attribution scores are retained, filtering out noisy or contradictory signals.

Result: Across seven knowledge-intensive QA datasets with local retrieval, LAPO achieves an average exact-match score of 0.326, outperforming the strongest step-reward baseline (IGPO) by 0.053. Ablation studies confirm that both backward attribution and sign-consistency gating provide complementary benefits.

Conclusion: Policy-derived retrospective attribution, implemented via backward leave-one-turn deletion and sign-consistency gating, provides effective process supervision for multi-turn search agents without any external infrastructure, demonstrating that self-generated signals can reliably distinguish helpful from harmful intermediate search turns.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning for multi-turn search reasoning typically relies on terminal outcome rewards, which cannot distinguish useful, redundant, and harmful intermediate interactions. We propose LAPO, a self-generated process-supervision method based on backward leave-one-turn attribution. For each search turn, LAPO replaces the turn and its retrieval observation with a fixed [DELETE] placeholder and measures the resulting change in the current policy's mean log-likelihood of the gold answer. This Answer-Likelihood Gain estimates the turn's contribution while preserving all downstream interactions, allowing early evidence to be evaluated in the complete reasoning context. LAPO further applies sign-consistency gating, retaining only normalized process advantages whose directions agree with their raw attribution scores. The method requires no additional reward model, teacher, verifier, or LLM-as-a-Judge. Across seven knowledge-intensive question-answering datasets with local retrieval, LAPO achieves an average exact-match score of 0.326, outperforming the strongest step-reward baseline, IGPO, by 0.053. Ablations show complementary benefits from backward attribution and sign-consistency gating, demonstrating that policy-derived retrospective attribution can provide effective process supervision for multi-turn search agents.

</details>


### [57] [How Far Can Root Cause Analysis Go on Real-World Telemetry Data?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13548)
*Athira Gopal,Ashwanth Krishnan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper addresses root cause analysis (RCA) in microservice failures using multimodal telemetry. It introduces a Structured Multi-Agent RCA pipeline that outperforms baselines on the challenging OpenRCA dataset, and diagnoses failure origins via reverse reasoning. Key findings: reasoning capability—not data access—is the bottleneck; model-level improvements are essential.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Root cause identification in production microservices requires reasoning over large-scale, multimodal telemetry (metrics, logs, traces). The OpenRCA benchmark demonstrates that both classical causal discovery methods and LLM-based multi-agent systems achieve consistently low accuracy, motivating the need for better approaches and a systematic diagnosis of why failures occur.

Method: The authors propose (1) a Structured Multi-Agent RCA pipeline operating in both domain-knowledge and knowledge-free modes; (2) a reverse reasoning agent that, given ground truth, identifies which anomaly signals support the correct answer and classifies failures as Reasoning Gap (evidence present but unused) or Data Ambiguity (evidence genuinely absent); and (3) an automated rule mining pipeline that extracts discrimination rules from reverse reasoning reports to reduce manual knowledge curation.

Result: The structured multi-agent pipeline substantially outperforms existing baselines on OpenRCA. Reverse reasoning reveals that the required evidence is present in the vast majority of failures—the bottleneck is reasoning ability, not data access. Stronger models embed more domain expertise, and explicit knowledge injection partially compensates for weaker models. Even with perfect evidence extraction, scaffold engineering and better data pipelines alone cannot close the reasoning gap.

Conclusion: Model reasoning capability and domain knowledge are the primary constraints in microservice RCA. Improving scaffolds or data pipelines yields diminishing returns; fundamental progress requires model-level improvements in reasoning. The reverse reasoning framework provides a principled way to diagnose and understand failure modes, guiding future research toward the true bottleneck.

Abstract: Identifying root causes in production microservice failures requires reasoning over large-scale, multimodal telemetry spanning metrics, logs, and traces, a problem that has proved resistant to both classical and LLM-based approaches. The OpenRCA dataset exemplifies these challenges: it is large-scale, multimodal, and lacks detailed domain knowledge, and yields consistently low accuracy across all existing methods. We show that classical causal discovery methods and existing LLM-based multi-agent systems fail to reliably identify root causes on this benchmark, and present a Structured Multi-Agent RCA pipeline that substantially outperforms existing LLM-based and classical baselines, supporting both domain-knowledge and knowledge-free operating modes. To diagnose where failures originate, we introduce a reverse reasoning agent that, given the correct answer, identifies which signals in the extracted anomalies support it and determines whether Stage~1 had access to those signals, classifying each failure as Reasoning Gap (evidence present but unused) or Data Ambiguity (evidence genuinely absent). This analysis reveals that the required evidence is present in the vast majority of failures: the bottleneck is not data access but the agent's ability to reason over it correctly. We further introduce an automated rule mining pipeline that systematically extracts discrimination rules from reverse reasoning reports, reducing reliance on manual knowledge curation. Across all configurations, model reasoning capability and domain knowledge are the primary constraints: stronger models embed more domain expertise, and explicit knowledge injection partially compensates for this gap. Reasoning performance remains practically bounded even when evidence extraction is perfect: scaffold engineering and better data pipelines alone cannot close this gap; progress requires improvements at the model level.

</details>


### [58] [Multi-Agent Collaborative Reasoning with Tool-Augmented Evidence for Urban Region Profiling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13558)
*Xixuan Hao,Yutian Jiang,Jiabo Liu,Yihang Yang,Guangyin Jin,Song Gao,Yuxuan Liang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: UrbanAgent reframes urban region profiling as reasoning-driven inference using multi-agent collaboration across data modalities, outperforming correlation-driven multimodal fusion methods by 8.1% in R² with strong generalization to unseen cities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing urban region profiling methods are correlation-driven, assume cross-modal consistency, and rely on static pipelines, limiting robustness in heterogeneous or unseen urban regions. There is a need for a framework that explicitly addresses cross-modal inconsistencies through reasoning rather than absorbing them into a single latent representation.

Method: UrbanAgent instantiates an independent agent for each data modality (satellite imagery, POIs, textual descriptions, 3D building information) and employs structured multi-agent collaborative reasoning. It extends indicator prediction into a closed-loop process combining active evidence acquisition with iterative reasoning, using tool-augmented retrieval of external knowledge optimized via reinforcement learning.

Result: Extensive experiments on global urban datasets for carbon emissions, GDP, and population estimation show UrbanAgent consistently outperforms existing baselines, achieving an average R² improvement of 8.1% and demonstrating strong generalization in unseen-city settings.

Conclusion: The agentic, reasoning-driven paradigm effectively handles cross-modal inconsistencies and improves generalization in urban region profiling, suggesting a promising direction beyond static multimodal fusion approaches.

Abstract: Urban region profiling constitutes a core problem in urban computing, supporting applications such as population estimation, economic assessment, and environmental monitoring. Existing methods typically formulate this task as multimodal representation learning, fusing heterogeneous urban data, e.g., satellite imagery, points of interest, textual descriptions, and 3D building information, into latent embeddings for prediction. However, these approaches are largely correlation-driven, assume cross-modal consistency, and rely on static pipelines, which limit their robustness in heterogeneous or unseen urban regions. We propose UrbanAgent, an agentic framework that reframes urban region profiling as a reasoning-driven inference problem. UrbanAgent instantiates an independent agent for each data modality and performs structured multi-agent collaborative reasoning to explicitly address cross-modal inconsistencies rather than absorbing them into a single representation. In addition, UrbanAgent extends indicator prediction as a closed-loop process of active evidence acquisition and iterative reasoning, enabling agents to verify uncertain inferences through tool-augmented retrieval of external knowledge optimized via reinforcement learning. Extensive experiments on global urban datasets for Carbon emissions, GDP, and Population estimation show that UrbanAgent consistently outperforms existing baselines, achieving an average improvement of 8.1% in R2, and exhibiting strong generalization performance in unseen-city settings.

</details>


### [59] [SAFETY SENTRY: Context-Aware Human Intervention via EXECUTE-ASK-REFUSE Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13594)
*Tianyu Chen,Chujia Hu,Wenjie Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Safety Sentry proposes a three-way routing guard model (EXECUTE, ASK, REFUSE) for LLM agent tool calls, replacing binary safe/unsafe classification. It uses a single decoding call with a tunable threshold, enabling per-instance decisions that outperform baselines on accuracy and safety recall while controlling false positives and false negatives.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing guard models for LLM agent tool calls use binary safe/unsafe labels, which conflate two distinct judgments: whether an action is inherently harmful versus whether it is appropriate given the user's context. Additionally, current approaches operate at the action-category level rather than per-instance, causing frequent false alarms that erode user autonomy and lead to alert fatigue. The authors aim to address these shortcomings with a more nuanced, instance-level routing mechanism.

Method: The authors reframe tool-call safety as a three-way routing decision: EXECUTE (safe and appropriate), ASK (requires user clarification), and REFUSE (outright harmful). They instantiate this with Safety Sentry, a lightweight guard model that produces the routing decision in a single decoding call. A single decoding-time threshold allows the same fixed checkpoint to be adaptively tuned for different deployment risk tolerances without retraining, enabling fine-grained control over both false-positive and false-negative error rates.

Result: Safety Sentry outperforms a broad set of both open-weight and frontier closed-source baselines on overall accuracy and safety-related recall, while simultaneously controlling both directional error rates (incorrectly allowing unsafe actions and incorrectly blocking safe ones).

Conclusion: A three-way routing paradigm with a lightweight, single-decoding guard model offers a more practical and precise safety mechanism for LLM agents than binary classification, enabling deployment-specific calibration and reducing unnecessary interruptions while maintaining high safety recall.

Abstract: LLM agents act on real-world environments through tool calls, and a single misjudged action can cause irreversible harm. The standard safeguard is a guard model that labels each proposed action as safe or unsafe, but this binary view conflates two distinct decisions: whether the action is harmful in itself, and whether it is appropriate given the user's context. It also operates at the granularity of action categories rather than individual instances, producing routine interruptions that erode autonomy and train users to wave through the most consequential alerts. We reframe the problem as a per-instance three-way routing decision over {EXECUTE, ASK, REFUSE} and instantiate it with Safety Sentry, a lightweight guard model whose inference reduces to a single decoding call. A single decoding-time threshold lets one fixed checkpoint be re-positioned across deployments of differing risk tolerance without retraining. Safety Sentry outperforms a broad set of open-weight and frontier closed-source baselines on overall accuracy and safety-related recall, while controlling both directional error rates simultaneously.

</details>


### [60] [Automatic Ordinary Differential Equations Discovery For Biological Systems Using Large Language Model Powered Agentic System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13608)
*David Krongauz,Arad Zulti,Eran Segal,Teddy Lazebnik*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MEDA is an LLM- and symbolic regression-powered agentic framework for discovering ODE models of biological dynamical systems. It retrieves domain knowledge, defines variables, generates mechanistic constraints, proposes and fits candidate equations, and demonstrates strong structural recovery across retrieval, extrapolation, and open-ended discovery tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing computational scientific discovery systems either focus narrowly on equation-discovery benchmarks or on broad end-to-end automation, with biological systems remaining underexplored. There is a need for a system that can recover mechanistic ODE models—not just fit data—by incorporating domain priors, constraints, and background knowledge.

Method: MEDA combines LLM-based agents with symbolic regression. The framework: (1) retrieves background knowledge, (2) defines admissible variables, (3) generates mechanistic constraints, (4) proposes candidate ODEs, and (5) fits and evaluates them. Evaluation spans canonical model retrieval, reasoning-based extrapolation to unseen variants, and open-ended discovery, with ablation and robustness analyses isolating component contributions.

Result: MEDA recovered correct state variables and achieved strong structural recovery in both retrieval and extrapolation tasks. It produced biologically plausible models in open-ended discovery. Ablations revealed that knowledge-guided formalization and mechanistic constraints are load-bearing; numerical fitting alone preserves trajectory-compatible but biologically incorrect equations.

Conclusion: The MEDA framework demonstrates that integrating LLM-based reasoning with symbolic regression—underpinned by formalized domain knowledge and mechanistic constraints—enables robust discovery of biologically meaningful ODE models, advancing beyond pure data-fitting toward genuine mechanistic model generation.

Abstract: Automatic scientific discovery has long been a goal of computational scholars - a machine that can discover nature's secrets on its own, moving computational systems beyond data-fitting tools toward the generation and refinement of mechanistic models of the universe. Recent advances in symbolic regression (SR) and large-language-model (LLM)-based agents suggest that such systems can recover equations from data, incorporate domain priors, and automate parts of the research workflow. However, most existing approaches either focus on narrow equation-discovery benchmarks or broad end-to-end automation pipelines, while biological systems remain comparatively underexplored. Here, we introduce the MEDA system, an LLM- and SR-powered agentic framework for discovering ordinary-differential-equation (ODE) models of biological and biologically inspired dynamical systems. MEDA retrieves background knowledge, defines admissible variables, generates mechanistic constraints, proposes candidate ODEs, and fits and evaluates them. We evaluate it across canonical model retrieval, reasoning-based extrapolation to unseen variants, and open-ended discovery, with and without experimental data. Across these settings, MEDA recovered the correct state variables, achieved strong structural recovery in retrieval and extrapolation tasks, and produced biologically plausible discovery-oriented models. Ablation and robustness analyses show that knowledge-guided formalization and mechanistic constraints are load-bearing components, whereas numerical fitting alone can preserve trajectory-compatible but biologically incorrect equations.

</details>


### [61] [STOCKTAKE: Measuring the Gap Between Perception and Action in LLM Agents with a Fair Oracle](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13618)
*Sagar Deb,Ashwanth Krishnan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: STOCKTAKE is a 26-week supply-chain benchmark using a factored POMDP with six hidden factors. It introduces a skill score and separate metrics for state estimation (detection lag) and control (knowing-doing rate), revealing that frontier LLM agents detect hidden failures well (84-88%) yet two of four models perform below a symptom-blind baseline due to both under-response and costly over-response.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM agent evaluations on multi-week decision tasks conflate two failure modes: misreading the world (state estimation error) versus reading correctly but failing to act appropriately (the knowing-doing gap). Reference policies are either absent or rely on privileged information unavailable to the agent, making it impossible to diagnose why agents fail.

Method: STOCKTAKE is a 26-week supply-chain replenishment task formulated as a factored POMDP with six hidden factor processes. A fair oracle is computed via exact Bayes filtering per factor and a rollout policy on the agent's identical observation stream. Each run is scored between a symptom-blind base-stock floor (0) and the oracle (1), yielding a skill score. Weekly written rationales are graded to produce a stated-belief detection lag and a knowing-doing rate, enabling separate measurement of state estimation and control.

Result: On 50 seeds with curated stress profiles, Claude Sonnet 5, GPT-5.4, DeepSeek-V4-Pro, and Grok 4.5 detect 84-88% of hidden failures (typically within one week). Skill scores span 0.62 to -0.23; two models fall below the symptom-blind floor despite naming factors slightly faster. In persistent stress, 34-43% of correctly diagnosed stress weeks still result in stockout for every model. The two sub-floor models stock out least on diagnosed weeks, indicating that under-response is only one failure mode — costly over-response is the other.

Conclusion: The knowing-doing gap has two faces: under-response to detected problems and over-response whose cost exceeds benefits. STOCKTAKE's factored POMDP design with paired metrics for estimation and control enables measuring both directions of this failure, providing a more granular diagnostic framework for LLM agent evaluation.

Abstract: LLM agents are increasingly evaluated on multi-week decision tasks in which the state that drives cost is never directly observed. On such tasks the final cost cannot say why an agent failed: it may have misread the world, or read it correctly and still failed to act (the knowing-doing gap). Existing evaluations cannot separate these two failures; their reference policies either read privileged information the agent never sees, or are missing altogether. We introduce STOCKTAKE, a 26-week supply-chain replenishment benchmark built as a factored partially observable Markov decision process with six hidden factor processes, designed so that a fair reference policy is computable: an exact Bayes filter per factor drives a rollout policy on the identical observation stream the agent receives. Scoring each run between a symptom-blind base-stock floor (0) and this oracle (1) yields a skill score, and grading each week's written rationale yields a stated-belief detection lag and a knowing-doing rate, so state estimation and control are measured separately. On fifty seeds with curated stress profiles, Claude Sonnet 5, GPT-5.4, DeepSeek-V4-Pro, and Grok 4.5 detect 84-88% of hidden failures, typically within a week of onset, yet span skill scores from 0.62 to -0.23: two of the four end below the symptom-blind floor while naming factors slightly faster than the two that beat it. The failure has two faces. Where stress persists, 34-43% of correctly diagnosed stress weeks still end in stockout for every model, a rate that partly reflects the severity of the weeks models notice. That rate also runs opposite to skill: the two models under the floor stock out least on diagnosed weeks, so under-response is only one face of the gap, and their traces point to the other, responses whose cost exceeds what they protect. STOCKTAKE measures both directions of that failure.

</details>


### [62] [UESF-Bench: Benchmarking and Probing for Unified Embodied Seeking and Following](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13621)
*Kun Yu,Jianhua Yang,Yixiang Chen,Changwei Wang,Hongyuan Yu,Yan Huang,Fushuo Huo,Ya Jing,Zhumin Chen,Keji He*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: UESF-Bench is a unified benchmark for embodied agents that combines language-guided person seeking and following in dynamic environments. The authors propose SeekFollow-VLA, a vision-language-action framework with task-driven routing for phase inference, achieving significant improvements over baseline methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks for human following assume the target is visible from the start, ignoring the realistic need to first locate a language-described person. Current approaches treat searching and following as separate tasks, lack unified evaluation, and rely on strong environmental priors. This motivates a unified benchmark and framework that integrates both phases.

Method: The authors introduce UESF-Bench, a large-scale diverse benchmark requiring semantic-guided exploration, reliable behavior switching/recovery, and delayed identity grounding. They propose SeekFollow-VLA, a vision-language-action framework featuring a task-driven routing mechanism for latent phase inference and transition modeling between seeking and following modes.

Result: SeekFollow-VLA achieves clear improvements over both single-head and dual-head baselines across single-person and multi-person environments, establishing a strong baseline for unified embodied seek-and-follow tasks.

Conclusion: Unifying seeking and following into a single benchmark and framework addresses a critical gap in embodied agent research. SeekFollow-VLA demonstrates that task-driven routing with latent phase inference effectively handles the complexities of semantic-guided exploration and behavior switching in dynamic multi-person settings.

Abstract: Language-guided human following is an important capability for embodied agents, but existing benchmarks typically assume that the target person is visible at the start of an episode. This setting simplifies the problem and overlooks a more realistic requirement: an agent often needs to first find a language-described target and then persistently follow that target in a dynamic environment. While recent work has started to study human search, existing settings are typically evaluated in task-specific scenarios and often rely on stronger prior knowledge of the environment. Moreover, they usually treat searching and following as separate tasks and still lack a unified benchmark for systematic evaluation. To address these limitations, we introduce the Unified Embodied Seeking and Following Benchmark (UESF-Bench), a large-scale and diverse benchmark for embodied human seeking and following. The benchmark requires agents to handle semantic-guided exploration, reliable behavior switching and recovery, and delayed identity grounding. To this end, we propose SeekFollow-VLA, a vision-language-action framework with a task-driven routing mechanism for latent phase inference and transition modeling between seeking and following. Experimental results show that SeekFollow-VLA achieves clear improvements over both single-head and dual-head baselines across single-person and multi-person environments, establishing a baseline for unified embodied seek-and-follow.

</details>


### [63] [Explaining Reinforcement Learning Agents via Inductive Logic Programming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13655)
*Celeste Veronese,Edoardo Zorzi,Daniele Meli,Alessandro Farinelli*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces objective, planning-oriented metrics (activation rate, feature coverage, syntactic distance, semantic distance) for quantifying the explainability of logical rule-based RL policy representations extracted via Inductive Logic Programming. These metrics reveal action-specific learning dynamics, fine-grained feature importance, and multi-agent coordination patterns beyond what global return or classical feature importance methods capture.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: XRL currently relies heavily on user studies targeting specific audiences and lacks shared, objective evaluation metrics. Simultaneously, logic-based XAI methods produce human-readable abstractions but lack systematic, principled ways to quantify how explainable those logical representations actually are. The authors aim to bridge both gaps.

Method: The authors employ Inductive Logic Programming (ILP) to extract symbolic policy representations from trained RL agents. They then define four novel explainability metrics: (1) activation rate — how often a symbolic rule fires during execution; (2) feature coverage — which domain features appear in the rules and how they contribute; (3) syntactic distance — structural similarity between rule sets; and (4) semantic distance — behavioral divergence between rule sets. These are evaluated across single-agent and multi-agent RL domains to track policy evolution, feature roles, and agent coordination patterns.

Result: Experiments demonstrate that the proposed metrics: highlight action-specific learning dynamics invisible to global return analysis; provide finer-grained feature importance insights than classical global estimation methods; uncover coordination, specialization, and adaptation patterns in MARL settings; and offer actionable insights for policy transfer and generalization.

Conclusion: The proposed metrics offer a principled, objective quantification framework for policy explainability in XRL, moving the field beyond subjective user studies and simple propositional logic assessments. They enable deeper understanding of RL policy behavior at the action level, benefiting both single-agent and multi-agent RL analysis, transfer, and generalization.

Abstract: Explainable Reinforcement Learning (XRL) seeks to make Reinforcement Learning (RL) policies more transparent and interpretable, a key requirement in safety-critical and human-centric scenarios. However, it is mostly based on user studies, thus targeting the needs of a specific audience and lacking shared evaluation metrics. On the other hand, logic-based approaches within eXplainable Artificial Intelligence (XAI) provide compact, human-readable abstractions of decision-making. However, the systematic quantification of the explainability degree of logical representations remains an open problem. This work aims to advance the state of the art in XRL by introducing objective and planning-oriented metrics for policy explainability in RL settings. At the same time, it contributes to the field of logic for XAI by providing a principled way to quantify the explainability of logical rules, moving beyond common-sense assessments and simple propositional fragments. We employ Inductive Logic Programming (ILP) to extract symbolic representations of RL policies and define a novel set of explainability metrics, including activation rate, feature coverage, syntactic distance and semantic distance. These metrics quantify alignment between symbolic rules and agent behavior, the role of features in decision-making, and the evolution of policies during training and across agents in single and multi-agent RL. Experiments across different RL domains show that the proposed metrics highlight action-specific learning dynamics beyond global return, provide fine-grained insights into domain features beyond classical approaches for global feature importance estimation, and uncover coordination, specialization, and adaptation patterns in MARL. Moreover, they provide crucial insights for the transfer and generalization of action-specific policies.

</details>


### [64] [CAVA: Canonical Action Verification and Attestation for Runtime Governance of Agentic AI Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13716)
*Zexun Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CAVA provides a runtime-semantics layer that converts heterogeneous agent actions into canonical, verifiable runtime action objects, enabling independent verification of what action was approved, the binding between approval and execution, and reproducible action identity across disparate runtimes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic AI systems operate through diverse, incompatible runtimes (local hooks, SDKs, browsers, APIs, workflow engines), causing the same operational act to produce heterogeneous records. This fragmentation makes governance intractable: determining what action was approved, what evidence binds approval to execution, and whether an independent verifier can reproduce action identity later.

Method: CAVA is positioned beneath Proof-Carrying Agent Actions (PCAA), formalizing canonical action identity, semantic pattern detection, approval binding, receipt integrity, runtime-portable projection, and optional attestation substrates. A reference implementation was evaluated through a 96-seed, 384-variant benchmark covering semantic equivalence, separation, wrapper bypass, false-positive control, approval binding, receipt reproducibility, attestation tamper detection, runtime portability, semantic pattern detection, policy degradation, and Azure deployment drills.

Result: The 384-variant benchmark demonstrates CAVA's capability across eleven evaluation dimensions including semantic equivalence detection, wrapper bypass resistance, receipt reproducibility, attestation tamper detection, and runtime portability, validating canonical action identity as a feasible systems substrate.

Conclusion: Action-level canonicalization with policy-addressable semantic patterns constitutes a necessary substrate for deployer-side AI governance, bridging heterogeneous runtime records into stable, independently verifiable action objects suitable for the PCAA governance process.

Abstract: Agentic AI systems increasingly act through heterogeneous runtimes: local coding hooks, SDK tools, browser automation, managed-agent traces, API gateways, and workflow engines. A single operational act such as publishing code, changing identity state, moving money, or exporting data may therefore be represented by many incompatible runtime records. This makes a basic governance question difficult to answer: what action was actually approved, what evidence binds the approval to execution, and can an independent verifier reproduce the same action identity later?
  This paper presents Canonical Action Verification and Attestation (CAVA), a runtime-semantics layer for converting heterogeneous agent activity into canonical runtime action objects. CAVA is positioned below Proof-Carrying Agent Actions (PCAA): PCAA defines the deployer-owned route-review-prove governance process, while CAVA defines the stable action object that process governs. The paper formalizes canonical action identity, semantic pattern detection, approval binding, receipt integrity, runtime-portable projection, and optional attestation substrates. We study a reference implementation through a 96-seed, 384-variant benchmark covering semantic equivalence, semantic separation, wrapper bypass, false-positive control, approval binding, receipt reproducibility, attestation tamper detection, runtime portability, semantic pattern detection, policy degradation, and Azure deployment drills. The contribution is a systems formulation of action-level canonicalization and policy-addressable semantic patterns as a necessary substrate for deployer-side AI governance.

</details>


### [65] [Experience Memory Graph: One-Shot Error Correction for Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13884)
*Wenjun Wang,Yuchen Fang,Fengrui Liu,Zibo Liang,Kai Zheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EMG reformulates LLM agent failure recovery as graph matching between failed and successful trajectories, extracting corrective edit paths stored in a memory graph to enable single-pass, loop-free execution without test-time trial-and-error.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents suffer from compounding errors in long-horizon tasks. Existing reflection-based self-correction is brittle, expensive (iterative trial-and-error loops, API costs), and produces task-specific memory that generalizes poorly.

Method: EMG converts failed exploration and successful expert trajectories into directed action decision graphs. Graph matching extracts common subgraphs and graph edit paths (add, delete, relabel actions). These are stored in a memory graph with intra-task nodes and cross-task edges. At test time, relevant insights are retrieved to guide single-pass, loop-free execution.

Result: On ALFWorld and ScienceWorld benchmarks, EMG consistently outperforms state-of-the-art reflection baselines in both success rate and average reward, while eliminating test-time trial-and-error entirely.

Conclusion: Reformulating failure recovery as graph matching enables efficient, generalizable self-correction for LLM agents, achieving superior performance without costly iterative reflection loops.

Abstract: Large Language Model (LLM) agents have shown remarkable capabilities in autonomous decision-making by generating sequential trajectories of states, actions, and observations. However, in complex, long-horizon tasks, these agents frequently suffer from compounding errors and struggle to recover from failures. Existing self-correction mechanisms rely on prompt-based reflection, which is inherently brittle, incurs heavy time and API costs due to iterative trial-and-error loops, and produces task-specific memory that may be hard to generalize to new scenarios. To address this, we propose Experience Memory Graph (EMG), a framework that reformulates agent failure recovery as a graph matching problem. At training time, we convert both failed exploration trajectories and successful expert trajectories into directed action decision graphs. By matching these graphs, we extract common subgraphs (successful workflows) and graph edit paths that explicitly indicate how to correct failures (e.g., which actions to add, delete, or relabel under a given observation), and store them in a memory graph with intra-task nodes and cross-task edges. At test time, EMG retrieves relevant insights and guides the agent in a single, loop-free execution. Experiments on ALFWorld and ScienceWorld show that EMG consistently outperforms state-of-the-art reflection baselines in success rate and average reward, while requiring no test-time trial-and-error.

</details>


### [66] [AIMO Interpretability Challenge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13899)
*Michal Štefánik,Philipp Mondorf,Andreas Waldis,Qianying Liu,Chuan Yang,Michal Spiegel,Josef Kuchař,Marek Kadlčík,Adam Vawda-Oomerjee,Chaoran Liu,Simon Frieder,Barbara Plank,Fazl Barez,Pontus Stenetorp*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A competition to distinguish robust mathematical reasoning from spurious shortcuts in frontier LLMs by analyzing internal model mechanisms, leveraging AIMO problems, symbolic variants, and adversarial robustness assessments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard reasoning benchmarks that only measure final-answer accuracy cannot distinguish between models that use stable, generalizable reasoning mechanisms and those that exploit brittle shortcuts. This gap undermines trust in the reliability of frontier AI systems for mathematical reasoning.

Method: The AIMO Interpretability Challenge provides: (1) newly published olympiad-level math problems with symbolic representations enabling functional variant generation; (2) access to frontier reasoning models; and (3) adversarial robustness assessments. Participants develop methods to identify robust solvers using these resources plus computing infrastructure support. The competition will produce an open robustness benchmark and baseline systems.

Result: The abstract describes the competition design and resources but does not report empirical results, as this is a proposal/announcement paper.

Conclusion: The competition aims to bridge interpretability and generalization research by addressing a foundational question: whether and to what extent the decision-making of frontier AI models is generalizable and reliable. It seeks to establish lasting infrastructure for benchmarking mathematical reasoning robustness.

Abstract: We propose the AIMO Interpretability Challenge, a competition on distinguishing robust from spurious reasoning in frontier mathematical language models based on the models' internal mechanisms. The challenge is motivated by a central limitation of standard reasoning benchmarks: strong final-answer accuracy does not reveal whether a model relies on stable reasoning mechanisms or exploits brittle reasoning shortcuts. Building on AI Mathematical Olympiad (AIMO) problems and submissions, together with resources from the Fields Model Initiative, the competition will provide (1) newly-published olympiad-level math reasoning problems and their symbolic representations, allowing generation of novel functional variants, (2) access to frontier reasoning models, and (3) assessments of models' adversarial robustness on these problems. Participants will use these resources, along with our computing infrastructure support, to develop methods for identifying which models solve problems robustly. Our competition will also create a new, open robustness benchmark and baseline systems, aiming to provide a lasting foundation for standard benchmarking in mathematical reasoning and interpretability. Scientifically, the competition connects interpretability and generalization research around a central question in AI research: can we determine if, and to what extent, the decision-making of frontier AI models is generalizable and thus, reliable?

</details>


### [67] [Do Agent Optimizers Compound? A Continual-Learning Evaluation on Terminal-Bench 2.0](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14004)
*Wenxiao Wang,Priyatham Kattakinda,Soheil Feizi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper investigates whether agent-optimization gains compound over sequential optimization rounds (continual learning) rather than just in one-shot static evaluations. Using Terminal-Bench 2.0, it compares GEPA, Meta Harness, and RELAI-VCL, finding that only RELAI-VCL—which incorporates regression control—both transfers positively to new tasks and continues improving after further optimization, achieving the highest lifelong pass rate (76.4%).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most agent-optimization methods report gains in a one-shot setting against fixed benchmarks, but real deployed agents face recursive optimization as new failures and tasks appear over time. The core unanswered question is whether optimizer-driven gains compound: can an agent be optimized again on new tasks without eroding prior improvements? This paper addresses the gap between static evaluation and realistic continual deployment.

Method: The authors design a two-phase continual-learning evaluation using hard tasks from Terminal-Bench 2.0. Three agent-harness optimization approaches (GEPA, Meta Harness, and RELAI-VCL) are compared under identical optimization budgets. Phase 1 optimizes against an initial task set; Phase 2 introduces new tasks and provides a second optimization budget. Transfer performance and compounding improvement are measured across both phases, with lifelong average pass rate as the aggregate metric.
