Result: All three methods improve over baseline in the static single-phase setting. However, under continual learning: GEPA's optimized agent transfers below the unoptimized baseline (catastrophic forgetting); Meta Harness transfers well but fails to improve further with a second optimization budget (saturation); RELAI-VCL is the only method that both transfers positively to unseen tasks and continues improving, achieving the highest lifelong average pass rate (76.4% vs. 66.0% GEPA, 64.6% Meta Harness, 58.7% baseline).

Conclusion: Optimization gains compound only when regression control is built into the optimization loop, providing an inductive bias against shortcut solutions that fail to generalize. RELAI's Verifiable Continual Learning (RELAI-VCL) is the only approach that satisfies both requirements for deployed agents: positive transfer and capacity for further improvement.

Abstract: Most reported gains from agent-optimization methods are one-shot: an agent is optimized against a fixed benchmark and the resulting improvement is reported as if it were a stable property of the method. This does not test the setting that matters for deployed agents, where optimization is applied recursively as new failures and new tasks appear over time. The central question this raises is whether optimizer-driven gains compound: after an agent has been optimized once, can it be optimized again on newly arrived tasks without eroding the gains the first round produced? We study this question with a two-phase continual-learning evaluation built from hard tasks in Terminal-Bench 2.0, comparing three approaches to agent-harness optimization (GEPA, Meta Harness, and RELAI's Verifiable Continual Learning, RELAI-VCL) under identical optimization budgets. All three methods improve over the baseline agent in the conventional, static, single-phase setting. However, once new tasks are introduced, the methods diverge sharply: GEPA's optimized agent transfers below the unoptimized baseline, Meta Harness transfers well but fails to improve further once given a second optimization budget, and RELAI-VCL is the only method that both transfers positively to unseen tasks and continues improving after those tasks are folded into the optimization objective, reaching the highest pass rate at every evaluated stage and the highest lifelong average pass rate overall (76.4% vs. 66.0% for GEPA, 64.6% for Meta Harness, and 58.7% for the baseline). Our key observation was that optimization gains compounded only when regression control was built into the optimization loop, providing an inductive bias against shortcut solutions that fail to generalize.

</details>


### [68] [A Self-Evolving Agent for Longitudinal Personal Health Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13940)
*Haoran Li,Jiebi Deng,Tong Jin,Jinghong Han,Yuxin Wang,Zexin Wang,Qingyi Si,Weikang Gong,Xiahai Zhuang,Jia You,Wei Cheng,Jianfeng Feng,Hongcheng Guo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HealthClaw is an open-source agent architecture for longitudinal personal health management that maintains a self-evolving memory system. It separates shared safety/medical knowledge from private profiles, updates memory through induction after each episode, and achieved 45.7% answer accuracy on longitudinal probes (vs. 0.2% for current-query prompting) while reducing context exposure by 71.7% compared to full-history prompting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most health AI systems treat each user request in isolation, ignoring the reality that personal health management unfolds over repeated encounters. A person's routines, preferences, measurements, and risks evolve over time, requiring an agent that can track longitudinal changes while maintaining privacy and safety.

Method: HealthClaw employs a modular architecture with two memory tiers: (1) shared, immutable safety rules and medical knowledge, and (2) private longitudinal memory comprising profile facts, reusable procedures, and episodic traces. After each interaction episode, an induction mechanism classifies new information into one of four categories: update the profile, revise a procedure, retain as episodic, or exclude entirely. Evaluation used a synthetic year-long benchmark with 900 longitudinal support probes, 100 privacy probes, and nine 200-case biomedical tasks.

Result: HealthClaw achieved 45.7% answer accuracy on 900 longitudinal support probes versus 0.2% with current-query prompting. Prompt-side context exposure was 71.7% lower than full-history prompting. On 100 privacy probes, HealthClaw outperformed both baselines in privacy-aware answer quality and had fewer unsafe disclosures. Across nine biomedical tasks, the mean absolute gain in task-specific primary metrics was 27.0 percentage points, with seven gains remaining significant after false-discovery-rate correction.

Conclusion: The offline benchmarks support the viability of governed, self-evolving memory for longitudinal personal health agents. However, the authors emphasize that clinical effectiveness requires prospective evaluation. HealthClaw is publicly available as open-source software.

Abstract: Personal health management unfolds over repeated encounters, yet most health AI systems treat each request in isolation. We developed HealthClaw, an open-source agent architecture that updates support as a person's routines, preferences, measurements and risks change. It separates shared safety rules and medical knowledge from private longitudinal memory containing profile facts, reusable procedures and episodic traces. After each episode, induction determines what should update the profile, revise a procedure, remain episodic or be excluded. We evaluated HealthClaw with a synthetic year-long benchmark and nine 200-case biomedical tasks. Across 900 longitudinal support probes, answer accuracy increased from 0.2% with current-query prompting to 45.7% with HealthClaw, while prompt-side context exposure was 71.7% lower than with full-history prompting. In 100 privacy probes, HealthClaw produced higher privacy-aware answer quality and fewer unsafe disclosures than both baselines. Across the biomedical tasks, the mean absolute gain in the task-specific primary metric was 27.0 percentage points, and seven gains remained significant after false-discovery-rate correction. These offline benchmarks support governed, self-evolving memory for longitudinal personal health agents, although clinical effectiveness requires prospective evaluation. HealthClaw is publicly available at https://github.com/HC-Guo/HealthClaw.

</details>


### [69] [AI-accelerated End-to-End Framework for Rapid Professional Upskilling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14044)
*Tam Nguyen,Hung Nguyen,Robert Ogburn*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An end-to-end AI-accelerated framework for workforce upskilling is presented, spanning five stages from knowledge acquisition to assessment. Validated by NASBA approval, successful learner certification outcomes, and a large risk-item dataset for multi-agent AI systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: By 2030, 59% of workers will need reskilling/upskilling, yet the time to close enterprise skills gaps grew from ~3 days (2014) to 36 days (2018). Existing frameworks only accelerate single stages of upskilling and lack industry validation, motivating a comprehensive, validated solution.

Method: An end-to-end framework applying AI acceleration across five stages: (1) knowledge acquisition, (2) content development, (3) content review and verification, (4) teaching, and (5) assessment development. Emphasis is placed on both production efficiency and learning efficiency.

Result: Three validation signals: (1) NASBA approval for continuing professional education credits; (2) 3 learners passed the NVIDIA Certified Professional in Agentic AI exam in significantly reduced time, with 14 more in progress; (3) the knowledge base enabled production of a 1,267-item risk dataset for managing multi-agent AI system risks.

Conclusion: The framework demonstrates that AI acceleration across all stages of upskilling—not just isolated stages—can yield industry-validated, efficient, and scalable workforce development outcomes, with measurable downstream analytical utility.

Abstract: By 2030, 59 of every 100 workers will need reskilling or upskilling, yet the average time to close an enterprise skills gap grew from roughly 3 days in 2014 to 36 days in 2018. Most current frameworks accelerate single stages of upskilling programs and generally lack industry validation. We present an end-to-end framework that applies AI acceleration across five stages of knowledge acquisition, content development, content review and verification, teaching, and assessment development; with a strong focus on both production and learning efficiency. Three strong external signals validates the framework: the US National Association of State Boards of Accountancy reviewed and approved an upskilling program built on the framework for continuing-professional-education credits; 3 learners followed the program and passed the NVIDIA Certified Professional in Agentic AI exam in a significantly short amount of time, with 14 more in progress; the program's knowledge base supports complex downstream analysis such as the production of a robust 1,267 risk item dataset for managing multi-agent AI system risks.

</details>


### [70] [Earthquaker-AI: A Retrieval-Augmented Generation Framework with Rubric-Based Assessment for Primary School Earthquake Education](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14046)
*Xanthi Kokkinou,Chaido Mizeli,Nafsika Koulaxidou,Marina Delianidi,Konstantinos Diamantaras*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Earthquaker-AI combines Lego WeDo2 robotics with a Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) conversational AI to teach primary-school students earthquake preparedness through progressive cognitive stages, using rubric-based assessment and achieving high accuracy with low hallucination.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To extend earthquake preparedness education beyond mechanical simulation into cognitive and metacognitive processing, fostering self-regulated learning and calm decision-making under emergency conditions among primary-school students.

Method: A hybrid framework integrating three components: (1) Lego WeDo2 robotics for hands-on seismic simulation with sensors/actuators; (2) a RAG-based conversational AI assistant that semantically matches student responses to official safety guidelines and provides rubric-based verbal feedback; (3) a progressive learning trajectory across grade levels — lower grades use multiple-choice with 2D rubrics, middle grades use sequence-based multiple-choice with 3-axis rubrics, and upper grades use short written responses evaluated via 4D rubrics including clarity of expression.

Result: Experimental evaluation demonstrated high groundedness and accuracy in the RAG system's responses, with a low hallucination rate.

Conclusion: Earthquaker-AI effectively combines robotics, rubric-driven assessment, and AI to promote technological literacy, self-regulation, and responsible digital system use, contributing to early crisis-management skill development in primary education.

Abstract: This paper presents Earthquaker-AI, a hybrid educational framework building upon a previously implemented educational robotics project by integrating a conversational AI assistant based on Retrieval-Augmented Generation. It aims to enhance earthquake preparedness and conscious action among primary-school students. The system extends the award-winning STEM project Earthquaker moving from mechanical simulation with Lego WeDo2 to cognitive and metacognitive processing. The robotics component uses Lego WeDo2 automation to simulate seismic response, letting students interact with sensors and actuators as tangible representations of protective actions. The assistant operates as a guided learning mechanism aligning student responses with safety guidelines, while providing rubric-based verbal feedback that supports self-regulated learning and calmness under emergency conditions. Earthquaker-AI follows a progressive learning trajectory aligned with cognitive development. In early grades, the focus is on basic recognition of safety actions through multiple-choice questions, assessed via a two-dimensional rubric. In middle grades, students identify correct action sequences through multiple-choice questions, evaluated via a three-axis rubric. In upper grades, the approach shifts to verbal production, requiring short written responses assessed via a four-dimensional rubric that includes clarity of expression. The dialogic module uses RAG to match student queries semantically with official guidelines, generating safe, accurate responses. Experimental evaluation shows high groundedness and accuracy, with a low hallucination rate. Overall, Earthquaker-AI combines hands-on engagement, information processing, and reflective practice. Combining robotics, rubrics, and AI promotes technological literacy, self-regulation, and responsible use of digital systems, contributing to early crisis-management skills.

</details>


### [71] [Deep Interaction: An Efficient Human-AI Interaction Method for Large Reasoning Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14049)
*Hefeng Zhou,Jinxuan Zhang,Jiong Lou,Yuxin Liu,Chaochao Lu,Jingjing Qu,Jie Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Deep Interaction enables precise, edit-based correction of Chain-of-Thought reasoning errors in LLMs. By directly editing erroneous steps and distilling corrections into a prompt, it improves correction success rate by 25%+ and reduces token usage by ~40% compared to baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current interaction paradigms for correcting LLM reasoning errors are inefficient: regenerating entire responses risks repeating mistakes, while conversational feedback often yields superficial acknowledgments without genuine correction. There is a need for a mechanism that allows precise, surgical correction while preserving valid reasoning steps.

Method: The authors propose Deep Interaction, which (1) allows direct human editing of erroneous steps within the original CoT response, preserving correct reasoning; (2) refines the edited CoT into a distilled prompt; and (3) uses this distilled prompt to steer the LLM along the corrected reasoning trajectory in subsequent interactions.

Result: On STEM reasoning tasks, Deep Interaction achieves over 25% improvement in correction success rate and approximately 40% reduction in token usage compared to baseline interaction approaches.

Conclusion: Direct, edit-level human intervention combined with prompt distillation provides a substantially more efficient and effective mechanism for correcting LLM reasoning errors than conventional regeneration or conversational feedback approaches.

Abstract: The emergence of Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning has significantly enhanced the ability of large language models (LLMs) to tackle complex, multi-step tasks. However, when errors occur, current interaction approaches typically involve re-generating another response that may make mistakes again, or users laboriously flag the faulty step in follow-up turns that may get responses <You are right, I made a mistake here> followed by similar errors recurring. To address this issue, we propose an efficient human intervention mechanism for precisely correcting reasoning errors in LLMs, termed Deep Interaction. Our approach enables direct editing of the original response, allowing erroneous parts to be corrected while preserving accurate reasoning steps. We refine the edited CoT into a distilled prompt, which then steers the LLM along the corrected reasoning path. Experimental results show that our method achieves over a 25% improvement in correction success rate and reduces token usage by approximately 40% on STEM tasks reasoning compared to baseline approaches.

</details>


<div id='cs.MA'></div>

# cs.MA [[Back]](#toc)

### [72] [Learning Latency-Aware Orchestration for Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13359)
*Xi Shi,Mengxin Zheng,Qian Lou*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: LAMaS proposes a latency-aware orchestration framework for multi-agent LLM systems that learns optimized execution graphs via critical-path-aware credit assignment and adds a lightweight runtime controller to prune redundant agent interactions, achieving >50% latency reduction while preserving accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing MAS orchestration methods optimize for task performance and inference cost but neglect latency. In MAS, end-to-end latency is determined by the critical execution path, so reducing total cost does not reliably reduce latency. Naive latency optimization can misassign credit at the operator level and degrade accuracy, making latency-accuracy co-optimization a non-trivial problem.

Method: LAMaS operates at two levels: (1) At training time, it learns latency-aware execution graphs using constrained optimization with critical-path-aware credit assignment to properly attribute latency contributions. (2) At inference time, a lightweight adaptive controller dynamically eliminates redundant future agent interactions based on runtime evidence, complementing the static graph.

Result: On four benchmarks, LAMaS achieves the best latency among learning-based MAS baselines, reducing end-to-end latency by over 50% while maintaining competitive or better accuracy. It is also modular and transfers to other MAS with minimal changes, consistently yielding latency reductions.

Conclusion: LAMaS demonstrates that latency-aware orchestration with critical-path-aware credit assignment and adaptive runtime pruning effectively addresses the latency bottleneck in multi-agent LLM systems without sacrificing task performance.

Abstract: Multi-agent systems (MAS) coordinate multiple LLM-powered agents through structured workflows, gaining reasoning power but incurring high inference latency from multi-step execution and repeated model invocations. Existing orchestration methods primarily optimize task performance and inference cost, leaving latency largely unaddressed. In MAS, end-to-end latency is governed by the critical execution path, so reducing total cost alone does not reliably reduce latency. Moreover, optimizing latency while preserving accuracy remains non-trivial: naive latency optimization can misassign operator-level credit and degrade task accuracy. To address this gap, we propose Latency-Aware Multi-agent System (LAMaS), a latency-aware orchestration framework for learning-based multi-agent systems. LAMaS addresses this challenge at two levels: at training time, it learns latency-aware execution graphs through constrained optimization with critical-path-aware credit assignment; at inference time, since a graph committed at training time cannot exploit runtime evidence, it complements graph construction with a lightweight controller that adaptively eliminates redundant future agent interactions as execution unfolds. Experiments on four benchmarks show that LAMaS achieves the best latency among evaluated learning-based MAS baselines, reducing end-to-end latency by over 50\% while maintaining competitive or better accuracy. LAMaS is also modular and transfers to other MAS with minimal changes, consistently yielding latency reductions.

</details>


### [73] [Equilibrium stability as a driver of cooperation among Q-learners](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13607)
*Janusz M. Meylahn,Maximilian Schäfer*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: This paper analyzes algorithmic collusion under constant exploration (rather than decaying exploration), focusing on the time-averaged fraction of cooperative play in Q-learning agents playing the repeated Prisoner's Dilemma. The authors derive a boundary predicting when cooperative strategies dominate and validate it through extensive simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing algorithmic collusion research assumes exploration decays over time, but real-world pricing algorithms likely maintain constant exploration to stay adaptive. Prior work asks whether algorithms converge to cooperation; under constant exploration, the relevant question shifts to what fraction of time they spend cooperating. This yields high-dimensional stochastic dynamics that are analytically intractable, motivating a need for predictive boundaries of cooperative dominance.

Method: The authors model the problem as two Q-learning agents in a repeated Prisoner's Dilemma with one-period memory under epsilon-greedy exploration. Rather than seeking convergence results, they analyze the time-averaged fraction of cooperative play. They derive a theoretical boundary for when cooperative strategies dominate, based on the expected dynamics of the Q-learning process, and validate this boundary through extensive simulations.

Result: Cooperative strategies can be dominant in the time-averaged sense under constant exploration. The derived boundary serves as a strong predictor for whether non-defection-dominated behavior emerges under epsilon-greedy Q-learning, as confirmed by extensive simulations.

Conclusion: Even with constant exploration — which prevents convergence to any fixed strategy profile — cooperative behavior can dominate over time. The proposed boundary condition effectively predicts when such cooperative dominance occurs, offering a practical framework for assessing collusion risk in real-world algorithmic pricing settings.

Abstract: Algorithmic collusion among pricing algorithms has raised concerns about sustained supra-competitive prices and their implications for social welfare. Existing work has largely focused on the probability that reinforcement-learning algorithms converge to cooperative strategies, typically under the assumption that exploration vanishes over time. Motivated by the observation that algorithms deployed in practice are likely to continue exploring in order to remain adaptive to changing environments, we study learning dynamics under constant exploration. In this setting, the relevant question is no longer whether an algorithm converges to a particular strategy profile, but rather what fraction of time the algorithms spend playing cooperative strategies. Even in the benchmark case of the repeated Prisoner's Dilemma with one-period memory, this yields high-dimensional stochastic learning dynamics, for which a complete analytic treatment is intractable. We show that cooperative strategies can be dominant in this time-averaged sense and derive a boundary predicting when such dominance arises, based on the expected dynamics of the Q-learning process. Extensive simulations show that this boundary is a strong predictor for non-defection-dominated behaviour under epsilon-greedy Q-learning.

</details>


### [74] [Extending Liquid Rank Toward Multi-Source Reputation Aggregation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13615)
*Nejc Znidar,Anton Kolonin*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: This paper extends liquid rank reputation systems by introducing a framework that aggregates and blends multiple heterogeneous reputation sources into a unified score, with explicit weighting mechanisms for fine-grained control in governance and coordination scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing liquid rank reputation systems typically rely on a single internal reputation source, limiting their applicability in complex socio-technical environments where reputation signals emerge from multiple, heterogeneous contexts and subsystems involving both human and machine agents.

Method: The authors propose an extension to liquid rank reputation systems that incorporates explicit weighting and blending mechanisms to aggregate multiple heterogeneous reputation sources—both externally sourced and internally generated—into a single unified reputation score, allowing fine-grained control over each source's relative impact.

Result: The resulting framework successfully enables the fusion of diverse reputational signals and provides a flexible, adaptable foundation for designing reputation-based governance mechanisms across varied coordination contexts.

Conclusion: The proposed extension broadens the applicability of liquid rank systems, offering a versatile substrate for reputation aggregation in complex multi-agent, multi-context environments and paving the way for more robust decentralized governance mechanisms.

Abstract: In this paper, we present an extension of liquid rank reputation systems that enables the aggregation and blending of multiple heterogeneous reputation sources into a unified reputation score. The proposed framework supports the incorporation of external reputational signals alongside internally generated reputation, allowing influence to reflect participation and contribution across multiple contexts and subsystems. By introducing explicit weighting and blending mechanisms, the model provides fine-grained control over the relative impact of individual reputation sources, making it adaptable to diverse governance and coordination scenarios involving both human and machine agents. The resulting approach extends existing liquid rank systems and offers a flexible foundation for designing reputation-based governance mechanisms in complex socio-technical environments.

</details>


### [75] [Social Simulations: from Agent-Based Modeling to Digital Twins](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13693)
*Erica Cau,Andrea Failla,Valentina Pansanella,Giulio Rossetti*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: This book chapter traces the evolution of social simulation across three paradigms: classical agent-based models (ABMs) with explicit behavioral rules, AI-enhanced simulations using Large Language Models (LLMs), and Social Digital Twins — data-driven, high-fidelity replicas of real socio-technical systems. The authors examine the methodological foundations, applications, strengths, and limitations of each, highlighting a progressive shift from abstract, mechanism-oriented models toward increasingly realistic, system-specific computational representations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a comprehensive overview of the methodological trajectory in social simulation, documenting the transition from rule-based agent models to LLM-augmented simulations and ultimately to Social Digital Twins, in order to clarify how each paradigm addresses different research questions — from general social mechanisms to specific, high-fidelity system representations.

Method: This is a survey/review book chapter that adopts a comparative-historical framework. It systematically examines three paradigms — classical agent-based models, LLM-enhanced simulations, and Social Digital Twins — by analyzing their methodological foundations, representative applications, advantages, and limitations, thereby mapping the conceptual and technical evolution of social simulation.

Result: The analysis reveals a clear evolutionary trajectory: classical ABMs excel at isolating and testing general social mechanisms but lack empirical fidelity; LLM-enhanced simulations introduce more realistic agent cognition and behavior but raise concerns about reliability and interpretability; Social Digital Twins offer the highest fidelity by grounding simulations in real-world data, enabling real-time, system-specific analysis, though they introduce challenges related to data integration, computational cost, and validation.

Conclusion: The field of social simulation is undergoing a paradigm shift from abstract, mechanism-focused models toward data-intensive, high-fidelity Social Digital Twins. This progression reflects a broader trend in computational social science: the move from "what-if" exploratory modeling to actionable, real-world socio-technical system analysis, with each paradigm retaining distinct value depending on the research objective.

Abstract: This book chapter covers the evolution of social simulation from classical agent-based models, in which agents interact according to explicitly defined behavioral rules, to AI-enhanced simulations based on Large Language Models and, ultimately, Social Digital Twins: high-fidelity, data-driven representations of real-world socio-technical systems. Along this trajectory, we discuss the main methodological foundations, applications, advantages, and limitations of each paradigm, highlighting the progressive shift from abstract models designed to investigate general social mechanisms toward increasingly realistic computational representations of specific social systems.

</details>


### [76] [Pezego-HITL: A policy-grounded large language model architecture for agricultural extension in Ghana](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13934)
*Shunbao Li,Zhipeng Yuan,Amoako Ofori,Benedicta Y. Fosu-Mensah,Yang Li,Manu Kenchappa Junjanna,Qing Xue,Po Yang*

Main category: cs.MA

TL;DR: P-EVAL is a policy-grounded evaluation framework for agricultural LLM decision support that jointly optimizes safety, utility, and latency. Tested on 1,240 simulated queries in Ghana, the Pezego-HITL architecture achieved PAR=0.94, AUR=0.95, and 55% latency reduction via memory-routed RAG, with validation from 66 local stakeholders.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agricultural LLM deployment in smallholder contexts requires more than output-quality benchmarks. High-stakes crop protection demands simultaneous compliance with safety policies, agronomic utility, operational latency constraints, and manageable expert supervision workloads — a multi-objective problem not addressed by standard LLM evaluation paradigms.

Method: The authors introduce P-EVAL, unifying policy-constrained LLM assessment as an adaptive compute allocation problem. Key components: (1) offline judge calibration against gold-standard human experts (κ=0.77); (2) Pezego-HITL architecture with validated-memory routing for structured retrieval-augmented generation; (3) evaluation on 1,240 simulated field queries measuring PAR, AUR, P95 latency, and cache reuse; (4) generalizability testing with Qwen3.5-9B-DeepSeek-V4-Flash; (5) socio-technical validation via questionnaires with 30 Extension Services Officers and 36 smallholder farmers in Ghana.

Result: Pezego-HITL under P-EVAL achieved PAR=0.94, AUR=0.95, P95 latency reduction from 28.6s to 12.9s (55% improvement), and 59.6% cache reuse ratio. The open-source Qwen variant achieved PAR=0.86 with 54.5% latency reduction to 10.2s. Human expert calibration reached κ=0.77 inter-rater agreement.

Conclusion: Policy-grounded structured RAG with validated-memory routing makes safety-utility-latency trade-offs explicit and provides a scalable template for trustworthy AI-driven agricultural extension in smallholder farming systems.

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly deployed in agricultural decision-support settings, yet high-stakes crop protection in smallholder agriculture requires more than output-quality benchmarks. Over a two-year design and evaluation programme, we formalise policy-constrained large language model assessment as an adaptive compute allocation problem that jointly captures safety compliance, helpfulness, operational latency, and expert supervision workload. We introduce P-EVAL (Policy-grounded Expert-calibrated VALidation protocol), a unified evaluation framework for policy-grounded decision support, evaluating the architecture on a simulated field query database consisting of 1,240 cases. The protocol is instantiated on the Pezego advisory architecture (Pezego-HITL) and evaluated in Ghana. Following offline judge calibration against gold-standard human expert decisions ($κ= 0.77$), we evaluate the architectural performance under simulated query workloads. Under P-EVAL, our memory-routed architecture improves the Policy Alignment Rate (PAR) to 0.94 and the Agronomic Utility Rate (AUR) to 0.95, while reducing P95 latency by 55% (from 28.6s to 12.9s) through a 59.6% cache reuse ratio. We also demonstrate generalisability using the open-source \texttt{Qwen3.5-9B-DeepSeek-V4-Flash} model, achieving a PAR of 0.86 and a 54.5% latency reduction (to 10.2s). To evaluate practical utility and socio-technical integration, we administer detailed questionnaires to Ghanaian Extension Services Officers ($N=30$) and smallholder farmers ($N=36$). Taken together, this work demonstrates how policy-grounded structured retrieval-augmented generation with validated-memory routing makes safety-utility-latency trade-offs explicit, offering a scalable template for trustworthy AI-driven extension in smallholder farming systems.

</details>


<div id='cs.IR'></div>

# cs.IR [[Back]](#toc)

### [77] [Where Does the Noise Come From? A Variance-Components Decomposition of Non-Determinism in LLM Brand Answers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13304)
*Dmitrij Żatuchin*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: LLM-based brand measurement suffers from major reproducibility issues. This paper decomposes variance in LLM brand sentiment into four sources (within-prompt resampling, prompt paraphrase, model identity, query language) using crossed random-effects/generalizability theory across 12,933 responses. Query language dominates systematic variance (26.5%), while brand identity is negligible (1.5%). The authors show that reliability is best improved by diversifying languages and models, not by resampling the same prompt repeatedly.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current practice in LLM-based brand measurement treats within-prompt resampling as the primary noise source, averaging ~5 repeats per prompt. However, brand scores vary for multiple confounded reasons beyond resampling. Without decomposing these variance sources, researchers cannot optimally allocate their query budget to achieve target reliability, leading to wasteful resampling strategies that fail to improve measurement precision.

Method: The authors specify a crossed random-effects decomposition grounded in generalizability theory (G-theory), partitioning total variance of a response-level binary sentiment outcome into four facets: within-prompt resampling, prompt paraphrase, model identity, and query language. They embed these variance components into a decision-study (D-study) to determine optimal allocation of repeats, paraphrases, models, and languages for a given reliability target. The design is applied to a fully crossed corpus of 12,933 LLM responses covering 20 Central/Eastern European brands, 8 languages, and 3 models (GPT-5.2, Gemini 3 Flash in parametric mode, Perplexity in grounded retrieval), with a stability subset of 1,435 cells resampled ~5 times each.

Result: Query language is the largest systematic facet at 26.5% of single-response variance, dwarfing brand identity (1.5%, ICC=0.0146), meaning a single AI answer carries almost no brand-discriminating signal. Pure resampling accounts for 34.8% of variance, and the brand-in-context interaction is 29.6%. Brand-by-language interaction is 8.6% (a bilingual penalty), while brand-by-model and brand-by-prompt interactions are near zero. Adding languages and models reduces relative-error variance far more than adding repeats: a repeat beyond the fifth reduces it by only 0.0003. Brand-ranking reliability is ~0.01 for a single answer and ~0.36 at the full crossed design.

Conclusion: Reliability in LLM-based brand measurement is achieved by spreading queries across languages and models, not by resampling the same prompt. The negligible brand ICC means single responses are near-useless for discriminating brands. The bilingual penalty (brand×language interaction) suggests sentiment is not translation-invariant. Researchers should budget for diversity of models and languages rather than within-prompt repeats when designing LLM-based brand studies.

Abstract: Teams measuring whether large language models (LLMs) recommend a brand face a reproducibility problem: ask the same question twice and the answer moves. Practice resamples each prompt a few times (commonly five) and averages, treating within-prompt resampling as the source of the noise. But a measured brand score moves for at least four separable reasons: within-prompt resampling, prompt paraphrase, model identity, and query language. We specify a crossed random-effects (generalizability-theory) decomposition that partitions the total variance of a response-level brand outcome into these four sources, and embed the components in a decision-study allocation that returns how many repeats, paraphrases, models, and languages to buy for a target reliability. We apply it to a fully crossed corpus of 12,933 LLM responses on 20 Central and Eastern European brands, 8 languages, and 3 models (GPT-5.2 and Gemini 3 Flash in parametric mode, Perplexity in grounded retrieval), with a stability subset of 1,435 cells resampled about five times. The outcome is per-response multilingual sentiment polarity. Query language is the largest systematic facet (26.5% of the variance of one response) against 1.5% for brand identity (ICC 0.0146), so a single AI answer carries almost no brand-discriminating signal. Once a cell term isolates pure resampling, resampling is 34.8% of variance and the brand-in-context interaction 29.6%; brand-by-language is 8.6% (a bilingual penalty) while brand-by-model and brand-by-prompt are near zero. Per unit of query budget, adding languages and models reduces relative-error variance far more than adding repeats: a repeat past the fifth reduces it by only 0.0003. Brand-ranking reliability stays low, near 0.01 for a single answer and about 0.36 at the full crossed design, so reliability is bought by spreading across languages and models, not by repeating one prompt.

</details>


### [78] [TMallGS: Scaling Unified Feature and Sequence Modeling for Generative E-commerce Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13398)
*Zhentao Song,Yufeng Gao,Xing Fang,Jing Wang,Guangxin Song,Bokang Wang,Yipin Dai,He Guo*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: TmallGS is a scalable Transformer-based ranking architecture for CTR prediction that addresses the heterogeneity of ranking features through hierarchical tokenization, field-adaptive gating, decoupled late fusion, context-aware bias modeling, and error-aware progressive training, achieving significant gains in UCTCVR and GMV on Tmall Search.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current approaches adapting LLM architectures for CTR prediction (e.g., OneTrans, Climber) use an all-in-tokenization strategy that ignores the heterogeneous nature of ranking features. There is a need for a scalable architecture that improves Model FLOPs Utilization (MFU) and leverages scaling laws while respecting feature diversity in industrial search systems.

Method: TmallGS comprises five components: (1) Hierarchical Distribution-Calibrated Tokenization with Field-wise Saliency Reweighting (FSR) and Distribution-Calibrated Projection (DCP); (2) a Field-Adaptive Gated Transformer Backbone using per-field QKV projections and noise-adaptive gating; (3) Decoupled FiLM Late Fusion to retain explicit high-frequency signals; (4) a Context-Aware Bias Net to separate systemic bias from user intent; and (5) Error-Aware Progressive Training with dynamically weighted losses.

Result: Extensive offline experiments and online A/B tests on Tmall Search demonstrate that TmallGS improves training throughput and achieves substantial gains in both UCTCVR (Unit Click-Through Conversion Rate) and GMV (Gross Merchandise Volume).

Conclusion: TmallGS successfully addresses the feature heterogeneity problem in LLM-adapted ranking architectures, delivering both computational efficiency and predictive performance at scale, as validated by production deployment metrics on Tmall Search.

Abstract: In industrial search and ranking systems, Click-Through Rate (CTR) prediction is shifting from traditional Deep Learning Recommendation Models (DLRM) toward unified, compute-intensive Transformer architectures. This transition is driven by the need to improve Model FLOPs Utilization (MFU) and achieve predictable gains through scaling laws. However, existing approaches such as OneTrans and Climber often adopt an all-in-tokenization strategy when adapting Large Language Model (LLM) architectures, overlooking the heterogeneous nature of ranking features. We propose TmallGS, a scalable ranking architecture for Tmall search. TmallGS includes five key components: (1) Hierarchical Distribution-Calibrated Tokenization, which combines Field-wise Saliency Reweighting (FSR) and Distribution-Calibrated Projection (DCP) to map diverse features into optimized subspaces; (2) a Field-Adaptive Gated Transformer Backbone with per-field QKV projections and noise-adaptive gating for refined semantic interaction; (3) Decoupled FiLM Late Fusion to preserve explicit high-frequency signals; (4) a Context-Aware Bias Net to decouple systemic bias from user intent; and (5) Error-Aware Progressive Training with dynamically weighted losses for robust learning. Extensive offline experiments and online A/B tests on Tmall Search show that TmallGS improves training throughput and achieves substantial gains in UCTCVR and GMV.

</details>


### [79] [Can We Steer the Black-Box? Towards Controllability-Centric Evaluation of Recommender Systems with Collaborative Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13418)
*Jiwen Zhou,Xiang Liu,Mingming Li,Pengbo Mo,Jiao Dai,Honglei Lv,Jizhong Han,Songlin Hu*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: CtrlBench-Rec is a multi-agent evaluation framework that systematically measures the controllability of recommender systems across three tasks: target content discovery, interest profile shaping, and popularity bias mitigation, exposing critical bottlenecks like resistance to long-tail content steering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recommender systems operate as black-boxes with no standardized way to evaluate how well they respond to explicit user or regulator guidance. Existing evaluation paradigms overlook controllability — defined as the system's ability to respond to explicit steering — leaving a critical gap in both research and algorithmic auditing.

Method: The authors propose CtrlBench-Rec, a collaborative multi-agent framework that formalizes three controllability tasks: (1) target content discovery (steering toward specific items via explicit commands), (2) interest profile shaping (implicit representation steering), and (3) popularity bias mitigation (overcoming algorithmic biases). The framework systematically quantifies steerability across these dimensions using real-world datasets and multiple recommendation models.

Result: Extensive experiments demonstrate that CtrlBench-Rec effectively quantifies controllability across models and exposes critical bottlenecks, most notably persistent resistance to guiding recommendations toward long-tail content, even when explicit steering signals are provided.

Conclusion: CtrlBench-Rec provides the first standardized toolkit for controllable recommendation research, enabling algorithmic auditing and user empowerment. The code is publicly released to facilitate adoption.

Abstract: Recommender systems operate as Black-Boxes, leaving users and regulators unable to steer their outputs toward specific intentions or audit their behavior. This lack of controllability, defined as the system's ability to respond to explicit guidance, remains an unaddressed dimension in existing evaluation paradigms. To fill this gap, we propose CtrlBench-Rec, a collaborative multi-agent framework for systematic assessment of controllability. We formalize three fundamental tasks: target content discovery, interest profile shaping, and popularity bias mitigation, which together measure steerability from explicit commands to implicit representation steering and finally to overcoming algorithmic biases.Extensive experiments on real-world datasets and multiple recommendation models demonstrate that our framework effectively quantifies controllability and exposes critical system bottlenecks, most notably persistent resistance to guiding long tail content. CtrlBench-Rec provides the first standardized toolkit for controllable recommendation research, algorithmic auditing, and user empowerment. Our code is released on https://github.com/caskcsg/CtrlBenchRec.

</details>


### [80] [Personalizing Incremental Video Search with Hybrid Text and ID Embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13493)
*Vivek Kanojiya,Vishalaksh Aggarwal,Daeho Baek,Lyndon Kennedy,Xuetao Yin*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: A personalization system for Apple TV search that combines text-based semantic embeddings and ID-based collaborative embeddings, injected into an XGBoost ranker. It shows strongest gains on ambiguous short prefix queries (+8.63% NDCG) and longer-history users, with significant online improvements in tap-through rate and conversion rate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Incremental video search (e.g., 1-3 character prefixes) suffers from underspecified user intent, and existing non-personalized ranking fails to leverage individual watch history to disambiguate queries. The authors aim to improve ranking quality for such ambiguous, incremental search scenarios through personalization.

Method: Two embedding models are trained: (1) TextEmb — a multilingual text encoder fine-tuned on co-engagement triplets using contrastive learning; (2) IdEmb — an ID-based collaborative embedding model trained on interaction-derived positives. At serving time, user representations are built from recent watch history, and cosine similarities (text-based and ID-based) between user and item embeddings are fed as features into a pairwise XGBoost ranker. The system is evaluated via temporal hold-out offline datasets and a three-week online A/B test.

Result: Offline: +2.99% NDCG@10 and +3.30% MRR over non-personalized baseline. On ambiguous prefix queries (1-3 chars): +8.63% NDCG@10 lift vs. +1.46% on fully-specified queries. Users with 51-100 history items see +4.37% NDCG lift vs. +2.13% for 1-5 items. Online: +1.14% tap-through rate, +1.23% conversion rate, +2.91% improvement in converted-item rank position (all statistically significant).

Conclusion: Personalization delivers the most value where default ranking underperforms—namely ambiguous short queries and users with richer histories. Combining semantic and collaborative signals via cosine similarity features in a learned ranker effectively balances coverage and precision. LLM-judged similarity labels help reduce click/exposure bias in offline embedding evaluation.

Abstract: Incremental video search requires high-quality ranking after each keystroke, where intent is often underspecified (e.g., 1-3 character prefixes). We present a personalization system for Apple TV search that combines complementary semantic and collaborative signals at ranking time. Our approach learns two item embedding spaces: (i) a text-based multilingual encoder (TextEmb) fine-tuned on co-engagement triplets via contrastive learning, and (ii) an ID-based collaborative embedding model (IdEmb) trained on interaction-derived positives. At serving time, we construct user representations from recent watch history and inject text- and ID-based user-item cosine similarities into a pairwise XGBoost ranker.
  We evaluate with temporally held-out offline datasets and a three-week online controlled experiment. Offline, for sessions with user history, the personalized ranker improves NDCG@10 by 2.99% and MRR by 3.30% over the non-personalized baseline. Slice analyses show that personalization is most needed in incremental search, where intent is still forming: on ambiguous prefix queries (1-3 characters), NDCG@10 lift is +8.63%, versus +1.46% on longer, fully specified queries. Longer-history users benefit more: NDCG lift rises from +2.13% for users with 1-5 history items to +4.37% for users with 51-100, even though baseline relevance is lower for these cohorts (NDCG@10 drops from 0.733 to 0.680), indicating that personalization adds the most value where default ranking underperforms. Online, treatment yields statistically significant gains of +1.14% tap-through rate and +1.23% conversion rate, with a 2.91% improvement in converted-item rank position. We further analyze coverage-precision trade-offs between semantic and collaborative embeddings via ablations isolating each signal, and evaluate embedding quality on a held-out corpus with LLM-judged similarity labels to reduce click/exposure bias.

</details>


### [81] [Cluster with Auctions for Vector Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13728)
*Swann Bessa,Pierre Fernandez,Gergely Szilvasy,Matthijs Douze,Hervé Jégou*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: CwA jointly learns a balanced database partition and a neural probing function for ANN search, using alternating optimization with gradient descent and a parallelizable auction algorithm, achieving up to 4.7× throughput gains when query and database distributions differ.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing ANN indexing methods typically use the same assignment function for both database vectors and queries, which is suboptimal when query and database distributions differ. Treating the probing function and database partition as separate, learnable entities can improve search performance.

Method: CwA alternates between (i) gradient descent to train a neural probing function that directly optimizes search performance for the query distribution, and (ii) a large-scale combinatorial optimization solved via a parallelizable auction algorithm that assigns database vectors to balanced clusters. The method also extends to a Cartesian product of clusters for finer granularity.

Result: CwA achieves up to 4.7× throughput over state-of-the-art at equal recall when query and database distributions differ. In the in-distribution setting, even a simple linear probing function trained with CwA outperforms competing deep neural methods.

Conclusion: Decoupling the probing function from the partition assignment function and jointly optimizing both yields significant gains in ANN search, particularly under distribution shift, while remaining effective even in standard in-distribution scenarios.

Abstract: Large-scale approximate nearest neighbor search commonly relies on partitions for indexing: database vectors are partitioned into clusters, and for each query a probing function selects the clusters to be scanned. The query probing function and the database partition are rarely treated as separate entities: most techniques assign queries with the same assignment function as the database vectors, which is suboptimal especially when database and query distributions differ. This paper introduces CwA (Cluster with Auctions), which addresses this limitation by jointly learning a balanced database partition and a neural probing function. CwA optimizes search performance directly for the query distribution. It minimizes its objective by alternating two steps: (i) gradient descent on the neural network of the probing function, and (ii) a large-scale combinatorial optimization of the cluster assignment for the database vectors. We solve the latter with a parallelizable auction algorithm that balances the partition by design. To further scale CwA, we extend the method to a Cartesian product of clusters that increases the partition's granularity. When database and query distributions differ, CwA achieves up to 4.7$\times$ throughput over the state-of-the-art at equal recall. In the in-distribution (ID) setting, even a simple linear probing function trained with CwA outperforms competing deep neural methods.

</details>


### [82] [Optimizing Visibility in Generative Engines: A Critical Survey of Generative Engine Optimization (2023-2026)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14035)
*Olivier Martinez*

Main category: cs.IR

TL;DR: This paper critically surveys 45 GEO studies (Nov 2023–Jul 2026), arguing that GEO is not a single ranking task but a stochastic, multi-stage pipeline. The foundational GEO gains are conditional and do not establish organic discoverability or durable traffic effects. The authors propose a multistage formal model, a visibility vector, an evidence hierarchy, and a reproducible evaluation protocol, concluding that no reviewed technique demonstrates stable, longitudinal causal effects on organic discoverability or downstream behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The rapid expansion of Generative Engine Optimization (GEO) research has produced heterogeneous terminology, metrics, and evidence standards. There is a need to critically assess whether widely cited gains in citation-oriented optimization translate to reproducible, generalizable, and economically meaningful outcomes beyond controlled experimental settings.

Method: A critical survey of 45 studies published between November 2023 and July 2026 (including one earlier EMNLP preprint), plus relevant RAG and evaluation literature. The authors synthesize findings, identify reproducibility levers, examine commercial audit data, and propose a multistage formal model, a visibility vector (discoverability, citation, absorption, economic outcomes), an evidence hierarchy, and a reproducible evaluation protocol emphasizing repeated measurements, paraphrases, controls, human validation, and multi-actor interference.

Result: Topical relevance and context position are the most reproducible optimization levers. Generic heuristics transfer poorly. Competition can erode individual gains. Citation-oriented rewrites can impair retrieval. Commercial audits show low source overlap, substantial run-to-run variability, and persistent fidelity gaps. Within the corpus, already-retrieved content can causally alter its citation or use, but no reviewed technique demonstrates stable, longitudinal, cross-platform causal effects on organic discoverability or downstream behavior.

Conclusion: GEO should be reconceptualized as a stochastic, partially observable pipeline rather than a single ranking task. The foundational paper's gains are valid only within narrow experimental conditions. The field requires higher evidentiary standards, including the proposed multistage model, visibility vector, evidence hierarchy, and reproducible protocol, to establish claims of genuine organic discoverability and durable traffic effects.

Abstract: Generative Engine Optimization (GEO) seeks to increase content's presence, likelihood of citation, or influence in answers produced by generative engines. Since the foundational GEO paper, the field has expanded rapidly, but terminology, metrics, and evidence standards remain heterogeneous. This critical survey reviews 45 studies selected under a November 2023-July 2026 publication window, including one earlier preprint published at EMNLP after the window opened, plus relevant RAG and evaluation work. We argue that GEO is not a single ranking task but a stochastic, partially observable pipeline spanning search activation, crawling and indexing, retrieval, reranking and context allocation, citation, prominence, factual absorption, fidelity, and user behavior. The foundational paper's widely cited gains are valid within its experimental setting but conditional on a source already being present in a fixed context; they establish neither organic discoverability nor durable traffic effects. Reviewed work indicates that topical relevance and context position are the most reproducible levers, generic heuristics transfer poorly, competition can erode individual gains, and citation-oriented rewrites can impair retrieval. Commercial audits further reveal low source overlap, substantial run-to-run variability, and persistent fidelity gaps. We contribute a multistage formal model, a visibility vector separating discoverability, citation, absorption, and economic outcomes, an evidence hierarchy, and a reproducible protocol based on repeated measurements, paraphrases, controls, human validation, and multi-actor interference. Within this corpus, the evidence is narrow: already-retrieved content can causally alter its citation or use, but no reviewed technique shows a stable, longitudinal, cross-platform causal effect on organic discoverability or downstream behavior.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [83] [Automatic Differentiation from Scratch: How PyTorch Computes Gradients in Physics-Informed Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13042)
*Abdeladhim Tahimi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper provides a fully explicit, node-by-node trace of PyTorch's AD engine during PINN training, demonstrating how two-level differentiation (network output derivatives and parameter gradients) is achieved through reverse-mode autograd with `create_graph=True`, verified against the P/Q sensitivity framework.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To demystify the "black box" of automatic differentiation in PINN training by providing explicit numerical values at every node, bridging the gap between theoretical AD frameworks (the P/Q sensitivity framework of Tahimi, 2026) and PyTorch's practical implementation of vector-Jacobian products across two levels of differentiation.

Method: Using a concrete 1-3-3-1 MLP and the IVP y'(t)+y(t)=0, y(0)=1, the paper manually traces: (1) the forward pass building the computational graph; (2) reverse-mode backward traversal computing all 22 parameter gradients via VJPs; and (3) the graph-on-graph mechanism where `create_graph=True` constructs a second computational graph over the physics derivative for correct second-level differentiation. Every adjoint value is verified against hand derivations.

Result: The complete trace shows the exact numerical values of all 22 parameter gradients, verifies that PyTorch's `create_graph=True` correctly implements the graph-on-graph mechanism for differentiating through physics residuals, and confirms that the VJP-based implementation matches the P/Q sensitivity framework's analytical derivations.

Conclusion: PyTorch's AD engine correctly handles two-level differentiation in PINN training through the graph-on-graph mechanism, with `create_graph=True` being the critical enabler. The explicit trace validates that the P/Q sensitivity framework of Tahimi (2026) is faithfully implemented by PyTorch's VJP-based autograd, providing a bridge between theory and practice.

Abstract: This paper traces, with explicit numerical values, how PyTorch's automatic differentiation (AD) engine computes gradients for Physics-Informed Neural Network (PINN) training -- a setting that requires two levels of differentiation: computing the physics derivative $\hat{y}'(t)=d\hat{y}/dt$ through the network, and computing parameter gradients $\nabla_θL$ of a loss that itself depends on $\hat{y}'(t)$. Using a 1-3-3-1 multilayer perceptron and the initial value problem $y'(t)+y(t)=0$, $y(0)=1$, we trace the complete pipeline at every node: the computational graph built during the forward pass, the reverse-mode backward traversal that computes all 22 parameter gradients in a single pass, and the graph-on-graph mechanism by which \texttt{create\_graph=True} enables correct differentiation through the physics-informed residual. Every adjoint value is verified against the hand derivations of Tahimi (2026), connecting the $P/Q$ sensitivity framework to the vector--Jacobian products used by PyTorch's autograd engine.

</details>


### [84] [Beyond Backbone Backpropagation: A Decoupled Strategy for Efficient Transfer Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13043)
*Daniel Vila-Cruz,Laura Morán-Fernández,Verónica Bolón-Canedo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A lightweight training strategy that adapts normalization layers to new domains and decouples feature extraction from classifier optimization, achieving significant training time and CO2 reductions with minimal accuracy loss across CNNs, Transformers, and medical imaging datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep learning models are computationally expensive and energy-intensive to deploy, particularly in resource-constrained clinical settings. There is a need for efficient domain adaptation methods that reduce training overhead without sacrificing classification performance.

Method: The proposed strategy has three components: (1) adapting only normalization layers to the target domain; (2) decoupling feature extraction from classifier optimization by precomputing features once; and (3) a redesigned classifier head with margin-based weighted loss to reduce class ambiguity, all without requiring end-to-end backpropagation.

Result: Evaluated on four CNN architectures (ResNet18, ResNet50, MobileNet, DenseNet121), three Transformer models (ViT, Swin, DeiT), and three medical datasets (Brain Cancer MRI, BreakHis, PatchCamelyon), the method significantly reduces training time with marginal accuracy trade-off, often matching or exceeding baseline performance, and reduces CO2 emissions by orders of magnitude.

Conclusion: The approach offers a practical, environmentally sustainable solution for model adaptation in resource-constrained clinical or prototyping environments, achieving competitive accuracy while dramatically reducing computational and energy costs.

Abstract: Deep learning models achieve state-of-the-art image classification but face deployment challenges due to computational costs and energy demands. We propose a lightweight training strategy that adapts normalization layers of the model to the new domain and decouples feature extraction from classifier optimization, reducing overhead by precomputing features only once. A redesigned classifier head with margin-based weighted loss further minimizes ambiguity without end-to-end backpropagation. Evaluated across four CNN architectures (ResNet18, ResNet50, MobileNet, DenseNet121), three Transformer models (ViT, Swin and DeiT) and three medical datasets (Brain Cancer MRI, BreakHis and PatchCamelyon), our approach significantly reduces the required training time with only a marginal accuracy trade-off, often matching or surpassing baseline performance. This efficiency translates to reducing CO2 by orders of magnitude, offering a practical and environmentally sustainable solution for resource-constrained clinical or prototyping environments.

</details>


### [85] [Federated Explainable Artificial Intelligence: Roles, Architectures, Evaluation, and Open Challenges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13045)
*Masoume Gholizade,Fabrizio Ruffini,Pietro Ducange,Francesco Marcelloni*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A systematic survey of Federated Explainable AI (FedXAI), which integrates explainability into the federated learning lifecycle. The paper proposes a taxonomy classifying methods by explainability role, model/explainer types, explanation scope, and FL settings, while identifying key challenges including non-IID explainability, security threats, and lack of standardized evaluation metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Federated Learning preserves data privacy but produces opaque models, while XAI provides transparency. Neither paradigm alone satisfies both requirements, motivating the FedXAI paradigm that jointly addresses privacy and explainability in distributed ML systems, especially for high-stakes domains.

Method: Systematic literature review and taxonomy construction. The taxonomy classifies FedXAI methods along six dimensions: role of explainability, model and explainer types, explanation scope (local/global), integration level, FL settings (cross-silo/cross-device), and data heterogeneity. The review spans model-agnostic explanations, interpretable federated models, and explainability-aware aggregation mechanisms, along with analysis of evaluation practices.

Result: The survey reveals explainability transitioning from a post-hoc tool to an integral FL lifecycle component, supporting aggregation, personalization, robustness, coordination, and system-level decisions. It identifies critical gaps: no standardized benchmarks/metrics for explanation quality, stability, privacy leakage, and computational overhead in FL contexts.

Conclusion: Key open challenges include explainability under non-IID data, explanation-centric security threats, communication-efficient XAI, continual FedXAI, and domain/regulatory integration. The survey serves as a reference framework for building trustworthy, transparent, privacy-preserving federated AI systems.

Abstract: Federated Learning (FL) has emerged as a key paradigm for privacy-preserving collaborative model training across distributed and heterogeneous data sources. By keeping raw data local, FL addresses data confidentiality concerns, yet it does not resolve the opacity of modern machine learning models. In parallel, Explainable Artificial Intelligence (XAI) has gained attention for improving transparency, trust, and accountability, particularly in high-stakes domains. Their intersection has given rise to Federated Explainable Artificial Intelligence (FedXAI) paradigm, which aims to jointly satisfy privacy and explainability requirements. This survey provides a systematic review of FedXAI, highlighting the transition of explainability from a post-hoc tool to an integral component of the FL lifecycle. We show how explainability supports aggregation, personalization, robustness, coordination, and system-level decision making. To organize the literature, we introduce a taxonomy that classifies FedXAI methods by the role of explainability, model and explainer types, explanation scope, integration level, FL settings, and data heterogeneity. We review approaches ranging from model-agnostic explanations to interpretable federated models and explainability-aware aggregation mechanisms. We also examine evaluation practices and discuss the lack of standardized benchmarks and metrics for measuring explanation quality, stability, privacy leakage, and computational overhead. Finally, we identify key challenges, including explainability under non-IID data, explanation-centric security threats, communication-efficient XAI, continual FedXAI, and the integration of domain knowledge and regulatory constraints. By consolidating existing work and identifying key gaps, this survey serves as a reference framework for designing trustworthy, transparent, and privacy-preserving federated AI systems.

</details>


### [86] [What Your Model Threw Away and Why You'll Want It Back: Masking, Fingerprinting, and Privacy from Discarded Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13046)
*Zachary P. Bradshaw*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces a framework for analyzing information discarded by ML models with Lie group symmetries, defining null fibers and stabilizers to characterize symmetries invisible to a learned function, with applications in data masking, fingerprinting, and privacy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Machine learning models often learn functions that are invariant or equivariant to group actions, but the specific symmetries that a trained model fails to detect (i.e., information it discards) are not well understood. The authors aim to formalize and computationally characterize this discarded information.

Method: Given a Lie group G acting on input space V via representation π and a learned function f: V → ℝ, the authors define the null fiber N_G(f,x) as the set of group elements whose inverse action on x leaves f unchanged. When independent of x, this equals the stabilizer Stab_G(f). For smooth f, the preimage theorem bounds the dimension of null fibers (≥ dim G − 1). For compact groups acting on themselves, the Peter–Weyl theorem provides a spectral characterization via Fourier coefficient matrices. Null fiber elements are computed efficiently using Newton iteration on the orbit map.

Result: The framework was experimentally validated on two domains: molecular property prediction under SO(3) and spherical image classification under the Möbius group PSL(2,ℂ). The method applies uniformly to classical neural networks and variational quantum circuits.

Conclusion: The null fiber framework provides a principled way to characterize symmetries invisible to a trained model, enabling practical applications in data masking, model fingerprinting, and privacy-preserving computation across both classical and quantum ML paradigms.

Abstract: We develop a framework for the information discarded by machine learning models whose inputs carry a Lie group action. Given a representation $π$ of a Lie group $G$ on a space $V$ and a learned function $f\colon V \to \mathbb{R}$, we define two objects measuring the symmetry invisible to $f$. The null fiber at a point $x \in V$ is the set $N_G(f,x) = \{g \in G : f(π(g^{-1}) \cdot x) = f(x)\}$ of group elements whose inverse action on $x$ is undetectable by $f$. When $N_G(f,x)$ is independent of $x$, it coincides with the stabilizer $\mathrm{Stab}_G(f)$, the largest subgroup of $G$ under which $f$ is invariant. For smooth maps to $\mathbb{R}$, the preimage theorem guarantees that null fibers have dimension at least $\dim G - 1$ at generic inputs, regardless of architecture. For compact groups acting on themselves, the Peter--Weyl theorem yields a spectral characterization of both objects in terms of the Fourier coefficient matrices of $f$. We show that null fiber elements can be computed efficiently via Newton iteration on the orbit map, at a cost comparable to a few gradient evaluations. Applications to data masking, model fingerprinting, and privacy-preserving computation are developed and tested experimentally on molecular property prediction under $\mathrm{SO}(3)$ and spherical image classification under the Möbius group $\mathrm{PSL}(2, \mathbb{C})$. The framework applies uniformly to classical neural networks and variational quantum circuits.

</details>


### [87] [Targeted Recovery of Weight-Space Mechanisms From Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13047)
*Antoine Vigouroux,Lee Sharkey*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Targeted Parameter Decomposition (tPD) efficiently identifies interpretable computational components in neural networks for specific inputs by using a high-rank catch-all component, drastically reducing compute costs compared to full PD while maintaining mechanistic faithfulness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Full parameter decomposition (PD) yields interpretable components but is computationally prohibitive for large models. The authors aim to make decomposition practical by only extracting components relevant to targeted inputs or subtasks, rather than decomposing the entire network.

Method: tPD introduces a high-rank "catch-all" component that absorbs all non-target data processing, allowing the decomposition to focus exclusively on recovering low-rank components for specified inputs. The method uses a structured optimization that jointly learns target-specific components and the catch-all component, with the latter handling everything outside the target distribution.

Result: tPD recovers reproducible, mechanistically faithful circuits in toy models and transformer LMs trained on The Pile. It extracts a CSS-only submodel from a 4-block transformer using only 7% of the FLOPs required by prior full decomposition. In a 12-block transformer, tPD enables surgical ablation and rewiring of memorized sequences with negligible side effects on other inputs.

Conclusion: tPD makes parameter decomposition scalable by focusing on targeted inputs, enabling circuit-level analysis and editing of large models at a fraction of the computational cost of full PD.

Abstract: Parameter decomposition (PD) decomposes neural networks into interpretable computational components that faithfully reflect the original network's operations. However, scaling PD to large models requires vast compute, making it a costly and risky endeavor. Here we propose targeted PD (tPD), which identifies only the components that process specific inputs of interest -- from isolated prompts to large subtasks -- by introducing a high-rank catch-all component that handles all non-target data. We validate tPD on toy models and on transformer language models trained on The Pile, where it recovers reproducible, mechanistically faithful circuits. We extract a CSS-only submodel of a 4-block transformer using 7% of the FLOPs of its published decomposition, and in a 12-block transformer we surgically ablate and rewire memorized sequences, with negligible side effects on other inputs.

</details>


### [88] [Uncertainty-Aware Sequential Decision Rules for Event-Triggered LLM Invocation in Streaming Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13048)
*Zhaohui Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper formalizes the decision of when to invoke expensive LLMs in streaming inference pipelines as a risk-based sequential stopping problem, proves six theoretical results (including sublinear regret bounds), and empirically validates the framework on turbofan degradation data with real LLM calls, demonstrating anomaly-score-driven triggers outperform baselines by an order of magnitude.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Streaming inference pipelines increasingly combine lightweight fast models with costly LLMs, but the question of when to invoke the LLM lacks formal treatment. The authors aim to provide a rigorous mathematical framework for this trigger decision, optimizing the trade-off between diagnostic quality and computational cost.

Method: The problem is cast as a risk-based sequential stopping problem where a trigger policy activates when a risk functional over the observation history exceeds a threshold. Within this framework, six theoretical results are proven: (1) a minimum inter-event time bound preventing trigger chattering, (2) optimality of threshold policies via smooth pasting, (3) approximate SPRT guarantees under estimated parameters, (4) O(√(T log T)) regret for stationary streams extending to O(√((C_T+1)T log T)) with changepoints, (5) O(1/√T) convergence of online gradient descent for adaptive thresholds, and (6) a calibration-to-miss-rate transfer inequality. The framework subsumes classical trigger families (event-triggered, optimal stopping, SPRT, CUSUM, Bayesian) as special cases. Empirical validation uses turbofan degradation data (CMAPSS) with real LLM calls, comparing against six baselines including RouteLLM-style routers and contextual bandits.

Result: Sublinear regret is confirmed with α < 1 for all principled triggers. Diagnostic quality is high: 92.9% of 1600 LLM diagnoses achieve a grounding score ≥ 0.75. Anomaly-score-driven risk functions dominate alternatives by roughly an order of magnitude on Pareto AUC. The theoretical assumptions are empirically verified, and cost sensitivity and LLM failure modes are analyzed.

Conclusion: The risk-based sequential stopping framework provides a principled, unified treatment of LLM invocation in streaming pipelines, with strong theoretical guarantees and compelling empirical performance, particularly when using anomaly-score-driven risk functions.

Abstract: Streaming inference pipelines increasingly pair lightweight fast models with Large Language Models (LLMs) that provide rich semantic understanding at substantial cost. The central question of when to invoke the LLM has received limited formal treatment. We cast this as a risk-based sequential stopping problem, where a trigger policy fires when a risk functional over the observation history exceeds a threshold. Within this framework, we prove six results: a minimum inter-event time bound excluding trigger chattering; optimality of threshold policies via smooth pasting; approximate SPRT guarantees under estimated parameters; O(sqrt(T log T)) regret for stationary streams, extending to O(sqrt((C_T + 1) T log T)) under C_T changepoints; O(1/sqrt(T)) convergence of online gradient descent for adaptive thresholds; and a calibration-to-miss-rate transfer inequality. Several classical trigger families, including event-triggered, optimal stopping, SPRT, CUSUM, and Bayesian triggers, can be expressed as special cases of this framework. On turbofan degradation data (CMAPSS) with real LLM calls, we empirically verify the theoretical assumptions, ablate the risk function design, compare against six baselines including a RouteLLM-style router and contextual bandits, and analyze cost sensitivity and LLM failure modes. The results confirm sublinear regret, with alpha < 1 for all principled triggers; high diagnostic quality, with 92.9 percent of 1600 LLM diagnoses reaching grounding score >= 0.75 under our rubric; and that anomaly-score-driven risk functions dominate alternatives by roughly an order of magnitude on the Pareto AUC.

</details>


### [89] [TSSM: Triaxial State Space Model for Global Station Weather Forecasting with Temporal-Variable-Historical Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13101)
*Songru Yang,Zili Liu,Tao Han,Ben Fei,Fenghua Ling,Lei Bai,Chang Liu,Xiangyang Ji,Zhenwei Shi,Zhengxia Zou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TSSM introduces a Triaxial State Space Model with period-aligned historical data to overcome the short-term reliance of existing weather forecasting methods, achieving SOTA accuracy, extreme event prediction, and robustness under missing observations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing global station weather forecasting methods rely heavily on short-term look-back windows, which are insufficient to capture chaotic weather dynamics—especially for extreme events and under partial observations—leading to limited accuracy gains and error accumulation.

Method: TSSM proposes a history-enhanced Temporal-Variable-Historical paradigm that stacks historical samples into period-aligned batches. Three scanning mechanisms—temporal (axial dependencies), variable (correlations), and historical (evolution)—are designed and hierarchically shared to model phenomena from seasonal patterns to extreme events while mitigating historical misalignment.

Result: On Weather-5K, TSSM achieves 10% accuracy gain and 61% improvement in extreme event metrics. It obtains 95% best/second-best results on human-involved datasets. Gains are magnified at long horizons (37.5% at 240h) and iterative forecasting (103.5% under 48h×5). Under 80% missing observations, TSSM retains >90% performance versus <43% for baselines.

Conclusion: TSSM demonstrates that incorporating period-aligned historical data via a triaxial state space architecture effectively addresses the fundamental limitations of short-term-reliant forecasting, delivering substantial gains in accuracy, extreme event prediction, long-horizon forecasting, and robustness to missing data for practical GSWF deployment.

Abstract: Global Station Weather Forecasting (GSWF) is pivotal for localized and extreme weather prediction over key regions. Despite efforts to exploit look-back windows, existing methods show limited accuracy gains and struggle with extreme events and error accumulation. These limitations stem from overreliance on short-term patterns, which are insufficient to capture chaotic weather dynamics, especially under partial observations. To address this problem, we propose a novel Triaxial State Space Model (TSSM) with a history-enhanced Temporal-VariableHistorical paradigm, which incorporates period-aligned historical weather data to compensate for long-term, large-scale periodic, and full-window weather patterns beyond the temporal lookback window.
