Specifically, TSSM stacks historical samples into period-aligned batches, where forecasting is causally supported by historical and current observations. Temporal, variable, and historical scanning are designed to capture axial temporal dependencies, variable correlations, and historical evolution. This structure is hierarchically shared to model seasonal to extreme events while alleviating misalignment across historical patterns. TSSM achieves SOTA performance on Weather-5K, the largest station weather dataset to date, with 10% and 61% gains in accuracy and extreme event metrics, and obtains 95% best or second-best results on human-involved datasets. Its advantages are more pronounced in long-horizon and iterative forecasting, reaching a 37.5% gain at 240h and up to 103.5% under a 48h times 5 iterative setting. Moreover, TSSM retains > 90% performance under up to 80% missing observations, compared with < 43% for baselines, demonstrating robustness and practical potential for reliable GSWF in global in-situ observation networks.

</details>


### [90] [Disentangling Knowledge States with Ability and Proficiency Modeling for Knowledge Tracing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13103)
*Duantengchuan Li,Yingqian Bi,Jinsong Chen,Rui Zhang,Mingwen Tong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PAKT decomposes student interactions into ability-building and proficiency-oriented phases, then uses a multi-branch Transformer with a type-aware readout module to capture both phase-specific and holistic knowledge states, achieving state-of-the-art KT performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing KT methods treat interaction sequences as a unified process, ignoring phase-specific learning behaviors. Preliminary observations indicate students transition from ability-building (correctly answering previously failed concepts after practice) to proficiency-oriented learning, motivating a phase-aware approach.

Method: PAKT introduces a tailored decomposition mechanism that splits student interactions into ability and proficiency phases. A multi-branch Transformer with a type-aware readout module then jointly models phase-specific and holistic knowledge states. A causal analysis further reveals and addresses confounding bias present in phase-agnostic models.

Result: Experiments on six public benchmarks show consistent outperformance over representative baselines, with a maximum AUC gain of 1.33% and an average gain of 0.82%.

Conclusion: Phase-aware decomposition of learning interactions effectively captures distinct learning dynamics, and the proposed PAKT framework robustly improves knowledge tracing performance across diverse datasets.

Abstract: Knowledge tracing (KT) aims to predict students' future performance by modeling their evolving knowledge states from historical interactions. Existing KT methods usually treat the raw interaction sequence as a unified behavioral process, overlooking the phase-specific nature of learning behaviors. Our preliminary observations show that students are more likely to correctly answer previously failed knowledge concepts after sufficient practice, suggesting a transition from ability-building to proficiency-oriented learning. Motivated by this, we propose Phase-Aware Knowledge Tracing (PAKT), a KT framework that decomposes student interactions into ability and proficiency phases based on the tailored decomposition mechanism. To effectively exploit the decomposed sequences, we design a multi-branch Transformer with a type-aware readout module to jointly capture phase-specific and holistic knowledge states. We further provide a causal analysis to reveal the confounding bias caused by entangling complex learning behaviors in phase-agnostic KT models. Extensive experiments on six public benchmarks demonstrate that our method consistently outperforms representative baselines, with a maximum AUC gain of 1.33% and an average gain of 0.82%.

</details>


### [91] [Gauge-Invariant, Parameter-Insensitive Regularization for Potential Recovery from Flow on Directed Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13609)
*Mohammad Forouhesh*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Recovering latent potentials from directed graph flows is ill-posed; standard ridge regularization destroys rank ordering by shrinking toward an arbitrary gauge origin. The authors propose using the gauge-invariant graph Dirichlet energy (H¹ seminorm) instead, which yields stable estimates across 4+ orders of magnitude of regularization, preserves dynamic range, and naturally connects to preventing oversmoothing in deep graph neural networks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The standard ridge (L²/Tikhonov) regularization for the discrete Poisson problem on directed graphs introduces a gauge-dependent origin, which collapses and inverts the recovered ranking of node potentials. This is both theoretically unsound and practically damaging for downstream tasks relying on relative ordering.

Method: The authors diagnose the gauge-invariance violation of ridge regularization, then replace it with the graph Dirichlet energy (H¹ seminorm) as the regularizer. They prove the resulting saddle-point system is symmetric positive definite (SPD), characterize its dynamic-range preservation properties, derive a Poisson residual for localizing absorbing boundaries, and ablate across extraction methods. They also extend gauge invariance to Graph Convolutional Networks (GCNs) by neutralizing the constant mode per layer to combat oversmoothing.

Result: On synthetic data, ridge inverts rank correlation from +0.81 to -0.42, while the gauge-invariant estimate remains stable across λ ∈ [10⁻², 10²]. On three public clickstream datasets, ridge collapses dynamic range to as little as 0.2%, whereas the proposed method retains 28–41% of interior dynamic range. The method is robust to the flow-extraction technique used. The gauge-invariant layer normalization prevents oversmoothing in deep directed GCNs.

Conclusion: The gauge-invariant Dirichlet energy resolves the ill-posedness without the destructive side-effects of ridge regularization, delivering parameter-insensitive, order-preserving potential recovery. The principle generalizes to graph neural networks, where neutralizing the constant mode per layer mitigates oversmoothing, linking a classical inverse problem to contemporary deep learning on graphs.

Abstract: Recovering a latent potential from observed flow on a directed graph (a discrete Poisson problem with Dirichlet boundaries) is ill-posed, and the standard fix backfires: ridge regularization shrinks toward a gauge-meaningless origin, collapsing and reversing the recovered ordering ($+0.81\to-0.42$ rank correlation against a planted ground truth). The gauge-invariant graph Dirichlet energy removes the hazard and delivers parameter-insensitivity: the estimate is stable across four orders of magnitude in $λ$, whereas ridge inverts the ordering for every $λ>0$. We prove the reduced solve is SPD and preserves dynamic range exactly where ridge collapses it, and localize absorbing boundaries from flow alone via a Poisson residual. The $H^1$ seminorm is classical; what is new is the gauge diagnosis, the parameter-insensitivity it buys, and an ablation showing the result is robust to the extraction method. On three public clickstream corpora the gauge-invariant estimate retains $28$--$41\%$ of the interior dynamic range while ridge collapses to as little as $0.2\%$. The same gauge invariance carries into graph neural networks -- neutralizing the constant mode per layer prevents the oversmoothing that collapses a deep directed GCN -- linking this classical inverse problem to a central question in graph learning.

</details>


### [92] [STKAN: Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks for Spatio-Temporal Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13108)
*Sicong Lai,Yuehong Hu,Siru Zhong,Si Qiao,Yuxuan Liang,Guangyin Jin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: STKAN introduces Taylor-polynomial Kolmogorov-Arnold Network modules into spatio-temporal forecasting, combining soft node-group assignment, group-wise spatial mixing, temporal compression, and self-attention to achieve competitive results on traffic benchmarks, outperforming MLP-based variants.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing spatio-temporal forecasting methods focus heavily on architectural innovations (graphs, attention, decomposition) while overlooking the impact of the underlying nonlinear function approximator. The authors aim to investigate whether KAN-based function approximators can serve as a useful complement to architecture design.

Method: STKAN employs three key components: (1) a learnable soft node-group assignment mechanism that constructs high-level spatial representations; (2) Taylor-polynomial KAN modules for group-wise spatial mixing and temporal mixing over compressed sequences; (3) spatial and temporal self-attention layers to capture long-range dependencies.

Result: Experiments on five traffic forecasting benchmarks demonstrate that STKAN achieves competitive performance and consistently outperforms an MLP-based variant under the tested settings.

Conclusion: The design of nonlinear function approximators (specifically KANs) represents a meaningful and underexplored dimension in spatio-temporal forecasting, offering a useful complement to architectural innovations.

Abstract: Real-world traffic data exhibit heterogeneous spatial correlations and nonlinear temporal dynamics, posing substantial challenges for accurate spatio-temporal forecasting. Existing approaches have developed increasingly sophisticated graph, attention, and decomposition architectures, while the influence of the underlying nonlinear function approximator has received comparatively less attention. In this work, we propose STKAN, a spatio-temporal forecasting architecture that introduces Taylor-polynomial Kolmogorov--Arnold Network modules into spatial and temporal token mixing. STKAN first constructs high-level spatial representations through a learnable soft node-group assignment mechanism, applies group-wise spatial mixing, and subsequently models temporal dependencies over the compressed sequence. Spatial and temporal self-attention layers are further employed to capture long-range interactions. Experiments on five traffic forecasting benchmarks show that STKAN achieves competitive performance and performs better than the evaluated MLP-based variant in the tested settings. These results suggest that the design of nonlinear function approximators can serve as a useful complement to architectural design in spatio-temporal forecasting.

</details>


### [93] [A Hybrid Mamba for Audio-Visual Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13110)
*Yi Wang,Yinfeng Yu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Samba proposes a hybrid Mamba-based architecture for audio-visual navigation that replaces outdated CNN/GRU backbones with Mamba State Encoders and Audio Mamba Encoders, achieving 11.3% SR improvement on Matterport3D and stronger gains on Replica.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The core backbone networks for audio-visual navigation have remained unchanged since 2020 (CNN + RNN paradigm), and these architectures are insufficient for efficiently representing dynamic multimodal sequences, particularly in capturing global time-frequency dependencies in audio spectrograms.

Method: Samba introduces two key components: (1) Mamba State Encoder (M-SE) with adaptive selection to replace conventional GRUs for temporal aggregation, and (2) Audio Mamba Encoder (AME) to overcome convolutional operators' limitations in modeling global time-frequency dependencies in spectrograms.

Result: Samba demonstrates exceptional generalization to unheard sound sources and unseen scenes. On Matterport3D, it improves navigation success rate (SR) by 11.3% over SOTA. Gains are even more pronounced on the finer-grained Replica dataset.

Conclusion: The modernized Mamba-based architectural reconstruction unlocks stronger embodied representation capabilities at lower computational cost, offering a robust technical pathway for paradigm evolution in audio-visual navigation.

Abstract: Since the paradigm centered on convolutional neural networks and recurrent architectures was established in 2020, the fundamental backbone networks for audio-visual navigation have undergone no essential changes for more than five years, making them inadequate to support efficient representation of dynamic multimodal sequences. This paper proposes Samba(A Hybrid Mamba for Audio-Visual Navigation). It uses the adaptive selection-enabled Mamba State Encoder (M-SE) to replace conventional GRUs for temporal aggregation, and constructs an Audio Mamba Encoder (AME) to remedy the limitations of convolutional operators in capturing global time-frequency dependencies in spectrograms. Experiments demonstrate that Samba exhibits exceptional generalization performance when facing unheard sound sources and unseen scenes. On the Matterport3D dataset, it improves the navigation success rate (SR) by 11.3\% compared with existing state-of-the-art models, and the performance gain is even more pronounced on the Replica dataset, which features finer scene structures. Such modernized architectural reconstruction unlocks stronger embodied representation capabilities at a lower computational cost, thereby providing a highly robust technical pathway for paradigm evolution in the field of audio-visual navigation.

</details>


### [94] [CoDiffGRN: Rethinking Gene Regulatory Network Inference via the BEELINE-KGC Benchmark and Co-evolutionary Discrete Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13120)
*Jiaze Song,Runhao Zhao,Minghao Xu,Bin Cui,Wentao Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper reformulates gene regulatory network (GRN) inference from single-cell data as an inductive, ranking-centric graph completion problem, introduces a new benchmark with inductive gene-holdout splits and knowledge graph completion metrics, and proposes a co-evolutionary discrete diffusion framework that jointly models discretized gene expression states and regulatory interactions, achieving state-of-the-art performance in discovering novel regulatory relationships.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing GRN inference methods are misaligned with real-world needs: researchers want a small set of high-confidence, experimentally validatable regulatory interactions, often involving unseen genes. Current benchmarks use transductive splits and global classification metrics, while models fail to generalize inductively to novel genes. There is a critical gap between evaluation protocols and practical utility.

Method: The authors propose: (1) a new benchmark that reformulates GRN inference as an inductive, ranking-centric graph completion problem with gene-holdout splits and knowledge graph completion metrics; (2) a co-evolutionary discrete diffusion framework that jointly models biologically coherent discretized gene expression states and regulatory interactions for robust inductive generalization; and (3) TF-ALL Subgraph Sampling (TASS) for scalable training.

Result: Extensive experiments on the proposed benchmark demonstrate that the method establishes new state-of-the-art performance, significantly outperforming existing methods in novel regulatory discovery. Ablation studies further validate the effectiveness of each design component.

Conclusion: Reformulating GRN inference as an inductive ranking problem with appropriate evaluation metrics, combined with a co-evolutionary discrete diffusion framework, substantially improves the discovery of novel regulatory interactions, better aligning computational predictions with experimental validation needs.

Abstract: Inferring gene regulatory networks (GRNs) from single-cell transcriptomic data is crucial for biological discovery, yet existing approaches suffer from a fundamental misalignment with real-world needs. Researchers typically seek a small set of high-confidence regulatory interactions for experimental validation, often involving previously unseen genes. However, current benchmarks rely on transductive splits with global classification metrics, while prevailing models struggle to generalize under inductive settings. To bridge this gap, we reformulate GRN inference as an inductive, ranking-centric graph completion problem and introduce \textbf{\benchmark}, a new benchmark that incorporates an inductive gene-holdout split together with knowledge graph completion metrics to better evaluate top-ranked predictions. Building on this, we propose \textbf{\method}, the first co-evolutionary discrete diffusion framework that jointly models biologically coherent discretized gene expression states and regulatory interactions for robust inductive generalization and improved top-ranked regulatory discovery. We further introduce TF-ALL Subgraph Sampling (TASS) for scalable training. Extensive experiments on {\benchmark} show that {\method} establishes new state-of-the-art performance, significantly outperforming existing methods in novel regulatory discovery, and ablation studies further verify the effectiveness of our design.

</details>


### [95] [ShortOPD: Recovering Pruned LLMs with Short-to-Long On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13124)
*Qingyu Zhang,Qianhao Yuan,Hongyu Lin,Yaojie Lu,Xianpei Han,Le Sun,Xiang Li,Ming Xu,Jiarui Li,Xiuyin Zhao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Structured pruning harms free-form generation quality (greedy decoding) due to suffix repetition, but On-Policy Distillation with a short-to-long schedule (SOPD) effectively recovers it by focusing training on non-repetitive prefix lengths, achieving ~9× improvement over unrecovered models and 1.6–4.4× over standard recovery methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Structured pruning for LLMs is typically evaluated on multiple-choice benchmarks, but the same compressed models fail catastrophically on free-form text generation — the actual deployment scenario. The authors identify that useful generations are "demoted" rather than erased (recoverable via pass@k), and that suffix repetition is the primary failure mode. Existing recovery methods (SFT, KD, SeqKD) are inadequate because they waste training budget on low-information repetitive suffixes.

Method: The authors propose SOPD (Short-to-long On-Policy Distillation), which: (1) uses the pre-compression model as a frozen teacher for token-level supervision on the compressed model's own on-policy rollouts; (2) detects teacher-confirmed repetitive suffixes via token-level agreement; (3) treats the surviving non-repetitive prefix as the effective length for each rollout; and (4) dynamically allocates future rollout budgets to effective lengths the policy can currently handle, gradually increasing from short to long sequences.

Result: Across math, code, and open-ended generation tasks, SOPD raises the compressed model's score to ~9× its unrecovered value and 1.6–4.4× over standard recovery recipes (SFT without KD, KD, SeqKD). It matches a fixed 8192-token rollout horizon within two points while using only 25% of the training time (8.5 vs. 35.9 hours) and 71% fewer rollout tokens.

Conclusion: SOPD provides a practical recipe to recover free-form generation quality in structured-pruned LLMs, moving the field beyond marginal perplexity/multiple-choice gains toward deployment-ready models. The short-to-long schedule efficiently allocates training compute by focusing on learnable prefix lengths first.

Abstract: Structured pruning is a hardware-friendly way to compress LLMs, but it is mostly validated on multiple-choice recognition tasks, while the same compressed checkpoints can collapse on the free-form generation that deployment actually requires. Two observations trace this gap. First, greedy \textsc{pass}@$1$ nearly vanishes after compression, yet \textsc{pass}@$k$ recovers substantially under repeated sampling: useful generations are demoted, not erased. Second, the recoverable regime fails mainly through suffix repetition. Recovery should therefore train on the compressed model's own on-policy states with dense token-level supervision, which On-Policy Distillation (OPD) provides by reusing the pre-compression model as a frozen teacher. However, long on-policy rollouts spend early recovery budget on low-information repetitive suffixes, delaying loss descent. To mitigate this waste, we propose \textbf{\shortopd}, a short-to-long OPD schedule that detects teacher-confirmed repetitive suffixes, treats the surviving prefix as each rollout's effective length, and allocates future rollout budgets to the effective lengths the policy can currently use. Across math, code, and open-ended generation, \shortopd\ raises the compressed model's score to about $9\times$ its unrecovered value and $1.6$--$4.4\times$ standard recovery recipes (SFT w/o KD, KD, and SeqKD), and it matches a fixed $8192$-token rollout horizon within two points using a quarter of the training time ($8.5$ vs.\ $35.9$ hours) and $71\%$ fewer rollout tokens. We hope this recipe helps move structured pruning beyond marginal gains on perplexity and multiple-choice benchmarks, a step closer to deployment-ready generation quality.

</details>


### [96] [HEDGEHOG: Hierarchical Evaluation of Drug Generators Through Rigorous Filtration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13155)
*Daria A. Ryabchenko,Pavel Gurevich,Shamil Kadyrov,Daria Frolova,Kseniia Fedisheva,Sergei A. Nikolenko,Alexander Shapeev,Marina A. Pak*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HEDGEHOG is a six-stage filtration benchmark for evaluating molecular generators, inspired by industrial hit identification. Testing 23 generators across 230,000 molecules revealed that only 0.65% survive all stages, exposing a critical gap between isolated metric performance and simultaneous multiparameter constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing metrics for evaluating generative molecular models are poorly aligned with medicinal plausibility and downstream utility, leading to false positives in model evaluation, incorrect assumptions about model performance, and inefficient use of computational resources. The authors argue for a workflow-aligned, multi-stage filtration benchmark that better reflects real-world drug discovery requirements.

Method: The authors propose HEDGEHOG, a unified six-stage filtration benchmark: (i) preprocessing, (ii) physicochemical descriptor screening, (iii) structural alerts and graph-sanity checks, (iv) synthesis feasibility assessment, (v) docking and binding affinity estimation, and (vi) three-dimensional pose and interaction checks. They evaluate 23 molecular generators from three model classes under a standardized protocol, processing 230,000 generated molecules.

Result: Only 0.65% of the 230,000 initially generated molecules survived all six filtration stages. Molecules that appear acceptable under isolated criteria consistently fail to simultaneously satisfy medicinal chemistry, synthetic accessibility, docking, and 3D pose filters.

Conclusion: Current molecular generators exhibit a central limitation: they cannot reliably produce molecules that satisfy the full spectrum of real-world drug discovery constraints simultaneously. The HEDGEHOG benchmark provides a more rigorous and holistic evaluation framework that exposes this gap, and the authors recommend adopting such multi-stage filtration to guide future model development more effectively.

Abstract: Generative molecular models can support early drug discovery by proposing new candidate compounds de novo. In practice, useful candidates must balance target-relevant activity, synthetic accessibility, physicochemical properties, and other multiparameter design constraints. However, metrics commonly used to evaluate molecular generators only weakly reflect whether the generated compounds are medicinally plausible and suitable for downstream computation. This can produce false positives in model evaluation, incorrect assumptions, and inefficient use of computational resources. We introduce HEDGEHOG, a unified six-stage filtration benchmark that is inspired by industrial hit identification workflows: (i) preprocessing; (ii) physicochemical descriptor screening; (iii) structural alerts and graph-sanity checks; (iv) synthesis feasibility; (v) docking and binding affinity estimation; and (vi) three-dimensional pose and interaction checks. We evaluate 23 molecular generators across three model classes under a standardized protocol. Across 230,000 generated molecules, only 0.65% of initial molecules survive all stages. Our results expose a central limitation of current molecular generators: molecules that appear acceptable under isolated criteria rarely satisfy medicinal chemistry, synthesis, docking, and 3D pose filters simultaneously.

</details>


### [97] [SteinGate: Tail-Sensitive Safe Reinforcement Learning via Stein Discrepancy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13175)
*Yassine Chemingui,Chenhua Fan,Honghao Wei,Janardhan Rao Doppa*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SteinGate introduces a boundary-aware distributional safety certificate using Kernelized Stein Discrepancy to detect rare tail-event violations in safe RL, dynamically switching between reward optimization and recovery behaviors. It reduces both frequency and severity of constraint violations while maintaining competitive returns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing safe RL methods bound expected cumulative costs, which fails to capture rare but catastrophic tail events (e.g., severe constraint violations that occur infrequently). Distributional approaches that attempt to fit the cost tail are fragile. A more robust method is needed to detect when a policy's cost distribution deviates from a safe reference, particularly in the tail region.

Method: The authors propose SteinGate, which uses Kernelized Stein Discrepancy (KSD) as a non-parametric goodness-of-fit test to check whether observed policy rollout costs are consistent with a safe reference distribution. A key technical contribution is handling "boundary atoms" — probability mass accumulating at the cost clipping boundary — which would otherwise invalidate the consistency check. SteinGate dynamically adapts the learning regime: when rollouts are consistent with the safe reference, it permits reward-improving updates; when the cost tail deviates, it switches to a recovery behavior to restore safety.

Result: Experiments on continuous-control benchmarks show that SteinGate significantly reduces both the frequency and severity of constraint violations during training compared to state-of-the-art baselines, while maintaining competitive returns.

Conclusion: SteinGate provides a principled, non-parametric alternative to expected-cost-based safety constraints, effectively mitigating tail risk in safe RL through distributional consistency checking with boundary-aware KSD. The approach enables safer policy learning without sacrificing task performance.

Abstract: Safe reinforcement learning typically enforces safety by bounding expected cumulative costs, a criterion that often fails to detect rare but catastrophic tail events. To overcome these limitations, this paper introduces SteinGate, a boundary-aware distributional safety certificate that replaces fragile tail fitting with a robust consistency check using Kernelized Stein Discrepancy while accounting for boundary atoms induced by clipped costs. SteinGate evaluates whether observed policy rollout costs remain consistent with a safe reference distribution, providing a non-parametric safety certificate. This certificate is used to dynamically adapt the learning regime: favoring reward-improving policy updates when rollouts remain consistent with the safe reference and switching to recovery behavior when the cost tail deviates. Experiments on continuous-control benchmarks demonstrate that SteinGate significantly reduces both the frequency and severity of constraint violations during training while maintaining competitive returns relative to state-of-the-art baselines.

</details>


### [98] [Concurrent Image Understanding and Generation: Self-Correcting Coupled Markov Jump Processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13188)
*Minh-Quan Le,Armand Comas,Alexandros Lattas,Stylianos Moschoglou,Pedro Vélez,Amit Raj,Aaron Germuth,Thabo Beeler,Dimitris Samaras,Di Qiu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces Self-Correcting Coupled Markov Jump Processes (SC-CMJP) and the CO₂Jump sampler, a training-free, single-pass method for joint text-image generation using Masked Diffusion Models. It enables modalities to influence each other within the same denoising step and introduces a remasking mechanism to correct cross-modal contradictions, achieving state-of-the-art performance on joint image understanding, editing, and visual reasoning tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current masked diffusion samplers for joint multimodal generation treat text and image modalities either sequentially or in parallel without within-step cross-modal feedback. Combined with MDMs' inability to remask tokens, this leads to undetected and unrepaired cross-modal contradictions. The authors aim to replicate the coupled, mutually-reshaping nature of human multimodal cognition (e.g., a teacher speaking and drawing simultaneously).

Method: The proposed SC-CMJP framework defines one modality's transition rates as functionals of the other modality's confidence scores, weighted by cross-modal attention. Crucially, a remasking jump mechanism retracts a modality's commitment when cross-modal evidence turns against it. The CO₂Jump sampler implements this as a training-free, single-pass algorithm for joint multimodal generation, enabling within-step coupling and self-correction.

Result: CO₂Jump achieves best joint performance on image understanding/editing and visual reasoning (maze and nonogram solving). Performance scales monotonically with denoising steps, demonstrating that cross-modal coupling benefits compound across the trajectory. Three large-scale joint multimodal generation corpora (JEdit-1M, JMaze-200K, JNono-200K) with in- and out-of-distribution benchmarks were created and will be released.

Conclusion: SC-CMJP and CO₂Jump establish that within-step cross-modal coupling with remasking significantly improves joint multimodal generation quality. The monotonic scaling with steps provides evidence that coupled decision-making compounds over the trajectory, closing the gap between sequential human-like multimodal reasoning and artificial generation systems.

Abstract: Human cognition does not separate understanding and generation. A teacher at a whiteboard speaks and draws $\textit{together}$, each modality reshapes the other. In this paper, we bring this coupled loop to artificial systems. Masked Diffusion Models (MDMs) are ideally suited to this task, yet existing samplers either decode text and image interleavedly or independently update them in parallel branches that share only previous-step history, but not the other modality's latest decisions $\textit{within}$ the same step; combined with MDMs' inability to remask, cross-modal contradictions are neither detected nor repaired. We introduce $\textbf{Self-Correcting Coupled Markov Jump Processes (SC-CMJP)}$, a framework in which one modality's transition rates are functionals of the other modality's confidence score, as weighted by cross-modal attention. Furthermore, a remasking jump retracts commitments the moment cross-modal evidence turns against them. In conjunction with SC-CMJP, we introduce $\texttt{CO}_\texttt{2}\texttt{Jump}$ (Self-$\underline{\text{CO}}$rrecting $\underline{\text{CO}}$upled $\underline{\text{Jump}}$), a novel training-free single-pass sampler for joint multimodal geneneration. For training and evaluation purposes, we have created and will release three large-scale joint multimodal generation corpora: $\text{JEdit-1M}$, $\text{JMaze-200K}$, $\text{JNono-200K}$, with matching in- and out-of-distribution benchmarks. $\texttt{CO}_\texttt{2}\texttt{Jump}$ achieves best joint performance for image understanding and editing as well as visual reasoning (maze and nonogram solving). The performance of the sampler scales monotonically with the number of denoising steps, evidence that the benefits of cross-modal coupling $\textit{compound}$ across the trajectory. Project page: https://coupled-jump.github.io

</details>


### [99] [EMAGN: Efficient Multi-Attention Graph Network via Learned Clustering for Scalable Traffic Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13241)
*Mingxing Xu,Rakesh Chowdary Machineni,Ke Liu,Xi Cheng,Chengqi Lu,Xin Hu,Lyuhao Chen,Xiangyu Li,Junwei You,Oliver Gao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EMAGN proposes an efficient graph attention network for traffic forecasting that uses learned adaptive clustering to reduce self-attention complexity from O(N²d) to O(NMd), achieving near-full-attention accuracy with substantial gains in speed and memory efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Self-attention mechanisms excel at modeling dynamic spatiotemporal dependencies in traffic forecasting but suffer from quadratic O(N²d) complexity, limiting scalability to larger graphs and more attention heads. Existing linearized attention methods (Linformer, Performer) are not tailored to traffic network structures, motivating a domain-aware efficient alternative.

Method: EMAGN linearizes spatial attention via learned clustering matrices C_k and C_v that adaptively group key and value vectors into M super-clusters, inspired by fast high-dimensional Gaussian filtering. This reduces per-head complexity from O(N²d) to O(NMd). The clustering is traffic-network-aware and learned end-to-end, preserving dynamic dependency modeling flexibility.

Result: On PEMS-BAY and METR-LA, EMAGN achieves MAE within 2.7–3.2% of full-attention GMAN while reducing training time by 32%, inference time by 38%, and GPU memory by 58%. At K=16 heads, full-attention GMAN exhausts 11 GB GPU memory while EMAGN remains operational. EMAGN outperforms Linformer and Performer in both accuracy and efficiency under the same backbone.

Conclusion: EMAGN successfully trades off a small accuracy margin for dramatic computational savings, demonstrating that traffic-network-aware adaptive clustering is a superior approach to generic linearized attention for spatiotemporal forecasting, and enabling model configurations previously infeasible due to memory constraints.

Abstract: Traffic forecasting is highly challenging due to complex and nonlinear spatial and temporal dependencies. Self-attention mechanisms have been widely adopted to model dynamic and long-range dependencies, achieving state-of-the-art performance, but suffer from limited scalability due to quadratic computational and memory complexity. To address this, we propose an Efficient Multi-Attention Graph Network (EMAGN) that linearises the spatial attention mechanism itself, inspired by the theory of fast high-dimensional Gaussian filtering. Two learned clustering matrices C_k and C_v adaptively group key and value vectors into M super-clusters, reducing complexity from O(N^2 d) to O(NMd) without sacrificing the flexibility of attention for dynamic dependency modelling. Experimental results on PEMS-BAY and METR-LA show that EMAGN achieves accuracy within 2.7-3.2% MAE of full-attention GMAN while reducing training time by 32%, inference time by 38%, and GPU memory by 58%. Critically, at K=16 attention heads, full-attention GMAN runs out of memory on a standard 11 GB GPU entirely while EMAGN continues to operate, demonstrating a categorical expansion of feasible model configurations. EMAGN also surpasses Linformer and Performer in both accuracy and efficiency within the same backbone, owing to its traffic-network-aware adaptive clustering.

</details>


### [100] [Reassessing Muon for Matrix Factorization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13246)
*Ali Parviz,Gal Mishne,Alex Cloninger*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper evaluates Muon optimizer on low-rank matrix factorization, finding it does not consistently outperform AdamW. Previously reported advantages of Muon may be artifacts of scale/architecture rather than inherent to its update rule. The authors advocate for controlled problem evaluations alongside end-to-end benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Muon has shown strong empirical results in large-scale deep learning (especially LLM training), but it is unclear whether its advantages are intrinsic to its spectrum-aware orthogonalization update rule or are confounded by modern deep network scale, architecture, and data. The authors aim to isolate the optimizer from these confounding factors.

Method: The authors conduct a controlled comparison by studying Muon on the simple, well-understood, spectrally structured problem of low-rank matrix factorization. They compare Muon against carefully tuned adaptive baselines (specifically AdamW) in this isolated setting.

Result: Muon does not consistently outperform AdamW on low-rank matrix factorization. Several previously reported advantages are sensitive to hyperparameter choices, suggesting those benefits may not be inherent to the update rule itself.

Conclusion: The findings provide a more nuanced picture of when spectrum-aware orthogonalization is beneficial. The authors argue that modern optimizers should be evaluated on controlled problems in addition to end-to-end benchmarks to disentangle algorithmic properties from confounding factors.

Abstract: Muon has recently emerged as a strong optimizer for large-scale deep learning, where it reshapes gradient updates through approximate orthogonalization and has been reported to outperform Adam and AdamW in large language model training. Its empirical success has motivated a growing body of theoretical work that interprets Muon as steepest descent under the spectral norm. Yet it remains unclear which of Muon's advantages stem from its update rule itself and which are artifacts of the scale, architecture, and data of modern deep networks. In this work, we isolate the optimizer from these confounding factors by studying Muon on a simple, well-understood, and spectrally structured problem: low-rank matrix factorization. Through a controlled comparison against carefully tuned adaptive baselines, we find that Muon does not consistently outperform AdamW in this setting and that several previously reported advantages are sensitive to hyperparameter choices. Our results provide a more nuanced picture of when spectrum-aware orthogonalization is beneficial and argue for evaluating modern optimizers on controlled problems in addition to end-to-end benchmarks.

</details>


### [101] [Deconstructing Actor-Critic: A Large-scale Empirical Study of Design Components for Practitioners](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13274)
*Haseeb Shah,Lingwei Zhu,Adam White,Martha White*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: An empirical analysis of over 33,000 experiments on a real water treatment control task reveals that common actor-critic defaults (e.g., Gaussian action distributions with pathwise gradients) are among the least reliable, while bounded distributions with adaptive update schedules offer superior robustness across hyperparameter settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reinforcement learning is increasingly applied to safety-critical, real-world control systems (fusion plasma, autonomous vehicles, water treatment) where reliability and sample efficiency are paramount, yet there is limited systematic understanding of how individual design components in actor-critic algorithms affect robustness and hyperparameter sensitivity.

Method: The authors use a control task derived from a real water treatment plant and conduct a large-scale empirical study of over 33,000 experiments, systematically varying actor-critic design decisions: policy update mechanisms, action distribution representations, gradient estimation methods, and update frequency relative to the value estimator.

Result: Common default configurations — specifically Gaussian action distributions paired with pathwise (reparameterization) gradient estimators — are among the least reliable. In contrast, bounded action distributions combined with adaptive update schedules demonstrate consistent robustness across diverse hyperparameter settings.

Conclusion: The findings provide concrete, component-level empirical guidance for practitioners adapting actor-critic methods to real-world control problems, recommending bounded distributions and adaptive update schedules over conventional defaults to improve reliability and reduce tuning burden.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning is increasingly being considered for controlling real-world systems, from fusion plasma and autonomous vehicles to drug discovery and drinking water treatment, where reliability is essential and tuning budgets are limited. Actor-critic algorithms share a set of design decisions, such as how the policy is updated, how it represents the distribution over actions, how its gradient is estimated, and how often it is updated relative to the value estimator. Using a control task derived from a real water treatment plant, we analyze over 33,000 experiments to determine how these components affect variability across runs and sensitivity to hyperparameters. Common defaults, such as Gaussian action distributions with pathwise gradient estimators, are among the least reliable configurations, whereas bounded distributions with adaptive update schedules remain robust across a wide range of settings. These findings offer empirical guidance to practitioners across scientific and engineering domains for understanding and making component-level decisions when adapting actor-critic methods to new real-world control settings.

</details>


### [102] [Tabular Foundation Models for Discrete Choice Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13314)
*Liu Liu,Dan Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Transformer-based tabular foundation models (TFMs) underperform on discrete choice problems due to structural mismatches. Encoding choice-set dependence and individual heterogeneity into a row-based framework enables TFMs to outperform hierarchical Bayesian models by 8% in holdout log-likelihood, with 16× faster runtime, especially in medium-data regimes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: TFMs achieve strong in-context learning on tabular data but assume row-independent observations. Discrete choice — a core demand estimation tool in marketing and operations — is inherently set-valued with persistent consumer preference heterogeneity, creating a structural mismatch that limits direct TFM application.

Method: The authors reformulate the discrete choice problem to encode both choice-set dependence and individual-level heterogeneity within a row-based TFM framework. They evaluate on a yogurt scanner panel dataset, comparing against hierarchical Bayesian estimation, and test fine-tuning on population choice data for consumers with shallow purchase histories.

Result: Individual heterogeneity encoding is the dominant driver of accuracy. The best reformulation outperforms hierarchical Bayes by 8% in holdout log-likelihood and 3.6% in hit rate, running 16× faster. Gains are largest in the medium-data regime (10–40 purchases per consumer), where Bayesian shrinkage distorts atypical consumers. Fine-tuning helps shallow-history consumers.

Conclusion: With proper structural encoding, TFMs can surpass Bayesian methods on discrete choice tasks, offering both accuracy gains and computational efficiency. This establishes a principled approach for applying foundation models to consumer choice problems broadly.

Abstract: Tabular foundation models (TFMs) generate predictions on structured data via in-context learning, without task-specific estimation. We ask whether TFMs can be effectively applied to discrete choice, a central demand estimation framework in marketing and operations, and find that directly applying TFMs yields limited performance. The gap is structural: TFMs assume row-independent observations, whereas discrete choice is inherently set-valued and subject to persistent consumer preference heterogeneity. We propose a reformulation that encodes both choice-set dependence and individual heterogeneity within a row-based learning framework. Evaluated on a yogurt scanner panel, individual-level heterogeneity encoding is the dominant driver of predictive accuracy. The best reformulation outperforms hierarchical Bayesian estimation by 8\% in holdout log-likelihood and 3.6\% in hit rate, running 16 times faster, a practical advantage for large-scale demand estimation. The advantage is largest in the medium-data regime (10--40 purchase occasions per consumer), where parametric Bayesian shrinkage most distorts estimates for atypical consumers. Fine-tuning on population choice data provides additional gains for consumers with shallow purchase histories, where in-context learning has limited individual-specific signal to condition on. These results establish a principled approach for applying foundation models to consumer choice problems more broadly.

</details>


### [103] [Accuracy-Preserving Stability Regularization for Large-Scale Retail Demand Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13331)
*Jize Li,Jiani He,Dishu Yang,Dingyan Shang,Jingjing Liu,Shiqi Huang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A training-time penalty on consecutive forecast movement is proposed to improve horizontal stability of retail demand forecasts without materially degrading point accuracy, outperforming post-hoc smoothing in the accuracy-stability trade-off.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Retail demand forecasts are reused across multiple operational planning cycles (replenishment, capacity, labor, transportation). Standard point-error objectives do not penalize abrupt, unstable movements between adjacent forecast revisions, and post-hoc smoothing is applied only after model training, which can degrade accuracy. The authors seek a training-time mechanism to directly optimize for forecast-path stability alongside accuracy.

Method: A stability-aware hybrid model with a training-time penalty on consecutive within-series forecast movement is proposed. The penalty is integrated into a temporal-structured pipeline using recent-demand embeddings combined with calendar, price, hierarchy, item, and store features. The method is evaluated on selected M5 competition demand series at 1000, 3000, and 4000-series scales, compared against XGBoost baselines and post-hoc exponential smoothing across three random seeds.

Result: The stability-aware model improves Forecast Stability Score over XGBoost by 6.91%, 6.66%, and 7.68% at the three scales respectively, while RMSE changes remain within 0.72%. Post-hoc exponential smoothing achieves lower raw movement but incurs larger RMSE degradation; the training-time regularization preserves more point accuracy and performs more favorably under normalized stability metrics.

Conclusion: Forecast evaluation should extend beyond point-error minimization to incorporate an accuracy-stability trade-off perspective. Training-time stability regularization offers a principled approach superior to post-hoc smoothing for operational retail forecasting pipelines.

Abstract: Retail demand forecasts are reused across replenishment, capacity, labor, and transportation planning cycles. Point-error objectives do not constrain abrupt movement between adjacent forecasts, while post-hoc smoothing acts only after model fitting. We ask whether a training-time penalty on consecutive within-series movement can improve horizontal forecast-path stability without materially changing point accuracy. The penalty is evaluated in a temporal-structured pipeline combining recent-demand embeddings with calendar, price, hierarchy, item, and store features. On selected M5 demand series at 1000, 3000, and 4000-series scales, the stability-aware hybrid model improves Forecast Stability Score over XGBoost by 6.91%, 6.66%, and 7.68%, respectively, while RMSE changes remain within 0.72% across three random seeds. Post-hoc exponential smoothing attains lower raw movement but incurs a larger RMSE cost; training-time regularization preserves more point accuracy and performs favorably under normalized stability. These findings extend forecast evaluation from point-error minimization toward an accuracy-stability trade-off perspective for operational retail forecasting.

</details>


### [104] [Agora: Collective and Permissionless Internet-Scale Pretraining of Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13332)
*Gil Avraham,Violetta Shevchenko,Hadi Mohaghegh Dolatabadi,Karol Pajak,James Snewin,Harry Xi,Rodney O'Donnell,Thalaiyasingam Ajanthan,Sameera Ramasinghe,Chamin Hewa Koneputugodage,Shamane Siriwardhana,Alexander Long*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Agora is a distributed training system that enables large language model pretraining over the internet using heterogeneous, volunteer-contributed consumer GPUs. It introduces Protocol Learning — collectively trained, collectively owned models where no single party holds the full weights. The system was validated with Pluralis-8B, an 8.6B-parameter model trained on 500B tokens by 330 contributor nodes over 40 days, achieving 63% of centralized H100 efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Frontier LLM training is monopolized by few organizations with massive homogeneous datacenters. An enormous pool of globally distributed, heterogeneous consumer and professional GPUs sits idle and unused for training due to fundamental systems challenges: preemptibility, low internet bandwidth, device heterogeneity, and lack of centralized coordination. Agora aims to democratize frontier training by making this compute usable.

Method: Agora combines three key techniques: (1) bandwidth-efficient pipeline-parallel model sharding designed for internet-grade links, where each participant holds only one stage of the model; (2) multi-party, fault-tolerant collective operations that handle node churn and preemption; and (3) asynchronous optimization for heterogeneous hardware. The Protocol Learning paradigm ensures no single party ever possesses the full model weights, enabling collectively owned models.

Result: The Pluralis-8B demonstration trained an 8.6B-parameter model on 500B tokens of FineWeb-Edu over 40 days with 330 contributor nodes (mostly consumer GPUs) joining and leaving throughout. The run sustained ~170k tokens/s and 4.2 tokens per TFLOP of pooled compute, achieving 63% of centralized H100 baseline efficiency, and converged close to a centralized reference run.

Conclusion: Agora demonstrates that internet-scale decentralized LLM pretraining is viable, achieving competitive efficiency relative to centralized clusters. Protocol Learning opens a path to open-source frontier training with economic sustainability by enabling collectively trained, collectively owned models on commodity hardware.

Abstract: Training large language models at the multi-billion to trillion parameter scale is confined to datacenters, where data-parallel (DP) and model-parallel (MP) techniques presume homogeneous accelerators, high-speed interconnects, and a single orchestrating entity. Frontier model development is thereby concentrated among the few groups able to assemble such clusters. Meanwhile, an enormous pool of compute remains unusable for training: consumer and professional GPUs that are heterogeneous, preemptible, individually owned, and connected only by the internet. We present Agora, a system that makes efficient use of this compute. Agora combines bandwidth-efficient pipeline-parallel model sharding over internet-grade links with multi-party, fault-tolerant collective operations. Each participant holds only one stage of the model, and no single party ever possesses the full weights. We term this setup Protocol Learning: it enables collectively trained, collectively owned models, opening a path to open-source frontier training with economic sustainability. This report presents the outcome of a research effort spanning communication-efficient parallelism, asynchronous optimization, and fault-tolerant systems design. It culminates in the first demonstration of its kind: Pluralis-8B, an open, permissionless pretraining run of an 8.6B-parameter model on 500B tokens of FineWeb-Edu. The model was trained over 40 days by 330 contributor nodes, predominantly consumer GPUs on internet connections, joining and leaving throughout. The run sustained ~170k tokens/s and 4.2 tokens per TFLOP of pooled compute, 63% of the efficiency of a centralized H100 baseline, and converged to within a small margin of a centralized reference run.

</details>


### [105] [Weight Feedback Computes the Jacobian Transpose Locally in Modern Deep Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13380)
*Junlong Shen,Xingyu Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes WF-Act-PC, a variant of Predictive Coding that replaces the non-local Jacobian-transpose used in error transport with fully local computations by exploiting layer structure (activation, normalization, and frozen statistics), achieving competitive performance with backpropagation on CIFAR and Tiny-ImageNet benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Eliminate the last non-local operation in Predictive Coding — the autograd Jacobian-transpose product used for routing errors between layers — to achieve a fully biologically plausible, locally computable learning rule while maintaining competitive accuracy.

Method: The authors factor the exact Jacobian-transpose for layers of the form f(x)=Act(Norm(L(x))) with frozen normalization statistics into three locally available terms: the weight transpose L^⊤, the activation derivative σ′(z), and the normalization gain s=γ/σ_run. Substituting this factored identity into Predictive Coding removes the autograd backward pass, yielding WF-Act-PC. Three assumptions are required: weight symmetry, soft spectral-norm control, and a nearest-neighbor MaxPool approximation.

Result: WF-Act-PC is the only PC method whose accuracy improves with depth. On CIFAR-10 (50 epochs, 5 seeds), it surpasses iPC by 2.7–22.3 pp. With per-architecture tuning, it matches or exceeds tuned backpropagation on deeper CIFAR-10 architectures (VGG-9: 93.57% vs 92.43%; ResNet-18: 92.76% vs 91.54%) and Tiny-ImageNet, though it trails BP on deeper CIFAR-100 VGG cells.

Conclusion: The Jacobian-transpose dependency in Predictive Coding can be largely avoided for a broad class of modern layers; restoring the normalization correction and activation derivative closes the transport gap that prior weight-feedback methods left open, enabling a fully local PC variant that scales effectively with depth and approaches backpropagation-level performance.

Abstract: Predictive Coding (PC) offers a biologically motivated alternative to backpropagation via local weight updates, yet routing error between layers still relies on an autograd Jacobian-transpose ($J^\top$) product - the last non-local operation in PC. We show that this dependency is largely avoidable. For any layer $f(x)=\mathrm{Act}(\mathrm{Norm}(L(x)))$ with frozen normalization statistics, the exact $J^\top$ factors into three locally available terms, $J^\top v = L^\top(s \odot σ'(z) \odot v)$, where $σ'$ is the activation derivative, $z$ is the pre-activation, and $s=γ/σ_{\mathrm{run}}$ is the normalization gain. Prior weight-feedback methods omitted both corrections; restoring them closes the transport gap for this layer class. Locality here holds up to three assumptions, which we state upfront: weight symmetry ($L^\top$ mirrors the forward operator, as assumed by all PC), a soft spectral-norm control that is not synapse-local, and a nearest-neighbour approximation for MaxPool. Substituting the identity into PC yields WF-Act-PC, which removes the autograd backward pass from error transport. On CIFAR-10/100 (50 epochs, 5 seeds), WF-Act-PC is the only PC method whose accuracy improves with depth, surpassing iPC - the strongest classical PC baseline - by 2.7-22.3 pp on CIFAR-10. With both methods tuned per architecture, it matches or exceeds a comparably-tuned backpropagation baseline on the deeper CIFAR-10 architectures (VGG-9: 93.57% vs. 92.43%; ResNet-18: 92.76% vs. 91.54%) and on the harder Tiny-ImageNet benchmark, while trailing tuned BP on the deeper CIFAR-100 VGG cells. Our WF-Act-PC implementation is publicly available at https://github.com/jlshen025/pcax

</details>


### [106] [Where Should RL Post-Training Compute Go? Model Size, Search, Learning, and Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13389)
*Patrick Wilhelm,Odej Kao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Under a fixed post-training compute budget, the optimal allocation among model size, training duration, rollout search, and reward-model inference changes with model scale, budget, and reward type. The paper introduces a FLOP-accounting framework and a diagnostic protocol (RACE) to characterize these trade-offs before committing to expensive validation runs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RL post-training for foundation models is typically reported with only a single total FLOP budget, obscuring critical allocation decisions. The paper aims to systematically characterize how constrained compute should be divided among policy size, training steps, rollout search breadth/depth, and reward-feedback computation, and to urge the community to report this breakdown transparently.

Method: The authors propose a FLOP-accounting framework that decomposes GRPO (Group Relative Policy Optimization) post-training compute into three components: rollout/search, policy-update/learning, and reward/feedback-model evaluation. They then conduct a grid search (RACE — Rollout–Allocation–Compute–Efficiency) over LoRA-adapted Qwen2.5 policies, varying model size, total FLOP budget, reward system (rule-based vs. PRM-style), and evaluation targets to map conditional allocation frontiers.

Result: Key findings: (1) optimal compute allocation is conditional on model size, budget, reward type, and downstream metric; (2) larger models consume more per-token FLOPs, reducing the number of updates or rollouts under the same budget, coupling model choice with allocation strategy; (3) rule-based rewards concentrate non-update compute on rollouts, whereas PRM-based rewards divert a visible fraction to reward-model inference; (4) RACE serves as a diagnostic protocol for identifying allocation regimes prior to full-scale validation.

Conclusion: There is no universally optimal allocation strategy for RL post-training under fixed compute budgets. The paper recommends that RL post-training publications report total FLOPs alongside a breakdown of how compute is distributed among model size, search, learning, and feedback, enabling more meaningful comparisons and efficient resource use.

Abstract: Reinforcement Learning (RL) post-training is increasingly used to adapt foundation models for reasoning, planning, and feedback-driven robot-learning pipelines, but constrained post-training resources are often summarized by a single total FLOP budget. We study the fixed-budget decision problem behind this practice: under the same post-training budget, should one use a larger policy, train a smaller policy longer, generate more rollout search, or spend compute on stronger reward feedback? We introduce a FLOP-accounting framework for GRPO post-training that decomposes compute into rollout/search, policy-update/learning, and reward- or feedback-model evaluation. Across LoRA-adapted Qwen2.5 policies, we find conditional allocation frontiers: the best observed allocation changes with model size, compute budget, reward system, and evaluation target. Same-FLOP model-size comparisons show that model choice and training allocation are coupled because larger policies consume more per-token compute and therefore buy fewer updates or rollouts under the same budget. Reward systems also change the accounting: rule-based rewards spend nearly all non-update compute on policy rollouts, while PRM-style feedback allocates a visible part of the budget to reward-model inference. We present RACE as a diagnostic pilot-grid protocol, not a guarantee of held-out improvement, for identifying allocation regimes before expensive validation runs; our results suggest that RL post-training papers should report total FLOPs together with how compute is divided among model size, search, learning, and feedback.

</details>


### [107] [Self-Improving is Often Sudden: Enlightenment-style Finetuning for Large-Scale Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13395)
*Jing-Xiao Liao,Tianwei Zhang,Yu-Hao Jiang,Feifei Zhang,Hang-Cheng Dong,Feng-Lei Fan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces "Enlightenment," a training-free post-tuning paradigm that unlocks latent capabilities in large models by modifying shortcuts (attention head-mixing for LLMs, scalar-modulated residual connections for VLMs) without weight updates, achieving significant performance gains across benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Inspired by the human brain's "aha moment" (sudden insight), the authors hypothesize that large pre-trained models possess latent capacities that can be suddenly unlocked. Existing training-free methods predominantly manipulate attention weights, but the authors argue that modifying shortcut pathways in key modules/layers can more effectively trigger this latent capability boost without any weight updates.

Method: The proposed "Enlightenment" paradigm modifies shortcuts in key modules without weight updates. Two instantiations are introduced: (1) For LLMs: attention head-mixing shortcuts that recalibrate attention weights by connecting the initial attention head's output to all other target heads, using an adaptive scaling factor initialization strategy. (2) For VLMs: a lightweight scalar-modulated factor applied to residual connections in decoder layers to regulate information flow. Both are architecture-specific and training-free.

Result: Extensive experiments demonstrate that Enlightenment efficiently unlocks latent potential in pre-trained networks, yielding remarkable performance improvements across diverse benchmarks and model types (both LLMs and VLMs).

Conclusion: Large models possess a latent "enlightenment" phenomenon — a capacity for sudden capability boosts. The proposed training-free post-tuning paradigm effectively triggers this phenomenon through targeted shortcut modifications, offering a computationally efficient alternative to fine-tuning for improving pre-trained model performance.

Abstract: The pursuit of autonomously self-improving models has attracted growing interest in the era of large-scale foundation models. Drawing inspiration from the concept of "enlightenment" or "aha moment" in human brain, we hypothesize that large models exhibit an analogous enlightenment phenomenon-a latent capacity for sudden capability boost. Then, we propose Enlightenment, a novel training-free post-tuning paradigm for large-scale models. Our approach modifies shortcuts for key modules/layers without weight updates, while existing training-free ones predominantly manipulate attention weights. We introduce two architecture-specific instantiations: i) For large language models, we propose attention head-mixing shortcuts that recalibrate attention weights by linking the initial attention head's output to all other target heads, modulated by an adaptive scaling factor initialization strategy. ii) For vision-language models, we apply a lightweight scalar-modulated factor to residual connections in the decoder layers, regulating information flow. Extensive experiments show that Enlightenment efficiently unlocks the latent potential of pre-trained networks, yielding remarkable performance improvements across diverse benchmarks and models.

</details>


### [108] [Is the Statistical Advantage Worth the Cost? An Empirical Comparison of KANs and MLPs for Structured Data Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13413)
*Matthew Steven P. Toledo,Justine Raphael H. Jacinto,Vivekjeet Singh Chambal,Rodolfo C. Camaclang,Jamlech Iram N. Gojo Cruz,Reginald Neil C. Recario*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: An empirical benchmark comparing KANs and MLPs on 12 tabular classification datasets finds KANs statistically outperform MLPs overall (medium effect size, d=-0.46), but at substantially higher computational cost, suggesting KANs for high-precision applications and MLPs for resource-constrained settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Growing interest in Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks (KANs) as an alternative function-approximating architecture to Multi-Layer Perceptrons (MLPs), with a need for rigorous empirical comparison on structured tabular classification tasks to guide architectural selection.

Method: Both KAN and MLP models were trained under standardized preprocessing, identical architecture, and fixed hyperparameter settings across 12 publicly available datasets covering binary, multiclass, multilabel, and ordinal classification problems. Evaluation used test accuracy, F1-Score, paired hypothesis testing, and effect size (Cohen's d) analysis.

Result: KANs statistically outperform MLPs in binary and multiclass domains and achieve a significant aggregate advantage across all datasets. The effect size is medium (d = -0.46). However, KANs incur substantially higher parameter counts and computational complexity relative to MLPs.

Conclusion: KANs are preferred for high-precision applications due to superior generalization via adaptive spline-based mappings. MLPs remain a robust, efficient choice for resource-constrained environments. Future work should extend benchmarking to additional data modalities.

Abstract: This study presents an empirical benchmarking comparison between Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks (KANs) and Multi-Layer Perceptrons (MLPs) on structured tabular classification tasks. Motivated by the growing interest in KANs as an alternative function-approximating architecture, we evaluate their out-of-the-box performance on twelve publicly available datasets spanning binary, multiclass, multilabel, and ordinal problems. Both models were trained under standardized preprocessing, architecture, and fixed hyperparameter settings, with performance assessed using test accuracy and F1-Score, paired hypothesis testing, and effect size analysis. Results show that KANs statistically outperform MLPs in binary and multiclass domains and achieve a significant aggregate advantage across all datasets. However, the observed medium effect size (d = -0.46) raises an important cost-benefit consideration: while KANs offer superior generalization through adaptive spline-based mappings, this advantage comes with substantially higher parameter and computational complexity relative to the MLP baseline. These findings suggest KANs are the preferred choice for high-precision applications, while MLPs remain a robust and efficient option for resource-constrained environments. Future work should extend this analysis to additional data modalities to further refine these architectural selection criteria.

</details>


### [109] [EXPLORE: Exploration with Guided Search for Analog Topology Generation using Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13416)
*Guanglei Zhou,Chen-Chia Chang,Yikang Shen,Jonathan Ku,Isaac Jacobson,Jingyu Pan,Yiran Chen,Xin Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EXPLORE integrates MCTS with transformer decoding for analog circuit topology generation, using simulator-guided search at test time to overcome one-shot generation limitations. It achieves 65% success rate vs. 12% (one-shot) and 33% (sampling-and-filter) on a 6-component benchmark at 0.01 tolerance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: One-shot sequence-to-sequence generation methods fail on complex analog circuits due to exponentially large search spaces and limited training data. A framework that can scale effectively at test time by strategically allocating simulator evaluations is needed.

Method: EXPLORE combines pretrained transformer language model decoding with Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS). The key insight is to bypass high-confidence structural tokens during search and allocate the expensive simulator budget only to topology-altering decisions, using simulator feedback to guide MCTS rollouts and node selection.

Result: On a 6-component benchmark at 0.01 tolerance: success rate improved from 12% (one-shot) and 33% (sampling-and-filter) to 65%; MSE reduced by over 20% compared to sampling-and-filter under equivalent search budget.

Conclusion: EXPLORE is the first framework to integrate structured test-time search with LM decoding for analog topology generation, representing a practical advance toward scalable LLM-driven design automation.

Abstract: Automating analog circuit topology design is essential to reduce the extensive manual effort required to meet increasingly diverse and customized application demands. Recent advances have applied sequence-to-sequence fine-tuning on pretrained language models to directly generate circuit topologies from user specifications in a single pass. However, these one-shot generation methods failed to generate complex circuits due to their exponentially growing search spaces and limited training datasets. In this paper, we present EXPLORE, a search-enhanced framework that integrates simulator-guided Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) with transformer-based decoding to enable test-time scaling for analog topology generation. By leveraging language-model priors and bypassing high-confidence structural tokens, EXPLORE allocates expensive simulator budget primarily toward topology-altering decisions during search. On a 6-component benchmark at a tight tolerance of 0.01, EXPLORE raises the success rate from 12% for one-shot generation and 33% for a sampling-and-filter baseline to 65%, and lowers MSE by over 20% relative to sampling-and-filter under the same search budget. These results establish EXPLORE as the first framework to integrate structured test-time search with LM decoding for analog topology generation, and a practical step toward scaling LLM-driven design automation.

</details>


### [110] [OrDA: Orthogonal Disentanglement of Access Habits Framework for Homepage Marketing Block Recommendations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13420)
*Lingxiao Zhang,Xiaobo Li,Tao Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: OrDA proposes a dual-tower framework with orthogonal regularization and gated allocation to disentangle content interest from access habits, then applies causal intervention at inference to rank items purely by interest, achieving 5.64% UCTR improvement in online A/B tests.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clicks on homepage marketing blocks reflect both genuine content interest and habitual access patterns. Habitual clicks create "Pseudo-Positives" where high-click items benefit from position advantage despite mediocre content quality, biasing the recommendation ecosystem and degrading user experience.

Method: OrDA employs a dual-tower architecture with a gated allocation layer that adaptively routes input features to separate interest and habit towers, minimizing interference. Orthogonal regularization is applied to constrain the latent manifolds of interest and habit to be geometrically perpendicular, ensuring rigorous disentanglement. At inference, causal intervention via do-calculus is performed to rank items solely by purified interest scores, blocking the spurious backdoor path through habits.

Result: Online A/B testing on the Zhima homepage marketing block and Zhima rent-floor recommendation shows a 5.64% improvement in user click-through rate (UCTR), with offline evaluations on large-scale datasets confirming OrDA outperforms state-of-the-art methods in predictive accuracy while effectively eliminating access-habit bias.

Conclusion: Causal disentanglement of interest and habit signals, enforced through orthogonal geometry and deployed via do-calculus intervention, provides an effective and practical solution for debiasing click-driven recommendation systems in commercial marketing environments.

Abstract: Clicks on homepage marketing blocks are driven by a dual-mechanism of content interest and access habits. However, habitual clicks often create Pseudo-Positives in marketing slots, where position advantage masks mediocre content quality, leading to biased recommendation ecosystems. We propose a framework called Orthogonal Disentanglement of Access habits (OrDA) to purify interest signals. OrDA utilizes a dual-tower structure with a gated allocation layer to adaptively route features and minimize interference. To ensure rigorous separation, we employ orthogonal regularization to constrain the latent interest and habit manifolds to be geometrically perpendicular. OrDA performs causal intervention (do-calculus) during inference to rank items solely by purified interest scores. Empirical online evaluations on large-scale datasets demonstrate that OrDA effectively eliminates access-habit bias, outperforming state-of-the-art methods in predictive accuracy. Online AB test 5.64% shows user click-through rates (UCTR) improvement on the Zhima homepage marketing block, Zhima rent-floor recommendation.

</details>


### [111] [Temperature Scaling Is Not Enough: Calibration Gaps Under Human Label Distributions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13423)
*Wisdom Dogah*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Temperature scaling, the standard post-hoc calibration method, assumes deterministic one-hot labels. This paper shows that when soft (distributional) labels reflecting genuine human disagreement are used instead, temperature scaling calibrated on hard labels consistently underperforms across all nine vision and language configurations tested, with the gap growing with model scale in most settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Temperature scaling's theoretical justification implicitly assumes one-hot, deterministic ground-truth labels. In practice, many real-world datasets contain soft labels from crowd-sourcing or distributional human annotations. The authors investigate whether temperature scaling retains calibration when this assumption is violated, and whether any degradation scales with model size.

Method: The authors evaluate three model scales per modality (vision and language) on CIFAR-10H and ChaosNLI — two datasets with human-annotated soft label distributions. They compare temperature scaling calibrated on hard (majority-vote) labels against an oracle calibrated directly on soft labels, measuring the Brier Score gap. Multiclass isotonic regression is used as a second post-hoc baseline. Training is conducted under both hard one-hot and soft distributional targets.

Result: A positive soft-label calibration gap exists in all nine configurations (Brier Score gaps: 0.002–0.134). The gap grows monotonically with model scale in vision and on SNLI-derived ChaosNLI splits. Language domain gaps (mean 0.079) are substantially larger than vision gaps (mean 0.003). A scale-ordering reversal on the MNLI-derived split persists after matched-domain training but is attributed to near-chance accuracy. Isotonic regression confirms the same qualitative pattern.

Conclusion: Calibration protocols relying on majority-vote labels systematically misstate model reliability when label ambiguity is structural. This has direct safety implications for deployment in settings where human disagreement is inherent rather than noise, such as medical diagnosis or content moderation.

Abstract: Temperature scaling is the dominant post-hoc calibration method in modern deep learning. Its theoretical justification rests on an assumption that is rarely stated explicitly: that ground-truth labels are one-hot and deterministic. In practice, labels are frequently soft, crowd-sourced, or genuinely distributional, reflecting real disagreement among human annotators rather than annotation noise. We study whether temperature scaling retains its calibration properties when this assumption is violated, and whether any resulting degradation depends on model scale. Using CIFAR-10H and ChaosNLI, two publicly available datasets with human-annotated soft label distributions, we evaluate three model scales per modality under both hard one-hot and soft distributional label targets. Across all nine configurations we find a positive soft-label calibration gap: temperature scaling calibrated on hard labels consistently underperforms an oracle calibrated directly on soft labels, with Brier Score gaps ranging from 0.002 to 0.134. The gap grows monotonically with model scale in the vision domain and on the SNLI-derived split of ChaosNLI, and is substantially larger in the language domain (mean gap 0.079) than in vision (mean gap 0.003). A scale-ordering reversal on the MNLI-derived split remains after matched-domain training; we treat it as inconclusive for the scale hypothesis and attribute it primarily to near-chance accuracy on that split. As a second post-hoc baseline, multiclass isotonic regression yields the same qualitative conclusion: positive soft-label gaps in all nine configurations, and larger gaps in language than in vision. These findings suggest that calibration protocols built on majority-vote labels systematically misstate model reliability wherever label ambiguity is structural, with direct consequences for deployment in safety-critical settings.

</details>


### [112] [Data-Efficient Adaptation of LLMs via Attention Head Reweighting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13425)
*Tuomas Oikarinen,Zixiao Chen,Charlotte Siska,Tsui-Wei Weng,Chandan Singh,Jianfeng Gao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AHR adapts LLMs to text classification tasks by learning a single scalar weight per attention head, achieving strong few-shot performance with ~0.0001% of model parameters—outperforming LoRA while being 200–1000× more parameter-efficient and offering interpretable insights into in-context learning mechanisms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In security and other data-scarce domains, labeled examples are limited. While LLMs show promise for data-efficient learning via parameter-efficient adaptation, they still struggle on difficult tasks with few samples. There is a need for an even more data-efficient and parameter-efficient adaptation method that can perform well under extreme few-shot constraints.

Method: Attention Head Reweighting (AHR): instead of updating full weight matrices or inserting adapters, AHR learns only a single scalar multiplier per attention head. This exploits the functional specialization of individual attention heads, drastically reducing trainable parameters. The learned scalars reweight each head's contribution to the final representation for the target classification task.

Result: On diverse open-source text classification datasets, AHR outperforms standard baselines like LoRA under few-shot settings, despite using 200–1000× fewer trainable parameters (modifying only ~0.0001% of the model). The learned head weights are also interpretable, revealing which attention heads drive in-context learning abilities.
