Conclusion: AHR demonstrates that leveraging attention head specialization via extremely sparse scalar reweighting is a viable and highly parameter-efficient strategy for few-shot adaptation of LLMs. It not only improves data efficiency but also provides a lens for interpreting model behavior, particularly in-context learning.

Abstract: Learning effectively from limited data is critical in domains like security where labeled examples are scarce. Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated some capabilities for data-efficient learning, especially through parameter-efficient adaptation methods, but continue to struggle when faced with few samples for difficult tasks. To meet this challenge, we propose Attention Head Reweighting (AHR), a data-efficient method that adapts LLMs to new text-classification tasks by learning only a single scalar per attention head. This drastically reduces the number of parameters that need to be learned by making use of the functional specialization of individual attention heads. Experiments on diverse open-source text classification datasets show that AHR can outperform standard baselines like LoRA when learning from limited samples, despite having 200-1000x fewer trainable parameters, as our AHR only modifies ~0.0001% of the model's parameters. In addition, our learned weights are easy to interpret and can be analyzed to better understand the mechanisms and attention heads responsible for in-context learning abilities in LLMs.

</details>


### [113] [PUe: Biased Positive-Unlabeled Learning Enhancement by Causal Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13428)
*Xutao Wang,Hanting Chen,Tianyu Guo,Yunhe Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A PU learning enhancement framework (PUe) that addresses selection bias in positive-unlabeled learning using normalized propensity scores and normalized inverse probability weighting, with theoretical guarantees and empirical validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing cost-sensitive PU learning methods rely on the Selected Completely At Random (SCAR) assumption, but real-world PU data often exhibits selection bias — positives and unlabeled examples are not uniformly labeled. The authors aim to relax this assumption and handle biased, non-uniform label distributions.

Method: Building on the SAR-PU propensity-weighted framework, PUe introduces: (1) a normalized inverse-probability-weighted PU risk formulation using normalized propensity scores; (2) theoretical analysis of normalized sample-weight error and common PU estimators under biased labeling; (3) regularized deep propensity-score estimation; (4) integration with modern cost-sensitive PU methods; and (5) support for selectively labeled negative classes.

Result: Experiments on MNIST, CIFAR-10, and ADNI demonstrate that PUe achieves consistent improvements over several PU baselines under non-uniform label distributions.

Conclusion: PUe effectively mitigates selection bias in PU learning through normalized propensity weighting, offering both theoretical rigor and practical gains across diverse datasets, including real-world medical imaging (ADNI).

Abstract: Positive-Unlabeled (PU) learning aims to achieve high-accuracy binary classification with limited labeled positive examples and numerous unlabeled ones. Existing cost-sensitive-based methods often rely on strong assumptions that examples with an observed positive label were selected entirely at random. In fact, the uneven distribution of labels is prevalent in real-world PU problems, indicating that most actual positive and unlabeled data are subject to selection bias. Building on the SAR-PU propensity-weighted framework of Bekker et al., we study a PU learning enhancement (PUe) framework using normalized propensity scores and normalized inverse probability weighting (NIPW). PUe's main contributions are a normalized inverse-probability-weighted PU risk formulation; additional theoretical analyses of normalized sample-weight error and common PU estimators under biased labeling; regularized deep propensity-score estimation; integration with modern cost-sensitive PU methods; and support for selectively labeled negative classes. Experiments on MNIST, CIFAR-10, and ADNI demonstrate improvements over several PU baselines under non-uniform label distributions.

</details>


### [114] [Discrete Diffusion Models: A Unified Framework from Tokenization to Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13431)
*Ye Yuan,Weien Li,Rui Song,Zeyu Li,Haochen Liu,Xiangyu Kong,Zixuan Dong,Linfeng Du,Zipeng Sun,Weixu Zhang,Jiaxin Huang,Changjiang Han,Yonghan Yang,Zichen Zhao,Xiuyuan Hu,Haolun Wu,Yankai Chen,Fengran Mo,Jikun Kang,Bowei He,Philip S. Yu,Xue Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a unified conceptual framework for discrete denoising diffusion models (DDMs), arguing that the construction of the discrete state space — including tokenization, vocabulary topology, and structural alphabets — is the fundamental design dimension that subsumes existing DDM formulations and reveals cross-cutting design trade-offs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Discrete DDMs have gained traction as alternatives to autoregressive models, but the field lacks a coherent organizing principle. Existing formulations (transition-matrix, masking, score-based) are typically treated as distinct approaches. The authors argue these are all manifestations of choices made in constructing the underlying discrete state space, and a unified framework is needed to systematically understand trade-offs and guide future research.

Method: The paper develops a conceptual framework that reinterprets discrete diffusion models through the lens of discrete state space construction. It identifies three key axes: the tokenization scheme (how data maps to discrete tokens), vocabulary topology (how tokens relate to one another), and domain-specific structural alphabets. The framework shows that existing DDM variants — transition-matrix, masking/absorbing-state, and score/ratio-based methods — are different instantiations within this common design space, and analyzes trade-offs across training objectives, inference algorithms, scaling, systems optimization, and evaluation.

Result: The framework reveals that all major discrete DDM formulations can be understood as specific choices in state space construction. It exposes common design tensions: the granularity-vs-semantics trade-off in tokenization, the locality-vs-expressivity trade-off in vocabulary topology, and the fidelity-vs-efficiency trade-off in inference. The analysis also identifies underexplored directions, including learnable tokenizers, non-Euclidean vocabularies, and adaptive noise schedules tailored to discrete structures.

Conclusion: Discrete state space construction is the central design dimension unifying discrete diffusion models. Explicitly treating it as a first-class design choice — rather than a preprocessing afterthought — opens promising research directions, including joint optimization of tokenization and diffusion, structured noise processes informed by domain-specific alphabets, and more principled evaluation protocols for discrete generative models.

Abstract: Discrete denoising diffusion models (DDMs) have recently emerged as a compelling alternative to autoregressive (AR) modeling for discrete data, offering parallel generation and iterative global refinement capabilities. Unlike continuous diffusion, where the state space is fixed, DDMs are fundamentally shaped by how the discrete state space is constructed: the tokenization scheme, the vocabulary topology, and domain-specific structural alphabets. This work introduces a unified conceptual framework that views discrete diffusion models through the construction of the underlying discrete state space. Within this framework, existing formulations, including transition-matrix, masking/absorbing-state, and score/ratio-based approaches, emerge as different instantiations of a common design space. The framework further exposes common design trade-offs across training objectives, inference algorithms, scaling behavior, systems optimization, and evaluation protocols, suggesting several promising directions for future research.

</details>


### [115] [Local Redundancy: An Information-Theoretic Measure of Plasticity from Synthetic Memorization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13432)
*Jiaxuan Cheng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces "local redundancy," an information-theoretic measure of neural network plasticity derived from universal compression theory. It proves that expected squared gradient norm on a synthetic memorization task provides a tractable lower bound, and shows this measure outperforms existing plasticity metrics (effective rank, dead neurons, weight norm) for predicting downstream continual/transfer learning performance and for pretraining checkpoint selection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing plasticity measures (effective rank, dead neuron fraction, weight norm) lack theoretical grounding and correlate poorly with actual performance on new tasks. A principled, theoretically justified measure is needed to reliably assess a network's capacity to adapt to new tasks, particularly for checkpoint selection during pretraining when validation loss plateaus.

Method: The authors define local redundancy using universal compression theory as the worst-case redundancy of a local model family — parameters within an infinitesimal neighborhood along gradient directions. Since exact computation is intractable, they prove that the expected squared gradient norm on a synthetic memorization task serves as an efficiently computable lower bound. This bound is then used as the practical plasticity measure in experiments.

Result: Experiments on continual image classification and time series transfer learning show that local redundancy (via its gradient norm lower bound) predicts downstream task performance more accurately than existing measures such as effective rank, dead neuron fraction, and weight norm. It also enables effective pretraining checkpoint selection even when validation loss has plateaued.

Conclusion: Local redundancy provides a theoretically grounded and practically effective measure of neural network plasticity. Its gradient norm lower bound offers a scalable proxy that outperforms heuristic alternatives, making it valuable for monitoring and selecting models during pretraining for improved transfer and continual learning outcomes.

Abstract: Plasticity -- a neural network's ability to adapt to new tasks -- is critical for continual and transfer learning. Existing measures, such as effective rank, dead neuron fraction, and weight norm, lack theoretical grounding and correlate poorly with performance on new tasks. We introduce local redundancy, an information-theoretic measure derived from universal compression theory. We define local redundancy as the worst-case redundancy of a local model family -- parameters in an infinitesimal neighborhood along gradient directions -- and show this is a principled measure of plasticity. Although local redundancy is intractable to compute exactly, we prove that the expected squared gradient norm on a synthetic memorization task provides an efficiently computable lower bound. Experiments on continual image classification and time series transfer learning demonstrate that local redundancy predicts downstream performance better than existing measures and enables pretraining checkpoint selection where validation loss plateaus.

</details>


### [116] [Distributionally Robust and Safe Imitation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13436)
*Ahmed Aboudonia,Naira Hovakimyan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A distributionally robust and safe imitation learning framework is proposed, combining Taylor Series Imitation Learning (TaSIL) for policy-induced distribution shifts with distributionally robust adaptive control for uncertainty-induced shifts, demonstrated on UAV navigation with safety constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Imitation learning suffers from performance degradation and safety risks due to distribution shifts. Two distinct types of shifts—policy-induced (from imperfect policy rollouts) and uncertainty-induced (from environmental uncertainty)—require a unified framework that jointly addresses both while enforcing safety constraints.

Method: The authors propose a unified framework integrating two components: (1) Taylor Series Imitation Learning (TaSIL) to mitigate policy-induced distribution shifts by leveraging higher-order Taylor expansions of the policy, and (2) distributionally robust adaptive control to handle uncertainty-induced shifts by optimizing worst-case performance over a distributional uncertainty set. Safety constraints are systematically incorporated into the IL optimization problem.

Result: The framework is validated on a UAV case study where the vehicle performs tasks in an uncertain environment while avoiding unsafe regions, demonstrating the effectiveness of the approach under both types of distribution shifts with safety guarantees.

Conclusion: The proposed distributionally robust and safe IL framework effectively addresses both policy-induced and uncertainty-induced distribution shifts while maintaining safety, offering a principled approach for safety-critical imitation learning applications.

Abstract: Imitation learning (IL) has achieved remarkable success in complex decision-making tasks. However, its performance is highly sensitive to distribution shifts, which can pose significant safety risks. We propose a distributionally robust and safe IL framework that explicitly addresses both policy-induced and uncertainty-induced distribution shifts. Our approach develops a unified framework leveraging Taylor Series Imitation Learning (TaSIL) to mitigate policy-induced shifts and distributionally robust adaptive control to handle uncertainty-induced shifts. This architecture enables the formulation of an IL problem that optimizes performance under distributional uncertainty while systematically accounting for safety constraints. We demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed approach on an unmanned aerial vehicle (UAV) case study where the UAV performs a task in an uncertain environment while avoiding unsafe regions.

</details>


### [117] [PQFA: Parallel Quantum Feature Augmentation of Fused Representations for Multimodal Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13466)
*Mingzhu Wang,Yun Shang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PQFA is a hybrid quantum-classical framework that uses shallow variational quantum circuits for parameter-efficient post-fusion feature augmentation in multimodal learning, consistently outperforming classical baselines while using ~10x fewer parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Post-fusion enhancement in multimodal learning remains underexplored compared to alignment and fusion methods. The authors investigate whether shallow variational quantum circuits can serve as an effective, parameter-efficient post-fusion augmentation strategy.

Method: PQFA extracts text and image features via frozen RoBERTa and ViT encoders, applies bidirectional cross-attention, attentive pooling, and adaptive gated fusion. The fused feature is amplitude-encoded into multiple parallel shallow variational quantum circuits. Measurement readouts are concatenated with the classical representation for final prediction. Evaluated on MM-IMDb and N24News with controlled comparisons (same encoders, fusion backbone, splits, projection dimension, and augmentation width).

Result: PQFA consistently outperforms both the fusion backbone without quantum augmentation and a width-matched MLP augmentation baseline, using ~2.2K parameters versus ~24.0K for MLP. Missing-modality experiments show improved robustness, especially under severe text degradation. Gains cannot be replicated by random feature mappings, increased classical width, or untrained quantum circuits. Quantum-state diagnostics reveal stable performance across simulated noise levels and distinct branch-specific state transformations.

Conclusion: PQFA is established as an effective, parameter-efficient strategy for post-fusion augmentation in hybrid quantum-classical multimodal learning, with evidence that the quantum circuits contribute meaningful, non-trivial feature transformations.

Abstract: Most multimodal learning methods improve how heterogeneous representations are aligned and fused, while post-fusion enhancement remains less explored. We propose Parallel Quantum Feature Augmentation (PQFA), a hybrid quantum-classical framework that applies multiple shallow variational quantum circuits to fused multimodal features. Text and image representations extracted by frozen RoBERTa and ViT encoders are processed through bidirectional cross-attention, attentive pooling, and adaptive gated fusion. The fused feature is then amplitude-encoded into parallel quantum circuits, whose measurement readouts are concatenated with the classical representation for prediction. We evaluate PQFA on MM-IMDb and N24News through controlled comparisons using the same encoders, fusion backbone, data splits, projection dimension, and augmentation output width. PQFA consistently outperforms both the fusion backbone without quantum augmentation and a width-matched MLP augmentation baseline, while using approximately 2.2K augmentation parameters compared with 24.0K for the MLP branch. Missing-modality experiments further show improved robustness when textual or visual inputs are incomplete, with particularly clear gains when the more informative textual modality is severely degraded. Controlled ablations and feature-space analyses indicate that the improvement cannot be reproduced by random feature mappings, increased classical width, or untrained quantum transformations. Quantum-state diagnostics additionally show stable predictive performance across the tested simulated noise levels and distinct branch-specific transformations of the encoded states. These results establish PQFA as an effective and parameter-efficient strategy for post-fusion augmentation in hybrid quantum-classical multimodal learning.

</details>


### [118] [Explainable Artificial Intelligence for Anomaly Detection in Banking Transactions: An Internal Audit Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13469)
*Anupa Lodhi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A paper proposing an explainable AI framework combining Isolation Forest for anomaly detection and SHAP for feature-level explanations, delivered via a Streamlit dashboard, to improve fraud detection in banking internal audits — achieving 0.91 precision and 0.88 recall.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional rule-based fraud detection systems suffer from high false-positive rates and lack output justification, limiting their usefulness for compliance teams in regulated banking environments where accountability and transparency are critical.

Method: An unsupervised Isolation Forest (iForest) model performs anomaly scoring on transaction data. A SHAP layer grounded in cooperative game theory provides transaction-level, feature-attributed explanations. These outputs are surfaced through a lightweight Streamlit dashboard designed for non-ML-expert audit professionals.

Result: The framework achieves 0.91 precision and 0.88 recall on a synthetic banking dataset, outperforming three unsupervised baselines. Expert feedback indicates that feature-level explanations measurably improve auditor confidence and decision quality.

Conclusion: The proposed XAI framework advances practical deployment of accountable, transparent AI in regulated financial settings by combining effective anomaly detection with interpretable, auditor-facing explanations.

Abstract: The banking sector increasingly relies on automated systems to monitor electronic transactions for signs of fraud, yet conventional rule-based approaches struggle with high false-positive rates and offer no justification for their outputs, limiting their utility for compliance teams. This paper introduces an Explainable Artificial Intelligence (XAI) framework tailored for banking transaction anomaly detection within internal audit workflows. An Isolation Forest (iForest) model performs unsupervised anomaly scoring, while a SHAP (SHapley Additive exPlanations) layer provides transaction-level, feature-attributed explanations grounded in cooperative game theory [8]. A lightweight Streamlit dashboard renders these outputs in a form accessible to audit professionals without machine learning expertise. Evaluation on a synthetic banking dataset yields 0.91 precision and 0.88 recall, outperforming three unsupervised baselines. Expert feedback confirms that feature-level explanations measurably improve auditor confidence and decision quality. The framework advances the practical deployment of accountable, transparent AI in regulated financial environments.

</details>


### [119] [DeepLoop: Depth Scaling for Looped Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13491)
*Shuzhen Li,Yifan Zhang,Jiacheng Guo,Quanquan Gu,Mengdi Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Looped Transformers reuse physical blocks to scale depth without increasing parameters, but this creates a "tied-depth" problem where shared weights accumulate gradients from repeated visits. The paper formalizes this via a perturbation bound with a visit-alignment coefficient κ_R, showing that stable training requires scaling the residual branch exponent from 1/4 to 1/2 as loop count grows. The proposed DeepLoop method applies corrected DeepNorm-style scaling and improves validation loss and downstream accuracy on GPT-2 scale looped language models once recurrent depth is activated.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In standard (untied) Transformers, each layer has its own parameters, so residual scaling rules like DeepNorm only need to account for nominal layer count. In looped (weight-tied) Transformers, the same physical block is visited multiple times, causing the same parameters to both contribute to and receive aggregated gradients from multiple forward-pass visits. Existing scaling rules ignore this tied-depth effect, leading to training instability when recurrent depth is activated. The authors aim to derive residual scaling rules that properly account for parameter revisitation.

Method: The authors formalize the tied-depth effect using a first-order perturbation analysis of the linearized forward pass. They introduce a visit-alignment coefficient κ_R that captures how correlated the inputs to a shared block are across different visits. The bound shows that when visits are decorrelated, the standard DeepNorm exponent (1/4) is recovered. However, in the conservative aligned regime, the exponent must increase to 1/2 as loop count grows. Based on this analysis, they propose DeepLoop, which uses Post-LN DeepNorm architecture with scaling parameters α = (2N)^{1/2} and β = (8N)^{-1/2}, where N is the unrolled depth (physical depth × loop count). They validate the method on GPT-2 small and medium-scale looped language models.

Result: DeepLoop is neutral (performs equivalently to baseline) when no physical block is revisited, confirming it does not harm non-looped settings. When recurrent depth is activated, DeepLoop improves both validation loss and downstream accuracy compared to unscaled or incorrectly scaled looped Transformers at GPT-2 small and GPT-2 medium scales.

Conclusion: Stable training of looped Transformers with recurrent depth requires residual scaling rules that account for parameter visits (tied-depth), not just nominal layer count. DeepLoop provides a theoretically grounded scaling recipe that enables effective use of recurrent depth in GPT-style language models.

Abstract: Looped Transformers scale sequential computation by applying a compact stack of physical blocks for multiple rounds, increasing unrolled depth without increasing stored parameters. This reuse changes the residual-scaling problem: in an untied Transformer, each residual branch receives and applies its own parameter update, whereas in a looped Transformer one shared update aggregates gradients from repeated visits and is read back by those same visits in the next linearized forward pass. We formalize this tied-depth effect through a first-order perturbation bound controlled by a visit-alignment coefficient $κ_R$. The bound recovers the DeepNorm exponent when visits decorrelate, but in the conservative aligned regime it requires the exponent to increase from $1/4$ to $1/2$ as loop count grows at fixed physical depth. The resulting method, \textbf{DeepLoop}, keeps the Post-LN DeepNorm architecture and sets $α=(2N)^{1/2}$ and $β=(8N)^{-1/2}$ for unrolled depth $N$. On GPT-style looped language models at GPT-2 small and GPT-2 medium scale, DeepLoop is neutral when no physical block is revisited and improves validation loss and downstream accuracy once recurrent depth is activated. These results show that stable recurrent depth requires residual scaling rules that account for parameter visits, not only nominal layer count.

</details>


### [120] [A VAE-Driven Multi-Task Satellite-Aided Semantic Communication Framework for 6G-Enabled Connected Autonomous Vehicles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13494)
*S. M. Abtahiul Alam,Niloy Das,Apurba Adhikary,Yu Qiao,Zhu Han,Choong Seon Hong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A VAE-based multi-task semantic communication framework for satellite-assisted autonomous driving that achieves 87–98% bandwidth reduction while maintaining robust traffic sign reconstruction and classification across varying SNR conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional communication systems transmit raw data regardless of task relevance, wasting scarce uplink bandwidth and suffering from large propagation losses in satellite channels. Semantic communication, which transmits only task-relevant features, offers a solution for bandwidth-efficient, reliable, low-latency 6G-connected autonomous vehicle networks.

Method: A Variational Autoencoder (VAE)-based multi-task semantic communication framework is proposed. Unlike deterministic autoencoders, it uses probabilistic latent representations for robust encoding. Learned features are transmitted over noisy wireless channels, and the framework is trained end-to-end to jointly optimize both traffic sign reconstruction and classification tasks.

Result: The proposed approach achieves bandwidth reduction of 87.23% to 98.17% compared to conventional raw-data transmission, while maintaining stable performance across varying signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) conditions.

Conclusion: VAE-based probabilistic semantic encoding is highly effective for multi-task satellite-assisted autonomous driving, delivering dramatic bandwidth savings without sacrificing task performance, making it well-suited for resource-constrained 6G vehicular networks.

Abstract: The development of smart transportation systems and the introduction of 6G wireless communication technologies have significantly changed vehicle network topologies. Future connected autonomous vehicle (CAV) networks require bandwidth-efficient, reliable, and low-latency communication for safety-critical applications such as traffic sign recognition and decision-making. Conventional communication systems transmit raw data regardless of task relevance, which is inefficient in resource-constrained satellite channels where uplink bandwidth is scarce and propagation losses are large. Semantic communication addresses this limitation by transmitting task-relevant information instead of full signal representations. It extracts and conveys essential semantic features and leverages deep learning to optimize task performance at the receiver. Therefore, we present a Variational Autoencoder (VAE)-based multi-task semantic communication framework for satellite-assisted autonomous driving. Unlike deterministic autoencoder-based methods, the proposed model uses probabilistic latent representations for more robust and efficient encoding. The learned features are transmitted over noisy wireless channels to perform traffic sign reconstruction and classification. The framework is trained end-to-end to jointly optimize both tasks. Results show that the proposed approach achieves significant bandwidth reduction of up to 87.23\% to 98.17\% while maintaining stable performance across varying signal-to-noise ratio conditions.

</details>


### [121] [Factorized Spectral Representations for Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13498)
*Junyi Wu,Dan Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FaStR factorizes the transition kernel as a three-mode tensor using CP decomposition, yielding separate state, action, and next-state encoders via a noise contrastive objective. This reduces sample complexity for representation learning and enables zero-shot transfer of the state encoder across actuator changes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Spectral representation methods for RL view the transition kernel as a matrix (states×actions vs. next states) and learn a low-rank factorization. The authors argue this is suboptimal: the transition kernel is naturally a three-mode tensor, and exploiting this structure via CP decomposition yields a more compact hypothesis class with better sample efficiency and transfer properties.

Method: FaStR models the transition kernel as a three-mode tensor over states, actions, and next states and fits a CP decomposition using a noise contrastive objective. This produces three separate encoders — one for states, one for actions, and one for next states — that collectively form a single spectral representation of the transition dynamics.

Result: The factored parameterization reduces the sample size required for representation learning by a factor scaling with the smaller of the state and action dimensions. Empirically, FaStR shows the strongest improvements on high-dimensional locomotion tasks where dynamics match the factored structure. The learned state encoder transfers without retraining when the actuator changes, requiring only the action encoder to be retrained.

Conclusion: Leveraging the three-mode tensor structure of the transition kernel via CP decomposition improves sample efficiency and enables modular transfer, making FaStR a promising approach for representation learning in continuous control RL.

Abstract: Learning a compact model of the world from interaction data is central to sample-efficient deep reinforcement learning. Spectral representation methods have become the leading paradigm for representation learning in continuous control by taking a matrix view of the transition kernel, with state-action pairs on one side and next states on the other, and learning a low-rank factorization through self-supervised contrastive objectives. We take this view one step further. The transition kernel is naturally a three-mode tensor over states, actions, and next states, and a CP decomposition gives one feature map per mode. We propose FaStR, which fits this decomposition with a noise contrastive objective, producing separate state, action, and next-state encoders that together form a single spectral representation. The factored form yields a smaller hypothesis class, and the sample size needed for representation learning shrinks by a factor that scales with the smaller of the state and action dimensions. Empirically, FaStR delivers its largest gains on high-dimensional locomotion tasks whose dynamics align with the factored structure, and the learned state encoder transfers intact across actuator shift while only the action encoder is retrained.

</details>


### [122] [CDS: Counterfactual Directionality Score for Structured Interventions in Spatial Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13508)
*Humaira Anzum,Md Ishtyaq Mahmud,Jagan Mohan Reddy Dwarampudi,Tania Banerjee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces a framework for estimating directional influence between node populations in spatial graphs using structured counterfactual interventions. It trains a Neighbor Influence Model (NIM) to predict node states from local neighborhoods, then applies constrained interventions to compute a Counterfactual Directionality Score (CDS) that quantifies directional effects. A core-level bootstrap provides valid uncertainty estimates. The method recovers known directional structure in synthetic data and yields biologically plausible results on spatial transcriptomics data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing graph-based methods (attention, attribution, correlation) capture associations but lack a principled framework for assessing directional causal effects under controlled perturbations, particularly in spatial biological systems where cell-cell interactions drive functional outcomes.

Method: The authors propose a Neighbor Influence Model (NIM) trained to predict node states from local neighborhood compositions. They apply constrained interventions that modify neighborhood composition (e.g., adding/removing specific node types) while preserving spatial and structural properties. The Counterfactual Directionality Score (CDS) is defined as the change in predicted node state induced by such targeted perturbations, with theoretical interpretation as a finite-difference measure of local intervention sensitivity. A core-level bootstrap procedure is introduced to generate valid uncertainty estimates accounting for within-core spatial dependencies.

Result: On synthetic spatial graphs with known directional structure, CDS successfully recovers ground-truth directional influence, remains well-calibrated under null (no-effect) conditions, and is robust to confounding signals. Preliminary application to spatial transcriptomics data reveals biologically plausible and consistent cell-type interactions across tissue cores.

Conclusion: The proposed counterfactual intervention framework provides a principled, robust method for quantifying directional influence between node populations in spatial graphs, with promising applicability to spatial transcriptomics and other graph-based biological modeling tasks.

Abstract: Quantifying directional influence between node populations is a fundamental problem in graph-based modeling, particularly in spatial biological systems where cell-cell interactions shape functional outcomes. Existing approaches based on attention, attribution, or correlation capture associations but do not provide a principled framework for evaluating directional effects under controlled perturbations. We introduce a framework for structured counterfactual interventions in graph-based models to estimate directional influence between node types. Our approach trains a Neighbor Influence Model (NIM) to predict node states from local neighborhoods and applies constrained interventions that modify neighborhood composition while preserving key spatial and structural properties. We define the Counterfactual Directionality Score (CDS), which measures the change in predicted node state induced by targeted perturbations, and provide a theoretical interpretation of CDS as a finite-difference measure of local intervention sensitivity. To obtain valid uncertainty estimates, we introduce a core-level bootstrap procedure that accounts for dependencies within spatial samples. Experiments on synthetic spatial graphs with known directional structure show that CDS recovers directional influence, remains well calibrated under null conditions, and is robust to confounding signals, while preliminary results on spatial transcriptomics data reveal biologically plausible and consistent interactions across tissue cores.

</details>


### [123] [ExTernD: Expanded-Rank Ternary Decomposition Ternary LLM PTQ with Accuracy Approaching Any Quantization Level](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13511)
*Chethan Reddy G. P*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ExTernD introduces a post-training factorization that decomposes LLM weight matrices into two ternary matrices and a diagonal scale vector, using an expanded inner rank to iteratively correct quantization error. The method can approach bf16 accuracy arbitrarily closely, achieves Q4_K-level accuracy at ~5.2-5.5 effective bits per weight, and scales continuously with memory/compute budget.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing ternary quantization schemes are limited by a fixed number of planes (rank-1 ternary components), which caps their representational capacity and cannot approach full-precision accuracy arbitrarily closely. The authors seek a ternary decomposition method where accuracy can be continuously traded against compute/memory, without being constrained to discrete bit-widths.

Method: Each weight matrix A (m×n) is factorized as B·diag(D)·C, where B and C are ternary matrices {-1,0,+1} of shape m×k and k×n, and D is a real-valued scale vector of length k. The inner rank k = μ·min(m,n) is expanded beyond full rank (μ > 1). The factorization is computed greedily: at each step, the best rank-1 ternary component is selected to minimize the Frobenius norm of the residual. Components beyond full rank correct quantization errors from earlier components. Sparsity is controlled via a threshold τ on scale magnitudes, and importance weighting optionally prioritizes salient weights.

Result: The authors prove the residual decreases monotonically in k and can be driven below any ε > 0. On Gemma-4-E2B and Qwen3.5-4B, ExTernD matches Q4_K per-matrix accuracy at 5.2-5.5 effective bpw (5.1-5.5 with importance weighting). A full Qwen3.5-4B conversion at μ=3 achieves 10.10 wikitext-2 perplexity vs. 9.78 for bf16 (+3.2%), placing it near the Q4_K/Q5_K accuracy band at ~5.7 effective bpw.

Conclusion: ExTernD demonstrates that ternary decomposition with expanded rank can bridge the gap between low-bit quantization and full-precision accuracy, offering a continuous accuracy-efficiency trade-off rather than discrete bit-width steps. This makes it a competitive alternative to traditional group-wise quantization schemes like Q4_K/Q5_K.

Abstract: We introduce ExTernD (Expanded-rank Ternary Decomposition), a post-training factorization of each LLM weight matrix $A \in \mathbb{R}^{m \times n}$ into $A \approx B \mathrm{diag}(D) C$ with ternary factors $B \in \{-1,0,+1\}^{m \times k}$, $C \in \{-1,0,+1\}^{k \times n}$ and a real scale vector $D \in \mathbb{R}^k$. The inner rank $k = μ\min(m,n)$ is deliberately expanded beyond full rank ($μ> 1$), so that components past full rank correct the quantization error of earlier ones. We prove the residual decreases monotonically in $k$ and can be driven below any $\varepsilon > 0$: ExTernD approaches bf16 accuracy arbitrarily closely, which no ternary scheme with a fixed plane count can do. Memory and compute scale continuously with $μ$, and factor sparsity continuously with a threshold $τ$, so an accuracy target is hit exactly rather than rounded to the next bit-width. ExTernD matches Q4_K's per-matrix accuracy at 5.2-5.5 effective bpw (5.1-5.5 with importance weighting) on Gemma-4-E2B and Qwen3.5-4B, and a full Qwen3.5-4B conversion at $μ= 3$ reaches 10.10 wikitext-2 perplexity against 9.78 for bf16 (+3.2%), placing it near the Q4_K/Q5_K accuracy band at ~5.7 effective bpw.

</details>


### [124] [Clustering algorithms for multivariate wind farm SCADA data filtering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13544)
*Nicolò Italiano,Vasilis Pettas,Tuhfe Göçmen,Nicolaos A. Cutululis*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper compares clustering algorithms for automated filtering of SCADA wind farm data, evaluating their accuracy against manual filtering using novel metrics for unlabeled data. Applied to three offshore turbines, cluster-based methods generally outperform manual filtering in detecting both obvious and subtle outliers, though expert oversight remains necessary.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: SCADA systems collect vast amounts of operational data, including anomalies, transients, and non-standard operating modes. For many downstream applications, only normal-operation measurements are needed, requiring data filtering. Manual filtering by experts through visual inspection is time-consuming and subjective, motivating the need for automated, scalable filtering methods.

Method: Multiple clustering algorithms are applied to 10-minute SCADA statistics across multiple data channels from three turbines in an offshore wind farm. The dataset includes typical anomalies plus numerous non-evident outliers from field tests. The authors introduce evaluation metrics suitable for unlabeled data, robust across applications, and compare filtering accuracy against manual expert filtering. Feature selection extends beyond the traditional power curve.

Result: Cluster-based methods generally achieve higher accuracy than manual filtering, detecting both evident and subtle outliers. However, accuracy and data retention vary considerably across models. Extending analysis beyond the power curve in both feature selection and evaluation metrics proves critical. Expert involvement is reduced but still required.

Conclusion: Clustering-based automated filtering is a viable alternative to manual SCADA data filtering, but no single model fits all cases. The authors provide recommendations for model calibration generalization across datasets and discuss use cases for each model, emphasizing the need to go beyond power-curve-based analysis.

Abstract: During wind farm operation, Supervisory Control and Data Acquisition (SCADA) systems record numerous anomalies, transients, and specific operational modes, leading to large datasets. However, for a wide range of applications, only measurements corresponding to normal operation are required and, therefore, the SCADA data must be filtered. For this purpose, several methods have been proposed to automate and replace manual filtering conducted by experts via visual inspection of the data. In this paper, we compare the filtering accuracy of multiple clustering algorithms against manual filtering, introducing evaluation metrics that are suitable for unlabeled data and robust across potential applications. Based on the results, we provide recommendations for generalizing model calibration to different datasets and discuss potential use cases for each model. The models are applied to the SCADA data of three turbines of an existing offshore wind farm, using 10-minute statistics across multiple data channels. In addition to the anomalies and operational modes typically recorded, the dataset presents a large number of non-evident outliers due to several field tests. Overall, the results highlight the importance of extending the analysis beyond the power curve, both in feature selection and in the design of evaluation metrics. In most cases, cluster-based methods are able to detect both evident and subtle outliers, achieving higher accuracy than manual filtering. However, the accuracy and the amount of data retained vary considerably depending on the model, and expert involvement remains necessary, though to a reduced extent compared to manual filtering.

</details>


### [125] [From Novice to Expert: Cost-Aware Bandits for Evolving Worker Performance in Crowdsensing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13546)
*Yin Huang,Qingsong Liu,Jie Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses budget-constrained online worker recruitment in mobile crowdsensing where workers' sensing quality improves with experience before saturating, and costs are unknown. The authors model it as a structured bandit with evolving rewards and develop a cost-aware learning framework that jointly learns reward trajectories, detects saturation, and optimizes budget allocation, providing theoretical guarantees and empirical validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing learning-based mobile crowdsensing recruitment methods unrealistically assume stationary sensing quality (fixed mean) and known costs. In practice, workers improve with experience before stabilizing, and costs vary due to device/context states. This gap motivates a more realistic model that captures experience-driven performance dynamics and unknown heterogeneous costs under budget constraints.

Method: The problem is formulated as a structured bandit model where each worker's expected reward follows an unknown increasing-then-converging function of participation count, with unknown expected costs. A cost-aware online learning framework is proposed that jointly: (1) learns evolving reward trajectories, (2) estimates heterogeneous costs, (3) detects performance saturation points, and (4) allocates limited budget to maximize long-term sensing utility. Theoretical performance guarantees (regret bounds) are derived.

Result: Extensive experiments demonstrate consistent improvements over baselines that ignore experience-driven dynamics or assume known costs. The proposed approach effectively balances exploration and exploitation under budget constraints, learning both reward evolution and cost structures to achieve superior long-term sensing utility.

Conclusion: Modeling experience-driven performance improvement with eventual saturation, combined with unknown heterogeneous costs, leads to more effective budget-constrained online worker recruitment in mobile crowdsensing. The cost-aware structured bandit framework provides both theoretical rigor and practical gains over simplified alternatives.

Abstract: Mobile crowdsensing (MC) recruits mobile users to perform sensing tasks using their smartphones, enabling large-scale applications such as traffic monitoring and environmental sensing. A fundamental challenge is online worker recruitment under uncertainty, where the platform must learn workers' sensing performance while operating with a limited budget. Existing learning-based MC recruitment methods typically assume that each worker's sensing quality is stationary with a fixed mean over time. In practice, however, worker performance often improves with experience and eventually stabilizes, while the incurred sensing cost can be unknown in advance due to time-varying device and context states. In this paper, we study a budget-constrained online recruitment problem in which the platform selects one worker in each round, observes the sensing quality and incurred cost, where the expected sensing quality of each worker increases with experience and eventually converges to a plateau, and repeats until the budget is exhausted. We formulate this problem as a structured bandit model where each worker's expected reward evolves according to an unknown increasing-then-converging function of its participation count, and each worker has an unknown expected cost. We develop a cost-aware online learning framework that jointly learns evolving reward trajectories and heterogeneous costs, detects performance saturation, and allocates the limited budget to maximize long-term sensing utility. We provide theoretical performance guarantees and validate the proposed approach through extensive experiments, demonstrating consistent improvements over baselines that ignore experience-driven dynamics or assume known costs.

</details>


### [126] [Structured Reinforcement Learning for Bayesian Persuasion : Application to Intelligent Interactive Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13576)
*Merlin Paul,Anup Aprem*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes an online structured reinforcement learning framework for Bayesian persuasive driving, where a lead vehicle strategically reveals traffic information to guide a connected vehicle's lane-selection decisions. The key algorithm, Supermodular Q-learning for Principal (SQP), exploits supermodular structure for computational efficiency and achieves 30% cost improvement over existing methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In interactive driving scenarios, a lead vehicle with real-time traffic data can influence connected vehicles' route choices. However, designing an optimal signaling strategy is computationally challenging because the agent is farsighted and responds strategically to maximize its own long-term reward. Existing methods lack computationally efficient solutions for this Bayesian persuasion problem with sequential, partially observable decision-making.

Method: The authors frame the problem as Bayesian persuasion with a principal (lead vehicle) and a far-sighted agent (connected vehicle). They propose: (1) MAPL, a structured policy learning algorithm for monotonic agents with approximate best response; (2) identification of sufficient conditions for supermodularity of the principal's Q-function; (3) conditions ensuring persuasiveness of the signaling strategy; and (4) SQP (Supermodular Q-learning for Principal), which exploits supermodular structure in the principal's action-value function to enable computationally efficient signaling strategy synthesis.

Result: Numerical analysis in a real-time Bayesian persuasive driving application for lane selection demonstrates that the proposed SQP method achieves 30% greater cost efficiency in optimizing travelling rewards for both the lead and connected vehicles compared to existing signaling strategy design methodologies.

Conclusion: Exploiting the supermodular structure of the principal's Q-function enables computationally efficient synthesis of persuasive signaling strategies for far-sighted agents. The SQP framework offers a practical and effective solution for interactive driving and potentially other Bayesian persuasion domains involving sequential decision-making.

Abstract: Interactive driving, wherein an intelligent lead vehicle equipped with real-time traffic data coordinates route choices of connected vehicles, offers a promising approach to dynamic traffic management. To address the challenge of harmonising decisions, this paper considers the strategic information revealing framework of Bayesian persuasion. Here, the principal (lead vehicle) aims to guide the agent's (connected vehicle) partially observable sequential decision making towards its own objectives by selectively revealing information, such as real-time traffic ahead, using signals. However, the agent's farsighted response to maximize its long-term reward, renders the principal's signaling strategy design computationally challenging. We propose an online structured reinforcement learning framework to synthesize computationally efficient signaling strategy which is persuasive for a far-sighted agent. The main contributions of the paper are as follows: (i) For a monotonic agent with approximate best response, we propose MAPL, a structured policy learning algorithm for faster online learning, (ii) Identification of sufficient conditions for the supermodular structure of the Q function of the principal for a monotonic agent, (iii) Identification of sufficient conditions to ensure the persuasiveness of the principal's signaling strategy, (iv) Supermodular Q learning for Principal (SQP), which leverages the supermodular structure of principal's action value to synthesize computationally efficient signaling strategy that is persuasive for a monotonic learning agent, (v) Numerical analysis considering a real-time application of Bayesian persuasive driving for lane selection demonstrates that the proposed method is 30% cost efficient for optimising travelling rewards of both the lead and connected vehicle compared to the existing methodologies for signaling strategy design.

</details>


### [127] [The SIGReg Objective as Variational Free Energy: A Theoretical Active-Inference Account of JEPA World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13612)
*Fabio Arnez,Alexandra Gomez-Villa*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper establishes a formal correspondence between Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures (JEPAs) and Active Inference (AIF), showing that a JEPA's training objective becomes a valid variational free energy depending on the choice of anti-collapse regulariser. It organizes four regularisers (VICReg, LogDet, PairDist, SIGReg) into an entropy-estimator hierarchy based on a prior-miscalibration gap, proves that only lower-bound or gap-eliminating estimators preserve the AIF surprise bound, and demonstrates that under SIGReg with isotropic-Gaussian embeddings the objective becomes an exact information bottleneck with the latent goal cost serving as an exact proxy for AIF pragmatic value.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: JEPAs dominate latent world model design but lack normative theoretical justification, being validated primarily by empirical performance. The authors seek to provide a principled bridge between JEPA training objectives and Active Inference variational free energy, thereby grounding JEPAs in a first-principles framework.

Method: The authors analytically map JEPA components (prediction loss, embedding regulariser) onto AIF variational free energy terms. They classify four non-contrastive regularisers into a hierarchy based on whether each overestimates or underestimates latent entropy relative to a prior (the prior-miscalibration gap). They then prove a correspondence theorem under a constant-noise encoder model, deriving the conditions under which the objective reduces to an exact information bottleneck and the surprise bound is preserved. Extensions cover multi-step expected free energy, ensemble epistemic value, and learned-policy regimes. All algebraic results are machine-verified in Lean 4.

Result: Key findings: (1) VICReg and LogDet produce unsafe upper bounds on latent entropy, breaking the AIF surprise bound; (2) PairDist provides a safe lower bound; (3) SIGReg eliminates the gap entirely. Under SIGReg with isotropic-Gaussian embeddings, the gap vanishes, the objective becomes an exact information bottleneck, and the latent goal cost becomes an exact proxy for AIF pragmatic value. VICReg leaves an irreducible second-order anisotropy term. The authors also identify the state-epistemic value (a future-state coverage signal) as the one AIF term no current JEPA world model computes.

Conclusion: The relationship between JEPAs and AIF is determined by the sign of the prior-miscalibration gap induced by the regulariser. SIGReg uniquely satisfies all conditions for a valid AIF variational free energy. The identified missing term — state-epistemic value — represents a concrete, testable prediction for future empirical work.

Abstract: Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures (JEPAs) are the dominant design for latent world models, yet they are usually justified by empirical performance rather than a normative principle. We show that the choice of anti-collapse regulariser determines whether a JEPA's training objective, a prediction loss plus a weighted embedding regulariser, is a valid Active Inference (AIF) variational free energy. We organise four non-contrastive regularisers (VICReg, LogDet, PairDist, and SIGReg) into an entropy-estimator hierarchy indexed by a prior-miscalibration gap, and show that the gap's sign, whether the estimator bounds the latent entropy from above or below, decides whether the AIF surprise bound survives: VICReg and LogDet are unsafe upper bounds, PairDist a safe lower bound, and SIGReg eliminates the gap. We then prove a correspondence theorem: under the standard constant-noise encoder model and successful SIGReg enforcement (isotropic-Gaussian embeddings), the gap vanishes, the objective becomes an exact information bottleneck, the surprise bound is preserved, and the latent goal cost becomes an exact proxy for AIF pragmatic value, whereas VICReg leaves an irreducible second-order anisotropy term. We extend the correspondence to multi-step expected free energy, ensemble epistemic value, and a learned-policy regime, and we identify the one AIF term no current JEPA world model computes: the state-epistemic value, a future-state coverage signal. The predictions differ in kind, not degree, and are stated here as theoretical consequences left for empirical test in separate work; full proofs are in Appendix A, and the algebraic core of every result is machine-verified in Lean 4 (Appendix D).

</details>


### [128] [FastCentNN: Accelerating Centroid Neural Network with Entropy Proxy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13613)
*Le-Anh Tran*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FastCentNN accelerates the Centroid Neural Network (CentNN) by replacing strict local stabilization with an early splitting strategy based on total centroid movement per epoch, achieving comparable clustering quality with up to 16% runtime reduction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CentNN suffers from prolonged low-movement training phases before model expansion because centroid splitting is only triggered after strict local stabilization, leading to training inefficiency. FastCentNN addresses this by introducing an early splitting strategy.

Method: FastCentNN uses total centroid movement per epoch as a training entropy proxy to trigger early centroid splitting, supporting both absolute and stage-relative movement thresholds for fixed or adaptive splitting criteria, while preserving the original winner-loser learning dynamics.

Result: On benchmark datasets, FastCentNN achieves clustering quality comparable to CentNN with up to 16% runtime reduction on synthetic 2D datasets and approximately 5% on high-dimensional datasets.

Conclusion: FastCentNN is a practical, efficient drop-in replacement for CentNN that retains its online adaptive learning behavior while offering a configurable speed-stability trade-off via interpretable splitting thresholds.

Abstract: Centroid neural network (CentNN) is an unsupervised competitive learning algorithm in which centroid splitting is triggered only after strict local stabilization, often leading to prolonged low-movement training phases before model expansion. This report proposes FastCentNN, an accelerated variant that addresses this inefficiency by introducing an early splitting strategy based on the total centroid movement per epoch, which serves as a training entropy proxy. As a result, FastCentNN reduces unnecessary reassignment epochs while preserving the original winner-loser learning dynamics. FastCentNN supports both absolute and stage-relative movement thresholds, allowing the splitting criterion to remain either fixed or adaptive throughout training. Experiments on some benchmark datasets show that FastCentNN consistently achieves clustering quality comparable to CentNN while reducing runtime by up to 16% on synthetic 2D datasets and about 5% on high-dimensional datasets. FastCentNN therefore provides a practical and efficient drop-in replacement for CentNN, retaining its online adaptive learning behavior while offering a simple and interpretable speed-stability trade-off through configurable splitting thresholds.

</details>


### [129] [How the Hessian-Spectrum of Neural Networks Depends on Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13631)
*Jasraj Singh,Enea Monzio Compagnoni,Antonio Orvieto*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper derives exact eigenvalues of the Hessian for linear networks of arbitrary width/depth with arbitrary datasets, revealing that sharpness in MSE classification is directly tied to the maximum class proportion. Empirical results show these predictions remain robust even when relaxing assumptions and adding nonlinearities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior Hessian analysis in deep learning has been either purely empirical or theoretically limited to overly simplified settings (shallow networks, restricted data distributions). A rigorous theoretical understanding of Hessian spectra for general architectures and datasets is lacking, particularly regarding what controls sharpness in practical training scenarios.

Method: The authors analytically derive the eigenvalue spectrum of the Hessian matrix for linear feedforward networks with arbitrary depth and width, using MSE loss, on datasets with arbitrary numbers of samples, features, and labels. The derivation links sharpness (largest Hessian eigenvalue) to the maximum proportion of samples in any single class for classification tasks. Empirical validation is conducted by systematically removing simplifying assumptions and introducing ReLU nonlinearities.

Result: For classification with MSE loss, the sharpness of the solution equals the maximum proportion of samples belonging to any class. This relationship holds robustly even when batch normalization, weight decay, cross-entropy loss, and nonlinear activations are introduced, demonstrating broader practical applicability.

Conclusion: The theoretical characterization of Hessian eigenvalues for deep linear networks provides a principled link between dataset class imbalance and loss landscape sharpness. The robustness of these predictions under practical relaxations suggests the results offer meaningful insight for real-world deep learning optimization and generalization.

Abstract: The Hessian matrix is an important quantity of interest when it comes to studying the loss landscape and optimization dynamics in deep learning, as well as designing measures of generalization, second-order learning algorithms, etc. Prior works have focused on empirical results or pursued a theoretical treatment under overly simplified settings. In this work, we derive the eigenvalues of the Hessian of linear networks with arbitrary widths and depths, and datasets with an arbitrary number of samples, features, and labels. Importantly, for classification tasks with MSE loss, we identify that the sharpness of the solution is directly related to the maximum proportion of samples belonging to any class. We empirically validate our predictions and systematically analyze the effects of shedding the impractical assumptions one at a time, as well as incorporating nonlinearities. We observe that our predictions are considerably robust in most cases, allowing us to extend our conclusions to more practical learning setups.

</details>


### [130] [Consensus as Privileged Context for Label-Free Self-Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13643)
*John Gkountouras,Josip Jukić,Ivan Titov*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CANON converts inter-solution consensus into dense, token-level supervision by conditioning a frozen teacher model on a majority-achieving solution, then using it to supervise all tokens of the model's own rollouts, yielding significant reasoning gains without labels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing consensus-based training methods for LLMs discard most information from agreeing solutions—using consensus only as filters, pairwise preferences, or scalar rewards. The authors argue that consensus contains richer, token-level training signal that remains untapped.

Method: CANON works as follows: (1) for each unlabeled prompt, sample multiple solutions and identify the majority answer; (2) select one solution that reaches the majority answer and feed it as a prefix to a frozen snapshot of the model, turning it into a "consensus-anchored teacher"; (3) use this teacher to provide token-level supervision (via KL divergence) on the original model's own rollouts, distilling the consensus signal densely across every token rather than just at outcome level.

Result: On mathematical and scientific reasoning benchmarks, CANON improves pass@1 by up to 12 points, outperforms label-free RL by 6 points at 1/7th the compute, approaches the performance of a teacher conditioned on gold solutions, transfers to held-out benchmarks when trained on pooled unlabeled data, and enables the model to solve problems it never solved in 32 prior attempts while also improving its own majority-vote accuracy.

Conclusion: Dense, token-level supervision derived from consensus is a highly effective and compute-efficient alternative to sparse reward signals, enabling strong reasoning improvements without any labeled data.

Abstract: Sampling multiple solutions and returning the majority answer is among the most reliable ways to improve the reasoning accuracy of large language models without labels, and a growing family of methods converts this consensus signal into training supervision. However, existing approaches use consensus only in restricted forms: as a filter that selects solutions for fine-tuning, as a preference between answers, or as a scalar reward for reinforcement learning, discarding most of the information that the agreeing solutions contain. We present CANON (Consensus-ANchored self-distillatiON), a label-free training method that turns consensus into dense, token-level supervision. For each unlabeled prompt, CANON samples multiple solutions, extracts the majority answer, and conditions a frozen snapshot of the model on a solution that reaches it; this consensus-anchored teacher then supervises the model on its own rollouts at every token. Experiments on mathematical and scientific reasoning benchmarks show that CANON improves pass@1 by up to 12 points, outperforming label-free reinforcement learning by 6 points at a seventh of its compute and approaching a teacher conditioned on gold solutions; trained on pooled unlabeled data, it transfers to held-out benchmarks, matching training methods that use gold labels. Analysis suggests that the improvements are not pure distribution sharpening: after training, the model solves problems it previously never solved in 32 attempts, and its majority vote itself becomes more accurate.

</details>


### [131] [Maximally Robust Satisficing Bayesian Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13652)
*Samuli Kinnunen,Petrus Mikkola,Antti Niskanen,Arto Klami*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces a Bayesian optimization method for finding satisficing (sufficiently good) solutions that are maximally robust to input perturbations occurring during deployment, rather than seeking the global optimum.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many design tasks require only satisficing solutions—those meeting a sufficiency threshold—rather than the global optimum. When multiple satisfactory solutions exist (forming a superlevel set), the key question is which to select. Robustness to deployment-time input perturbations is a natural and practical criterion, yet prior Bayesian optimization work either focuses on finding the optimum or handles perturbations during optimization rather than post-deployment.

Method: The authors propose a Bayesian optimization approach that explicitly targets satisficing solutions robust to maximally large input perturbations. They assume precise input control during optimization but anticipate perturbations after deployment, and use this framework to efficiently explore the superlevel set to identify the solution with the largest perturbation tolerance.

Result: The method efficiently identifies satisficing solutions that tolerate maximally large input perturbations, outperforming approaches that do not account for post-deployment robustness. It provides a principled way to select among multiple satisfactory candidates.

Conclusion: Robustness to deployment perturbations is a compelling criterion for selecting among satisficing solutions. The proposed Bayesian optimization method effectively finds such robust satisficing designs with minimal trials, addressing a practical gap in the optimization literature.

Abstract: Many design tasks can be cast as black-box function optimization, enabling use of Bayesian optimization to find an ideal design with minimal number of trials. However, often we do not actually need the optimum but instead a sufficiently good solution is enough, for instance a material that is durable enough for its intended use. In most cases there are multiple satisfactory solutions, forming a superlevel set of the function, raising a key question of which one to prefer. We answer this by explaining why robustness to input perturbations that may occur when the solution is deployed is a good criterion and by introduce a Bayesian optimization method that efficiently finds satisficing solutions that are robust to maximally large perturbations. In contrast to previous works, we assume the inputs can be accurately controlled during optimization, but will be perturbed after the deployment.

</details>


### [132] [The Hyperspherical Geometry of CLIP Latent Space: A Semantic Mixture Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13660)
*Zijie Yu,Gaowen Liu,Ramana Rao Kompella,Philip S. Yu,Yue Song*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CLIP's latent space is inherently hyperspherical, but existing probabilistic models incorrectly use Gaussian assumptions. This paper proposes a Mixture of von Mises-Fisher (MovMF) distribution on the unit hypersphere, learned via EM, yielding closed-form likelihoods aligned with CLIP's geometry. The model improves out-of-distribution detection, long-tailed recognition, and provides interpretable semantic decomposition of embeddings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CLIP embeddings are governed by cosine similarity, reflecting an intrinsic hyperspherical geometry. However, prior probabilistic interpretations (e.g., for OOD detection or density estimation) rely on Gaussian assumptions that fail to capture the directional, multimodal structure of this space. There is a need for a density model that is geometrically faithful to the unit hypersphere.

Method: The authors propose modeling the CLIP latent space using a Mixture of von Mises-Fisher (MovMF) distribution on the unit hypersphere. The von Mises-Fisher distribution is a natural exponential family on the sphere, parameterized by a mean direction and a concentration parameter. Parameters are learned via the Expectation-Maximization (EM) algorithm, where each mixture component corresponds to a coherent semantic concept. This yields a closed-form likelihood naturally suited to cosine similarity-based representations.

Result: The MovMF model significantly outperforms Gaussian-based baselines on long-tailed detection and out-of-distribution (OOD) detection tasks. It also enables a natural semantic decomposition where each embedding is expressed as a sparse probabilistic combination of interpretable semantic concepts.

Conclusion: CLIP's latent space is better characterized as a hyperspherical semantic mixture rather than an isotropic Gaussian. The proposed MovMF framework provides a simple, geometrically consistent probabilistic model for understanding and modeling multimodal representations.

Abstract: Contrastive Language-Image Pretraining (CLIP) representations form a semantic embedding space governed by cosine similarity, reflecting an intrinsic hyperspherical geometry. However, existing probabilistic interpretations typically rely on Gaussian assumptions, which fail to capture this directional and multimodal structure. We propose a principled density model for the CLIP latent space based on Mixtures of von Mises-Fisher (MovMF) distributions defined on the unit hypersphere. Using the Expectation-Maximization (EM) algorithm, we efficiently learn a probabilistic model in which each mixture component corresponds to a coherent semantic concept. This formulation yields a closed-form likelihood naturally aligned with hyperspherical geometry, enabling accurate and interpretable density estimation. Empirically, our model significantly improves long-tailed and out-of-distribution detection and provides a natural semantic decomposition, representing each embedding as a sparse probabilistic combination of interpretable concepts. These results suggest that CLIP latent space is more faithfully characterized as a hyperspherical semantic mixture rather than an isotropic Gaussian, establishing a simple and geometrically consistent probabilistic framework for modeling and understanding multimodal representations. Project page is available at https://xiaoyuzhizi.github.io/movmf-clip/.

</details>


### [133] [Optimal and Efficient Contextual Combinatorial Semi-bandits with General Function Approximation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13686)
*Hao Qin,Chicheng Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces SquareCB.Comb, a computationally efficient algorithm for contextual combinatorial semi-bandits with general function approximation. It solves a convex optimization problem each round to balance exploration and exploitation, achieving a minimax optimal regret bound of O(√(m A T log|F|)) without structural assumptions on the action set beyond a cardinality bound m.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for contextual combinatorial bandits either impose restrictive structural assumptions (e.g., slates, rankings) or lack computational efficiency with general reward function approximation. The authors aim to bridge this gap by developing an algorithm that handles arbitrary combinatorial actions with only a cardinality constraint while matching state-of-the-art regret guarantees.

Method: The proposed SquareCB.Comb algorithm operates by, at each round, solving a convex optimization problem to sample a combinatorial action. It leverages the SquareCB framework, extending it to the combinatorial setting. The sampling distribution is derived from solving a convex program that balances exploration and exploitation, scaling to large arm sets with no structural assumptions beyond a per-action cardinality bound of m.

Result: SquareCB.Comb achieves a regret bound of O(√(m A T log|F|)), which is minimax optimal. In the realizable setting, this matches state-of-the-art guarantees from policy search-based algorithms restricted to slate recommendation, while generalizing to arbitrary combinatorial structures and general reward function approximation.

Conclusion: SquareCB.Comb provides the first computationally efficient, minimax optimal algorithm for contextual combinatorial semi-bandits with general function approximation and arbitrary action structures, significantly broadening the applicability beyond prior slate- or ranking-restricted settings.

Abstract: We study the contextual combinatorial semi-bandit (CCSB) problem with general reward function approximation. At each round, the learner observes a context, selects a combinatorial action consisting of a subset of basic arms, and receives the reward of each selected arm; the goal is to maximize the cumulative reward over time. We propose SquareCB.Comb, a computationally efficient algorithm that, at each round, solves a convex optimization problem to sample a combinatorial action that balances exploration and exploitation. SquareCB.Comb scales to large arm sets and imposes no structural assumptions on the action set beyond a cardinality bound of $m$ on each combinatorial action. We prove that SquareCB.Comb achieves a minimax optimal regret bound of $O(\sqrt{m A T \log |\mathcal{F}|})$, where $A$ is the number of arms, $m$ is the maximum number of arms in a combinatorial action, $T$ is the time horizon, and $\mathcal{F}$ is the reward function class. In the realizable setting, this bound matches the state-of-the-art regret guarantees achieved by policy search-based algorithms in the more restricted slate recommendation settings, while simultaneously generalizing to arbitrary combinatorial action structures and general reward function approximation.

</details>


### [134] [Microstructure-Conditioned Surrogate Models for Graded Multiscale Optimization of Mycelium Composites](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13688)
*J. Storm,I. B. C. M. Rocha,S. Schyck,K. Masania,F. P. van der Meer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A hypernetwork-based conditioned surrogate model enables efficient multiscale simulation and optimization of microstructured sustainable materials, demonstrated through a mycelium-woodchip composite where peak stress was reduced by 42% via functionally graded design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multiscale optimization of sustainable materials with engineered microstructures is computationally prohibitive because data-driven microscale surrogates require massive training datasets for every microstructure variation. There is a need for a method that can generalize across microstructural variability with limited data.

Method: The authors condition a hybrid physics-data surrogate model on microstructural variables using a hypernetwork — a neural network that generates the weights of another network. This is further extended to condition directly on manufacturing variables. The approach is applied to simulate functionally graded mycelium-woodchip composites and validated against full FE² simulations. A graded multiscale disk is optimized to minimize peak stress.

Result: The conditioned surrogate enables tractable multiscale simulations of functionally graded structures with small training datasets. Optimization of the graded disk achieved a 42% reduction in peak stress compared to a random microstructure baseline. The manufacturing-variable conditioning successfully captured complex microstructure-property relationships.

Conclusion: Conditioning surrogate models on microstructural and manufacturing variables via hypernetworks is a practical and effective route for multiscale optimization of hierarchical sustainable materials, significantly accelerating their development and design.

Abstract: Emerging sustainable materials increasingly rely on engineered hierarchy and microstructure to achieve control of their properties and mechanical behavior. Optimizing these materials with controllable microstructures requires efficient multiscale simulations. Data-driven surrogate models for the microscale can accelerate multiscale simulations, but require large amounts of data even for a fixed microstructure. When a range of microstructures is considered, as is the case in multiscale optimization, even more data is needed to train a surrogate. To overcome this challenge, we condition a hybrid physics-data surrogate on microstructural variables using a hypernetwork. This approach enables accurate predictions of multiscale mechanical behavior for a mycelium-woodchip composite material, even when trained on small datasets. The conditioned surrogate makes multiscale simulations of functionally graded structures tractable, and we validate it against a full FE^2 simulation. We optimize a graded multiscale disk, and reduce the peak stress by 42% compared to one with a random microstructure. Then, we go one step further, conditioning the network directly on manufacturing variables that can have a complex influence on the microstructure. This is a practical route to engineer the microscale for desired macroscale behavior. This contribution highlights the benefits of microarchitectured structures and demonstrates how conditioned surrogate models enable their multiscale optimization, which will accelerate the development and design of future sustainable materials and structures.

</details>


### [135] [Conditional Invertible Neural Networks for Data-Driven UAV Control: A 2-D Proof of Concept](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13703)
*Christian Wittke,Stephan Myschik,Oliver Niggemann*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper explores conditional invertible neural networks (cINNs) as learned probabilistic inverse-dynamics models for a coaxial multicopter, trained from an INDI teacher. Open-loop fidelity is strong, closed-loop tracking nearly matches INDI, and failures are attributed to attitude divergence and phase lag under aggressive/high-frequency commands.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
