Motivation: Traditional model-based control (e.g., INDI) requires accurate physical models that are difficult to obtain and maintain. A learned probabilistic inverse-dynamics model could capture residual uncertainties, provide uncertainty estimates, and reduce reliance on explicit system identification, but must retain closed-loop stability and tracking performance.

Method: The authors use a conditional invertible neural network (cINN) with rational-quadratic spline coupling layers and invertible linear mixing to model the conditional distribution p(u | s_t, c_t). The cINN is trained via maximum likelihood on state-action-command tuples collected from an INDI teacher controlling a planar X8 coaxial multicopter. Evaluation spans open-loop metrics (R², CRPS, log-probability-error correlation) and 15 closed-loop scenarios with varying reference trajectories.

Result: Open-loop: R² = 0.944, mean CRPS = 0.0915, ρ = -0.60 between log-probability and error magnitude. Closed-loop: position RMSE 9.7 m (cINN) vs. 9.5 m (INDI), with 47% of episodes rated as acceptable tracking. Failures separate into two modes: attitude divergence under aggressive step references and phase lag under high-frequency sinusoidal references.

Conclusion: cINNs can serve as viable probabilistic inverse-dynamics models matching INDI-level tracking in many scenarios. Dominant failure mechanisms — attitude divergence and phase lag — are linked to limited command bandwidth and insufficient data coverage, not fundamental model inadequacy, suggesting clear paths for improvement.

Abstract: We investigate conditional invertible neural networks (cINNs) as probabilistic inverse-dynamics models for multirotor control. For a planar X8 coaxial multicopter, we learn $p(u \mid s_t, c_t)$ from an incremental nonlinear dynamic inversion (INDI) teacher using rational-quadratic spline coupling and invertible linear mixing. Open-loop reproduction reaches $R^2 = 0.944$, mean CRPS 0.0915, and log-probability-error correlation $ρ= -0.60$. Over 15 closed-loop scenarios, position RMSE matches INDI (9.7 vs. 9.5 m), with 47 percent tracking acceptably; failures separate into attitude divergence under aggressive steps and phase lag under high-frequency references, isolating command bandwidth and data coverage as dominant failure mechanisms.

</details>


### [136] [DAGR: State-Conditioned Goal Representations via Difference-Aware Goal Cross-Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13731)
*Xing Lei,Wenyan Yang,Xuetao Zhang,Donglin Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DAGR introduces state-conditioned refinement of goal embeddings for goal-conditioned RL via gated cross-attention with a difference-aware bias, improving navigation on OGBench but showing task-dependent transferability, with gains attributed primarily to the gated residual rather than the difference bias.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing goal encoders (contrastive, metric, temporal-distance, information-theoretic) all produce state-independent embeddings that fail to encode which aspects of the goal remain unachieved. The policy must implicitly invert both state and goal encoders to recover this progress signal, imposing an unnecessary representational burden.

Method: DAGR (Difference-Aware Gated Refinement) augments any late-fusion goal encoder with multi-scale gated cross-attention. A near-identity gated residual preserves the base static embedding, while a per-token state-goal discrepancy map biases cross-attention scores, yielding a state-conditioned goal representation.

Result: On OGBench navigation tasks, DAGR yields consistent improvements over baselines. Ablation reveals the gated residual drives the gain, not the namesake difference bias. On manipulation and puzzle domains, DAGR matches or underperforms the base encoder, demonstrating task-specific effectiveness.

Conclusion: DAGR is a structured architectural refinement rather than a universal improvement. It demonstrates that state-conditioning goal representations benefits navigation but does not generalize uniformly across all goal-conditioned RL domains.

Abstract: Goal-conditioned reinforcement learning hinges on how the goal is encoded. Contrastive, metric, temporal-distance, and information-theoretic encoders differ in objective. They still share one trait. None of them sees the current state. Such a state-independent embedding cannot mark which part of the goal still needs action. The policy must then recover that cue by inverting both encoders. We propose DAGR. It refines the static embedding of any late-fusion encoder into a state-conditioned one through multi-scale gated cross-attention. A near-identity gated residual preserves the base representation. Difference-aware Goal Cross-Attention then biases the attention scores using a per-token state-goal discrepancy map. On OGBench, DAGR improves navigation. Our ablations trace the gain to the gated residual, not to the difference bias that names the method. On manipulation and puzzle tasks it matches or falls below the base. DAGR is a structured refinement, not a universal improvement.

</details>


### [137] [Constraint-Driven Model Optimization: An Industry Framework for Selecting Compression and Acceleration Techniques in Modern Machine Learning Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13735)
*Dhruv Shivkant,Saket Mohanty,Utkarsh Wadhwa*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes a unified, constraint-driven framework for model optimization in production ML deployments, categorizing techniques along five interacting dimensions (data, latency, memory, accuracy, retraining) rather than algorithmic categories, and provides prescriptive pipelines for real-world industrial scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite extensive research on individual optimization techniques (quantization, pruning, distillation, PEFT, etc.), practitioners lack a principled methodology for selecting and combining them. Current approaches rely on heuristics rather than systematic, constraint-aware engineering decisions tailored to specific deployment contexts.

Method: The authors develop a taxonomy of five constraint dimensions (data availability, latency budget, memory budget, accuracy tolerance, retraining budget) that characterize any production deployment. They then synthesize empirical gains from the literature, mapping reported improvements to operational constraints instead of algorithmic categories, and construct a prescriptive decision framework with optimization pipelines for four representative industrial scenarios.

Result: The framework yields four concrete optimization pipelines mapped to distinct industrial scenarios, each addressing a unique constraint profile. The synthesis demonstrates how empirical gains from disparate techniques can be systematically organized by constraint dimensions rather than algorithmic families, enabling principled technique selection.

Conclusion: This work represents one of the first structured attempts to formalize model optimization as a constraint-aware, multi-objective engineering process, bridging the gap between algorithmic research and production deployment decisions.

Abstract: The rapid deployment of machine learning systems across cloud, edge, and enterprise environments has brought model optimization to the forefront of systems-engineering. Despite a rich literature spanning quantization, pruning, knowledge distillation, parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT), and inference-time optimization, practitioners are often left navigating these techniques through heuristics rather than principled methodology. We argue that optimization should be formulated as a constraint-driven, multi-objective engineering decision and introduce a unified framework that characterizes any production deployment along five interacting constraint dimensions: data availability, latency budget, memory budget, accuracy tolerance, and retraining budget. Building on this taxonomy, we synthesize empirical gains reported across the research literature and map them to operational constraints rather than algorithmic categories. To ensure practical relevance, we selected these techniques by reviewing recent literature for methods that report measurable improvements against critical deployment bottlenecks. We propose a prescriptive decision framework and provide optimization pipelines for four representative industrial scenarios to illustrate it in practice. To the best of our knowledge, this work provides one of the first structured attempts to formalize model optimization as a constraint-aware, multi-objective engineering process, synthesizing quantitative evidence from the research literature.

</details>


### [138] [Implementations of Quantum and Classical Topology-Aligned Architectures for Molecular Property Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13737)
*James T. Pegg,Hubert Okadome Valencia,Ronin Wu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes a topology-aligned inductive bias where model architecture mirrors molecular bond graphs, instantiated in both quantum (Iso-QGNN) and classical (Iso-CGNN) models. Benchmarked on QM9, both achieve strong performance with only 64 parameters, demonstrating that topology alignment—not quantumness—drives parameter efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To achieve parameter-efficient learning in low-data, resource-constrained quantum chemistry settings, and to provide a fair matched-baseline comparison between quantum and classical models to identify what truly drives performance gains.

Method: A topology-aligned inductive bias is introduced where atoms map to fixed computational units and bonds determine interacting pairs via shared learnable parameters. This is realized in two architectures: a variational quantum circuit (Iso-QGNN) and a parameter-matched classical message-passing neural network (Iso-CGNN). Both are evaluated on HOMO-LUMO gap and dipole moment binary classification tasks from QM9, with only 64 trainable parameters.

Result: Both models achieve test AUCs of ~0.88 (quantum) and ~0.91 (classical) on HOMO-LUMO classification, and ~0.78 on dipole moment classification. They reach 90% of asymptotic performance with ~250 training molecules and maintain stable gradient norms throughout training.

Conclusion: The topology-aligned inductive bias is the key factor driving parameter efficiency at QM9 scale, not quantum effects per se. This underscores the importance of matched-baseline benchmarking in quantum machine learning research.

Abstract: For low-data and resource-constrained regimes typical of quantum chemistry, parameter-efficient learning is a key objective. Here, we propose a topology-aligned inductive bias in which the model architecture mirrors the molecular bond graph: atoms map to a fixed register of computational units, and bonds determine which pairs interact through shared learnable parameters. This principle is instantiated in two architectures: a variational quantum circuit (Iso-QGNN), and a parameter-matched classical message-passing model (Iso-CGNN). The models are benchmarked on HOMO-LUMO and dipole moment binary classification tasks over the QM9 benchmark. With 64 trainable parameters, the implementations achieve test AUCs of approximately 0.88 (quantum) and 0.91 (classical) on the gap task, and close to 0.78 (both) on the dipole task. The models reach 90% of asymptotic performance within about 250 training molecules and gradient norms remain stable throughout training. These results indicate that the topology-aligned inductive bias is the active ingredient driving parameter efficiency at QM9 scale, with implications for matched-baseline benchmarking in quantum machine learning.

</details>


### [139] [Algebraic Representability as the Limiting Regime of Grokking: An Exactly Solvable Model with Holomorphic Activations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13749)
*Chon-Fai Kam,Xavier Cadet,Miloud Bessafi,Frederic Cadet*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper studies two-layer neural networks with a monomial activation σ(z)=z^k on modular arithmetic tasks. When the activation is a pure monomial, the network output collapses to a (k+1)-dimensional algebraic subspace regardless of width. The authors prove that a task is representable iff its Fourier support lies on u+v=k (mod p), and that non-representable targets cannot even be memorized — yielding a clean binary outcome (instant success or outright failure) with no grokking regime, representing the limiting case of the capacity-grokking spectrum.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Grokking — delayed generalization after memorization — is known to depend on model capacity. This paper investigates the extreme endpoint of this spectrum: what happens when the architecture's expressible function class collapses to a finite-dimensional algebraic variety, asking whether grokking persists, disappears, or transforms into a qualitatively different phenomenon.

Method: The authors analyze two-layer networks with holomorphic monomial activation σ(z)=z^k trained on modular arithmetic tasks encoded via roots of unity. They derive a complete algebraic characterization of the network's output subspace using discrete Fourier analysis on (Z_p)², proving that representability requires Fourier support on the diagonal u+v=k (mod p). They also prove a width-independent lower bound on training loss for non-representable targets, and validate predictions across 585 runs. A bottleneck ablation connects this extreme setting to standard networks with richer activations.

Result: The algebraic prediction matches observed outcomes with 99.8% accuracy across 585 runs. Outcomes are strictly binary: representable targets achieve instant success, non-representable targets fail outright. No memorization regime and no grokking are observed. The bottleneck ablation reveals a continuous path from representational failure → memorization without generalization → grokking with shrinking generalization gap as capacity increases, positioning the monomial case as the zero-capacity limit of this spectrum.

Conclusion: When the expressible function class shrinks to a fixed algebraic object (via monomial activation), the question of grokking dissolves into whether the target can be represented at all. This binary behavior is the limiting case of the capacity-grokking relationship, clarifying that grokking fundamentally requires sufficient representational capacity for both memorization and generalization to coexist as distinct phases.

Abstract: Neural networks trained on modular arithmetic exhibit grokking, a delayed transition from memorisation to generalisation known to depend on model capacity: too little and the network memorises slowly or not at all, too much and it generalises almost immediately. What happens at the extreme of this spectrum, when the architecture's expressible function class collapses to a finite-dimensional algebraic variety? We study two-layer networks with a holomorphic monomial activation sigma(z)=z^k, trained on modular tasks encoded via roots of unity. Here the network output, regardless of hidden width, is confined to a (k+1)-dimensional subspace of characters of (Z_p)^2, an O(k/p^2) slice of the full function space. We give a complete algebraic characterisation of this subspace: a task is representable if and only if its discrete Fourier support lies on the diagonal u+v = k (mod p), which for linear-phase targets reduces to the arithmetic criterion m+n=k. This is not merely a constraint on eventual generalisation but on memorisation itself: because the outputs are algebraically confined, a non-representable target cannot be fit even on the training set, and we prove a positive lower bound on the training loss, independent of width. Across 585 runs the algebraic prediction matches the observed outcome with 99.8% accuracy, with no memorisation regime and no grokking; outcomes split cleanly into instant success and outright failure. This binary behaviour is the limiting case of the capacity-grokking relationship: when the expressible class shrinks to a fixed algebraic object, the question of when a network will grok dissolves into whether it can represent the target at all. A bottleneck ablation connects this extreme to standard networks, tracing a continuous path from representational failure, through memorisation without generalisation, to grokking with a shrinking gap as capacity grows.

</details>


### [140] [MxGPS: Multiplex Graph Transformers for a Power Grid Foundation Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13763)
*Charilaos Papaioannou,Ioannis Tsantilas,Dimitris Giannakakos,Vasilis Michalakopoulos,Sotiris Pelekis,Vangelis Marinakis,Arsam Aryandoust,Antonello Monti,Ricardo J. Bessa,Perdo P. Vergara,Jochen Cremer,Elissaios Sarmas*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MxGPS addresses "topology overfitting" in GNNs for power grids—where low in-distribution error masks catastrophic degradation under topology shift—by jointly training a multiplex graph transformer on SSE and PF tasks. The shared encoder, constrained by complementary gradient signals, achieves 0% boundary violation rate on unseen topologies with only 1.6M parameters, inverting the typical accuracy-vs-robustness trade-off.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Single-task GNN fine-tuning for power grid problems systematically exhibits topology overfitting: models with the best in-distribution performance collapse most severely under topology shift. This failure mode arises because task-specific gradients encode relational structure tied to training topologies rather than underlying physics, undermining generalization to unseen grid configurations. The authors aim to expose, diagnose, and rectify this phenomenon.

Method: The authors propose MxGPS (Multiplex GPS), a multiplex graph transformer with K task-specialized GPS branches over a shared node encoder. The model is trained via self-supervised pre-training followed by multi-task fine-tuning on Static State Estimation (SSE) and AC Power Flow (PF). A cross-branch attention module is evaluated in ablation. The joint objective forces the shared encoder to reconcile complementary gradient signals from both tasks, preventing specialization to topology-specific structure. Evaluation uses 3-fold sliding-window cross-validation across four unseen topologies (14-, 24-, 162-, and 300-bus).

Result: MxGPS achieves 0% boundary violation rate (BVR) on all four zero-shot Power Flow topologies. In contrast, models with significantly lower in-distribution PF error degrade by 190%–1400% under topology shift, while MxGPS degrades by only 39%. This inversion directly implicates topology overfitting—not insufficient capacity—as the primary failure mechanism. MxGPS uses only 1.6M parameters, 12× fewer than the GridFM baseline.

Conclusion: Multi-task joint training with complementary objectives (SSE + PF) is a principled and parameter-efficient mechanism for achieving topology-agnostic generalization in power grid foundation models. Topology overfitting is a fundamental failure mode that must be explicitly addressed in GNN-based grid learning.

Abstract: Single-task fine-tuning of graph neural networks (GNNs) for power grid problems exhibits a systematic failure mode: models that achieve the lowest in-distribution error degrade the most under topology shift. We term this topology overfitting: the tendency of task-specific gradient signals to encode relational structure particular to the training topologies rather than the underlying physics, causing models to fail on unseen grids despite strong in-distribution performance. To expose and address this failure mode, we introduce MxGPS (Multiplex GPS), a multiplex graph transformer that runs K task-specialised GPS branches over a shared node encoder, jointly trained on Static State Estimation (SSE) and AC Power Flow (PF) via a self-supervised pre-training and multi-task fine-tuning protocol, with a cross-branch attention module evaluated in ablation. The joint SSE+PF objective forces the shared encoder to simultaneously satisfy complementary gradient signals, preventing it from overfitting to topology-specific relational structure. Under a 3-fold sliding-window cross-validation spanning four unseen topologies (14-, 24-, 162-, and 300-bus), MxGPS attains 0% boundary violation rate (BVR) on all four zero-shot Power Flow topologies. Critically, models with substantially lower in-distribution PF error degrade by 190% to 1400% under topology shift, whereas MxGPS degrades by only 39%, an inversion that directly implicates topology overfitting as the failure mechanism rather than insufficient model capacity. With only 1.6M parameters (12x fewer than the GridFM reference baseline), MxGPS demonstrates that multi-task joint training is a principled and parameter-efficient mechanism for topology-agnostic generalisation in power grid foundation models.

</details>


### [141] [Mono-Z Dark Matter Search with Neural Spline Flows Using CMS Run 2015D Open Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13771)
*Hitesh Rasineni,Bhavishya Chebrolu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A search for dark matter produced with a leptonically decaying Z boson using CMS open data (2.32 fb⁻¹, 13 TeV) employs Neural Spline Flows to model background and signal densities, constructing a likelihood-ratio test statistic. Observed 95% CL upper limits on signal strength are μ < 0.0177 (scalar), 0.0362 (vector), and 0.0498 (axial-vector), weaker than expected due to high-MET background mismodeling. First application of NSFlow likelihood-ratio scoring to a mono-Z DM search with CMS open data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dark matter searches in the mono-Z channel traditionally rely on hard MET thresholds that discard potentially signal-rich phase space. The authors aim to improve sensitivity by leveraging the full kinematic phase space via likelihood-ratio scoring with normalizing flows, while also demonstrating the feasibility of such advanced methods on open data.

Method: Events are selected in the mono-Z → ℓ⁺ℓ⁻ final state (μμ and ee). Forty kinematic observables from MINIAOD are cleaned to 37 features. Five independent Neural Spline Flows are trained to estimate background and signal (scalar, vector, axial-vector mediators) densities. A per-event log-likelihood ratio test statistic is constructed. A simultaneous profile-likelihood fit across both channels yields 95% CL upper limits on the signal-strength parameter μ.

Result: Observed (expected) 95% CL upper limits: μ < 0.0177 (0.0018) for scalar, μ < 0.0362 (0.0039) for vector, μ < 0.0498 (0.0069) for axial-vector mediators. Observed limits are weaker than expected due to residual high-MET background-modeling discrepancies, not a DM signal.

Conclusion: Neural Spline Flow likelihood-ratio scoring is successfully demonstrated for a mono-Z dark matter search using CMS open data, providing sensitivity across the full kinematic phase space. The observed limits are consistent with background-only hypothesis within modeling uncertainties, and the approach represents a novel application of flow-based density estimation to collider DM searches with open data.

Abstract: We report a search for dark matter (DM) produced in association with a leptonically decaying \(Z\) boson at \(\sqrt{s}=13\) TeV using CMS Run 2015D open data corresponding to an integrated luminosity of \(2.32\,\mathrm{fb}^{-1}\) together with simplified-model Monte Carlo simulation. Events are selected in the mono-\(Z\rightarrow\ell^+\ell^-\) final state in both the \(μμ\) and \(ee\) channels. Forty kinematic observables are extracted from MINIAOD and MINIAODSIM, cleaned with physics-motivated selections, and reduced to a 37-dimensional feature vector. Five Neural Spline Flows are trained independently to model Standard Model background and mediator-specific DM signal densities. The per-event test statistic is constructed from the log-likelihood ratio between the signal and background density estimates, providing sensitivity across the full kinematic phase space without requiring a hard upper \(\mathrm{MET}\) threshold. A simultaneous profile-likelihood fit combining the two channels yields observed (expected) 95\% confidence level upper limits on the signal-strength parameter of \(μ<0.0177\) (\(0.0018\)) for the scalar mediator, \(μ<0.0362\) (\(0.0039\)) for the vector mediator, and \(μ<0.0498\) (\(0.0069\)) for the axial-vector mediator. The observed limits are weaker than expected because of a residual high-\(\mathrm{MET}\) background-modeling discrepancy rather than evidence for a DM signal. To our knowledge, this is the first application of Neural Spline Flow likelihood-ratio scoring to a mono-\(Z\) dark matter search using CMS Run 2015D open data simultaneously in the \(μμ\) and \(ee\) channels.

</details>


### [142] [NodeImport: Imbalanced Node Classification with Node Importance Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13837)
*Nan Chen,Zemin Liu,Bryan Hooi,Bingsheng He,Jun Hu,Jia Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: NodeImport introduces a balanced meta-set approach for class-imbalanced node classification in GNNs, using a theoretically-derived node importance metric to dynamically select valuable training nodes (labeled, unlabeled, and synthetic) that improve unbiased model performance, while decoupling synthetic generation from filtering for broad compatibility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Class imbalance in graph node classification causes GNNs to overfit majority classes and underrepresent minority classes. Existing solutions—prioritizing nodes by class size or synthesizing minority nodes—are insufficient. A principled, fine-grained method is needed to identify training nodes that genuinely improve performance under unbiased conditions.

Method: The authors propose a framework built around a balanced meta-set that approximates the overall feature distribution. They theoretically derive a direct node importance formula that measures whether a training node improves model performance on this unbiased meta-set, providing an intuitive threshold for selection. This metric guides dynamic filtering of labeled, unlabeled, and synthetic nodes. Crucially, synthetic node generation is decoupled from the filtering process, ensuring compatibility with any generation method. A high-quality meta-set construction strategy ensures robust per-class representation.

Result: NodeImport was evaluated across multiple datasets using popular GNN architectures and demonstrated superiority over existing baselines in class-imbalanced node classification, showcasing both flexibility and effectiveness.

Conclusion: The NodeImport framework effectively mitigates class imbalance through importance-based node selection grounded in a balanced meta-set, offering a flexible, theoretically-motivated solution that improves GNN performance on imbalanced graph data and is compatible with various synthetic node generation techniques.

Abstract: In real-world applications, node classification on graphs often faces the challenge of class imbalance, where majority classes dominate training, resulting in biased model performance. Traditional GNNs often struggle in such scenarios, as they tend to overfit to majority classes while underrepresenting minority classes. Existing solutions, which either prioritize nodes based on class size or synthesize new nodes for minority classes, often fall short of effectively addressing this imbalance issue. This paper introduces an approach to class-imbalanced node classification by utilizing a balanced meta-set for importance measurement, where a training node is considered significant if it enhances model performance under an unbiased setting. Our method identifies important nodes that can counteract class imbalance and utilizes them for model training, allowing for fine-grained and dynamic node selection throughout the training process. We theoretically derive a formula to directly assess node importance, reducing computational overhead and providing an intuitive threshold for node selection. Guided by this metric, we develop a novel framework that filters valuable labeled, unlabeled, and synthetic nodes that enhance model performance in an unbiased context. A key advantage of this framework is its separation of the synthetic node generation process from the filtering process, ensuring compatibility with various node generation methods. Furthermore, we introduce a strategy to construct a high-quality meta-set that closely approximates the overall feature distribution, ensuring robust representation of each class. We evaluate our framework, NodeImport, across multiple datasets using popular GNN architectures, demonstrating its superiority over existing baselines. Our results highlight the flexibility and effectiveness of the framework in mitigating class imbalance, leading to improved outcomes.

</details>


### [143] [Heavy-Tailed Flow Matching via Random Clocks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13841)
*Zhouhao Yang,Yezhen Wang,Kenji Kawaguchi,Vladimir Braverman,Haoyang Cao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HTFM frames heavy-tailed distributions as Gaussian scale mixtures via random clocks, enabling flow matching to handle heavy-tailed data. Conditioning on clock paths (encoded via logsignature features) yields flexible tail modeling, improving mode coverage and tail recovery while retaining fast sampling. The framework also provides a practical tail-control interface.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard diffusion and flow-matching models assume Gaussian source distributions, which are a poor inductive match for heavy-tailed data arising in imbalanced datasets, finance, and weather extremes. Gaussian sources fail to capture rare, high-impact events, motivating a principled framework that accommodates heavy-tailed behavior while preserving the tractability of Gaussian-conditioned flows.

Method: HTFM casts heavy-tailed source distributions as mixtures of clock-conditioned Gaussian sources. For a given clock path, the source and flow remain Gaussian, but marginalizing over the random clock yields Gaussian scale mixtures covering Gaussian, α-stable, and Student-t families. The clock path is encoded via truncated logsignature features, allowing the velocity field to adapt to the conditional space with minimal overhead. The same architecture can vary tail heaviness by changing only the clock law or tail parameter.

Result: On 2D imbalanced α-stable mixtures, CIFAR10-LT, and HRRR weather fields, HTFM improves mode coverage, sample quality, and tail-statistic recovery over Gaussian flow matching and competitive heavy-tailed baselines, while retaining the low-NFE (number of function evaluations) sampling advantage of flow matching.

Conclusion: HTFM successfully bridges heavy-tailed modeling and flow matching through the random-clock formulation, offering improved fidelity for rare events without sacrificing sampling efficiency. The tail-control interface further enhances practical utility across diverse distribution families.

Abstract: Heavy-tailed data arise in many domains where rare events carry disproportionate importance, such as imbalanced image datasets, financial returns, and weather extremes. Standard diffusion and flow-matching models typically begin from Gaussian noise or Gaussian source distributions, which yield tractable training targets but provide a poor inductive match for heavy-tailed data. We propose Heavy-Tailed Flow Matching via Random Clocks (HTFM), a framework that portrays heavy-tailed sources as mixtures of clock-conditioned Gaussian sources. Conditioning on a given clock path, the source distribution and flow are Gaussian; marginalizing over the clock gives a Gaussian scale mixture covering Gaussian, $α$-stable, and Student-t families. To make the clock-conditioned vector field practical, we encode the path-valued clock using truncated logsignature features, allowing the velocity field to adapt to the realized conditional space with negligible overhead. Empirically, on 2D imbalanced $α$-stable mixtures, CIFAR10-LT, and HRRR weather fields, HTFM improves mode coverage, sample quality, and tail-statistic recovery over Gaussian flow matching and competitive heavy-tailed baselines, while retaining the low-NFE sampling advantage of flow matching. Moreover, the random-clock formulation further provides a practical tail-control interface: by varying only the clock law or tail parameter, the same architecture can calibrate the ``heaviness'' of generated tails across different distribution families.

</details>


### [144] [Relevance-Aware Rule: Structural Deletion of Irrelevant Conditions in Decision Trees](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13874)
*Jung-Sik Hong,Jeongeon Lee,Min Kyu Sim,Sangheum Hwang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Decision trees contain irrelevant conditions (IRCs) due to the structural mechanism of binary splitting. This paper establishes a theoretical foundation identifying that splits create C1-links and C0-links based on class proportion shifts. A structural IRC deletion framework is proposed that matches links relative to leaf-class proportions, flags mismatched links as suspicious, then selectively deletes only those confirmed as structurally and empirically irrelevant, achieving substantial rule simplification without sacrificing reliability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing IRC deletion methods either preserve the original tree too loosely (compromising reliability) or too strictly (failing to achieve meaningful simplification) because they overlook the structural mechanism by which IRCs arise during tree splitting. This work aims to provide a theoretically grounded approach to reliable IRC deletion.

Method: The authors first establish theoretical foundations: a binary split shifts class proportions in opposite directions, generating a C1-link (increasing class-1 proportion) and a C0-link (increasing class-0 proportion) along sibling branches. The proposed framework then: (1) matches links that increase the leaf-class proportion relative to each leaf; (2) flags links that increase the opposite leaf-class proportion as structurally suspicious IRC candidates; (3) rigorously diagnoses relevance by assessing prediction reliability; and (4) selectively deletes only conditions that are both structurally and empirically irrelevant while protecting those whose removal would harm rule reliability.

Result: Experimental results demonstrate that the proposed structural IRC deletion framework achieves substantial rule simplification while maintaining the reliability of the original tree.

Conclusion: By grounding IRC deletion in the structural mechanism of binary splitting, the proposed framework overcomes the limitations of prior methods, enabling reliable and meaningful simplification of decision tree rules without compromising predictive reliability.

Abstract: Decision trees generate interpretable if--then rules, yet they contain irrelevant conditions (IRCs). These IRCs arise from the structural mechanism of tree splitting and persist even in modern optimal sparse tree induction algorithms. Existing IRC deletion methods overlook this structural mechanism; therefore, they either preserve the original tree too loosely to remain reliable, or too strictly to achieve meaningful simplification. This study provides theoretical foundations for reliable IRC deletion by establishing theorems and propositions related to the underlying IRC mechanism. The key finding is that a binary split shifts class proportions in opposite directions relative to the parent. Specifically, an increase in the class-1 proportion along one branch necessitates an increase in the class-0 proportion along its sibling, thereby generating a C1-link and a C0-link. Based on this structural fact, we propose a structural IRC deletion framework. Relative to each leaf, links that increase the leaf-class proportion are matched, whereas links that increase the proportion of the opposite leaf-class are mismatched. These mismatched links are flagged as structurally suspicious IRC candidates. Rather than deleting them outright, the framework rigorously diagnoses their relevance by assessing prediction reliability. It selectively deletes conditions that are structurally and empirically irrelevant, while strictly protecting those whose deletion would reduce the rule's reliability. Experimental results confirm that the proposed framework achieves substantial rule simplification without sacrificing the reliability of the original tree.

</details>


### [145] [AI-Augmented Adaptive Digital Twin Modeling for Brain Tumor Evolution Prediction and Treatment Scheduling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13877)
*Wenxi Liu,Michael Trimboli,Xianqi Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes a brain tumor digital twin framework combining a reaction-diffusion model with 3D residual learning and online updating, plus MPC-based treatment scheduling. Hybrid modeling reduces MSE by 84.3% and improves Dice by 43.5% over RD baseline; online updating yields further 45.9% MSE reduction and 22.4% lower final tumor burden in scheduling simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Brain tumor progression is spatially heterogeneous, patient-specific, and interactively complex with surrounding anatomy, making accurate long-term prediction and treatment scheduling challenging. Existing purely mechanistic models (e.g., reaction-diffusion) capture general dynamics but struggle with heterogeneous tumor burden in long-horizon predictions, motivating a hybrid data-driven correction approach with adaptive updating.

Method: An AI-augmented adaptive digital twin framework integrating: (1) an interpretable reaction-diffusion (RD) model as the mechanistic backbone; (2) a 3D residual learning module that corrects model-form errors of the RD model; (3) patient-specific DT updating during recursive rollout to adapt to evolving tumor states; and (4) model predictive control (MPC) for constrained chemotherapy and radiotherapy scheduling. Evaluation uses 387 synthetic tumor trajectories (120 steps each), generated with patient-data-informed parameters.

Result: The baseline RD model captures tumor location and overall temporal trends but underestimates heterogeneous burden in long-horizon prediction. The hybrid RD-residual model reduces masked voxel-wise MSE by 84.3% and increases Dice overlap by 43.5% over the RD baseline under dense observations. Online DT updating further reduces MSE by 45.9% and improves Dice by 9.6% versus the non-updated hybrid model. In MPC scheduling, the updated DT controller achieves 22.4% lower final tumor burden compared to a fixed treatment schedule.

Conclusion: The unified framework demonstrates effective integration of patient-specific initialization, mechanistic modeling, adaptive learning, and constrained treatment optimization. While validated on synthetic rather than clinical longitudinal data, the approach establishes a foundation for future translation to real-world adaptive treatment planning.

Abstract: Brain tumor progression exhibits spatially heterogeneous growth, patient-specific treatment response, and complex interactions with surrounding anatomy, making accurate long-term prediction challenging. We propose an AI-augmented adaptive digital twin (DT) framework for brain tumor evolution prediction and treatment scheduling. The framework integrates an interpretable reaction--diffusion (RD) model, a 3D residual learning module for model-form correction, patient-specific DT updating during recursive rollout, and model predictive control (MPC) for constrained chemotherapy and radiotherapy scheduling. Experiments on 387 synthetic tumor trajectories with 120-step evolution show that the baseline RD model captures tumor location and overall temporal behavior but underestimates heterogeneous tumor burden during long-horizon prediction. Hybrid RD--residual modeling reduces masked voxel-wise mean squared error by 84.3% and increases Dice overlap by 43.5% relative to the RD baseline under dense simulated observations. Online DT updating further reduces mean squared error by 45.9% and improves Dice overlap by 9.6% compared with the non-updated hybrid model. In MPC-based scheduling simulations, the updated DT controller reduces final tumor burden by 22.4% relative to a fixed treatment schedule under the terminal-burden objective. Together, these results demonstrate a unified framework for patient-specific initialization, mechanistic modeling, adaptive learning, and constrained treatment optimization. Although validated using patient-data-informed synthetic trajectories rather than clinical longitudinal data, the proposed framework establishes a foundation for future translation to real-world adaptive treatment planning.

</details>


### [146] [Task-Oriented Sensing and Covert Transmissions for Collaborative Multi-AUV Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13880)
*Xueyao Zhang,Chenyang Yan,Bo Yang,Xuelin Cao,Zhiwen Yu,Bin Guo,George C. Alexandropoulos,Merouane Debbah,Chau Yuen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes SVR-MARL, a framework that characterizes the actual utility of shared perceptual information for cooperative underwater tasks under realistic communication and covertness constraints, demonstrated through multi-AUV cooperative localization and tracking.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Underwater AUVs operating in covert missions cannot use active sonar or frequent communications due to exposure risk, forcing reliance on passive observations that yield incomplete local perception. Existing MARL approaches model communication as ideal information flow, while traditional communication optimization focuses only on link-level metrics — both fail to capture how perceptual information actually contributes to cooperative task performance under realistic covert communication constraints (long delays, interference, low reliability, exposure risk).

Method: The authors propose the Sensed Information Value Realization Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (SVR-MARL) framework, which leverages practical information to characterize the utility of shared perceptual information for cooperative tasks and learns distributed cooperative policies that operate under realistic communication and covert constraints.

Result: Through a case study on covert multi-AUV cooperative localization and tracking, the framework demonstrates improved collaborative task efficiency while simultaneously reducing unnecessary communication and exposure risks.

Conclusion: The SVR-MARL framework effectively bridges the gap between idealized communication models in MARL and real-world covert underwater communication constraints, showing that explicitly reasoning about the value of sensed information enables more efficient and stealthy multi-AUV cooperation.

Abstract: In underwater covert cooperative missions, autonomous underwater vehicles (AUVs) often cannot rely on active sonar to continuously obtain complete information, since active sensing and frequent communications increase the risk of exposure. As a result, AUVs primarily rely on passive observation, an approach that yields incomplete local perception and limited task efficiency. Although underwater acoustic communications can mitigate this limitation through information sharing, they are simultaneously constrained by long delays, severe interference, low reliability, and the risk of covert exposure. Existing communications-oriented multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) studies often model communication as an ideal information flow, whereas traditional communication optimization primarily focuses on link-level performance. However, both are insufficient to characterize the actual contribution of perceptual information to cooperative tasks under realistic conditions of covert physical communications. This paper proposes a Sensed Information Value Realization Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (SVR-MARL) framework that leverages practical information to characterize the utility of information for cooperative tasks and learns distributed cooperative policies under realistic communication and covert constraints. Through a case study of covert multi-AUV cooperative localization and tracking, the potential of the proposed framework to improve collaborative task efficiency while reducing unnecessary communication and exposure risks is demonstrated.

</details>


### [147] [PiVoT: A Variational Solution for Real-time Large-scale Multi-object Detection and Tracking under Heavy Clutter](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13891)
*Runze Gan,Qing Li,Simon J. Godsill,Mike E. Davies,James R. Hopgood*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PiVoT is a training-free, variational-inference-based multi-object tracker that jointly performs detection and tracking of numerous objects in noisy point clouds. It leverages Doppler measurements and several computational innovations to achieve real-time, clutter-resilient performance that scales to thousands of objects, rivaling deep-learning benchmarks on automotive radar data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current Bayesian multi-object trackers using Poisson measurement models are training-free but suffer from poor accuracy and efficiency under severe clutter, large object counts, and full-resolution Doppler point clouds. There is a need for a fast, clutter-resilient tracker capable of end-to-end detection and tracking without external clustering or detectors.

Method: PiVoT performs joint variational inference over object states, shapes, existence probabilities, data association, and measurement rates. Key innovations include theoretically justified birth pruning, quadratic-to-linear complexity reductions for exact updates, and a computationally efficient Doppler Poisson model. The tracker handles both positional and Doppler measurements natively.

Result: PiVoT substantially outperforms existing Bayesian trackers in challenging scenes, demonstrates scalability to a thousand objects, maintains robustness to clutter visually inseparable from objects, and achieves real-time operation on full-scale modern automotive radar datasets with performance comparable to a deep-learning detection benchmark.

Conclusion: PiVoT advances the state of the art in training-free multi-object tracking by combining variational inference with Doppler-aware Poisson modeling, delivering a detector-free, real-time solution that scales effectively and rivals learned methods, making it highly suitable for data-scarce radar applications.

Abstract: Multi-object detection and tracking from noisy point clouds remain challenging in many data-scarce radar applications. Current Bayesian trackers based on Poisson measurement models offer a training-free solution but struggle to achieve accuracy and efficiency under severe clutter, large object populations, and full-resolution Doppler point clouds. We address this with PiVoT, a fast, clutter-resilient multi-object tracker for both positional and Doppler measurements. PiVoT performs end-to-end detection and tracking of a large and time-varying number of objects without external clustering or detectors, through joint inference of object states, shapes, existence probabilities, data association, and measurement rates. Its efficiency is driven by several variational inference innovations, such as theoretically justified birth pruning, quadratic-to-linear complexity reductions for exact updates, and a computationally efficient Doppler Poisson model. Experiments show that PiVoT substantially outperforms existing Bayesian trackers in challenging scenes, while also demonstrating exceptional scalability to a thousand objects, robustness to clutter visually inseparable from objects, and real-time operation on full-scale modern automotive radar datasets, where it attains performance comparable to a deep-learning detection benchmark as a training-free joint detector and tracker.

</details>


### [148] [RF Spectrogram Anomaly Detection with Quantum Kitchen Sinks: Architecture, Representation, and Hardware Validation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13897)
*Abdallah Aaraba,Alexis Vieloszynski,Remon Polus,Ola Ahmad,Soumaya Cherkaoui*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper extends Quantum Kitchen Sinks (QKS) with multi-depth data re-uploading and ring entanglement for RF spectrogram anomaly detection. Using a rigorous five-stage ablation protocol, the authors show that DCT input representations with moderate-depth entangled QKS outperform baselines, achieving 0.8778 AUROC and 0.7995 F1 on held-out test data, with real-device validation on ibm_quebec showing minimal deviation from simulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Wireless RF networks are vulnerable to anomalous and malicious transmissions due to the broadcast nature of channels. Quantum Kitchen Sinks present a lightweight hybrid quantum feature map suitable for near-term devices, but their behavior on structured signal data is not well understood. This gap motivates a systematic investigation of QKS architectures for RF anomaly detection under realistic conditions.

Method: The authors extend the standard QKS template with multi-depth data re-uploading and ring entanglement, then apply it to controlled RF spectrogram anomaly detection. They introduce a validation-locked five-stage ablation protocol that isolates effects of shallow architecture, re-uploading depth, episode budget, input representation (raw, PCA, DCT), and classical readout. Experiments use real sub-6 GHz cellular signals and real-device validation on the ibm_quebec QPU.

Result: DCT representations consistently dominate raw and PCA inputs. Moderate-depth entangled QKS configurations yield the strongest performance. QKS improves over matched classical direct-readout baselines across all representation-readout pairs on the held-out test set. The best configuration achieves a test AUROC of 0.8778 and test F1 of 0.7995. Real-device validation on ibm_quebec shows AUROC deviations below 0.013 relative to simulation.

Conclusion: The study provides a practical, reproducible framework for deploying QKS-based anomaly detection in wireless networks, bridging two levels of realism: real measured cellular signals and real-device QPU validation. The results demonstrate that QKS is a viable lightweight quantum-classical approach for secure spectrum management.

Abstract: The broadcast nature of wireless channels exposes radio-frequency (RF) networks to anomalous and malicious transmissions, making anomaly detection a fundamental requirement for secure spectrum management. Quantum Kitchen Sinks (QKS) offer a lightweight hybrid quantum feature map suitable for near-term quantum devices, yet their behavior on structured signal data remains poorly understood. In this paper, we extend the standard QKS template with multi-depth data re-uploading and ring entanglement, and evaluate the resulting pipeline on controlled RF spectrogram anomaly detection. We introduce a validation-locked five-stage ablation protocol that systematically separates the effects of shallow architecture, re-uploading depth, episode budget, input representation, and classical readout. Across the completed benchmark, Discrete Cosine Transform (DCT) representations consistently dominate raw and Principal Component Analysis (PCA) inputs, moderate-depth entangled QKS configurations form the strongest operating regime, and QKS improves over matched classical direct-readout baselines across all evaluated representation-readout pairs on the held-out test set, with the best configuration reaching a test Area Under the Receiver Operating Characteristic curve (AUROC) of 0.8778 and a test F1 of 0.7995. The study bridges two levels of realism: real measured sub-6\,GHz cellular signals on the data side and real-device validation on the ibm_quebec Quantum Processing Unit (QPU) on the computing side, with AUROC deviations below 0.013 relative to simulation. These results provide a practical, reproducible framework for deploying QKS-based anomaly detection in wireless networks.

</details>


### [149] [An Efficient Newton Algorithm for Nonnegative Matrix Factorization with the Kullback-Leibler Divergence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13919)
*Damien Lesens,Jérémy E. Cohen,Bora Uçar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes a Newton-type KL-NMF algorithm using second-order Taylor expansion instead of separable majorants, generalized via HALS, achieving provable convergence and superior empirical performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing KL-divergence NMF algorithms rely on minimizing separable majorants of the loss, an approach the authors argue has reached its limits in terms of efficiency and convergence speed.

Method: Replaces the separable majorant with a second-order Taylor expansion of the KL loss, yielding a non-separable surrogate. This surrogate is minimized using a generalized Hierarchical Alternating Least Squares (HALS) algorithm, resulting in a Newton-type method with provable convergence guarantees.

Result: The proposed algorithm competes favorably with state-of-the-art KL-NMF methods across a large variety of datasets.

Conclusion: Moving beyond separable majorants to Newton-type methods with non-separable surrogates is a fruitful direction for KL-NMF, offering both theoretical convergence and practical performance gains.

Abstract: Nonnegative Matrix Factorization (NMF) is a fundamental tool in unsupervised learning, which approximates a nonnegative matrix by the product of two low-rank nonnegative factors. The Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence is best suited to measure the data to model discrepancy when the decomposed data sample follows a Poisson distribution, which is the case for count datasets such as term-document matrices or images. Most KL-NMF algorithms in the literature minimize a separable majorant of the loss to find their next iterate. We argue that this method has reached its limits and propose to use instead the second-order Taylor expansion of the loss, leading to a Newton-type method. We minimize this non-separable surrogate by proposing a generalization of the well-known HALS algorithm. This yields an efficient KL-NMF algorithm which provably converges and which competes favorably with state-of-the-art algorithms on a large variety of datasets.

</details>


### [150] [VAIOM: Continuous-Input, Discrete-Output Decoder-Only Financial Sequence Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13929)
*Yiming Ma,Xinyu Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: VAIOM introduces a decoder-only Transformer for probabilistic next-return modeling on FX bars, using continuous vector inputs with a categorical return distribution, outperforming LightGBM baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Financial time series are continuous, heterogeneous, and noisy, yet standard decoder-only next-token models operate on discrete symbolic inputs. This mismatch motivates a model that preserves the numerical structure of multivariate financial event vectors at input while retaining a categorical output for tractable training and likelihood evaluation.

Method: VAIOM is a decoder-only Transformer that separates input representation (continuous multivariate financial-event vectors with categorical asset metadata) from output likelihood (categorical distribution over volatility-normalized return buckets). It employs a Mixture-of-Market-States return head, Gap/volatility-regime/Ordinal auxiliary objectives, and full-sequence supervision. Models are fit on pre-2024 training data, selected on 2024H2 validation, and evaluated on two 2025 test periods across three independent seeds.

Result: Every VAIOM model outperforms a fixed single-bar LightGBM baseline in both test halves. The canonical checkpoint achieves paired gains of 0.029 and 0.043 bits per event over LightGBM. Ablations show: continuous input beats discrete-token input, full-sequence supervision beats last-position training, and auxiliary representation shaping plus mixture-structured return heads improve return likelihood. The smallest complete architecture rung achieves the strongest validation likelihood.

Conclusion: Continuous vector input with a categorical return objective forms an effective design for decoder-only Transformers on noisy financial data. Auxiliary objectives, mixture heads, and full-sequence supervision each contribute positively to likelihood performance.

Abstract: Financial observations are continuous, heterogeneous, and noisy, whereas decoder-only next-token models are usually built around discrete symbolic inputs. We introduce Vector-Input Autoregressive Inference for Ordinal-Return Modeling (VAIOM), a decoder-only Transformer for probabilistic next-return modeling on one-hour foreign-exchange bars. VAIOM separates input representation from output likelihood: continuous multivariate financial-event vectors preserve numerical structure at the input, while a categorical distribution over the next volatility-normalized return bucket supports cross-entropy training and likelihood evaluation. The selected 0.9M Hybrid Continuous Input model combines continuous event features with categorical asset metadata, a Mixture-of-Market-States return head, Gap, volatility-regime, and Ordinal auxiliary objectives, and full-sequence supervision. Models and preprocessing are fit using pre-2024 Train data; models are selected on 2024H2 Validation and evaluated without refitting on two 2025 Test periods. Across three independent training seeds, every model outperforms fixed single-bar LightGBM baseline in both Test halves. For the canonical checkpoint, paired gains over LightGBM are 0.029 and 0.043 bits per event. Validation experiments show that continuous input improves over discrete-token input under the same categorical return objective, full-sequence supervision improves over last-position training, and auxiliary representation shaping together with a mixture-structured return head improves return likelihood in controlled comparisons. A supporting capacity study finds that the smallest evaluated complete architecture rung achieves the strongest Validation likelihood on the present corpus.

</details>


### [151] [TRACE: Turn-level Reward Assignment via Credit Estimation for Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13988)
*Leitian Tao,Baolin Peng,Wenlin Yao,Tao Ge,Hao Cheng,Mike Hang Wang,Jianfeng Gao,Sharon Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TRACE proposes a dense credit-assignment method for multi-turn RL agents that derives per-action rewards from TD changes in log-ratio state values computed via a frozen reference model, requiring no critic or process labels. It substantially improves long-horizon tool-use ability, raising BrowseComp-Plus scores dramatically for Qwen3 models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Outcome rewards become sparse, high-variance, and misleading in multi-turn agent trajectories with tens to hundreds of tool calls. A failed rollout may contain many useful intermediate actions, yet outcome-only training assigns uniformly negative advantage, wasting valuable learning signal.

Method: TRACE represents rollouts as state transitions at tool-call boundaries, obtains gold-answer log-probabilities from a frozen reference model, converts them into log-ratio state values, and computes per-action rewards as Temporal-Difference changes in those values. The one-step log-ratio TD component telescopes across redundant tool calls, requiring no additional critic network or process-label training.

Result: On BrowseComp-Plus, TRACE raises Qwen3-4B from 7.2→35.6 and Qwen3-30B-A3B from 8.4→42.6 using pure RL without cold-start SFT, agentic mid-training, or live-web data. Learning curves show earlier improvement and faster convergence, and search behavior transfers to open-web benchmarks.

Conclusion: Dense credit assignment via log-ratio TD learning is highly effective for agentic RL, enabling substantial tool-use gains without auxiliary networks or supervised pre-training stages.

Abstract: Multi-turn agents solve complex tasks through extended sequences of tool interactions before producing a final answer, making credit assignment a fundamental challenge during post-training. Outcome rewards provide reliable supervision for short-horizon reasoning, but become sparse and high-variance as trajectories grow to tens or hundreds of tool calls. They can also be misleading: a failed rollout may contain many useful actions that move the agent closer to the goal, yet outcome-only training assigns them the same negative advantage as the eventual mistake. We propose TRACE (Turn-level Reward Assignment via Credit Estimation), a dense credit-assignment method for agentic reinforcement learning. TRACE represents rollouts as state transitions at tool-call boundaries, obtains gold-answer log-probabilities from a frozen reference model, transforms them into log-ratio state values, and derives per-action rewards as Temporal-Difference changes in those values. This requires no additional critic or process-label training, and its one-step log-ratio TD component telescopes across redundant tool calls. On long-horizon complex search, TRACE substantially improves base-model tool-use ability using pure RL, without a cold-start supervised fine-tuning stage, an agentic mid-training stage, or training on live-web data. On the closed-web BrowseComp-Plus benchmark, it raises Qwen3-4B from $7.2$ to $35.6$ and Qwen3-30B-A3B from $8.4$ to $42.6$. The learned search behavior also transfers to open-web benchmarks, and the learning curves show earlier improvement and faster convergence during RL training.

</details>


### [152] [Lyapunov Exponent as Physics-Informed Dense Reward: RL Discovery of Stabilization Beyond the Kapitza Pendulum](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14001)
*Slava Andrejev*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes using the Lyapunov characteristic exponent as a dense reward signal in reinforcement learning to stabilize an inverted pendulum with vertical motion, successfully achieving both Kapitza-like oscillatory stabilization and strict upright damping.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reinforcement learning for stabilizing an inverted pendulum with vertical motion requires an informative reward signal. Sparse rewards (e.g., success/failure) make credit assignment difficult. The authors seek a principled, dense reward derived from dynamical systems theory — specifically, the Lyapunov characteristic exponent — to guide the agent toward stable policies.

Method: The authors employ the Lyapunov characteristic exponent (LCE), which quantifies the average exponential rate of divergence or convergence of nearby trajectories, as a dense reward signal in a reinforcement learning framework. The agent controls the vertical motion of an inverted pendulum, and the LCE is computed from the system's dynamics to provide continuous feedback on stability.

Result: The RL agent, guided by the LCE reward, autonomously discovered the oscillatory stabilization strategy known as the Kapitza pendulum. Furthermore, the agent learned to damp the pendulum's pivoting motion entirely, achieving a strictly upright (inverted) equilibrium configuration.

Conclusion: The Lyapunov characteristic exponent serves as an effective dense reward for stabilizing unstable mechanical systems via reinforcement learning. It not only recovers known analytical solutions (Kapitza pendulum) but also discovers alternative stabilization strategies, demonstrating the promise of dynamics-informed reward design.

Abstract: We suggest using the Lyapunov characteristic exponent (LCE) as a dense reward signal for the reinforcement learning problem of stabilizing the inverted pendulum with vertical motion. With LCE, the agent not only successfully found the oscillatory motion known as the Kapitza pendulum but also damped the pendulum's pivoting, leaving it in a strictly upright position.

</details>


### [153] [Lighthouse RL: Sample-Efficient Circuit Optimization via Strategic Reset Points](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14008)
*Mustafa Emre Gürsoy,Stefan Uhlich,Ryoga Matsuo,Yağız Gençer,Arun Venkitaraman,Chia-Yu Hsieh,Andrea Bonetti,Eisaku Ohbuchi,Lorenzo Servadei*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Lighthouse RL introduces a strategic reset mechanism that initializes RL episodes from previously discovered high-performing configurations ("lighthouses"), dramatically improving sample efficiency, success rate, generalization, and objective maximization for analog circuit sizing compared to standard RL and Bayesian optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard RL methods for analog circuit sizing waste computational resources exploring unpromising regions of the design space, and traditional methods lack generalization across different performance targets. A more efficient exploration strategy is needed for computationally expensive black-box optimization.

Method: The authors propose a strategic reset strategy where episodes are initialized from "lighthouses" — high-performing configurations discovered during training that are closer to target objectives. These lighthouses guide exploration toward promising regions, and the reset mechanism is designed as a plug-and-play enhancement for any RL-based optimization method.

Result: On a 2D benchmark and two analog circuit problems, Lighthouse RL achieved up to 1.72x faster sample efficiency, 100% success rate (vs. 0-87% for baselines), 75% extrapolation success (vs. 0-50%), and superior objective maximization compared to standard RL and Bayesian optimization approaches.

Conclusion: The lighthouse reset strategy is an effective, general-purpose enhancement for RL-based black-box optimization, particularly valuable for computationally expensive problems like analog circuit sizing where sample efficiency is critical.

Abstract: In this paper, we introduce Lighthouse RL, a sample-efficient reinforcement learning (RL) approach for analog circuit sizing. Traditional methods lack generalization across different performance targets, while standard RL approaches waste resources exploring unpromising regions. Our method addresses these inefficiencies through a strategic reset strategy that initializes episodes from high-performing configurations discovered during training, called "lighthouses". These states, which are closer to the target objectives, guide exploration toward promising regions. When compared to RL and Bayesian optimization methods from the literature, we demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach on a 2D benchmark problem and on two analog circuits, showing significant improvements in sample efficiency (up to 1.72x faster), optimization performance (100% vs. 0-87% success rate), generalization (75% vs. 0-50% extrapolation success), and objective maximization. This efficiency is particularly valuable for computationally expensive black-box optimization problems, and our reset strategy can be used as a plug-and-play enhancement for any RL-based optimization approach.

</details>


### [154] [Transforming Rank: How Architecture Navigates the Spectral Pathologies of Depth](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14018)
*Katie Everett*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper analyzes how Transformer feedforward block components (skip connections, normalization placement, two-matrix expansion-contraction) affect gradient rank preservation across depth at initialization. It reframes these architectural choices as navigating a tradeoff among rank collapse, ensemble-like behavior, and parameter count.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Architectural design choices in Transformers (skip connections, normalization placement, width expansion) are well-known empirically but poorly understood theoretically in terms of how they affect signal propagation and trainability. The authors aim to provide a unified rank-based framework explaining why these components matter for preserving gradient information across depth.

Method: The authors analyze the input-output Jacobian rank at initialization across Transformer blocks, using random matrix theory (including Marchenko-Pastur law) to characterize how each architectural component — skip connections, normalization placement (Pre-Norm vs. Post-Norm), two-matrix structure with width expansion, and activation functions — affects rank collapse. They validate predictions with CIFAR-10 training experiments.

Result: Skip connections route gradients around rank-reducing residual branches, trading rank collapse against ensemble-like behavior based on branch-to-skip scale ratios. Normalization placement controls this ratio: Post-Norm causes rank collapse while Pre-Norm maintains a plateau. The second matrix in the two-matrix structure decorrelates a coherent mean spike, preventing representation collapse. Width expansion preserves branch Jacobian rank following a Marchenko-Pastur law. The initialization Jacobian rank predicts CIFAR-10 trainability.

Conclusion: Deep network architecture design can be recast as managing an intrinsic tradeoff among three competing objectives: preventing rank collapse (information loss), enabling ensemble-like behavior (shallow gradient paths), and minimizing parameter count. This unifies much of the normalization placement and depth scaling literature under a single rank-preservation framework.

Abstract: We investigate how each component of the Transformer feedforward block architecture design determines how much rank survives across depth at initialization. We reinterpret skip connections and normalization, long understood as controlling magnitude, as mechanisms for preserving gradient rank across depth, since the very matrix multiplications and nonlinear activations that make the network expressive also reduce the rank. We show that skip connections trade off rank collapse against ensemble-like behavior, controlled by the relative scales of the branch and the skip: skip connections route the gradient around the residual branch, where rank is lost, rather than along the long gradient paths that encourage the layers to compose. The placement of the normalization layer controls this same tradeoff by setting the branch-to-skip ratio across depth, unifying much of the normalization placement and depth scaling literature, in particular why rank collapses for Post-Norm but plateaus for Pre-Norm. Other aspects of the architecture, like the two-matrix structure that expands and contracts the width, use additional parameters to preserve the representation or branch Jacobian rank. The second matrix decorrelates a coherent mean spike that would grow across blocks with a single matrix and uncentered activation, preventing the residual representation from collapsing. The width expansion between the two matrices keeps the branch Jacobian full rank: applying the rank-reducing activation in this expanded space leaves enough directions to span the original, at a width that follows a Marchenko--Pastur law. The initialization rank of the input--output Jacobian predicts which networks train on CIFAR-10. Taken together, we recast architecture design for deep networks as navigating an intrinsic tradeoff among rank collapse, ensemble-like behavior, and parameter count.

</details>


### [155] [Improving Wind and Solar Power Prediction with Efficient Wrapper-based Feature Selection: An Empirical Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14024)
*Daniel Grillmeyer,Marius Hadry,Michael Stenger,Vanessa Borst,Veronika Lesch,Samuel Kounev*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper addresses the lack of systematic feature selection in renewable energy prediction (wind turbine power curve modeling and photovoltaic power forecasting). It proposes Cluster-based Sequential Feature Selection (CSFS), a model-agnostic wrapper method that achieves comparable predictive performance to standard Sequential Feature Selection while reducing computational cost by 21% on average.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Renewable energy prediction relies on numerous monitoring and environmental variables, yet existing approaches employ limited or unsystematic feature selection methods. This gap motivates the need for an automatic, efficient, and reliable feature selection technique tailored to renewable energy prediction pipelines.

Method: The authors propose Cluster-based Sequential Feature Selection (CSFS), a novel model-agnostic, clustering-based wrapper method. It is empirically evaluated on two use cases — wind turbine power curve modeling and photovoltaic power prediction — and benchmarked against wrapper-based Sequential Feature Selection (SFS), filter-based methods, and Random Forest's embedded feature importance. An open-source implementation is provided on GitHub.

Result: Wrapper-based methods overall yield better-performing feature selections. CSFS achieves predictive performance comparable to standard SFS while reducing computational cost by an average of 21%.

Conclusion: CSFS offers an effective and efficient solution for automatic feature selection in renewable energy prediction tasks, balancing predictive accuracy with reduced computational overhead. Its open-source release supports reproducibility and broader adoption.

Abstract: With rising global energy demand and growing awareness of climate change and its impacts, the share of renewable energies in the global energy mix continues to grow. Unlike conventional power generation, the output of renewable energy sources cannot be controlled as consistently due to their dependence on environmental conditions. Therefore, reliable prediction of current and future energy production is essential. In this paper, we report findings from two structured literature reviews on real-world renewable energy prediction tasks: wind turbine power curve modeling and photovoltaic power prediction. For the former, we conducted a comprehensive literature review ourselves, while for the latter, we synthesize the key findings regarding frequently selected input features based on an existing survey. Across both domains, our analysis reveals that despite the large number of available monitoring and environmental variables, only limited or unsystematic methods for feature selection exist. To address this gap, we propose Cluster-based Sequential Feature Selection (CSFS), a novel, model-agnostic, clustering-based wrapper method for automatic, efficient, and reliable feature selection in renewable energy prediction pipelines. To support reproducibility and reuse, we provide an open-source implementation of CSFS on GitHub. We empirically evaluate the proposed approach on both use cases and compare it with established feature selection techniques such as wrapper-based sequential feature selection (SFS), filter-based methods, and Random Forest's embedded feature importance. The results show that the wrapper-based methods overall provide better-performing selections of features. CSFS achieves a predictive performance comparable to SFS while reducing computational cost by an average of 21%.

</details>


### [156] [MetaPerch: Learning from metadata for bioacoustics foundation models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14072)
*Mustafa Chasmai,Vincent Dumoulin,Jenny Hamer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MetaPerch is a bioacoustic foundation model that leverages metadata (location, time, etc.) as auxiliary supervision signals alongside vocalization data, achieving strong species identification across diverse domains. The paper presents an extensive empirical study of 9 metadata sources on 17 bioacoustic datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large-scale citizen science platforms like Xeno-Canto provide rich metadata (location, time, etc.) that remains underutilized in bioacoustic models. Metadata carries species-distribution correlations that can encourage richer, more robust representations, helping models generalize better to species distribution shifts and acoustic domain shifts — key challenges in real-world passive acoustic monitoring (PAM) deployment.

Method: The authors introduce MetaPerch, a foundation model that incorporates auxiliary metadata losses during training. Rather than relying solely on species vocalization supervision, the model jointly learns from audio and 9 diverse metadata sources (e.g., location, time), treating metadata as additional supervision signals. The approach is evaluated through extensive empirical study across 17 bioacoustic datasets.

Result: MetaPerch achieves strong species identification performance across multiple challenging domains. The empirical study quantifies the effects of 9 different metadata sources on model performance across 17 bioacoustic datasets, demonstrating that metadata-augmented training improves robustness to distribution and domain shifts.

Conclusion: Metadata auxiliary supervision is an effective and underexploited resource for bioacoustic foundation models. Integrating location, time, and other metadata signals yields more generalizable representations, offering a practical path toward more robust PAM systems in real-world deployment scenarios.

Abstract: Bioacoustic foundation models rely on large-scale citizen science platforms like Xeno-Canto for geographically and ecologically diverse data. Recent work has shown that supervision alone can produce SotA species detection models when trained on this large-scale data -- however, there remains unutilized potential in the form of recording metadata readily available within these community-driven data hubs. In this work, we explore the use of metadata -- such as location and time -- as auxiliary supervision signals, allowing the model to leverage species-metadata correlations in its learned representation. Auxiliary metadata losses provide additional information beyond vocalizations alone that can encourage a richer, more robust representation that generalizes better to species distribution and acoustic domain shifts -- important challenges for deployment in real-world passive acoustic monitoring (PAM) settings. We introduce MetaPerch, a new foundation model that achieves strong species identification performance across multiple challenging domains and present an extensive empirical study of the effects of 9 diverse metadata sources on 17 bioacoustic datasets.

</details>


### [157] [Linear Independent Component Analysis via Optimal Transport](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14081)
*Ashutosh Jha,Michel Besserve,Simon Buchholz*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper proposes OT-ICA, a novel ICA algorithm that uses the squared Wasserstein distance (W₂²) to a standard Gaussian as a direct measure of non-Gaussianity, proving it is maximized when recovering independent components, and demonstrates its superiority over classical proxy-based methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical ICA methods rely on proxy contrast functions (e.g., kurtosis, negentropy approximations) or parametric log-likelihoods because exact negentropy optimization is intractable. These proxies introduce distributional assumptions that may not hold, limiting performance when latent source distributions deviate from those assumptions.

Method: The authors prove theoretically that the squared Wasserstein distance (W₂²) between a standard normal distribution and linear projections of the data is maximized precisely when the projection recovers an independent component.
