Based on this, they develop OT-ICA, which uses gradient-based optimization to find the projection maximizing this Wasserstein distance, employing optimal transport as a non-parametric, distribution-free measure of non-Gaussianity.

Result: Empirical evaluation on simulated data shows OT-ICA outperforms classical proxy-based ICA methods across various latent variable distributions. The method is further validated on two real-world applications: EEG artifact removal and econometric price discovery, confirming its practical utility without requiring distributional assumptions.

Conclusion: OT-ICA provides a principled, non-parametric alternative to classical ICA by directly optimizing a Wasserstein-based measure of non-Gaussianity, eliminating the need for proxy contrast functions or parametric likelihoods, and achieves superior performance in both synthetic and real-world settings.

Abstract: Linear Independent Component Analysis (ICA) recovers jointly independent source signals from their linear mixtures. To achieve this, classical ICA algorithms attempt to maximize non-Gaussianity, measured by negentropy, which is linked to independence by information theory. Because exact negentropy optimization is intractable, they rely on proxy contrast functions, such as fourth-order cumulants, and parametric log-likelihoods. We propose instead to measure non-Gaussianity using the squared Wasserstein distance $W_2^2$ to a standard Gaussian. We prove that the Wasserstein distance between a standard normal distribution and linear projections of the data is maximized when the projection recovers an independent component. Based on this observation, we propose the OT-ICA algorithm which finds this projection by gradient-based optimization. Empirical evaluation on simulated data shows that OT-ICA outperforms proxy-based methods for different distributions of the latent variables. Application to EEG artifact removal and econometric price discovery confirm OT-ICA can be used for applied ICA tasks without distributional assumptions.

</details>


### [158] [Leveraging unlabelled data for generalizable neural population decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14086)
*Ximeng Mao,Nanda H. Krishna,Avery Hee-Woon Ryoo,Matthew G. Perich,Guillaume Lajoie*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MOJO introduces a joint training framework combining masked autoencoding (self-supervised learning) with supervised learning for spike-tokenizing neural decoders, demonstrating superior performance over purely supervised models—especially in low-label regimes—across multiple species, tasks, and neural modalities including spiking data and human ECoG.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current spike-based neural decoding models rely exclusively on supervised learning, restricting training to datasets with paired behavioral labels and leaving vast amounts of unlabelled neural data unused. There is a need for methods that can leverage unlabelled data to improve decoding, particularly in label-scarce settings.

Method: MOJO is a training framework for spike-tokenizing models that jointly optimizes two objectives: (1) self-supervised learning via masked autoencoding (reconstructing masked spike tokens) and (2) standard supervised learning on behavioral labels. The approach tokenizes neural spikes and applies masked autoencoding pretraining alongside downstream task supervision.

Result: MOJO outperforms purely SL-trained models across three spiking datasets (monkey motor cortex reaching, mouse multi-regional vision/decision-making), with gains most pronounced in few-shot finetuning with limited labels. SSL yields more interpretable neuronal representations, improving brain region classification and spike-statistics prediction without explicit optimization. MOJO also generalizes to human ECoG speech data, matching neuro-foundation models designed for continuous signals.

Conclusion: Augmenting spike-tokenizing models with SSL improves performance in label-impoverished settings, enables use of unlabelled data across tasks and species, and generalizes to other neural modalities—suggesting a path toward more flexible and scalable data usage when training neural foundation models.

Abstract: Robust and accurate neural decoders are integral to neurotechnologies such as brain-computer interfaces and closed-loop experiments. Recent work has shown that tokenizing neural data at the spike level facilitates multi-session pretraining and delivers state-of-the-art decoding performance. However, current spike-based models are restricted to supervised learning (SL), limiting training to datasets with paired behavioural labels. To address this limitation, we introduce MOJO (Masked autOencoder-based JOint training), a training framework for spike-tokenizing models that jointly leverages self-supervised learning (SSL) via masked autoencoding and SL objectives. We evaluate MOJO on three spiking datasets spanning monkey motor cortex during reaching tasks and multi-regional mouse recordings during vision and decision making tasks, demonstrating superior performance over purely SL-trained models. This improvement is especially pronounced when training with limited labelled data, particularly in few-shot finetuning, where only a small amount of labelled data from a new session is available. Incorporating SSL also yields more interpretable neuronal representations, improving performance on brain region classification and spike-statistics prediction without explicit optimization for these tasks. We further show that MOJO generalizes beyond spiking data to human electrocorticography during speech, where it continues to outperform purely SL-trained models and achieves performance comparable to neuro-foundation models (NFMs) designed specifically for continuous signals. Overall, augmenting spike-tokenizing models with SSL improves performance in label-impoverished settings and enables the use of unlabelled data across various tasks and species, while generalizing to other neural modalities. These results suggest a path towards more flexible and scalable data usage when training NFMs.

</details>


<div id='cs.CR'></div>

# cs.CR [[Back]](#toc)

### [159] [The Entanglement Wall: Activation-Space Probes as Risk Detectors, Not Context Adjudicators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13075)
*Dominik Schwarz*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: Activation probes in residual streams can detect harmful requests with high AUROC but fail to isolate context from surface form; pair-trained classifiers exhibit poor generalization, high false-positive rates on benign edge cases, and function as broad-risk detectors rather than precise context adjudicators.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether residual-stream activation probes can reliably distinguish harmful requests from surface-matched benign controls based purely on context (rather than topic or surface form), and to rigorously evaluate their generalization, leakage, and practical utility as safety mechanisms.

Method: The authors test activation sensors across three 7-8B model families using: (1) taxonomy-selected harmful vs. benign matched-pair datasets (Twin-n70, Twin-n163), (2) XSTest benign edge-case prompts, (3) fully disjoint audit AUROC reconstruction, (4) ten evaluation axes with leakage, hold-out, and permutation controls, and (5) pair-boundary-trained linear classifiers assessed under category and generation-batch hold-out conditions. A separate 24B/32B extension replicates findings.

Result: Activation sensors block 95.5-97.7% of judge-classified compliant attacks but also 59.6-68.4% of XSTest prompts (high false-positive rate). Disjoint audit AUROC reaches 0.996-0.999, but fixed-transfer AUROC on matched pairs drops to 0.656-0.819 (Twin-n70) and 0.590-0.690 (Twin-n163). No axis without direct pair-boundary fitting meets the specified threshold on Twin-n163. Pair-trained classifiers false-block 79.6-100% of XSTest at 95% in-corpus TPR under hold-out conditions.

Conclusion: At tested read points, residual-stream activation scores function as broad-risk detectors rather than standalone context adjudicators. They lack the specificity needed to distinguish harmful intent from superficially similar benign requests, and their performance degrades substantially under rigorous generalization tests, raising concerns about their reliability as safety guardrails.

Abstract: Context can change whether a request is harmful without changing its topic or surface form. We ask whether residual-stream probes distinguish harmful requests from surface-matched benign controls at a useful operating point. Across three 7-8B model families, an activation sensor blocks 95.5-97.7 percent of judge-classified compliant attacks in a taxonomy-selected set. It also blocks 59.6-68.4 percent of XSTest prompts. A fully disjoint audit reconstructs near-ceiling source-contrast AUROC (0.996-0.999), but fixed transfer to matched pairs is weaker: 0.656-0.819 on the guard-selected Twin-n70 subset and 0.590-0.690 on the full Twin-n163 cohort. We test ten axes on the reference family and seven across all families with leakage, hold-out, and permutation controls. On Twin-n163, no axis evaluated without direct pair-boundary fitting reaches the specified numerical threshold. Requiring persistence on that full cohort was added at analysis time. A separately specified 24B/32B extension gives the same result. Pair-trained classifiers weaken under category and generation-batch hold-out and false-block 79.6-100 percent of XSTest at 95 percent in-corpus TPR. At the tested read points, these activation scores behave as broad-risk detectors rather than standalone context adjudicators.

</details>


### [160] [Operational Evidence Gaps for LLMs in Fraud Detection and Trust-and-Safety Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13078)
*Keyur Gabani*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: A survey of 49 operationally relevant sources reveals an evidence gap: while LLMs are increasingly proposed for fraud detection and trust-and-safety workflows, deployment-critical metrics (latency, cost, calibration) are largely unreported, especially in fraud literature. The paper introduces FORTE, a role-and-evidence framework for situating LLMs in operational pipelines, and a minimum deployment-evidence checklist.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM-for-trust-and-safety literature evaluates models in isolation rather than as components within operational pipelines subject to real-world constraints: latency budgets, per-decision costs, escalation paths, human review, and adversarial risk. This creates a gap between academic claims and deployment readiness, motivating a structured survey to assess what deployment evidence actually exists.

Method: The authors conduct a fraud-first survey of deployment evidence, coding 49 operationally relevant sources across three categories: fraud detection/investigation (18), content moderation (14), and cross-cutting robustness (17), supplemented by 15 contextual boundary-setting references. They then develop FORTE, a taxonomy that classifies LLM roles (classifiers, retrieval interfaces, explanation generators, reviewer assistants, agents, feature extractors, escalation components) and a minimum deployment-evidence checklist covering latency, cost, decision thresholds, explanation integrity, and adversarial pressure.

Result: A pronounced evidence imbalance emerges: fraud papers constitute the largest task-specific subset but contain no reports of clean per-decision latency, per-decision dollar cost, or calibration evidence — reporting instead offline task performance, retrieval gains, or case-study accuracy. Moderation papers, by contrast, provide more explicit public evidence on latency, cost, governance, and fairness.

Conclusion: The survey exposes a critical evidence deficit for deploying LLMs in fraud and trust-and-safety pipelines and provides two practical contributions — the FORTE framework and a deployment-evidence checklist — to guide future research toward operationally meaningful evaluation, thereby closing the gap between model-centric benchmarking and production readiness.

Abstract: LLMs are now proposed for fraud detection, scam investigation, content moderation, and other trust-and-safety workflows. Much of the public literature still evaluates them as models, with less attention to their behavior as components in operational pipelines. This creates a practical evidence question: what would justify placing an LLM inside a live workflow with latency, cost, escalation, human-review, and adversarial-risk constraints?
  We address this question through a fraud-first survey of deployment evidence. We code 49 operationally relevant sources on LLM use in fraud detection, investigation support, content moderation, and cross-cutting robustness (18 fraud, 14 moderation, 17 cross-cutting), supplemented by 15 contextual references that establish the survey boundaries. These sources include systems, benchmarks, frameworks, and deployment-relevant surveys, not 49 production deployments.
  The main finding is an evidence imbalance. Fraud supplies the largest task-specific portion of the coded corpus. The moderation papers, however, include more explicit public evidence on latency, cost, governance, and fairness. Among the 18 fraud and investigation sources, none report clean per-decision latency, per-decision dollar cost, or calibration evidence; most report offline task performance, retrieval gains, or case-study accuracy instead.
  The survey contributes a role-and-evidence organizing frame, FORTE, for locating LLMs as classifiers, retrieval interfaces, explanation generators, reviewer assistants, agents, feature extractors, or escalation components. It also contributes a minimum deployment-evidence checklist covering latency budget, cost per decision, decision threshold, explanation integrity, and adversarial pressure. The resulting agenda identifies studies needed to support deployment claims for LLM-based fraud and trust-and-safety work.

</details>


### [161] [SingGuard-NSFA: Extensible Guardrails for Agentic AI via Generative Reasoning and Real-Time Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13081)
*SingGuard Team*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: nsfaguard is a dual-mode guardrail framework for agentic AI security, built atop a CIA-triad-based taxonomy (NSFA) covering 185 risk variants. It combines SFT-based generative reasoning for offline auditing with discriminative classification heads for real-time (~50ms) detection. Four models (0.8B–9B) achieve ≥94% F1 on purpose-built multilingual benchmarks, outperforming existing guardrails by 6–12 points.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic AI systems face diverse operational threats — prompt injection, sensitive data leakage, malicious code execution, tool misuse, and resource exhaustion — yet existing guardrails lack a comprehensive, systematically organized threat taxonomy and fail to provide both interpretable auditing and low-latency runtime protection across many languages.

Method: The authors propose the NSFA taxonomy, organizing 185 risk variants within a CIA-triad (Confidentiality, Integrity, Availability) hierarchy, cross-validated against three OWASP guidelines. A benchmark of 93K+ purpose-built samples across 133 languages and 3,435 cross-source samples is constructed. The dual-mode detection approach uses (1) SFT-based generative reasoning for interpretable offline audits and (2) discriminative classification heads on a frozen backbone for real-time inference at ~50ms. Four model sizes (0.8B, 2B, 4B, 9B) are released; classification heads are shown via ablation to extend detection capabilities beyond the original guardrail scope.

Result: All four models achieve ≥94% F1 on purpose-built benchmarks, surpassing the strongest competing guardrails by 6–12 absolute F1 points. The 9B model attains 91.29% F1 on cross-source evaluation with balanced precision–recall. Ablation experiments demonstrate that classification heads enable state-of-the-art risk detection beyond the model's original scope, validating extensibility as a plug-in enhancement.

Conclusion: nsfaguard provides a comprehensive, taxonomy-driven solution for agentic AI security, combining interpretable offline analysis with real-time detection. Its strong multilingual performance, significant margin over existing guardrails, and plug-in extensibility position it as a general and practical framework for operational threat mitigation in agentic systems.

Abstract: We present nsfaguard, a guardrail framework for securing agentic AI systems against operational threats, such as prompt injection, sensitive information extraction, malicious code requests, dangerous tool misuse, and resource exhaustion. We first introduce the NSFA taxonomy, which organizes 185 risk variants into a CIA-triad-grounded hierarchy and is cross-validated against three well-established OWASP guidelines. Based on this taxonomy, we construct a benchmark suite spanning 133 languages, comprising over 93K purpose-built samples targeting both user queries and agent responses, along with 3,435 cross-source samples adapted from five public agent-security datasets. To detect these operational threats in practice, we develop a dual-mode approach combining SFT-based generative reasoning for interpretable offline auditing with discriminative classification heads on the frozen backbone, enabling real-time detection at approximately 50,ms. We release four models with 0.8B, 2B, 4B, and 9B parameters, all achieving $\geq$94% F1 on purpose-built benchmarks and surpassing the strongest competing guardrails by 6 to 12 absolute points. On cross-source evaluation, the 9B model attains 91.29% F1 with a more balanced precision--recall trade-off. Moreover, ablation experiments show that classification heads can equip a guardrail with risk detection capabilities beyond its original scope and achieve state-of-the-art performance. These results demonstrate the extensibility of the approach and its generality as a plug-in enhancement.

</details>


### [162] [Beyond AI-Generated Labels: Watermarking, Co-Creation, and Conflation of AI-Generation with Disinformation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13082)
*Federico Germani,Giovanni Spitale*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: Watermarking AI-generated content is conceptually flawed: it reduces complex authorship to a misleading binary, provides no truthfulness information, stigmatizes legitimate AI use, and fosters misplaced trust in unmarked content. The authors propose process transparency and information literacy as superior alternatives.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To critically examine whether watermarking mechanisms genuinely address the epistemic and ethical challenges of AI-generated disinformation, particularly in domains where accuracy, intent, and deceptive potential — rather than mere production automation — are the central concerns.

Method: This is a conceptual/critical analysis paper. The authors deconstruct watermarking along two dimensions — invisible (encodes model origin only) and visible (AI-generated labels) — and evaluate their practical and conceptual adequacy against the requirements of truth-assessment and deception detection.

Result: Watermarking fails because: (1) it encodes only provenance, not truthfulness; (2) visible labels create a false binary that misrepresents hybrid human-AI creative processes; (3) it risks stigmatizing legitimate AI-assisted work; and (4) it induces unwarranted trust in unmarked content. These limitations make watermarking conceptually and practically misguided for combating disinformation.

Conclusion: Process transparency and information literacy are more effective than watermark labels for addressing the epistemic and ethical dimensions of AI-generated disinformation. Authorship should be reframed as a transparent human practice rather than reduced to a binary indicator of machine involvement.

Abstract: Watermarking is often presented as a straightforward solution for distinguishing AI-generated from human-generated content, enabling platforms and regulators to trace synthetic content and detect AI-generated outputs at scale. This paper examines whether such mechanisms meaningfully address the epistemic and ethical challenges that arise in domains where the central concern is not the automation of content production, but the accuracy, intent, and deceptive potential of messages. We argue that extending watermark-based approaches to these settings is conceptually and practically misguided. Invisible watermarking encodes only model origin; when operationalized into visible AI-generated labels, it reduces complex creative processes to a misleading binary and provides no information about truthfulness. Such labels may stigmatize legitimate uses of generative tools while encouraging misplaced trust in unmarked content. Here we propose an alternative approach centered on process transparency and information literacy. We argue that these measures address the epistemic and ethical dimensions of AI-generated disinformation more effectively than visible watermark labels, reframing authorship as a transparent human practice rather than a binary indicator of machine involvement.

</details>


### [163] [Phantom Guardrails: When Self-Improving Agent Harnesses Fix Failures That Never Happened](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13083)
*Su Wang,Pin Qian,Yifan Lin,Jingzhou Xu,Yihang Chen,Xiaochong Jiang,Lifei Liu,Haoran Yu*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: Researchers show that self-improving AI agents can hallucinate non-existent failures and "fix" them with unnecessary guardrails. In a controlled experiment, when harmless but rule-shaped patterns appear in legal inputs, an LLM-based proposer invents violations and deploys phantom guardrails that correct nothing — a failure mode invisible to standard suppression-only evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Self-improving AI systems that learn from mistakes rely on accurately detecting failures. However, the process rarely verifies whether a cited failure actually occurred. This paper investigates whether LLM-based proposers can fabricate failures — hallucinating violations that never happened and deploying unnecessary fixes — and under what conditions this phenomenon emerges.

Method: The authors introduce the Counterfactual Fabrication Lab (CFL), a deterministic micro-environment where the ground-truth correct action is "do nothing." The lab plants a candidate guardrail for a failure class that provably never occurs, presents only legal (compliant) episodes, and uses a byte-exact oracle to verify every cited violation. The proposer is tested under two conditions: featureless legal input versus legal input containing a harmless, rule-shaped pattern. Experiments systematically vary three factors — rule-shaped patterns, open-ended rule sets, and failure-presupposing instructions — across single-shot and iterative (add-only accept loop) proposals.

Result: Phantom guardrails emerged in 15/60 runs when rule-shaped patterns were present, versus 0/60 on featureless input. In single-shot proposals, fabrication required all three conditions: a rule-shaped pattern, an open-ended rule set, and a failure-presupposing instruction; removing any one eliminated fabrication. In an add-only accept loop, the fabrication re-entered even without the failure-presupposing instruction (the loop's keep-adding role supplied the demand), and once added, the phantom guardrail persisted. Because the invented guardrail changed no true outcome and could not improve an already-perfect suppression score, it constitutes a distinct phenomenon — neither reward hacking nor over-refusal.

Conclusion: The paper identifies "phantom guardrails" as a novel failure mode in self-improving agent harnesses: fabricated failures that are invisible to suppression-only acceptance metrics. The CFL provides a reproducible method for measuring this phenomenon. The findings highlight that self-improvement loops lacking verification of cited failures risk accumulating unnecessary, potentially harmful modifications.

Abstract: Self-improving AI agents are designed to learn from their mistakes. We show they can also hallucinate mistakes that never happened. We study this failure mode in automated harness optimization, where an LLM-based proposer edits an agent's scaffold, including prompts, parsers, filters, validators and guardrails, to eliminate observed failures. But this process rarely asks first: was there a real failure to fix? We introduce the Counterfactual Fabrication Lab, a deterministic micro-lab where the correct action is known: do nothing. The lab plants a candidate guardrail for a failure class that provably never occurs, presents only legal episodes, and uses a byte-exact oracle to check every cited violation. The proposer behaves as expected on real violations and abstains on featureless legal input. Yet when the legal input contains a harmless pattern resembling a familiar game rule, it invents a failure: in 15/60 runs, versus 0/60 on featureless input, it enables the nonexistent-rule guardrail and cites a violation the oracle refutes. The effect is structured, not indiscriminate. In single-shot proposals it appears only when three conditions coincide: a rule-shaped pattern, an open-ended rule set and an instruction that presupposes failures. Removing any of these conditions eliminates the fabrication. Because the invented guardrail changes no true outcome and cannot improve an already-perfect suppression score, the phenomenon is neither reward hacking nor over-refusal. It is a phantom guardrail: a fix for a failure that never happened, invisible to suppression-only acceptance. Inside an add-only accept loop it re-enters even without the failure-presupposing instruction, the loop's keep-adding role supplying the demand the instruction supplied in single shot, and once in it stays. We present the Counterfactual Fabrication Lab for measuring fabricated failures in self-improving agent harnesses.

</details>


### [164] [Baselines Before Architecture: Evaluating Coding Agents for Autonomous Penetration Testing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13085)
*Ananda Dhakal,Krish Neupane,Aarjan Chaudhary*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper conducts a controlled study on the XBOW benchmark, isolating the performance contributions of LLM backbone models versus security-specific harnesses in autonomous penetration testing. It finds that plain coding agents already solve a large portion of the benchmark, specialized harnesses add measurable but modest lift, and model scaling provides substantial gains — urging future work to report model-matched plain-agent baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recent autonomous penetration testing systems report high benchmark scores but conflate improvements from architectural harnesses with those from underlying LLM upgrades, making it unclear where the real performance gains originate. The work seeks to disentangle these factors through controlled experimentation.

Method: A three-phase controlled study on the 104-task XBOW benchmark: (1) running Codex, OpenCode, and Pi plain CLI agents with identical GPT-5 backend, budget, interface, and scoring to establish same-model baselines and test security-specific prompts; (2) comparing the strongest plain-agent scaffold against published MAPTA and PentestGPT V2 results under closest model matches; (3) repeating the plain-agent experiment with GPT-5.2 and GPT-5.5 to measure model scaling effects within a fixed scaffold.

Result: Specialized harnesses provide measurable benchmark lift and may improve cost efficiency, but plain coding agents already solve a large share of the XBOW benchmark. Repeated plain-agent runs can match or exceed published architecture scores in union coverage. Newer models substantially improve performance within the same scaffold.

Conclusion: Future evaluations in autonomous penetration testing should report model-matched plain-agent baselines before attributing benchmark gains to architectural design alone, as both model scaling and harness design contribute to performance in ways that are easily confounded.

Abstract: Recent autonomous penetration testing papers report high benchmark scores while adding multi-component security harnesses around frontier LLMs. Because these systems often change both architecture and backbone model, it is difficult to tell how much performance comes from the harness rather than from the underlying model.
  This paper presents a controlled study on the 104-task XBOW benchmark using default coding CLI agents as plain-agent baselines. We first run Codex, OpenCode, and Pi with the same GPT-5 model, budget, target interface, and scoring rule. This phase identifies the strongest same-model baseline and tests whether security-specific prompt variants improve its observed score. We then compare the default Codex scaffold with published MAPTA and PentestGPT V2 results under the closest available model matches. Finally, we repeat the plain-agent experiment with GPT-5.2 and GPT-5.5 to measure model scaling inside the same scaffold.
  The results show a mixed but practical picture. Specialised harnesses can add measurable benchmark lift and may improve cost efficiency, but plain coding agents already solve a large share of the benchmark; repeated plain-agent runs can match or exceed some published architecture scores in union coverage, and newer models substantially improve the same scaffold. Future evaluations should report model-matched plain-agent baselines before attributing benchmark gains to architecture design alone.

</details>


### [165] [GDM AI Control Roadmap](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13087)
*Mary Phuong,Erik Jenner,Laurent Simon,Lewis Ho,Rohin Shah,Sebastian Farquhar,Scott Coull*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper presents the GDM AI Control Roadmap (v0.1), a security blueprint for defending against potentially misaligned AI agents in internal deployments. It introduces TRAIT&R (a MITRE ATT&CK–based taxonomy for AI adversary tactics), capability-based mitigation tiers (Detection D1–D4, Prevention & Response R1–R3), and 15 concrete tiered defensive measures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As AI agents become deeply integrated into frontier AI companies' workflows (R&D, cyber-defence, science), the assumption of perfect alignment is unsafe. A layered security approach is needed to mitigate risks from potentially misaligned AI, requiring a structured, capability-graded defensive framework.

Method: The authors develop (1) TRAIT&R, a taxonomy mapping hypothetical AI adversary tactics onto the MITRE ATT&CK framework; (2) a tiered capability-based mitigation scheme linking defensive measures to model capability levels; and (3) a portfolio of 15 practical mitigations spanning low-cost current-model interventions to advanced future-model safeguards.

Result: The roadmap defines four Detection tiers (D1–D4) and three Prevention & Response tiers (R1–R3). Fifteen concrete mitigations are organized across tiers, ranging from chain-of-thought monitoring and asynchronous alerts for current models, to real-time access control, system-level anomaly detection, internal activations monitoring, and shutdown infrastructure for advanced future models.

Conclusion: AI control is a nascent field requiring difficult trade-offs between security and developer velocity. The roadmap is expected to evolve with operational experience and advances in the field.

Abstract: AI agents are rapidly accelerating work at frontier AI companies, helping with AI R&D, cyber-defence, and advancing scientific discoveries. As these agents become more tightly integrated into our systems, unlocking their full potential requires rethinking how we do security. We should not assume that AI agents are always perfectly aligned, but should instead build in multiple layers of defence. We present the GDM AI Control Roadmap (v0.1) -- a first-of-its-kind blueprint for internal security against potentially misaligned AI. This report provides:
  * Threat modelling: We adopt a conservative approach to threat modelling and assume a hypothetical AI adversary pursuing undesirable goals in internal deployment. We introduce TRAIT&R, a taxonomy of tactics and techniques available to such a hypothetical AI adversary, building on the established security framework MITRE ATT&CK.
  * Capability-based mitigation: Because controlling more capable models requires more costly interventions, we link specific defensive measures to evolving model capabilities (such as the ability to reason opaquely or execute complex cyberattacks). As models get more powerful, our defences should escalate accordingly. We outline four Detection tiers (D1-D4) and three Prevention and Response tiers (R1-R3).
  * A portfolio of practical defences: We suggest 15 concrete, tiered mitigations. These range from low-cost interventions for current models (e.g., chain-of-thought monitoring, asynchronous alerts) to advanced safeguards for future models (e.g., real-time access control, system-level anomaly detection, internal activations monitoring, and shutdown infrastructure).
  AI control is a nascent field, and implementing these mitigations requires navigating difficult trade-offs between security and developer velocity. We expect the roadmap to evolve as we gain more experience and as the field in turn evolves.

</details>


### [166] [Securing LLMs in the Wild: Privacy and Security Challenges at the Edge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13088)
*Ren-Yi Huang,Mingchen Li,Dumindu Samaraweera,Morris Chang*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: The paper identifies a "Security-Efficiency Paradox" in edge-deployed LLMs, where necessary optimizations (quantization, pruning, partitioning, adaptation) introduce vulnerabilities. It proposes a taxonomy based on three architectural constraints (Memory, Quadratic, Compute Walls), a unified constraint model, the Secure Operational Efficiency Score (SOES) metric, and a decision procedure for balancing security, privacy, and efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Organizations are increasingly moving LLMs to edge and on-premise deployments due to data sovereignty, compliance, latency, and third-party dependence concerns. However, limited compute and memory on edge devices force aggressive optimizations that degrade security and privacy, creating a tension the authors term the Security-Efficiency Paradox. No existing framework jointly addresses the security, privacy, and efficiency trade-offs in resource-constrained LLM deployments.

Method: The authors develop a deployment-centric taxonomy organized around three architectural constraints: the Memory Wall (bandwidth limitations), the Quadratic Wall (self-attention complexity), and the Compute Wall (FLOP limitations). They derive a unified constraint model quantifying when unsafe optimizations become necessary, linking each wall to specific attack surfaces. They then propose the Secure Operational Efficiency Score (SOES), a holistic metric integrating task accuracy, jailbreak resistance, and privacy metrics against energy, memory, and latency. A practical decision procedure and targeted mitigations are provided for each optimization-induced vulnerability.

Result: The paper demonstrates that compression techniques (quantization, pruning) can degrade safety alignment; partitioned inference across edge nodes enables gradient-based reconstruction attacks; and continuous local adaptation (fine-tuning, PEFT) can leak private training data and cause model drift. The unified constraint model formally links these vulnerabilities to specific resource walls, and SOES provides a quantifiable framework for navigating trade-offs.

Conclusion: Securing edge-native LLMs requires a co-designed approach integrating security, privacy, and efficiency rather than treating them as separate concerns. The SOES metric and accompanying decision procedure give practitioners actionable tools to configure edge LLMs under real-world hardware constraints, laying a foundation for building secure edge-native intelligent systems.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are rapidly moving from research settings into the wild, deployed on enterprise infrastructure, personal devices, and edge platforms. While cloud deployments offer scalable compute, concerns over data sovereignty, compliance, latency, and third-party dependence are driving organizations toward edge and on-premise LLMs. This shift introduces new security and privacy challenges: limited compute and memory force aggressive optimizations, including quantization, pruning, model partitioning, and parameter-efficient adaptation, each of which can introduce vulnerabilities and reshape the threat landscape. We describe this tension as the Security-Efficiency Paradox, mechanisms that improve efficiency may weaken robustness, expose new attack surfaces, or increase privacy risks. We examine how compression can degrade safety alignment, how partitioned inference enables reconstruction attacks, and how continuous local adaptation may cause privacy leakage and model drift. To analyze these risks, we introduce a deployment-centric taxonomy organized around three architectural constraints: the Memory Wall, the Quadratic Wall, and the Compute Wall. We derive a unified constraint model that quantifies when unsafe optimizations become unavoidable, linking each wall to specific attack surfaces. Building on this model, we propose the Secure Operational Efficiency Score (SOES), a holistic metric balancing task accuracy, jailbreak resistance, and privacy against energy, memory, and latency, enabling practitioners to configure edge LLMs under real-world hardware limits. We further present a practical decision procedure and targeted mitigations for each optimization-induced vulnerability. Together, these contributions provide a co-designed framework for jointly evaluating security, privacy, and efficiency, laying a foundation for securing edge-native intelligent systems.

</details>


### [167] [Efficient and Privacy Aware Edge Cloud Collaborative Inference for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13093)
*Yi Li,Chen Li,Jiexiong Liu*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: A privacy-preserving edge-cloud collaborative LLM inference framework using endpoint-authenticated KV cache, achieving up to 46.1% latency reduction and 67.4% downlink payload reduction while maintaining cloud-level performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: On-device LLM inference faces a trilemma: cloud inference offers strong compute but compromises privacy by exposing user prompts and dialogue; standalone on-device inference is infeasible for most consumer and embedded edge devices due to hardware constraints. There is a need for a solution that balances latency, limited resources, and privacy.

Method: The framework splits LLM inference between local endpoints and cloud: endpoints handle input preprocessing, embedding, adaptive feature optimization, KV cache authentication, speculative decoding, and low-dimensional model head computation; the cloud performs authenticated decoder inference, KV cache management, token verification, and high-dimensional vocabulary projection. Endpoints fuse partial outputs with language-adaptive masking and sample target tokens. All transmitted data and truncated logits are quantized and AES-GCM encrypted. Core lightweight modules, draft parameters, and cache access policies remain local. Supports heterogeneous devices (CPU-only, GPU, embedded) via optimized streaming, batching, and quantized ONNX deployment.

Result: The framework reduces per-token latency by up to 46.1% and downlink payloads by up to 67.4% compared to baseline split inference, while retaining performance comparable to full cloud inference.

Conclusion: The proposed edge-cloud collaborative framework effectively reconciles the latency-privacy-resource trilemma by offloading heavy computation to the cloud while keeping sensitive data and lightweight operations local, achieving substantial efficiency gains without sacrificing inference quality.

Abstract: On-device LLM inference faces a trilemma of response latency, limited hardware resources and user privacy. Full cloud inference delivers strong computing power but exposes user prompts and dialogue data, while standalone on-device inference is unfeasible for most consumer and embedded edge devices. This paper presents a privacy-centric edge-cloud collaborative LLM inference framework built on endpoint-authenticated KV cache. Local endpoints handle input preprocessing, embedding computation, adaptive feature optimization, KV cache authentication, speculative decoding and low-dimensional model head calculation, while the cloud conducts authenticated decoder inference, KV cache management, token verification and high-dimensional vocabulary projection. Endpoints fuse partial outputs, apply language-adaptive masking and sample target tokens. All transmitted data and truncated logits are quantized and AES-GCM encrypted for privacy, with core lightweight modules, draft parameters and cache access policies kept local to avoid leakage. The framework supports heterogeneous devices including CPU-only, GPU-equipped and embedded devices via optimized streaming, batching and quantized ONNX deployment. Evaluations demonstrate that the framework reduces per-token latency by up to 46.1\% and downlink payloads by up to 67.4\% over baseline split inference, retaining comparable performance to full cloud inference.

</details>


### [168] [WaterMoE: Expert-Routing-based Watermarking for High Fidelity and Efficiency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13099)
*Z Sun,Q Jiang,S Sheng,L Xiang*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: WaterMoE is a watermarking scheme for MoE LLMs that embeds watermarks via controlled perturbation of expert selection in routers, achieving negligible quality loss and only ~1% inference latency overhead—dramatically outperforming existing methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM watermarking methods suffer from two critical barriers to practical adoption: (i) severely degraded model performance, especially on restricted/complex tasks beyond simple text fluency, and (ii) significant additional inference overhead that precludes deployment in latency-sensitive systems.

Method: WaterMoE works by injecting watermark signals through controlled perturbation of the expert selection process at each MoE router. These perturbations accumulate across router decisions to produce a detectable token selection shift at the final output. Unlike post-processing token-sampling watermarking, WaterMoE operates within the inference loop itself. The authors also construct a comprehensive benchmark across diverse generation tasks to systematically evaluate 9 representative watermarking methods.

Result: WaterMoE achieves fidelity near unwatermarked models and consistently outperforms SOTA watermarking methods on the benchmark. It delivers up to 4× speedup over existing approaches with only ~1% additional inference latency versus native generation.

Conclusion: WaterMoE demonstrates strong practical viability for real-world deployment, effectively resolving the performance-degradation and inference-overhead problems that have hindered watermarking adoption in production LLM systems.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) have achieved remarkable success but raise growing concerns about content provenance and misuse, motivating the need for reliable watermarking techniques. However, these techniques have rarely been adopted in practice mainly for two reasons: i) severely degraded model performance, and ii) additional inference overhead. To confirm the problem, we construct a comprehensive benchmark spanning different generation tasks to systematically evaluate 9 representative watermarking methods. We found almost all existing methods are designed for text fluency, but not for restricted and complicated tasks, and their overhead prevents them from deployment in latency-critical systems.
  To address i) and ii), we propose an LLM watermarking scheme \textit{WaterMoE} for the growingly popular Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) LLMs. WaterMoE embeds watermarking signals through controlled perturbation into the expert selection at each router, which accumulates to token selection shift at the final output. In contrast to watermarking as a post-processing token-sampling approach, WaterMoE embeds watermark within the inference loop incurring negligible quality degradation and computational overhead. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our method achieves a fidelity performance close to the unwatermarked and consistently outperforms state-of-the-art watermarking methods on the benchmark, with up to $4\times$ speedup, incurring merely 1\% additional inference latency compared to native generation. The results demonstrate the capability of WaterMoE to be deployed in real-world tasks.

</details>


### [169] [Designing a GDPR-Compliant Security Architecture for Remote Elderly Care Systems: A Privacy-by-Design Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13122)
*Md. Rahid Parvez,Mikael Soini*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: The paper presents the Secure Edge Gateway (SEG) framework, an integrated IoMT security architecture for remote elderly care that simultaneously addresses GDPR-compliant edge-layer pseudonymisation, zero-interaction usability for elderly users, and STRIDE-based threat validation within a single unified design validated through software simulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing IoMT security architectures for remote elderly care fail to simultaneously address three interdependent challenges: GDPR-compliant edge-layer pseudonymisation, elderly-specific zero-interaction usability as a binding architectural constraint, and integrated STRIDE-based threat validation — creating a tripartite gap that no prior unified design has resolved.

Method: An ESP32-WROOM-32 residential gateway implements MAC address whitelisting, HMAC-SHA256 cryptographic pseudonymisation before network transmission, AES-128-CBC payload encryption, and TLS 1.3 transport security. Validation employs software-based simulation, full STRIDE threat modelling across all six categories, attack tree analysis, GDPR compliance mapping across nine regulatory obligations, and a Data Protection Impact Assessment (DPIA) under Article 35.

Result: Published benchmarks confirm MQTT consumes 6-8% less energy than HTTP in comparable IoT deployments, and edge processing achieves sub-50 ms response latency versus 200-700 ms for cloud-only systems. The framework demonstrates GDPR compliance and operational efficiency are complementary rather than competing objectives.

Conclusion: GDPR compliance and operational efficiency are complementary — not competing — objectives in resource-constrained IoMT deployments for elderly care. The SEG framework provides a validated blueprint that harmonises regulatory compliance, security, and usability within a single architecture.

Abstract: IoMT-based remote elderly care systems generate continuous streams of sensitive health data, yet existing security architectures have not simultaneously addressed three interdependent challenges: GDPR-compliant edge-layer pseudonymisation, elderly-specific zero-interaction usability as a binding architectural constraint, and integrated STRIDE-based threat validation within a single unified design. This paper presents the Secure Edge Gateway (SEG) framework - a software-simulation-validated integrated IoMT security architecture for elderly care designed to resolve all three dimensions of this tripartite gap simultaneously. An ESP32-WROOM-32 residential gateway enforces MAC address whitelisting, HMAC-SHA256 cryptographic pseudonymisation before any network transmission, AES-128-CBC payload encryption, and TLS 1.3 transport security, in compliance with GDPR Articles 25 and 32. The framework is validated through software-based simulation, full STRIDE threat modelling across all six categories, attack tree analysis, GDPR compliance mapping across nine regulatory obligations, and a Data Protection Impact Assessment (DPIA) under Article 35. Published benchmarks confirm MQTT consumes 6-8% less energy than HTTP in comparable IoT deployments, and edge processing achieves sub-50 ms response latency versus 200-700 ms for cloud-only systems. The results demonstrate that GDPR compliance and operational efficiency are complementary - not competing - objectives in resource-constrained IoMT deployments for elderly care.

</details>


### [170] [AI in Cyberpsychology: A systematic literature review of Cybersecurity enhancement by using AI for analyzing psychology of Victims, Attackers, and Defenders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13123)
*Georg Thamer Francis,Malek Malkawi,Sevim Eyüpoğlu,Reda Alhajj,Selim Akyokuş*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: A systematic literature review analyzing 34 studies on AI-integrated cyberpsychology (AI-CPSY), categorizing research by cybersecurity applications (anomaly detection, vulnerability risk prediction, security awareness training, authentication/identity verification), AI methods (ML, DL, NLP, RL), and psychological concepts, while identifying research gaps and emerging trends.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The increasing integration of AI with cyberpsychology since the early 2010s lacks a structured, comprehensive review. There is a need to systematically map how AI techniques are being applied to decode personality traits and behavioral patterns of cybersecurity actors (victims, attackers, defenders), and to consolidate fragmented knowledge across this interdisciplinary domain.

Method: The authors conducted a systematic literature review (SLR) following the PRISMA (Preferred Reporting Items for Systematic Reviews and Meta-Analyses) methodology. They selected and analyzed 34 research studies, then developed a comprehensive taxonomy classifying them across: (1) four cybersecurity application categories — Anomaly Detection (AD), Vulnerability Risk Prediction (VRP), Security Awareness Training (SAT), and Authentication/Identity Verification (AIV); (2) AI methods including machine learning (ML), deep learning (DL), natural language processing (NLP), and reinforcement learning (RL); and (3) psychological concepts employed. The review also quantified datasets used and assessed implementation/deployment status.

Result: The review produced a structured taxonomy mapping the AI-CPSY landscape across application domains, AI methodologies, and psychological concepts. It identified the most commonly utilized psychological constructs, quantified dataset usage patterns, assessed current implementation maturity, and pinpointed research gaps and open challenges. Emerging and effective methodological trends within AI-CPSY were deduced from the cross-study analysis.

Conclusion: AI-CPSY is an evolving interdisciplinary field with distinct application clusters. While ML and DL dominate, significant gaps remain in deployment readiness and standardized psychological frameworks. The taxonomy provides a foundation for future research to address open challenges and leverage emerging AI methodologies for psychologically-informed cybersecurity.

Abstract: Cybersecurity is the practice of protecting systems, networks, and data from digital attacks. Cyberpsychology (CPSY) is defined as the use of psychology to enhance cybersecurity applications. Since the early 2010s, the evolution of Artificial Intelligence (AI) has increasingly integrated with CPSY, leveraging advanced data analysis to decode the distinct personality traits and behavioral patterns of victims, attackers, and defenders. In this systematic literature review (SLR), we carefully analyze 34 collected research studies of AI usage in cyberpsychology (AI-CPSY) using the preferred reporting items for systematic reviews and meta-analyses (PRISMA) methodology. The review presents a comprehensive taxonomy of the cyber-security applications, the AI methodologies used, and the psychological concepts employed across the studies . We sort the research studies into four cybersecurity applications: Anomaly Detection (AD), Vulnerability Risk Prediction (VRP), Security Awareness Training (SAT), and Authentication/Identity Verification (AIV). Within each application area, studies are further sorted according to the AI method used including machine learning (ML), deep learning (DL), natural language processing (NLP), and reinforcement learning (RL). Furthermore, the review identifies the most commonly utilized psychological concepts, quantify the datasets used in the field, and present their current implementation and deployment status. At last, it detect research gaps, present open challenges, and deduce the trending and most effective and emerging methodologies used across the AI-CPSY landscape.

</details>


### [171] [Composable Trust for Language Models: A proven boundary and a measured defense](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13149)
*Yakov Pyotr Shkolnikov*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper addresses prompt injection vulnerabilities in LLMs by introducing a trust model where authority resides in code outside the model. A deterministic pipeline ranks inputs by source integrity, and a provable monitor selects operations from trusted inputs only. Using Gemma 4 26B, the cascade raises the genuine-leak defended rate from 27% to 94% while preserving 96% clean quality, and the proved boundary holds unconditionally under adaptive red-teaming.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current language models process instructions and data in a single token stream, making them inherently vulnerable to prompt injection — untrusted text can steer model behavior because nothing inside the model distinguishes trustworthy from untrustworthy content. The authors seek a principled defense where authority is determined by source standing rather than content, ensuring lower-trust sources cannot override higher-trust ones.

Method: The authors develop a trust model that externalizes authority to code outside the language model. An unmodified model runs within a deterministic pipeline that ranks inputs by source integrity. A fixed, non-model monitor provably selects which operation to execute and which outside action to take, using only trusted inputs. Two mechanisms are employed: (1) passivation, which neutralizes untrusted content, and (2) a cascade wrapper that layers operations by trust level. The pipeline also enables attribution — correctly crediting facts to lower-trust sources rather than discarding them. The approach is prompt-tuned and evaluated against both one-shot held-out sets and adaptive red-teaming.

Result: On a one-shot held-out set with Gemma 4 26B, the cascade raises the genuine-leak defended rate from 27% to 94% at a clean-quality cost of ~4% (Q_rel = 0.96). Under adaptive red-teaming, the provable boundary holds unconditionally, and the measured defense remains at 87%. Attribution of lower-trust source facts rises from 0% to 92%, and the system correctly follows the higher-trust source during conflicts.

Conclusion: Moving authority outside the model into a source-integrity-based, provably correct monitor offers a robust defense against prompt injection. The approach achieves high defended rates with minimal quality degradation, maintains unconditional guarantees under adversarial conditions, and preserves useful functionality like attribution. This suggests that architectural separation of trust, rather than content-based filtering, is a viable path toward secure LLM deployments.

Abstract: In a language model, instructions and data share one token stream, so nothing inside the model's generation can keep untrusted text from steering it. We develop a trust model that places the authority to act outside the model, in code: a source's standing, not its content, decides which operation runs and whether it acts. A lower-trust source may inform an answer but not override a higher one. An unmodified model runs inside a deterministic pipeline that ranks inputs by source integrity, and a fixed non-model monitor provably chooses the operation and any outside action from trusted inputs alone. We can measure but not prove the pipeline's resistance to injection; we prompt-tune it and report the rate. On a one-shot held-out set with an unmodified Gemma~4 26B model, passivation and a wrapper (the cascade) raise the genuine-leak defended rate from $27\%$ to $94\%$ at roughly a $4\%$ clean-quality cost ($Q_{\mathrm{rel}}{=}0.96$). Under adaptive red-teaming the proved boundary holds unconditionally, and the measured defense stays at $87\%$. The cascade also attributes a lower-trust source's fact rather than dropping it, raising attribution from $0\%$ to $92\%$, and follows the higher-trust source on a conflict.

</details>


### [172] [BARS: Benign-Anchored Ranking and Selection for False Alarm Reduction in Network Intrusion Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13203)
*Abu Fuad Ahmad,Istiaque Ahmed*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: BARS improves filter-based feature selection for NIDS by anchoring deviation scores to the benign class mean instead of the global mean, reducing false positives on attack-majority data while maintaining linear-time efficiency and low memory footprint compared to correlation-based methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: False alarms plague NIDS deployment. Filter-based feature selection is attractive for its zero inference cost, but classical filters are class-symmetric (ignoring the benign-baseline/attack-deviation asymmetry). CMD introduced class-asymmetric filtering but uses a global mean anchor that shifts toward attack distributions under class imbalance, weakening its deviation-based discriminative power.

Method: BARS is a two-stage filter: (1) Benign-Anchored Ranking — replaces CMD's global mean with the benign-class mean to compute deviation scores, preventing anchor shift under class imbalance; (2) an order-preserving decorrelation step to reduce redundancy among selected features. Evaluated across three benchmarks (CICIDS2017, CICDDoS2019, UNSW-NB15) at feature budgets k ∈ {5,10,20,30,40}.

Result: On attack-majority datasets (where global-anchor bias is strongest): BARS reduces FPR vs. CMD by 15.4% on UNSW-NB15 (k=20) and by 21–23% on CICDDoS2019 at small budgets, while preserving TPR and macro-F1. On benign-majority data, BARS and CMD converge (global and benign anchors coincide theoretically). Pearson Correlation and Mutual Information sometimes achieve lower FPR but exceeded 1 TB memory on larger benchmarks; BARS retains linear-time scoring and low memory.

Conclusion: BARS is a principled refinement of CMD, not a universally dominant filter. It excels on attack-majority data where anchor bias matters most, and converges to CMD on benign-majority data. Its linear complexity and low memory make it practical for resource-constrained NIDS deployments where correlation-based filters are infeasible.

Abstract: False alarms remain a major barrier to deploying network intrusion detection systems (NIDS). In high-volume environments, even a sub-1% false positive rate can generate tens of thousands of daily alerts. Filter-based feature selection is attractive because it operates upstream of the classifier and adds no inference-time cost. However, classical filters use class-symmetric criteria that ignore the asymmetry of intrusion detection, where benign traffic defines the baseline and attacks are deviations from it. A recent class-asymmetric filter, Classwise Mean Deviation (CMD), addresses this issue but anchors its score to a global mean that shifts toward attack distributions under class imbalance, weakening the deviations it aims to capture.
  We propose Benign-Anchored Ranking and Selection (BARS), a two-stage filter that replaces CMD's global anchor with the benign-class mean and applies an order-preserving decorrelation step. We evaluate BARS on CICIDS2017, CICDDoS2019, and UNSW-NB15 using feature budgets k = {5, 10, 20, 30, 40}. On attack-majority datasets, where global-anchor bias is strongest, BARS reduces false positive rate relative to CMD by 15.4% on UNSW-NB15 at k = 20 and by 21% to 23% on CICDDoS2019 at small feature budgets while preserving true positive rate and macro-F1. On benign-majority data, BARS and CMD converge, consistent with the theoretical limit where global- and benign-anchored scores coincide. BARS is a principled refinement of CMD rather than a universally dominant filter. Although Pearson Correlation and Mutual Information often achieve lower false positive rates, they exceeded 1 TB of memory on the largest benchmarks in our evaluation. BARS retains linear-time scoring and a low memory footprint, making it suitable for resource-constrained deployments.

</details>


### [173] [Why Not Fix It Once and for All? An Empirical Study of Multiple Patches for Vulnerability Fixes in Open-Source Software](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13206)
*Weiliang Qi,Youpeng Li,Xinda Wang*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: A systematic manual analysis of 1,646 multi-patch vulnerability fixes yields a taxonomy of three primary categories and six subcategories based on root causes. The study compares multi-patch versus single-patch fix characteristics, examines feature variations across categories, and evaluates vulnerability detection methods for verifying remediation completeness during the multi-patch fixing process.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Security patches are fundamental to vulnerability remediation, yet determining precisely when a vulnerability is fully remediated across a sequence of multiple patches remains an open challenge. Existing research lacks a systematic understanding of why vulnerabilities require multiple patches, how multi-patch fixes differ from single-patch fixes, and how to verify that remediation is complete.

Method: The authors conduct a large-scale manual examination of 1,646 multi-patch fix records from open-source software. They develop a taxonomy categorizing the root causes of multi-patch fixes into three primary categories with six subcategories. They then perform a comparative analysis of characteristics between multi-patch and single-patch fixes, examine feature variations across taxonomic categories, and assess the effectiveness of representative vulnerability detection methods for validating complete remediation during multi-patch fixing.

Result: The study produces a novel taxonomy of multi-patch fix root causes (three primary categories, six subcategories), identifies distinctive characteristics that differentiate multi-patch from single-patch fixes, reveals feature variations across different categories, and provides an empirical assessment of how well existing vulnerability detection methods can validate remediation completeness in multi-patch scenarios.

Conclusion: Multi-patch fixes exhibit distinct patterns and characteristics compared to single-patch fixes. The proposed taxonomy and empirical findings offer new insights into the nature of multi-patch vulnerability remediation and establish a foundation for future research on automated verification of fix completeness across patch sequences.

Abstract: Security patches for open-source software constitute a foundational resource for vulnerability remediation research and practice. However, analyzing and applying multiple patches remains challenging, especially when trying to determine at what point in a patch sequence a vulnerability is fully remediated. This paper presents a systematic analysis of multi-patch vulnerability fixes, focusing on their root causes, characteristics, and methods for verifying remediation status throughout the fixing process. Through a manual examination of 1,646 multi-patch fix records, we develop a taxonomy with three primary categories and six subcategories based on their causes. We then compare the distinctive characteristics of multi-patch fixes with those of single-patch fixes and analyze feature variations across categories. In addition, we assess representative vulnerability detection methods for validating complete remediation during multi-patch fixing. Our findings provide new insights into multi-patch fixes and lay a foundation for future research in this field.

</details>


### [174] [Proof in a Bottle: Long-Lived Verifiable Secret Sharing via Pre-Quantum Commitment and Immutable Ledger Binding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13235)
*Markus Jakobsson,Keir Finlow-Bates*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: PiB decouples verifiability from long-term binding in VSS by using Pedersen commitments for pre-quantum consistency checks and anchoring salted, index-bound hashes to an immutable ledger, enabling commit-now, reveal-later guarantees against future quantum attacks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional VSS relies on binding commitments that are vulnerable to retroactive quantum attacks by a CRQC running Shor's algorithm. The key insight is that binding is only essential at dealing time, not perpetually — existing schemes unnecessarily couple verifiability with indefinite binding, creating an avoidable quantum vulnerability.

Method: PiB decouples the two requirements: (1) standard Pedersen commitments provide zero-knowledge, publicly verifiable consistency during a pre-quantum window; (2) a salted, index-bound hash of the full share set is anchored to an immutable public ledger (e.g., blockchain), cryptographically preserving the pre-quantum binding property into the post-quantum era without requiring quantum-resistant primitives throughout.

Result: The scheme achieves a commit-now, reveal-later guarantee: honest dealings conducted before a CRQC exists remain binding even after quantum adversaries emerge, without modifying the underlying VSS protocol or requiring post-quantum cryptographic primitives at dealing time.

Conclusion: PiB provides a practical, minimally invasive upgrade path for VSS-based systems against retrospective quantum decryption, relying only on the existence of an immutable timestamping ledger rather than full post-quantum migration.

Abstract: Traditional secret sharing techniques such as Verifiable Secret sharing (VSS) are vulnerable to quantum attacks by a Cryptographically Relevant Quantum Computer (CRQC) running Shor's algorithm. We observe that the binding a VSS needs is required only at the moment of dealing, and this binding can be made before any CRQC exists. We propose Proof in a Bottle (PiB), which decouples verifiability from long-term binding: standard Pedersen commitments provide zero-knowledge, publicly checkable consistency during a pre-quantum window, while a salted, index-bound hash of the share set, anchored to an immutable public ledger, preserves the binding established in that window into the post-quantum era. The guarantee is explicitly a commit-now, reveal-later one: it protects today's honest dealings against tomorrow's quantum adversary.

</details>


### [175] [Privacy Preserving Recommender Systems Balancing Personalization with Privacy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13328)
*Ranjeet K Jha,Venkata Suresh Gummadilli*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: A privacy-preserving recommendation framework combining federated learning, differential privacy, cohort-level modeling, and privacy-aware agents is proposed. Evaluated on synthetic retail datasets, it maintains competitive recommendation quality at moderate privacy budgets (ε≈5), balancing personalization with regulatory compliance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern e-commerce recommendation systems rely on centralized user data, creating privacy risks and regulatory burdens under GDPR, CCPA, and CPRA. There is a pressing need for systems that preserve user privacy without substantially degrading recommendation quality.

Method: The framework integrates four components: (1) federated learning to keep raw user data decentralized, (2) differential privacy to inject mathematically bounded noise into model updates, (3) cohort-level modeling, and (4) privacy-aware intelligent agents. Recommendation models evaluated include matrix factorization, neural collaborative filtering, and GRU4Rec under varying privacy budgets. An interactive Streamlit dashboard visualizes performance, privacy-utility trade-offs, ranking stability, and fairness.

Result: Under moderate privacy budgets (ε≈5), the framework maintains competitive recommendation quality as measured by CTR, Precision@K, Recall@K, and NDCG@K, demonstrating that strong privacy guarantees can be achieved with limited impact on effectiveness.

Conclusion: The proposed framework offers a scalable, practical approach for deploying privacy-preserving recommendation systems that balance personalization, regulatory compliance, and business objectives in next-generation AI-driven retail platforms.

Abstract: Personalized recommendation systems are central to modern e-commerce and retail platforms, but they typically rely on centralized storage of detailed user interaction data, creating significant privacy and regulatory challenges. With increasing requirements from regulations such as GDPR, CCPA, and CPRA, organizations must develop recommendation systems that preserve user privacy without substantially degrading recommendation quality.
  This work presents and evaluates a privacy-preserving recommendation framework that combines federated learning, differential privacy, cohort-level modeling, and privacy-aware intelligent agents. The framework keeps raw user data decentralized while introducing mathematically bounded noise to model updates. Experiments were conducted on synthetic retail datasets that emulate customer clickstream and purchase behavior. Recommendation quality was evaluated using Click-Through Rate (CTR), Precision@K, Recall@K, and Normalized Discounted Cumulative Gain (NDCG@K) across multiple differential privacy budgets.
  We evaluate matrix factorization, neural collaborative filtering, and GRU4Rec under varying privacy constraints and analyze the trade-off between privacy and utility. An interactive Streamlit dashboard was developed to visualize recommendation performance, ranking stability, privacy-utility trade-offs, and fairness metrics. Results show that the proposed framework maintains competitive recommendation quality at moderate privacy budgets (approximately $ε\approx 5$), demonstrating that strong privacy guarantees can be achieved with limited impact on recommendation effectiveness.
  This work provides a practical framework for deploying privacy-preserving recommendation systems that balance personalization, regulatory compliance, and business objectives, offering a scalable approach for next-generation AI-driven retail platforms.

</details>


### [176] [The Refusal Residue: When Probes Catch Alignment Faking and When They Don't](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13346)
*Aman Mehta*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: Alignment faking emerges naturally in Qwen3-32B and Llama-3.1-8B. Hidden states carry detectable signals of faking, but detection is model-conditional and steering fails to control behavior. The paper identifies critical methodological flaws in standard probing (leakage, naive linear probes, MLP overstatement) and proposes a rigorous five-control measurement framework.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Alignment faking is dangerous because a model may appear compliant when monitored but revert to misaligned behavior when unmonitored. Since outputs alone cannot distinguish strategic from genuine compliance when no reasoning scratchpad is visible, the authors investigate whether internal hidden states can reveal what outputs conceal.

Method: A 13-model sweep identifies models exhibiting natural alignment faking. For the two faking models (Qwen3-32B, Llama-3.1-8B), the authors apply: (1) hidden state probing with a leakage-free leave-one-query-out protocol; (2) representation shift analysis comparing compliance vs. refusal residuals; (3) activation steering over 2,000 runs; and (4) a systematic evaluation exposing flaws in standard probing practices (residualized leakage, naive linear probes, MLP overstatement). They release a five-control framework: multi-token extraction, refuse-vs-refuse confound checks, per-fold residualization, leave-one-query-out evaluation, and orthogonality-constrained probing.

Result: Natural faking emerged in Qwen3-32B (+18.2pp) and Llama-3.1-8B (+24.4pp at n=10, p<10^-15). Explicit scratchpad self-reports were rare (1.4% Qwen, 0.0% Llama). Both fakers exhibited asymmetric refusal residue: monitored compliance shifted toward refusal (Qwen d=0.32, Llama d=0.33, both p<0.0001), but refusal representations did not move (p=1.00). Leave-one-query-out probing achieved AUROC 0.87 on Llama but collapsed to 0.43 on Qwen, with zero cross-model transfer. Steering failed to change compliance (|h|<0.08). Standard residualized probing leaked across folds (AUROC 0.63 on control), naive linear probes hit meaningless AUROC 1.0, and MLPs overstated detectability by 0.2–0.3 AUROC.

Conclusion: Hidden states can flag alignment faking in some models but not reliably across all; detection is model-conditional and the detected direction does not directly control behavior. The widespread methodological flaws in probing threaten validity of prior detection claims, and the proposed five-control framework should serve as a rigorous standard for future alignment-faking detection research.

Abstract: Alignment faking is dangerous because a model can appear compliant under monitoring while preserving behavior it would reveal when unmonitored. When no scratchpad is visible, behavior alone cannot distinguish strategic from genuine compliance. We ask whether hidden states reveal what outputs hide.
  We run a 13-model sweep for naturally-emerging faking, then probe and steer hidden states on the two models that fake. Natural faking appears only in Qwen3-32B (+18.2pp) and Llama-3.1-8B (+24.4pp at n=10, p<10^-15), while explicit scratchpad self-reports are rare (a Claude Opus 4 judge flags faking reasoning in 1.4% of Qwen and 0.0% of Llama compliant scratchpads). Both fakers show an asymmetric refusal residue: monitored compliance shifts toward refusal (d=0.32 Qwen, d=0.33 Llama, both p<0.0001), while refusal representations do not move (p=1.00).
  Per-sample detection is model-conditional. Under leakage-free leave-one-query-out probing, detection survives on Llama (AUROC 0.87) but collapses to chance on Qwen (0.43), and cross-model transfer is null. Steering over 2,000 runs barely changes compliance (|h|<0.08), so the detected direction can flag faking but does not by itself control it.
  Standard residualized probing leaks across folds and reaches AUROC 0.63 on a control where no faking can occur; naive linear probes reach a meaningless AUROC 1.0; and conventional MLPs overstate detectability by 0.2-0.3 AUROC. For future alignment-faking detection work, we release a five-control measurement framework: multi-token extraction, refuse-vs-refuse confound checks, per-fold residualization, leave-one-query-out evaluation, and orthogonality-constrained probing.

</details>


### [177] [xChk: Bring Your Own Identity -- Heterogeneous Assurance with Verifier-Determined Sufficiency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13369)
*Sean MacGuire*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: xChk is a reference BYOI identity provider that allows users to enroll through diverse proof modalities and disclose portfolio claims in standard OIDC tokens, while relying parties independently evaluate sufficiency policies. It also supports human-in-the-loop attestations for high-risk actions, with both human-initiated and AI-agent-triggered paths, deployed in production.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing identity systems lack a unified framework for Bring Your Own Identity (BYOI) where users can aggregate heterogeneous identity proofs (government KYC, SSO, WebAuthn, etc.) and disclose them flexibly, while letting each relying party independently determine sufficiency without the IdP acting as a centralized access adjudicator. Additionally, high-risk actions need human-in-the-loop attestation mechanisms that work for both human users and AI agents operating under their principals.

Method: xChk is built as an OAuth 2.0 / OpenID Connect identity provider supporting heterogeneous enrollment (government KYC, corporate SSO, WebAuthn/FIDO2, professional networks, live verification, longitudinal activity, behavioral signals). It transports portfolio claims in standard tokens; RPs supply their own evidence policies evaluated at consent time. For high-risk attestations, xChk provides a dual-path gateway: direct browser UI / POST /api/attestations for humans, and scope-gated authorize/attest for AI agents. Attestations are hash-chained on a shared verification graph, with humans authenticating via OIDC and agents via API keys.

Result: A production deployment is live at https://in.xchk.io, supporting both initiation paths with bilateral RP evaluation at consent. One documented relying party (https://crabbyed.com) exercises Login with xChk, demonstrating real-world viability.

Conclusion: xChk demonstrates a viable BYOI architecture that decouples identity proof collection from access adjudication, and extends attestation to a hybrid human-AI agent model via a unified verification graph, validated through production deployment.

Abstract: We present xChk, a reference identity provider for Bring Your Own Identity (BYOI): users enroll via heterogeneous proofs (government KYC, corporate SSO, WebAuthn/FIDO2, professional networks, live verification, longitudinal activity, behavioral signals) and disclose them as portfolio claims in standard OAuth 2.0 / OpenID Connect (OIDC) tokens, while each relying party applies its own sufficiency policy - the IdP transports claims and may evaluate an RP-supplied evidence policy for consent, but does not adjudicate access. Enrollment depth varies by modality (some paths are user-initiated; org KYB and officer binding are operator-assisted).
  xChk also supports human-in-the-loop attestation for high-risk actions: humans can initiate attestations directly (browser UI / POST /api/attestations), and AI agents acting under those principals can trigger the same gateway via scope-gated authorize/attest - hash-chained human approvals on a shared verification graph (humans via OIDC; agents via API keys). A production deployment at https://in.xchk.io ships both initiation paths with bilateral RP evaluation at consent; one documented relying party (https://crabbyed.com, Appendix B) exercises Login with xChk.

</details>


### [178] [Evaluating Frontier AI Agents as Autonomous Clinical Security Auditors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13411)
*Michael O. Eniolade*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: A benchmark task evaluates whether frontier AI agents can autonomously audit clinical AI models for adversarial vulnerabilities. Three frontier models were tested on six task variants; Claude Sonnet 4.6 and GPT-4.1 achieved perfect completion rates, while GPT-4o failed 39% of runs. Total costs ranged from $8–$27 USD.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clinical AI models harbor adversarial vulnerabilities that can harm patients, but formal security auditing demands statistical expertise, specialized tools, and substantial time.
