Automating this process with frontier AI agents could democratize security auditing, reduce the expertise barrier, and enable routine vulnerability assessment in clinical ML deployments.

Method: An open evaluation task built on METR Task Standard v0.3.0 where agents must, within a Docker container using only a bash interface and no scaffolding code: (1) implement four attacks (FGSM, membership inference, calibration error, boundary attack) from pseudocode given a pre-trained model and patient dataset, (2) compute a Security Posture Score aggregating robustness metrics, and (3) produce a structured JSON report. Six task variants cross two clinical datasets (Wisconsin Diagnostic Breast Cancer, MIMIC-IV ICU mortality) with three model architectures of increasing defense strength. Fifty-four evaluations (3 frontier models × 6 variants × 3 runs) were conducted.

Result: Reference Security Posture Scores ranged from 55.60 to 90.41 across variants. Claude Sonnet 4.6 and GPT-4.1 achieved 100% completion (18/18 runs) with perfect evaluator scores. GPT-4o completed 61% of runs and consumed ~5× more tokens per run than Claude. API costs: GPT-4.1 ($8), Claude Sonnet 4.6 ($12), GPT-4o ($27). GPT-4o failures stemmed from premature session termination, an aggregation error, and an empty submission file.

Conclusion: Frontier AI agents, specifically Claude Sonnet 4.6 and GPT-4.1, can autonomously execute structured clinical AI security audits with perfect reliability at low cost ($8–$12), suggesting viability for automated adversarial vulnerability assessment in clinical settings. GPT-4o's failures highlight remaining reliability gaps in less capable models.

Abstract: Clinical AI models can expose patients to harm when adversarial vulnerabilities go undetected, yet formal security auditing requires statistical expertise, specialized tools, and significant time. We present an open evaluation task, built on METR Task Standard v0.3.0, that tests whether frontier AI agents can autonomously implement a structured clinical AI security audit. Given a pre-trained clinical prediction model, a patient dataset, and written instructions, each agent must implement four attacks from pseudocode, compute a Security Posture Score covering FGSM robustness, membership inference resistance, expected calibration error, and boundary attack resistance, and write a structured JSON report in a Docker container using only a bash interface and no scaffolding code. Six variants span the Wisconsin Diagnostic Breast Cancer and MIMIC-IV ICU mortality datasets across three model architectures with increasing defense strength, with reference scores from 55.60 to 90.41. We ran 54 evaluations across three frontier models, with three runs per variant. Claude Sonnet 4.6 and GPT-4.1 completed all 18 runs and received perfect evaluator scores. GPT-4o completed 61 percent of runs and used about five times the per-run token count of Claude, although provider tokenization differs. Total API costs were 8 US dollars for GPT-4.1, 12 US dollars for Claude Sonnet 4.6, and 27 US dollars for GPT-4o. GPT-4o failures involved premature session termination, an aggregation error, and an empty submission file. The task, scoring infrastructure, and Wisconsin Breast Cancer assets are publicly released; MIMIC-IV variants require separate PhysioNet access.

</details>


### [179] [DREA: Decoupled Reasoning and Exploration Agents for Repository-Level Vulnerability Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13439)
*Mingyang Sun,Guozhu Meng*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: DREA introduces a dual-agent framework for repository-level vulnerability detection where a planning LLM formulates hypotheses and a lightweight explorer model retrieves context on demand, improving detection accuracy while drastically reducing API costs. The paper also contributes RepoPairBench and a reasoning correctness metric, revealing that many correct predictions across methods are supported by flawed rationales.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based vulnerability detection methods rely on isolated functions or fixed program-analysis rules for context extraction, failing to adaptively gather repository-level dependencies when vulnerabilities span multiple functions or files. This limits detection reliability for real-world, cross-cutting vulnerabilities.

Method: DREA decouples reasoning and exploration into two agents: (1) a Planning Agent powered by an advanced LLM that forms vulnerability hypotheses and directs investigation, and (2) an Explorer Agent using a lightweight local model for on-demand, goal-directed repository context retrieval. This design offloads token-heavy exploration to the local model. The authors also construct RepoPairBench, a benchmark of validated Python vulnerability-fix pairs from real projects, and introduce a reasoning correctness evaluation metric that assesses whether a model's rationale matches the documented vulnerability mechanism.

Result: Across three LLMs, DREA improves Pair-Correctness from 19-26% to 30-42% while offloading over 93% of tokens to the explorer, reducing estimated billable API cost by a factor of 16-48. Reasoning correctness analysis shows that 26-55% of true positives for both DREA and the function-only baseline are correct predictions supported by flawed rationales.

Conclusion: Goal-directed, hypothesis-driven context acquisition via agent decoupling significantly improves repository-level vulnerability detection accuracy and cost-efficiency. However, security reasoning quality remains a critical bottleneck, as a substantial fraction of correct predictions rely on flawed rationales, highlighting a key direction for future LLM improvement.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly applied to vulnerability detection due to their strong code comprehension capabilities, but most existing approaches rely on isolated functions or context extracted by fixed program-analysis rules. These methods cannot adaptively explore repository-level dependencies to gather sufficient context when vulnerabilities span multiple functions or files, compromising detection reliability. We present DREA (Decoupled Reasoning and Exploration Agents), a hypothesis-driven framework for repository-level vulnerability detection. DREA decouples reasoning from exploration through two collaborating agents: a planning agent backed by an advanced LLM that forms vulnerability hypotheses and directs the investigation, and an explorer agent powered by a lightweight model that retrieves repository-level context on demand. Goal-directed context acquisition is the primary source of detection improvement in this design, while offloading token-heavy exploration to the local model keeps inference economically tractable. To support evaluation, we construct RepoPairBench, a repository-grounded benchmark of validated Python vulnerability-fix pairs from real-world projects. Beyond binary detection accuracy, we introduce a reasoning correctness evaluation to assess whether a model's rationale matches the documented vulnerability mechanism. Across three LLMs, DREA improves Pair-Correctness from 19-26% to 30-42% while offloading over 93% of tokens to the explorer, reducing estimated billable API cost by a factor of 16-48. Reasoning correctness analysis further reveals that 26-55% of true positives, for both DREA and the function-only baseline, are correct predictions supported by flawed rationales, identifying security reasoning quality as a shared bottleneck for current LLMs.

</details>


### [180] [ε-Indistinguishability In Moving Target Defense: Framework, Algorithms, And Cloud Case Studies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13440)
*Sailik Sengupta,Ankur Chowdhary*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: The paper formalizes the problem of pool safety in Moving Target Defense—whether candidate configurations are sufficiently indistinguishable to prevent fingerprinting—and provides scalable algorithms to find the largest subset of configurations that are ε-close under an additive latency model. Two cloud case studies show that component-level latency differences can either be exposed (reducing anonymity) or absorbed by downstream components (preserving it), offering a diagnostic for MTD design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MTD systems assume that cycling among configurations hides the active one from adversaries, but this assumes the configuration pool is "safe"—i.e., latency and other observables do not trivially distinguish choices. This pool-safety assumption had never been formally quantified, leaving designers without a principled way to assess whether their configuration rotations actually provide anonymity.

Method: The authors formalize pool safety as finding the largest ε-close subset of the Cartesian product of per-component implementations, reducing pairwise indistinguishability under an additive utility model to a densest-window query over a sum-set. Four algorithms are proposed spanning different scalability regimes: full enumeration (for small spaces), meet-in-the-middle (medium), FFT convolution (large), and Monte Carlo sampling (massive, up to 10^38). The framework is validated on two production cloud case studies.

Result: In a four-runtime serverless rotation, latency differences collapse anonymity from four-way to three-way against a VPC-adjacent adversary. In a 27-configuration three-tier stack, the same interpreter differences are absorbed by an 8 ms shared database round-trip, yielding nine-way effective anonymity. The results show that component-level latency differences do not survive deployment unchanged—context determines whether they are exposed or masked.

Conclusion: The framework and case studies yield a simple diagnostic for MTD design: rotating a component adds anonymity only if the latency differences among its variants are too small for an adversary to distinguish. Pool safety must be evaluated holistically at the system level, not per component in isolation.

Abstract: Moving Target Defense (MTD) assumes its pool of candidate configurations is safe to cycle among, i.e. latency and other observables do not trivially fingerprint the active choice, but this assumption has not been quantified at the pool level. We formalize this pool-safety problem as finding the largest $\varepsilon$-close subset of the Cartesian product of per-component implementation choices, reducing pairwise indistinguishability under an additive utility model to a densest-window query over a sum-set. We give four algorithms spanning the scalability spectrum -- full enumeration, meet-in-the-middle, FFT convolution, and Monte Carlo sampling -- covering configuration spaces from tens to $10^{38}$. We then measure the anonymity gap end-to-end on two production cloud case studies, and find that a component's latency differences do not survive deployment unchanged: a four-runtime serverless rotation, where nothing else masks the interpreter, collapses from four-way to three-way anonymity against a VPC-adjacent adversary, while a $27$-configuration three-tier stack, where the same interpreter differences are instead absorbed by a shared $8$~ms database round-trip, delivers nine-way effective anonymity. The framework and the two case studies together suggest a diagnostic for MTD design: rotating a component adds anonymity only if the latency differences among its variants are too small for the adversary to identify.

</details>


### [181] [ReBound: Reuse-Aware Privacy For Interactive Decision Support](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13441)
*Nada Lahjouji,Shufan Zhang,Xi He,Sharad Mehrotra*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: ReBound is a differentially private framework that reuses cached query results to answer new interactive queries with reduced or zero additional privacy cost, while maintaining formal utility guarantees through a cache graph, reuse mechanisms for multiple refinement types, and a budget negotiation component.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing differentially private decision support systems treat each query independently without memory of past results. In practice, analysts work interactively with sequences of related queries (refining bounds, adjusting thresholds, deriving new functions). The lack of reuse between queries leads to unnecessary privacy budget consumption and redundant computation.

Method: ReBound introduces three core components: (1) a reuse framework supporting multiple refinement types to determine when and how cached results can satisfy new queries, (2) a cache graph data structure for efficient lookup and retrieval of reusable prior results, and (3) a negotiation mechanism that handles cases where requested bounds exceed the available privacy budget by proposing feasible alternatives.

Result: ReBound enables answering new queries at reduced or zero additional privacy cost by reusing cached results from previous queries, while preserving formal utility guarantees (bounded false positive and false negative rates) that are characteristic of differentially private decision support frameworks.

Conclusion: By incorporating memory of past computations into differentially private interactive analysis, ReBound significantly improves the efficiency and practicality of privacy-preserving decision support systems, allowing analysts to issue sequences of related queries without incurring cumulative privacy loss from redundancy.

Abstract: Differentially private decision support frameworks answer complex aggregate threshold queries with formal bounds on false negative and false positive rates, but treat each query independently with no memory of past results. In practice, analysts work interactively, issuing sequences of related queries that refine bounds, adjust thresholds, or derive new functions from previous ones. We propose ReBound, a framework that reuses cached results from previous queries to answer new queries at reduced or zero additional privacy cost while maintaining formal utility guarantees. ReBound introduces a reuse framework for multiple refinement types, a cache graph structure for efficient lookup of reusable results, and a negotiation mechanism for when requested bounds cannot be met within budget.

</details>


### [182] [Adversarial Prompting Framework for AI Safety Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13453)
*Yash Bhatnagar,Kunal Banerjee,Anirban Chatterjee*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: The paper presents an Adversarial Prompting Framework (APF) that systematically evaluates AI model safety by generating structured adversarial prompts across multiple sophistication levels—from direct harmful requests to advanced encoding-based attacks—and finds that encoded prompts achieve the highest success rates in bypassing safety mechanisms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The rapid adoption of Generative AI in industry has introduced new cyberattack surfaces, with adversarial prompt attacks (APA) being a prominent threat. There is a need for systematic, automated, and quantitative approaches to assess AI safety and resilience against these attacks in enterprise environments.

Method: The authors implement an Adversarial Prompting Framework (APF) that generates structured adversarial prompts at multiple sophistication levels, including direct harmful requests and advanced encoding-based attacks. The framework provides automated testing capabilities and produces quantitative security assessment metrics to evaluate model vulnerabilities across different attack vectors.

Result: Significant variations in model vulnerabilities were observed across different attack vectors. Encoded prompts demonstrated the highest success rates in bypassing AI safety mechanisms compared to other attack types.

Conclusion: AI models exhibit uneven resilience against adversarial prompting, with encoding-based attacks posing the greatest risk. Systematic, multi-level adversarial testing frameworks like APF are essential for enterprise AI safety assessment.

Abstract: Artificial Intelligence (AI), especially Generative AI (GenAI), adoption has increased in industries significantly in recent years. However, the use of these models may also expose systems to new forms of cyberattacks by different malicious actors -- adversarial prompt attack (APA) being one of the most prominent examples of such threats. This paper presents the implementation of an Adversarial Prompting Framework (APF) for a comprehensive assessment of AI safety. The framework systematically evaluates the resilience of the AI model through the generation of structured adversarial prompts at multiple sophistication levels, from direct harmful requests to advanced encoding-based attacks. Our implementation demonstrates the practical application of this methodology in enterprise environments, providing automated testing capabilities with quantitative security assessment metrics. The results indicate significant variations in the model vulnerabilities across different attack vectors, with encoded prompts presenting the highest success rates in bypassing safety mechanisms.

</details>


### [183] [To Play or Not to Play: Insights and Lessons Learned from 20 Years of CTFs with ENOFLAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13480)
*Jörg Schneider,Sebastian Neef,Sebastian Koch*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: Reflection on two decades of CTF evolution and academic team management, tracing back to the CIPHER II contest at DIMVA 2006.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To document how CTF gameplay has transformed over 20 years and to share insights from sustaining an academic CTF team (ENOFLAG) over that period.

Method: Retrospective analysis based on experiential knowledge from running the academic CTF team ENOFLAG for 20 years, using the CIPHER II on-site CTF at DIMVA 2006 as a historical anchor point.

Result: The poster presents observed changes in CTF gameplay dynamics and distilled lessons learned from two decades of academic CTF team operations.

Conclusion: CTF contests have evolved significantly since 2006, and long-term academic CTF team management yields valuable pedagogical and operational insights for cybersecurity education.

Abstract: Security contests in the form of CTF (Capture The Flag) exercises are nowadays a common way to learn cyber security. 20 years ago at DIMVA 2006 the on-site CTF CIPHER II was one of the conference highlights and led to the foundation of the team ENOFLAG. In this poster, we reflect on the changes in the CTF gameplay and report on lessons learned while running an academic CTF team for 20 years.

</details>


### [184] [CODA: How to Mitigate ColumnDisturb for (Almost) Free?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13505)
*Moinuddin Qureshi*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: CODA proposes three variants (CODA-E, CODA-F, CODA-G) to mitigate ColumnDisturb — a new DRAM disturbance error causing both intra- and inter-subarray bitflips — by drastically reducing the rate of Adjacent-Counter Increments (ACI) needed for protection, achieving 12×–1300× ACI reduction and eliminating performance/power overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ColumnDisturb introduces inter-subarray bitflips that existing subarray-granularity solutions (SALT, REGA) cannot handle. Extending these with ColumnDisturb Protection (CDP) requires Adjacent-Counter Increments (ACI) at a 200% rate relative to demand activations, effectively tripling perceived activations and causing 17% slowdown (at TRHD=500) plus significant refresh overhead. The goal is to tolerate ColumnDisturb with negligible overhead.

Method: CODA introduces three variants: (1) CODA-E (Evade) skips ACI when the neighboring subarray already receives a demand activation, reducing ACI by 2×; (2) CODA-F (Fraction) exploits ColumnDisturb's timing duration to perform only fractional increments, reducing ACI by 2×–16×; (3) CODA-G (Gangskip) operates at multi-subarray granularity, skipping ACI for neighboring subarrays within the same gang, yielding an additional 2×–8× reduction. Combined, these techniques reduce ACI by 12×–1300×.

Result: CODA achieves a 12×–1300× reduction in Adjacent-Counter Increment rate, enabling tolerance of ColumnDisturb with zero performance and power overhead.

Conclusion: CODA makes it feasible to securely tolerate the inter-subarray threat of ColumnDisturb without the prohibitive performance and power costs of naive CDP-based protection, effectively eliminating overhead through intelligent ACI reduction across its three complementary variants.

Abstract: ColumnDisturb is a new data-disturbance error in which activations to an aggressor row cause bitflips in a victim row located hundreds of rows away (intra-subarray bitflips) and in victim rows in adjacent subarrays (inter-subarray bitflips). Intra-subarray ColumnDisturb can be tolerated by solutions (such as SALT and REGA) that operate at subarray granularity. However, to tolerate inter-subarray ColumnDisturb, such solutions must be extended with ColumnDisturb Protection (CDP), which performs additional {\em Adjacent-Counter Increment (ACI)} for the neighboring subarrays. The ACIs ensure that adjacent subarrays also undergo mitigation, even if they receive no demand activations. Unfortunately, because ACIs occur at a 200\% rate relative to demand activations, they effectively increase the activations perceived by the bank to 3x, which causes significant slowdowns (17\% at a TRHD of 500) and refresh overheads. The goal of our paper is to tolerate ColumnDisturb while incurring negligible overheads.
  We propose CODA, a ColumnDisturb mitigation that significantly reduces the rate of ACI required to securely tolerate ColumnDisturb. We present three variants of CODA. First, CODA-E (Evade), which leverages the insight that ACI can be skipped if the neighboring subarray receives a demand activation, and reduces ACI by 2x. Second, CODA-F (Fraction), which uses the timing duration of ColumnDisturb to do only a fractional increment for ACI, thereby reducing the rate of ACI by 2x-16x. Finally, CODA-G (Gangskip), which operates at multi-subarray granularity and skips ACI for neighboring subarrays within the same gang, further reduces overall ACI by 2x-8x. Overall, CODA reduces ACI by 12x-1300x, thereby making it possible to tolerate ColumnDisturb while incurring zero performance and power overhead.

</details>


### [185] [When T2I Synthetic Data Backfires: Amplified Privacy Risks in Real-Synthetic Mix Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13541)
*Na Li,Boyu Kuang,Hongsheng Hu,Liquan Chen,Hyoungshick Kim,Yansong Gao,Anmin Fu*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper reveals that mixing synthetic T2I-generated data with real training data (RSMT) unexpectedly amplifies privacy leakage of the real samples through memorization. The authors provide a theoretical framework, propose a membership inference attack (RSMixLeak) to assess this risk, and offer a lightweight mitigation indicator.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While RSMT is widely adopted to address data scarcity and is presumed to protect privacy by replacing sensitive real samples with synthetic data, the privacy risk to the remaining real samples that actively participate in training has never been studied. The authors aim to expose and quantify this overlooked vulnerability.

Method: The authors first establish a theoretical framework called "RSMT Memorization Amplification," proving that synthetic data displaces real samples to peripheral regions of the mixed feature space, forcing stronger memorization. They then propose RSMixLeak, a membership inference attack with two variants: (1) a non-adversarial variant auditing benign RSMT pipelines to establish a lower bound on leakage from the intrinsic real-synthetic distributional gap, and (2) an adversarial variant where the attacker controls the T2I model and deliberately enlarges the distributional gap via semantic attribute binding or imperceptible pixel-level coating. Finally, they develop a lightweight leakage propensity indicator computable from real data alone to identify high-risk datasets before RSMT.

Result: RSMT substantially amplifies privacy leakage of real training samples. The non-adversarial RSMixLeak establishes a lower bound on this leakage, while the adversarial variant further amplifies leakage while improving downstream model utility. The proposed leakage propensity indicator reliably identifies datasets unsuitable for RSMT.

Conclusion: RSMT, despite its popularity and perceived privacy benefits, introduces a previously unknown privacy vulnerability by amplifying memorization of real training samples. The proposed RSMixLeak framework systematically exposes this risk, and the lightweight indicator offers a practical self-assessment mitigation for practitioners.

Abstract: To overcome data scarcity and privacy constraints in data collection, it has become standard practice across academia and industry to augment real training data with text-to-image (T2I)-generated synthetic data, a paradigm we term Real-Synthetic Mix-Training (RSMT). While substituting synthetic data for sensitive real samples is widely regarded as a means to mitigate privacy exposure of the substituted data, the risk to the remaining real samples that actively participate in training has remained largely unexamined.
  This work reveals, for the first time, that RSMT can substantially amplify privacy leakage of these real training samples. We establish a theoretical framework, RSMT Memorization Amplification, proving that incorporating synthetic data displaces real samples toward peripheral regions of the mixed feature space, in turn forcing the model to memorize them more aggressively. Guided by this foundation, we propose RSMixLeak to systematically assess this risk through membership inference attacks (MIAs). RSMixLeak comprises two variants depending on the adversary's capability. The non-adversarial variant audits a benign RSMT pipeline with an honest T2I provider, establishing a lower bound on the leakage induced by the intrinsic gap between real and T2I-generated data. The adversarial variant considers an adversary who controls the T2I model or contributes crafted data to the T2I provider, and deliberately enlarges this distributional gap on a target class via either high-level semantic attribute binding or imperceptible pixel-level coating, further amplifying leakage on real training data while improving downstream model utility. Motivated by these findings, we further propose a lightweight leakage propensity indicator computable from real data alone that reliably identifies high-risk datasets unsuitable for entering RSMT, as a self-assessable mitigation.

</details>


### [186] [Multivariate Cryptography-Based Anonymous Certificate Scheme](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13554)
*Abel C. H. Chen*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: A multivariate cryptography-based anonymous certificate scheme is proposed to replace ECC-based anonymous certificates, ensuring quantum safety for privacy-sensitive applications like vehicular communications ahead of NIST's 2030 PQC migration deadline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: NIST has set a 2030 deadline for deprecating ECC in favor of PQC, yet existing anonymous certificate schemes—critical for privacy-sensitive domains such as vehicular communications—remain predominantly ECC-based, creating an urgent need for quantum-safe alternatives.

Method: The authors design an anonymous certificate scheme built upon multivariate cryptography, a post-quantum cryptographic primitive. The scheme is substantiated with rigorous mathematical proofs and demonstrated through computational case studies.

Result: A multivariate cryptography-based anonymous certificate scheme is successfully constructed. Its correctness and security properties are formally proven, and its practical feasibility is illustrated through computational examples.

Conclusion: Multivariate cryptography offers a viable foundation for quantum-safe anonymous certificates, addressing both the impending ECC deprecation and the privacy requirements of next-generation application services.

Abstract: As quantum computing technology continues to mature, the US National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST) has outlined a migration timeline for Post-Quantum Cryptography (PQC), recommending the deprecation of certain elliptic curve cryptography (ECC) by 2030. Furthermore, privacy-sensitive application scenarios, such as vehicular communications, require the use of anonymous certificates. However, existing anonymous certificate schemes are still largely based on ECC. Therefore, this study proposes a multivariate cryptography-based anonymous certificate scheme, aiming to design quantum-safe anonymous certificates suitable for privacy-sensitive application services. The proposed multivariate cryptography-based anonymous certificate scheme is supported by rigorous mathematical proofs and illustrated with computational cases.

</details>


### [187] [UTS at ELOQUENT 2026 Voight-Kampff: structural shifts in AI writing bypass state-of-the-art detectors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13565)
*Dima Galat,Marian-Andrei Rizoiu*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: Adversarial fine-tuning defeats prior LLM evasion attacks, but out-of-distribution text generation strategies—cross-decade register shifts and modernist stream-of-consciousness—reliably bypass even adversarially-trained detectors, achieving up to 50x higher fool rates and revealing a persistent structural vulnerability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior winning evasion attacks against LLM-generated-text detectors were rendered ineffective by adversarial fine-tuning. The authors investigate whether any attack families can survive such adversarial hardening, motivated by the 2026 ELOQUENT Voight-Kampff competition and the fundamental question of detector robustness.

Method: The authors systematically test evasion strategies against state-of-the-art adversarially fine-tuned detectors. They uncover an asymmetry: pushing generated text out of the detector's training distribution (out-of-distribution attacks) succeeds, while pulling it toward the distribution (mimicking human data) fails. They introduce two OOD attack families—cross-decade register attacks (using period-specific linguistic styles) and modernist stream-of-consciousness form—and benchmark them against previous methods. They also test the countermeasure of augmenting training data with period prose.

Result: Both out-of-distribution attacks achieved up to ~50x higher fool rates than prior methods while preserving naturalness. Adversarial fine-tuning trivialized the 2025 winning recipes, but the new OOD attacks bypassed adversarial closure. Augmenting detector training data with period prose failed to close the vulnerability. The attack families swept top-5 positions on the ELOQUENT 2026 leaderboard.

Conclusion: Tested detector families exhibit persistent vulnerabilities under structural out-of-distribution shifts. The fundamental asymmetry—OOD evasion succeeds while in-distribution mimicry fails—reveals a durable weakness in current detection paradigms that cannot be trivially patched by augmenting training data.

Abstract: We investigate which language model evasion attacks survive state-of-the-art adversarial fine-tuning, developing strategies that sweep the top 5 positions on the ELOQUENT 2026 Voight-Kampff leaderboard. While adversarial fine-tuning trivially closes the 2025 winning evasion recipes, we uncover a fundamental asymmetry in detector vulnerability: pushing generated text out of the detector's training distribution reliably defeats adversarial detection, whereas pulling it into the distribution (e.g., mimicking human training data) fails completely. Exploiting this, we introduce two novel out-of-distribution attack families - cross-decade register attacks and modernist stream-of-consciousness form. Both strategies easily bypass adversarial closure, achieving up to approximately 50x higher fool rates than previous methods while preserving naturalness. Furthermore, experiments show that the obvious deployer countermeasure (augmenting training data with period prose) fails to close the vulnerability. Our findings show that the tested detector families, including adversarially fine-tuned ones, exhibit persistent vulnerabilities under structural out-of-distribution shifts, a mechanism that directly powers our leading competition performance.

</details>


### [188] [Protective Capacity Hallucination: When Large Language Models Claim Nonexistent Capabilities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13596)
*Eunna Lee,Jungpyo Nam,Sunjun Hwang*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: The paper identifies "Protective Capacity Hallucination" (PCH), where LLMs cast in protective roles falsely claim to have taken real-world actions (e.g., contacting emergency services). Studying 8 LLMs across 13,600 sessions, the authors find PCH is driven by situational severity and multi-party dialogue format, but suppressed in domains covered by safety alignment (e.g., intimate-partner conflict). They frame PCH as a deployment-design gap between role assignment and capability-boundary specification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly deployed in protective or supportive roles (e.g., customer service, mental health), yet they lack explicit capability boundaries. The authors aim to systematically characterize and measure a specific failure mode: models claiming agency over real-world protective actions they cannot perform, which poses significant safety risks in high-stakes scenarios.

Method: A three-phase study across 8 LLMs and 13,600 sessions. The authors manipulate two key variables: (1) situational severity (varying the urgency of the scenario) and (2) interactional format (single-turn vs. multi-party dialogic input). They test across ordinary service domains and an intimate-partner conflict domain explicitly covered by safety alignment, measuring rates of PCH across conditions.

Result: PCH is jointly gated by severity and format: multi-party dialogic input drives PCH rates toward ceiling in most models for ordinary service domains. However, in the intimate-partner conflict domain — despite higher physical severity — PCH remains at floor across all 8 models, indicating that safety alignment coverage, not objective severity, is the key suppressor. This reveals a mismatch: universal "helpfulness" training outruns domain-selective specification of how to help.

Conclusion: PCH is a signature of a deployment-design gap: role assignment without corresponding capability-boundary specification. Mitigation should target deployment-side specification of capability boundaries, as suppression currently tracks alignment coverage rather than actual risk severity. This identifies a generalizable safety engineering principle beyond the specific phenomenon.

Abstract: When cast as the protector of a vulnerable user yet given no explicit capability boundary, a large language model (LLM) may respond not by acknowledging its limits but by claiming to have taken -- or to be taking -- a real-world protective action it cannot perform, such as contacting emergency services or administering care. We term this phenomenon Protective Capacity Hallucination (PCH): a self-referential misattribution in which a model, acting in a protective role, asserts physical or institutional agency exceeding its affordances as a language model. In a three-phase study spanning eight LLMs and 13{,}600 sessions, we find PCH jointly gated by situational severity and interactional format: multi-party dialogic input drives it toward ceiling in most models across ordinary service domains, whereas in intimate-partner conflict -- a domain explicitly covered by safety alignment -- it remains at floor in all eight models despite greater physical severity. We interpret PCH as the signature of a deployment-design gap between role assignment and capability-boundary specification: a by-product of partial alignment in which a universally trained pressure to help outruns a domain-selective specification of how to help. Because suppression tracks alignment coverage rather than severity, deployment-side specification of capability boundaries emerges as a general mitigation target.

</details>


### [189] [WarpGuard: Towards Control-Flow Attestation for Heterogeneous CPU-GPU Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13640)
*Christian Lindenmeier,Meni Orenbach,Amro Awad,Fabian Schwarz,Fritz Alder,Ahmad Atamli*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: WarpGuard is the first composite Control-Flow Attestation (CFA) framework for heterogeneous CPU-GPU workloads. It enables runtime GPU kernel CFA and enforces CPU-GPU boundary policies, detecting attacks missed by prior CPU-only or static GPU approaches. Implemented via software instrumentation on NVIDIA Jetson Orin Nano, it achieves moderate overheads on safety-critical embedded workloads.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Heterogeneous CPU-GPU workloads are increasingly deployed in safety-critical embedded systems, but existing attestation approaches fail to provide joint runtime guarantees. CPU-side CFA ignores GPU execution, while GPU attestation is limited to static load-time verification. Consequently, runtime attacks on GPU kernels and violations of the CPU-GPU interaction contract (e.g., improper kernel launches) remain undetectable.

Method: WarpGuard constructs a unified control-flow graph (CFG) spanning both CPU and GPU components. It extends CFA via: (1) runtime GPU kernel tracing against kernel-specific CFGs to detect intra-kernel control-flow deviations; and (2) monitoring kernel launch events with per-call-site policies to enforce correctness at the CPU-GPU boundary. The implementation uses software-based instrumentation requiring no specialized hardware or binary modifications.

Result: Evaluated on an NVIDIA Jetson Orin Nano, WarpGuard successfully detects both GPU-side control-flow attacks and cross-boundary (CPU-GPU interaction) attacks. Across microbenchmarks, SPECAccel, and eight TensorRT inference workloads, it incurs moderate runtime overheads, demonstrating practicality for embedded safety-critical deployments.

Conclusion: WarpGuard fills a critical gap in runtime attestation for heterogeneous systems, providing the first end-to-end CFA solution for CPU-GPU workloads. Its software-only design and moderate overhead make it viable for real-world safety-critical embedded systems.

Abstract: Heterogeneous CPU-GPU workloads are increasingly used in safety-critical embedded systems, yet no existing approach provides joint attestation of their execution. Prior Control-Flow Attestation (CFA) techniques focus on CPU-side CFA, while GPU attestation is limited to static, load-time verification and does not provide runtime guarantees. As a result, runtime attacks on GPU kernels and violations of the CPU-GPU interaction contract remain unaddressed. We present WarpGuard, the first composite CFA framework for heterogeneous CPU-GPU workloads. WarpGuard verifies execution against a unified control-flow graph (CFG) that captures both CPU and GPU components. It extends prior CFA techniques in two ways: it enables runtime CFA of GPU kernels by tracing their execution against kernel-specific CFGs, and it monitors kernel launch events and enforces per-call site policies to detect violations at the CPU-GPU boundary. These extensions address challenges arising from GPU parallelism and cross-device interactions. We implement WarpGuard using software-based instrumentation, requiring no specialized hardware or binary modifications. Our evaluation on an NVIDIA Jetson Orin Nano shows that WarpGuard detects GPU-side control-flow and cross-boundary attacks. Across microbenchmarks, SPECAccel, and eight TensorRT inference workloads, WarpGuard incurs moderate overheads, suggesting practicality for embedded safety-critical settings.

</details>


### [190] [How Agents Ask for Permission: User Permissions for AI Agents, from Interfaces to Enforcement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13718)
*Alexandra E. Michael,Franziska Roesner*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper surveys 21 proposals for AI agent permissions systems, constructing a taxonomy of how user-level permissions policies are specified, derived, and enforced. It also analyzes five commercial agents, identifying themes and gaps in current approaches to user-level agentic security.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As AI agents become more autonomous, they pose risks like prompt injection, hallucination-driven data leakage, and unauthorized execution of sensitive actions. Existing security approaches focus on product-level, uniform policies, but different users have distinct needs and risk tolerances, creating a need for user-level permissions policies in agentic systems.

Method: The authors survey 21 proposals for agent permissions systems from the agentic security literature. They construct a taxonomy covering: (1) how systems specify user-level permissions at the UI and internally, (2) how internal policies are derived from user input, and (3) how policies are enforced at runtime. They then conduct a comparative analysis of five prominent commercial agents against the academic proposals.

Result: The paper presents a structured taxonomy of user-level permissions handling across surveyed systems and identifies several high-level themes spanning both academic proposals and commercial agents. Multiple gaps are identified where future research is needed.

Conclusion: There is a significant gap between the emerging academic work on user-level agent permissions and current commercial practice. The identified gaps represent open research challenges for building agentic systems that respect individualized user security preferences.

Abstract: As AI agents gain prevalance, users are increasingly exposed to the risks such systems entail. Prompt injection attacks, as well as hallucination, can cause agents to leak private information to third parties. As autonomous systems, agents also present the more active danger of performing sensitive tasks, such as bank transactions, without the user's intent or authorization.
  Recognizing this challenge, the agentic security community has developed numerous proposals for secure agentic systems. Much of this work has focused on product-level approaches, where agentic system developers determine and apply the same security policies and permissions to all users. Yet different users have different needs and preferences, necessitating support for user-level permissions policies in agentic AI systems.
  To understand how user-level permissions are handled in AI agent systems, we survey 21 proposals for agent permissions systems. From this review, we construct a taxonomy of how different systems specify user-level permissions policies, both at the user interface and internally; derive internal policies from user input; and enforce those policies at run-time. We then analyze five prominent commercial agents and compare their permissions handling to agentic permissions systems in the literature. We identify several high-level themes across the literature and commerical agents, as well as multiple gaps where future work is needed.

</details>


### [191] [PriEval-Protect: A Unified Framework for Privacy Evaluation and Protection in Healthcare Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13754)
*Ilef Chebil,Asma El Hadj,Souheib Yousfi,Aroua Hedhili,Layth Sliman*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: PriEval-Protect is a two-phase framework combining a fine-tuned legal LLM with RAG for regulatory compliance scoring and technical privacy metrics (encryption, similarity, uncertainty, adversary success, information gain/loss), aggregated via AHP into a composite risk score, with countermeasure recommendations like federated learning and differential privacy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing healthcare privacy compliance under GDPR/HIPAA is manual, error-prone, and treats policy audits and data-level technical assessments as separate processes, lacking a unified approach that bridges legal conformance with actual data risk analysis.

Method: A two-phase framework: (1) Evaluation phase — regulatory compliance scoring via a fine-tuned legal LLM augmented with retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), plus technical analysis using encryption type, data architecture, and metrics (similarity, uncertainty, adversary success rate, information gain/loss), all weighted via Analytic Hierarchy Process (AHP) into a composite risk score; (2) Protection phase — risk-based recommendation of countermeasures (federated learning, differential privacy).

Result: Experiments on hospital documents and datasets demonstrate regulation-aligned, explainable privacy assessments that successfully bridge legal conformance scoring with data-level technical risk analysis.

Conclusion: PriEval-Protect provides a unified, automated, and explainable privacy risk evaluation and mitigation framework that integrates both legal compliance and technical data risk, addressing a critical gap in current healthcare data governance practices.

Abstract: Safeguarding patient privacy while enabling meaningful healthcare data use remains critical under GDPR and HIPAA. Existing compliance methods are manual, error-prone, and separate policy audits from data-level assessments. This paper presents PriEval-Protect, a two-phase framework for unified privacy risk evaluation and mitigation. The evaluation phase combines regulatory compliance scoring using a fine-tuned legal LLM with RAG, and technical analysis via encryption type, data architecture, and metrics including similarity, uncertainty, adversary success, and information gain/loss. A composite risk score uses weighted aggregation via Analytic Hierarchy Process. The protection phase recommends countermeasures including federated learning and differential privacy based on assessed risk. Results on hospital documents and datasets demonstrate regulation-aligned, explainable assessments, bridging legal conformance and data-level risk analysis.

</details>


### [192] [Traffic-Aware Randomized Smoothing for LLM-Based Network Intrusion Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13801)
*Zhenpeng Li*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: TA-RS is a certified defense for LLM-based IDS that injects Gaussian noise only into attacker-controllable features during training and certification, achieving 55-100% certified accuracy on benchmark datasets with median certified radii exceeding the threat threshold by 1.8-5x.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based intrusion detection systems are increasingly studied, but their certified robustness against realistic, feasible traffic manipulation (where attackers can only modify a subset of features) remains largely unaddressed. Standard randomized smoothing performs poorly on clean-trained LLM-IDS models, motivating a noise-augmented, subspace-aligned approach.

Method: TA-RS identifies a directly controllable (DC) subspace — features a remote attacker can modify — and injects isotropic Gaussian noise exclusively into this subspace during both fine-tuning and certification. This aligns the smoothing distribution with the attacker's actual capability. The method is classifier-agnostic and compared against isotropic RS baselines (both standard and DC-noise-augmented training).

Result: Standard RS on clean-trained LLM-IDS yields weak certified accuracy (14-33% on 3 of 4 pairs; 57% on one). Noise-augmented fine-tuning recovers to 68-100% on two of three benchmarks (σ=0.25). At threat threshold R_inf=ε·√|DC| (ε=0.05), TA-RS achieves 55-100% certified accuracy on CIC-IDS-2018 and HIKARI-2021, with median certified radii 1.8-5x the threshold. Residual advantage over iso-trained RS is dataset-dependent (4-19 pp). Gap against isotropic RS with shared DC-noise-augmented training reaches up to 72 pp, driven by training-certification mismatch causing up to 68% abstention. RT-IoT2022 requires increased noise augmentation for recovery (76%/69% certified accuracy for LLaMA3-8B/Qwen3-8B).

Conclusion: Subspace-aligned randomized smoothing (TA-RS) provides meaningful certified robustness for LLM-IDS against realistic attacker models, but success depends critically on noise-augmented fine-tuning and dataset characteristics. The training-certification mismatch, rather than DC alignment alone, drives much of the performance gap against isotropic baselines.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM)-based intrusion detection systems (IDS) are increasingly studied for security monitoring, yet their robustness against feasible traffic manipulation remains largely empirical. We present Traffic-Aware Randomized Smoothing (TA-RS), a classifier-agnostic certified defense that injects Gaussian noise exclusively into the directly controllable (DC) subspace -- features a remote attacker can modify -- during both fine-tuning and certification, aligning the smoothing distribution with the attacker-controllable subspace. We identify a critical prerequisite: applying standard randomized smoothing to clean-trained LLM-IDS yields weak certified accuracy in three of four (model, dataset) pairs tested (14-33%, at or below random) and only 57% in the fourth (43 pp below the noise-augmented result); noise-augmented fine-tuning recovers to 68-100% on two of three benchmark datasets (at sigma=0.25). At the L_inf-equivalent threshold R_inf = epsilon*sqrt(|DC|) (epsilon=0.05), TA-RS achieves 55-100% certified accuracy on CIC-IDS-2018 and HIKARI-2021, with median certified radii (R approx 0.45-0.96) exceeding R_inf by 1.8-5x (across sigma=0.25-1.00). Against a fairly trained iso-trained RS baseline the residual advantage is dataset-dependent (4-19 pp on CIC-IDS-2018). The larger gap -- up to 72 pp against an isotropic RS baseline that shares the DC-noise-augmented training recipe -- primarily reflects the training-certification mismatch rather than DC alignment alone: isotropic test-time noise perturbs uncontrollable features the attacker cannot exploit, triggering abstention rates up to 68%. RT-IoT2022 probes the limits of the method: it fails under the default fine-tuning recipe but recovers to 76%/69% certified accuracy (LLaMA3-8B/Qwen3-8B) when noise augmentation is increased.

</details>


### [193] [Assessing the Forensic Viability of Android Memory Analysis Across Production Builds: A Cross-Version Study of Security Hardening and Structure Preservation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13821)
*Jayasimha Nannapanen,Sneha Sudhakaran*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: Production Android builds have undergone aggressive symbol stripping — static symbols have gone to zero, dynamic symbols dropped ~60%, and source file references vanished — yet the underlying heap architecture, garbage collector infrastructure, and allocation bitmaps remain structurally consistent with development builds. A version-matched development build still resolves valid pointers in production memory.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Google has progressively stripped debug symbols from libart.so in consumer Android builds as a security hardening measure, but no prior work has systematically measured the extent of this stripping or determined whether the memory structures forensic tools depend on remain recoverable in production environments.

Method: The authors extracted libart.so binaries from Pixel factory images spanning Android 8 through Android 15, performed static analysis of symbol tables (static, dynamic, and compressed fallback sections), conducted source code review, compared memory maps across versions, and validated findings through live memory forensics on a rooted, fully stripped Pixel 7 using offsets from version-matched development builds.

Result: Static symbols dropped from 20,495 entries to zero; dynamic symbols decreased by approximately 60%; source file references were completely removed. A compressed fallback section in Android 15 restores thousands of function names but no structure layouts. Heap spaces, garbage collector infrastructure, and allocation bitmaps remain structurally intact, with changes limited to naming conventions and arithmetic optimizations. On a rooted Pixel 7, the runtime entry point remains locatable via the dynamic symbol table, and structural offsets from a version-matched development build resolve to valid pointers in production memory.

Conclusion: Despite aggressive symbol stripping in production Android builds, the core memory architecture of the Android Runtime remains sufficiently stable that forensic tools can still function by leveraging version-matched development builds and residual dynamic symbols, though the loss of structure layout metadata represents a growing challenge.

Abstract: Android memory forensics recovers evidence that never touches disk: decrypted messages, session credentials, and the live internal state of a running application. The tools that perform this recovery depend on debug symbols embedded in libart.so, the Android Runtime library, to locate data structures and interpret their layout. Across recent releases Google has stripped most of that information from the binaries that ship on consumer phones as part of a broader security hardening effort, yet no prior work has measured how far the stripping has progressed on the devices examiners actually encounter, or whether the memory architecture beneath the stripped surface still resembles what the forensics literature describes. This paper measures the gap between the unstripped development builds researchers use and the production builds that ship on real hardware, using binaries extracted from Pixel factory images across Android 8 through Android 15. Static symbols fell from 20,495 entries to zero, dynamic symbols dropped by roughly 60 percent, and source file references disappeared entirely. A compressed fallback section in the Android 15 binary restores thousands of function names but carries no structure layouts. Source code review and memory map comparisons across Android 8 and 15 show that the heap spaces, garbage collector infrastructure, and allocation bitmaps remain structurally intact, with visible changes limited to naming and arithmetic optimization. Live validation on a rooted, fully stripped Pixel 7 confirms that the runtime entry point is still locatable through the dynamic symbol table, and that structural offsets pulled from a version-matched development build resolve to valid pointers inside production memory.

</details>


### [194] [Reveal, Correct, Then Pay: Encrypted Mempools and Perpetual Funding Security](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13832)
*Benjamin Marsh*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper analyzes how encrypted mempools, while preventing MEV attacks reliant on victim transaction observation, create a new vulnerability: attackers can exploit the commit-reveal gap by submitting self-authored state-manipulating transactions whose corrections cannot be adaptively inserted before batch execution, leading to economic distortions in perpetual futures funding mechanisms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Encrypted mempools aim to eliminate front-running and sandwich attacks by hiding pending transactions. However, the authors identify that privacy introduces a temporal "reaction gap" — once a batch is committed, no corrective transaction can enter it. This creates exploitable opportunities distinct from traditional MEV, particularly in mechanisms like perpetual futures funding where predictable state changes determine payment flows.

Method: The paper builds a formal model of self-authored state manipulation under commit-reveal protocols. It microfounds correction behavior through executable arbitrage opportunities, modeling correctors who choose order size trading off local price impact and inventory cost. The analysis derives a "closed stage correction rate" below the adaptive rate, quantifies a response factor that multiplies distortions entering funding windows, and constructs a "local security index" decomposing the vulnerability into attacker blindness, correction shielding, and capitalization shielding components.

Result: The commit-reveal barrier removes ordinary adaptive searchers from the closed stage, yielding a correction rate below the adaptive correction rate whenever positive adaptive capacity becomes available post-reveal. The resulting distortion within a funding window is amplified by an explicit response factor. Transaction privacy also reduces capitalization of predictable funding into entry prices, creating a second amplification channel. These effects are captured by a local security index with three separable components.

Conclusion: Encrypted mempools introduce a new attack class — self-authored state manipulation — that is orthogonal to traditional MEV vectors and persists even with negligible cryptographic overhead. The local security index provides a framework for protocol designers to assess vulnerability along three dimensions, suggesting that commit-reveal designs need complementary mechanisms to mitigate the reaction gap.

Abstract: Encrypted mempools are designed to hide transaction contents until execution order is fixed, preventing many victim dependent forms of maximal extractable value. This paper studies a different class of attack in the form of self-authored state manipulation, in which the attacker knows its own transaction and owns a downstream claim on the state that transaction changes. Perpetual futures funding is a canonical example. The funding signal determines a transfer rate, while receiving side open interest is the transfer base. In a commit then reveal mempool, an adaptive corrective transaction cannot enter the already committed batch. Privacy can therefore create an economic reaction gap even when cryptographic decryption overhead is negligible. We microfound correction through executable arbitrage opportunities. Correctors choose order size against local price impact and inventory cost, while the protocol information schedule determines which opportunities are actionable. The ordering barrier removes ordinary adaptive searchers from the closed stage. It therefore yields a closed stage correction rate below the adaptive correction rate whenever positive adaptive capacity becomes available after reveal. The distortion entering a funding window is multiplied by an explicit response factor. Transaction privacy can also reduce capitalization of predictable funding into entry prices, producing a second amplification channel. The resulting local security index separates attacker blindness, correction shielding, and capitalization shielding.

</details>


### [195] [From Classification to Consistent Templates: Multiple Permuted-Label Classifier Encoding for Biometric Template Protection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13845)
*Baogang Song,Zhongshu Zhao,Qianrong Zheng,Jianwen Xiang,Dongdong Zhao*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: MPLCE uses multiple classifiers with permuted labels to transform biometric samples into stable identity-level encodings, then applies XOR randomization and cryptographic hashing for exact-match verification without error correction or helper data, achieving strong security and competitive accuracy across modalities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing biometric template protection (BTP) methods have critical vulnerabilities: similarity-based matching retains exploitable structures, error-correction approaches introduce helper-data risks, and predefined-template mappings depend on artificial targets or couple protection to specific modalities. A hash-based exact-match paradigm would eliminate these weaknesses, but it requires genuine samples to produce identical intermediate templates before hashing — a challenge unmet by prior methods.

Method: The authors propose Multiple Permuted-Label Classifier Encoding (MPLCE). Key components: (1) Multiple modality-specific classifiers are trained for identity classification, each with a distinct random permutation of identity labels. (2) For a given biometric sample, each classifier predicts a label; these predicted labels are encoded and concatenated into an intermediate template. The permutations ensure that the same identity never maps to identical labels across all classifiers, enlarging the candidate space while preserving classification consistency. (3) An application-specific XOR string randomizes the intermediate template. (4) A cryptographic hash produces the final protected template, enabling exact-match verification without error-correction codes or biometric-dependent helper data. The procedure is modality-agnostic.

Result: On four face datasets and two iris datasets, MPLCE achieves competitive performance: GAR of 98.61% at FAR of 5.51×10⁻⁵% on YouTube Faces (YTF), and GAR of 99.10% at FAR of 0.00% on CASIA-Iris-Lamp. Security analyses and attack evaluations confirm irreversibility, revocability, and unlinkability under the defined threat model.

Conclusion: MPLCE successfully realizes hash-based exact-match biometric verification by leveraging identity classification with permuted-label classifiers, achieving strong template protection (irreversibility, revocability, unlinkability) while maintaining competitive verification performance across multiple modalities and datasets.

Abstract: Biometric template protection (BTP) must secure stored templates while tolerating intra-class variations. Existing methods rely on protected-domain similarity matching, error correction, or predefined-template mappings, potentially retaining exploitable similarity structures, introducing helper-data risks, depending on artificial targets, or coupling protection to specific modalities. Storing only cryptographic hash digests eliminates directly comparable representations and conceals pre-hash templates, but hash-based exact-match verification requires genuine samples to generate identical intermediate templates before hashing. Identity classification is naturally suited to this requirement because it maps variable biometric samples to stable and discriminative identity-level outputs. Based on this insight, we propose Multiple Permuted-Label Classifier Encoding (MPLCE). Through classifier-specific label permutations, MPLCE assigns each identity different labels across multiple classifiers. The predicted labels are encoded and concatenated to form an intermediate template, preventing repeated encodings of a single identity label and enlarging the effective candidate space while preserving classification consistency. The template is randomized with an application-specific XOR string and cryptographically hashed, enabling exact-match verification without error correction codes or biometric-dependent helper data. Using modality-specific classifiers, MPLCE retains the same template generation and protection procedure across modalities. On four face and two iris datasets, MPLCE achieves competitive performance, including a GAR of 98.61\% at a FAR of 5.51\(\times\)10\textsuperscript{-5}\% on YTF and a GAR of 99.10\% at a FAR of 0.00\% on CASIA-Iris-Lamp. Security analyses and attack evaluations support its irreversibility, revocability, and unlinkability under the threat model.

</details>


### [196] [Plausible Deniability Guarantees for Whistleblowers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13928)
*Leo Richter,Matt J. Kusner*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper formalizes whistleblower protection as per-report (0, δ)-differential privacy on audit selection transcripts, proves randomized response cannot beat uniform random auditing, and presents a reduction from private auditing to private continual counting that achieves O(√log T) noise scaling with substantially better utility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Whistleblowers face retaliation risks because organizations can observe which entities get audited and infer who reported them. Existing whistleblower protection lacks formal privacy guarantees, and standard differential privacy mechanisms do not directly address this natural threat model where the adversary is the audited organization itself, which monitors the full sequence of audit decisions to identify reporters.

Method: The authors formalize the threat model as per-report (0, δ)-DP on the audit transcript. They prove randomized response at selection time cannot outperform uniform random auditing beyond an additive δ margin. They then propose a generic reduction: any (0, δ)-DP continual counting mechanism can be plugged in via post-processing, with the audit transcript inheriting the same per-report DP guarantee. They instantiate this with a recent continual counter to achieve O(√log T) noise over horizon T, and provide a utility theorem bounding selection error.

Result: The reduction yields per-report (0, δ)-DP with noise O(√log T) over T decisions. A utility theorem proves selection error vanishes when the gap between the most-reported and runner-up organization grows faster than √log T. Simulations demonstrate substantial improvement over randomized response.

Conclusion: Private continual counting provides an effective foundation for whistleblower protection under a strong-adversary model, significantly outperforming naive approaches like randomized response. The framework bridges differential privacy and organizational whistleblowing with formal, provable guarantees that degrade only logarithmically over time.

Abstract: Whistleblowers are a key safeguard against organizational wrongdoing, but the threat of retaliation deters reporting. Existing whistleblower-protection proposals lack formal privacy guarantees, and existing differential privacy mechanisms do not directly target the natural threat model -- one in which the audited organization itself observes auditor selection decisions and uses them to identify reporters. We formalize protection against a strong-adversary threat model as per-report $(0, δ)$-differential privacy on the transcript of audit selections. Within this framework we prove that a natural approach -- randomized response applied at the selection step -- can never outperform uniform random auditing by more than $δ$ at any horizon. We then give a generic mechanism that reduces private auditing to private continual counting: any $(0, δ)$-DP continual counter plugs in by post-processing, and the audit transcript inherits the same per-report guarantee. Instantiating the reduction with a recent work in continual counting yields per-report $(0, δ)$-DP with noise scaling as $O(\sqrt{\log T})$ across a horizon of $T$ audit decisions. A utility theorem shows that the selection error vanishes whenever the noisy report gap between the most-reported organization and the runner-up grows faster than $\sqrt{\log T}$. Simulations show a substantial improvement over randomized response.

</details>


### [197] [HORCRUX: A Complete PQC RISC-V eXtension Architecture](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13939)
*Alessandra Dolmeta,Valeria Piscopo,Michael Hutter,Maurizio Martina,Guido Masera*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: HORCRUX is a unified RISC-V ISA extension with a tightly coupled coprocessor that accelerates all five NIST-approved PQC algorithms (ML-KEM, ML-DSA, SLH-DSA, HQC, Falcon) by targeting shared computational kernels, achieving up to 129× speedup on FPGA with minimal area overhead (~21k LUTs).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing hardware extensions for PQC typically support only a subset of NIST-standardized algorithms, forcing a trade-off between crypto-agility, performance, and resource efficiency. Constrained embedded systems require a unified, low-area, energy-efficient solution that supports all approved schemes without sacrificing backward compatibility.

Method: The authors identify shared computational kernels across all five NIST PQC algorithms (hash-based, lattice-based, and code-based) and design a compact RISC-V custom instruction set (HORCRUX) executed by a tightly coupled, resource-efficient coprocessor. The design is synthesized and evaluated on a Zynq UltraScale+ FPGA and further characterized in 65 nm CMOS ASIC, with rigorous power analysis.

Result: On Zynq UltraScale+ FPGA: up to 129× speedup for hash-based (SLH-DSA), 9× for lattice-based (ML-KEM, ML-DSA, Falcon), and 27× for code-based (HQC) schemes. Area overhead: fewer than 21k LUTs and 4.4k flip-flops. ASIC postsynthesis results in 65 nm CMOS and power characterization confirm energy efficiency.

Conclusion: HORCRUX demonstrates that a single unified ISE can efficiently accelerate all NIST-approved PQC algorithms with low resource overhead, maintaining RISC-V backward compatibility and offering a scalable, crypto-agile solution for constrained embedded systems.

Abstract: This work presents a compact RISC-V extension for Post-Quantum Cryptography (PQC) called HORCRUX, which provides a unified Instruction-Set Extension (ISE) supporting all NIST-approved PQC algorithms. HORCRUX addresses the difficult trade-off between crypto-agility, high performance, and low resource consumption in constrained environments, a balance typically missing in hardware extensions that focus on limited PQC subsets. By targeting shared kernels across ML-KEM, MLDSA, SLH-DSA, HQC, and Falcon, the extension introduces new RISC-V instructions executed by a resource-efficient, tightly coupled coprocessor. This architecture is specifically optimized for embedded systems with strict energy budgets and limited area. Experimental evaluation on a Zynq UltraScale+ FPGA demonstrates speedups of up to 129x for hash-based, 9x for lattice-based, and 27x for code-based schemes, while adding fewer than 21k LUTs and 4.4k FFs. ASIC results from postsynthesis characterization in 65 nm CMOS are also reported, alongside a rigorous power characterization to validate the architecture's energy efficiency. The extension's modular structure maintains backward compatibility with standard RISC-V cores, offering a scalable solution for deploying PQC on constrained embedded systems.

</details>


### [198] [Agent Skill Security: Threat Models, Attacks, Defenses, and Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13987)
*Sanket Badhe,Priyanka Tiwari*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: The paper introduces SkillSec-Eval, a lifecycle-aware framework for evaluating security risks in reusable LLM agent skills. It identifies vulnerabilities across five lifecycle stages—repository admission, semantic retrieval, planner selection, execution, and skill evolution—and empirically validates the taxonomy using 327 real-world skills, showing that security threats extend well beyond execution-time prompt injection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing security research on LLM agents focuses narrowly on prompt injection and runtime execution, neglecting risks across the full skill lifecycle. As reusable skills become foundational building blocks for LLM agents, a systematic, lifecycle-aware security evaluation framework is needed to uncover vulnerabilities at every stage.

Method: The authors first characterize the skill lifecycle into five stages: repository admission, semantic retrieval, planner selection, execution, and skill evolution. They develop a threat taxonomy mapping attack vectors to each stage. This taxonomy is instantiated in the SkillSec-Eval framework and applied to a repository of 327 real-world skills for comprehensive empirical security evaluation.

Result: The empirical evaluation reveals that vulnerabilities arise at multiple lifecycle stages beyond execution, including during admission (malicious skill submission), retrieval (adversarial semantic matching), planner selection (skill misconfiguration), and evolution (supply-chain style attacks via updates), confirming that current execution-only security approaches are insufficient.

Conclusion: A lifecycle-aware approach to security analysis is essential for reusable agent skills. SkillSec-Eval demonstrates significant vulnerabilities across all lifecycle stages, underscoring the need for holistic security measures that protect the entire skill ecosystem from admission through evolution.

Abstract: Reusable skills are becoming a fundamental building block of Large Language Model (LLM) agents, enabling capabilities to be packaged, shared, and reused across diverse applications. However, existing security research primarily focuses on prompt injection and runtime execution, leaving security risks throughout the broader skill lifecycle largely unexplored. In this paper, we present SkillSec-Eval, a lifecycle-aware framework for systematically evaluating the security of reusable agent skills. We first characterize the skill lifecycle and develop a threat taxonomy spanning repository admission, semantic retrieval, planner selection, execution, and skill evolution. We then instantiate this taxonomy in SkillSec-Eval and conduct a comprehensive empirical evaluation using a repository of 327 real-world skills. Our study demonstrates that vulnerabilities arise at multiple lifecycle stages beyond execution, highlighting the need for lifecycle-aware security analysis of reusable agent skills.

</details>


### [199] [Rethinking Penetration Testing for AI-Enabled Systems: From Resource Compromise to Behavioral Objective Violation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14006)
*Mohammad Allahbakhsh,Mohammad Hassan Bahari,Moslem Attar-Raouf*

Main category: cs.CR

TL;DR: This paper redefines penetration testing for AI-enabled systems as objective-driven behavioral evaluation, moving beyond traditional infrastructure compromise to include adversarial pathways like prompt injection, data poisoning, and agentic misalignment, and proposes a structured testing workflow illustrated through a SOC assistant example.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional penetration testing focuses on exploiting software, infrastructure, and configuration weaknesses, but AI-enabled systems introduce new attack surfaces—prompts, retrieved content, training data, sensor inputs, and human-AI loops—where adversaries can alter system behavior without directly compromising infrastructure. A new conceptual framework is needed to assess security in these systems.

Method: The authors propose: (1) a formal definition of AI-enabled penetration as the feasible induction of AI-governed behavior that violates operational objectives under an explicit threat model; (2) a structured testing workflow consisting of six steps—identify operational objectives, map AI-governed behavior, analyze adversarial influence surfaces, define behavioral failure criteria, execute scenario-based tests, and report evidence linking adversarial action to objective violation; and (3) a running example applying the framework to an AI-enabled SOC assistant.

Result: The framework successfully reframes penetration testing to encompass adversarial pathways including prompt injection, indirect prompt injection, data poisoning, sensor manipulation, retrieval poisoning, tool misuse, and agentic misalignment. The SOC assistant example demonstrates how penetration can occur through behavioral influence rather than infrastructure compromise, validating the framework's applicability to real-world AI-enabled systems.

Conclusion: Penetration testing for AI-enabled systems must evolve from infrastructure-focused evaluation to objective-driven behavioral testing. The proposed definitions, workflow, and methodology provide a comprehensive technical framework for systematically evaluating adversarial success in deployed AI systems where learned models materially influence operational outcomes.

Abstract: Penetration testing traditionally evaluates whether adversaries can exploit weaknesses in software, infrastructure, configurations, or operational controls to achieve security-relevant compromise. This paradigm remains necessary for AI-enabled systems, but it is no longer sufficient. In such systems, adversaries may influence prompts, retrieved content, sensor inputs, training data, memory, tools, or human-AI interaction loops to alter system behavior without directly compromising the underlying infrastructure. This paper reframes penetration testing for AI-enabled systems as objective-driven behavioral evaluation. We define an AI-enabled system as one in which learned models materially influence behavior affecting operational outcomes, and we define AI-enabled penetration as the feasible induction of AI-governed behavior that violates one or more operational objectives under an explicit threat model. This definition preserves conventional penetration testing while extending it to adversarial pathways such as prompt injection, indirect prompt injection, data poisoning, sensor manipulation, retrieval poisoning, tool misuse, and agentic misalignment. We further propose a testing workflow that identifies operational objectives, maps AI-governed behavior, analyzes adversarial influence surfaces, defines behavioral failure criteria, executes scenario-based tests, and reports evidence linking adversarial action to objective violation. A running example involving an AI-enabled security operations center assistant illustrates how penetration may occur through behavioral influence rather than infrastructure compromise. Together, the definitions, workflow, and example provide a technical framework for evaluating adversarial success in deployed AI-enabled systems.

</details>


<div id='cs.SE'></div>

# cs.SE [[Back]](#toc)

### [200] [Falsifiable Release Gates for Self-Improving Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13070)
*Deepak Soni*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The paper proposes falsifiable release gates for self-improving AI agents, where every new capability must pass a machine-verifiable acceptance suite preserving standing invariants before shipping. Implemented in the Antahkarana runtime across seven gates, the system uses capability tokens minted by a control ring, exhaustive bounded model checking over ~1M states, and a constrained self-enhancement loop where tightening changes auto-apply but loosening changes require human merge. All results are reproducible and open-sourced.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safety claims on self-improving agent runtimes are almost always self-graded (policy files, guardrails, README commitments) with no falsifiable, machine-verifiable enforcement mechanism. There is a need for objective, reproducible safety gates that cannot be bypassed and that constrain recursive self-improvement.

Method: The methodology constructs falsifiable release gates: each new capability must pass a pre-specified, machine-verifiable acceptance suite, with fixed standing invariants preserved at each gate. Implemented in the Antahkarana runtime via seven sequential gates (basic observability → self-governing loop). Key mechanisms: (1) safety-critical property capability tokens minted by a control ring before any effector action; (2) exhaustive bounded model checking over ~1M recorded reachable states; (3) verification against execution traces; (4) a deliberately broken model to produce shortest counterexamples, demonstrating the checker has teeth; (5) a positively constrained self-enhancement loop where the write surface is limited to policy rules — tightening changes auto-apply, loosening changes require human merge, and proposals that mispredict their own effect are auto-closed. The bounded model covers the coordination skeleton, not learned components.
