Result: Published acceptance measured results for all seven gates, with precise scope delineation (bounded to the coordination skeleton). The runtime, command-line tools, and gate suite are openly released for reproduction. The entire gate suite runs via a single non-bypassable command in seconds. The broken-model counterexample confirms the checker's efficacy.

Conclusion: Falsifiable, machine-verifiable release gates are feasible for constraining self-improving agent runtimes. The Antahkarana implementation demonstrates that safety invariants can be exhaustively checked at each capability gate, with the self-enhancement loop structurally constrained so that dangerous changes cannot propagate automatically. Reproducibility is ensured through open release of the runtime and gate suite.

Abstract: Safety claims on self-improving agent runtimes are almost always self-graded: a policy file, a guardrail, or a README commitment. We describe falsifiable release gates, and a methodology to build and validate such systems, such that every new capability must pass a pre-specified, machine-verifiable acceptance suite before it ships, and a fixed set of standing invariants is preserved at each gate. I think we applied the method in the Antahkarana, an open runtime, via seven gates from basic observability into a self governing loop that suggests changes to its own policy. no action goes to an effector without a safety-critical property capability token minted by a control ring is exhaustively machine-checked over the one million recorded reachable state space of a bounded model and re-checked against execution traces. A purposely broken model gives the shortest counterexample, so the checker has teeth, is apparent. The self-enhancement loop is positively Constrained: the entire write surface is policy rules, tightening changes. may auto-apply loosening changes always require a human merge and a proposal autoclosed is one that mispredicts its own effect; We publish the acceptance measured results for all the seven gates, define precisely the scope of each claim (a bounded of the coordination skeleton (not the learned components) and free the runtime, either command line tools and the gate suite, so the results reproduce, and gates can run against other agent frameworks. Reviewers may repeat the single command central non-bypass in seconds.

</details>


### [201] [Compaction as Epistemic Failure: How Agentic LLM Tools Fabricate Confirmed Results from Killed Processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13071)
*Hiroki Tamba*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Claude Code's session compaction mechanism erroneously treats partial, timed-out command output (exit code 143) as confirmed results, propagating false positives across sessions without re-verification. This reveals a deeper conflation between terminal observation and durable persistence in agentic LLM workflows.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To document and analyze a specific failure mode in agentic LLM coding tools — namely that compaction summaries inherit and propagate unverified partial outputs as ground truth — and to extend prior work on LLM-as-judge reliability deficits to the domain of agentic self-reporting on operational outcomes.

Method: The authors performed an empirical case-study analysis of Claude Code's session compaction behavior, focusing on how timed-out commands (exit code 143) with partial standard output are recorded. They examined the mechanism by which terminal-observable information is conflated with durably persisted information, and drew parallels to previously documented non-determinism failures in LLM-as-judge grading frameworks.

Result: Timed-out commands producing partial stdout (exit code 143) are recorded in compaction summaries as confirmed results. These false positives propagate across subsequent sessions and model versions without re-verification, revealing a systemic conflation of observation (terminal output) with persistence (durable storage) in the agentic tooling architecture.

Conclusion: Agentic LLM tools exhibit reliability deficits analogous to those in LLM-as-judge grading when self-reporting operational outcomes. Any workflow relying on agentic session continuity — data processing, scientific computation, multi-step automation — is exposed to this failure mode. The finding calls for architectural safeguards that distinguish transient terminal output from verified, persisted results.

Abstract: Agentic LLM coding tools compress long session histories into compaction summaries that subsequent sessions inherit as ground truth. This paper documents a failure mode in Claude Code where partial standard output from timed-out commands (exit code 143) is recorded in compaction summaries as confirmed results, propagating false positives across sessions and model versions without re-verification. The underlying mechanism is a conflation of observation and persistence, where information that appeared in the terminal is treated as equivalent to information written to durable storage. This finding extends the analysis of LLM self-evaluation failures reported in prior work on non-determinism in LLM-as-judge grading by showing that agentic tools exhibit analogous reliability deficits when reporting on their own operational outcomes. The failure has direct implications for any workflow that relies on agentic session continuity for data processing, scientific computation, or multi-step automation.

</details>


### [202] [Inference Economics of Enterprise Coding Agents: A Case Study of Cloud vs. On-Premise LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13080)
*Sheng-Wei Peng,Yi-Hsun Lin,Yi-Pei Lee*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: A longitudinal case study comparing API-based Claude Opus with on-premise quantized GLM models finds that aggressive prompt caching makes API cost competitive, but local deployment incurs a significantly higher defect-repair burden (FCR 74.9% vs 45.9%), with hybrid routing offering a cost-quality trade-off rather than a clear win.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Engineering organizations face a tension between API-based frontier models (strong reasoning, high token cost) and on-premise open-weights models (low marginal cost, data sovereignty, reduced reasoning fidelity). The authors aim to empirically characterize this trade-off using real-world production data rather than synthetic benchmarks.

Method: A single-developer, non-randomized longitudinal case study over two contiguous 28-day periods on a production monorepo. Period 1 uses API-based Claude Opus 4.7/4.8 via Claude Code; Period 2 uses on-premise GLM-5.1/5.2 quantized to NVFP4 on NVIDIA Blackwell hardware via Opencode. Analysis draws on LLM telemetry, Git history, Fix Commit Ratio (FCR), Mantel-Haenszel odds ratios stratified by difficulty tier, timestamp-based developer-experience indicators, and offline replay of hybrid routing gateways. Total Cost of Ownership (TCO) is modeled under Taiwan-market parameters with shared vs. dedicated GPU allocation.

Result: Prompt caching achieved a 99.3% hit rate, reducing realized API cost by 88.6% to $0.57/M tokens — below the $2.83 amortized on-premise unit cost. However, the local configuration produced an FCR of 74.9% versus 45.9% for the API baseline (Mantel-Haenszel OR=3.61; odds of a repair commit 2.6–4.9× higher across difficulty tiers). Under shared GPU allocation, on-premise TCO was 40.1% lower than cached API; dedicated GPU reservation cost 43.8% more. Shared-allocation penalties manifested as developer-experience degradation (debugging spirals, slower commit cadence). Hybrid routing gateways traced a cost-quality frontier rather than dominating the pure-API baseline.

Conclusion: The API vs. on-premise decision is not binary: aggressive prompt caching makes API economically viable even against amortized local hardware, but the true cost of on-premise under shared allocation is a measurable developer-experience tax — higher defect rates and slower iteration — that TCO figures alone obscure. Hybrid routing offers a tunable compromise along a cost-quality frontier.

Abstract: Autonomous coding agents force engineering organizations to choose between API-based frontier models -- strong reasoning at high token cost -- and on-premise quantized open-weights models, which promise low-marginal-cost scaling and data sovereignty at some loss of reasoning fidelity. We study this trade-off through a single-developer, non-randomized longitudinal case study over two contiguous 28-day periods on a production monorepo: an API-based Claude Opus 4.7/4.8 configuration using Claude Code versus an on-premise GLM-5.1/5.2 configuration using Opencode, quantized to NVFP4, on NVIDIA Blackwell hardware. Analyzing LLM telemetry and Git history, we find that prompt caching (99.3% hit rate) cuts realized API cost by 88.6% to an effective \$0.57 per million tokens -- below even the \$2.83 amortized unit cost of the shared on-premise slice (a utilization-dependent inversion; total realized spend and total cost of ownership (TCO) are the robust quantities). At comparable gross code churn, the local configuration was associated with a far higher defect-repair burden: a Fix Commit Ratio (FCR) of 74.9% versus 45.9%, with the odds of a commit being a repair 2.6 to 4.9 times higher within every difficulty tier (Mantel-Haenszel OR = 3.61). Under Taiwan-market parameters and a symmetric labor model, on-premise deployment nonetheless saves 40.1% of true TCO under shared GPU allocation, whereas dedicated reservation costs 43.8% more than the cached API. Under shared allocation, the genuine penalty is not monetary but a measurable developer-experience burden -- timestamp indicators show more work trapped in debugging spirals and a slower commit cadence -- and an offline replay shows hybrid routing gateways trade defect rate for infrastructure savings along a cost-quality frontier rather than dominate the pure-API baseline.

</details>


### [203] [Self-Improving AI Coding Agents Through Accumulated Behavioral Rules: A Closed-Loop Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13091)
*Aditya Aggarwal,Nahid Farhady Ghalaty*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: A closed-loop framework that converts human code review feedback into persistent behavioral rules, enabling LLM-based coding agents to avoid repeating mistakes across sessions without any model weight updates. Deployed on a 35+ microservices platform, rule set grew from 5 to 18 behavioral rules, achieving 0% recurrence of ruled-against error classes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based coding agents repeatedly make the same classes of mistakes across sessions because they lack any mechanism to retain and apply corrections from human review feedback. This leads to wasted review effort and prevents agents from accumulating engineering knowledge over time.

Method: A closed-loop framework with three integrated components: (1) a version-controlled instruction file that accumulates behavioral rules codified from every accepted review comment; (2) a self-review checklist executed before code submission to enforce compliance; and (3) automated validation to maintain rule set integrity as it grows. Rules are derived from real review feedback rather than pre-specified heuristics.

Result: Deployed across a 35+ service microservices platform, the rule set expanded from 5 to 18 behavioral rules, 15+ language-specific standards, and a 15-item self-review checklist. Across 11 recorded working sessions (code generation, PR review, incident investigation, cross-service refactoring), accumulated rules shifted review effort from low-level correctness to design-level validation and achieved a measured 0% recurrence rate for ruled-against error classes. Rules transferred across heterogeneous agent interfaces.

Conclusion: The framework demonstrates persistent cross-session learning without weight updates on production codebases, addressing behavioral consistency over time — an orthogonal dimension unmeasured by existing benchmarks. The result is a coding agent that improves with every review cycle by accumulating human collaborators' engineering wisdom.

Abstract: LLM-based coding agents repeat the same classes of mistakes across sessions because they lack a mechanism to retain corrections from human review feedback. We present a closed-loop framework in which every accepted review comment is codified as a persistent behavioral rule, progressively expanding the set of error classes the agent can self-detect. The framework combines an accumulating rule set in a version-controlled instruction file, a self-review checklist executed before code submission, and automated validation that ensures rule set integrity as it grows. In deployment across a 35+ service microservices platform, the rule set grew from 5 to 18 behavioral rules, 15+ language-specific standards, and a 15-item self-review checklist, all derived from real review feedback. We present empirical results from 11 recorded working sessions spanning code generation, PR review, incident investigation, and cross service refactoring. We observe that accumulated rules shift review effort from low-level correctness toward design-level validation, achieve a measured 0% recurrence rate for ruled-against error classes, and transfer across heterogeneous agent interfaces. We compare our approach against related work in experiential LLM learning (Reflexion, ExpeL, Voyager) and automated code review (CodeReviewer, SWE-bench agents), showing that our framework achieves persistent cross-session learning without weight updates, operates on production codebases rather than synthetic benchmarks, and addresses an orthogonal dimension (behavioral consistency over time) that existing benchmarks do not measure. The result is a coding agent that improves with every review cycle, accumulating the engineering wisdom of its human collaborators without changing a single model weight.

</details>


### [204] [SemaDiff: Identifying Semantic-Changing Commits with Generated Code and Tests](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13111)
*Maha Ayub,Michael Konstantinou,Ahmed Khanfir,Nikolaos Tsantalis,Mike Papadakis*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: SemaDiff identifies whether commits are purely semantic-preserving (behaviour-unchanged) by comparing test execution outcomes between pre- and post-commit versions. It uses LLM-generated calling methods and automatically generated tests to exercise modified code, classifying commits based on whether all tests produce identical results. Evaluated on 183 manually annotated Java commits, it achieves ~76% accuracy with 100% precision in detecting semantic-changing commits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing approaches can detect refactoring commits but cannot guarantee a commit is *purely* semantic-preserving — refactorings may be interleaved with behaviour-changing modifications. This limitation adversely affects downstream tasks including debugging, fault localisation, bug dataset construction, rollback analysis, and bug fix backporting. A reliable method for distinguishing purely semantic-preserving commits from those that change behaviour is needed.

Method: SemaDiff employs behaviour-based analysis via differential test execution. For a given commit, it: (1) analyses the diff to identify modified code and extracts unchanged dependent code that calls it; (2) uses a large language model (LLM) to generate an additional dependent class that exercises the changed code in both versions; (3) automatically generates tests for the dependent code, yielding identical tests applicable to both pre- and post-commit versions; (4) classifies the commit as semantic-preserving only if all generated tests produce identical outcomes across both versions.

Result: Evaluated on a manually constructed and annotated dataset of 183 commits from well-known open-source Java projects, SemaDiff correctly distinguishes semantic-preserving from semantic-changing commits in approximately 76% of cases, achieving 100% precision in semantic-changing commit detection (i.e., zero false positives for behaviour-changing classification).

Conclusion: SemaDiff provides the first reliable approach for identifying purely semantic-preserving commits through behaviour-based differential test execution, with perfect precision on semantic-changing commits. This fills a critical gap in mining software repositories and enables more reliable downstream analyses.

Abstract: Distinguishing semantic-preserving commits from changing ones remains an open challenge in software repository mining. While existing approaches detect refactoring commits accurately, they cannot ensure that a commit is purely semantic-preserving, without any interleaving behaviour-changing modification. This limitation can impact several tasks, such as debugging, fault localisation, bug dataset construction, rollback analysis, and bug fixes backporting. To fill this gap, we propose SemaDiff, a novel approach for identifying semantic-preserving commits through behaviour-based analysis; comparison of similar test execution on pre- and post-commit versions. As code impacted by the refactoring is often hard to test and different accross both versions, we propose generating additional calling methods to that code, which serve as testing target. Given a commit, SemaDiff analyses the diff to identify modified code and extracts unchanged dependent code that calls it. It then generates an additional dependent class using a large language model to exercise the changed code in both versions, and automatically generates tests for the dependent code. This way, we obtain the same tests for the different code versions, enabling the behavioural-difference detection. The commit is classified as semantic-preserving only if all generated tests produce identical outcomes across the two versions. To evaluate SemaDiff, we construct and annotate manually a dataset of 183 commits, gathered from well-known open-source Java projects. The obtained results show that SemaDiff distinguishes accurately semantic-preserving from -- changing commits in about 76% of the cases, with a 100% precision in semantic-changing commit detection.

</details>


### [205] [From Human-Centric to Agentic Code Review: The Impact of Different Generations of Generative AI Technology on Review Quality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13196)
*Suzhen Zhong,Shayan Noei,Bram Adams,Ying Zou*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Analysis of 1.02M pull requests across 207 GitHub projects reveals three AI reviewer adoption practices (Gradual, Rapid LLM, Rapid AI Agent). Agent-involved collaboration patterns correlate with faster review decisions under certain adoption modes, but efficiency gains do not improve review quality. Human-AI collaboration patterns emerge as the strongest predictor of review efficiency once AI reviewers participate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The code review process is undergoing a fundamental shift from human-centric to AI-supported workflows involving LLM and AI agent reviewers. However, there is a lack of empirical evidence on how this transition affects review efficiency and review quality, leaving practitioners without guidance on effective AI adoption strategies.

Method: The authors analyzed 1.02 million reviewed pull requests from 207 GitHub projects spanning three code review eras: human-centric, LLM-assisted, and agentic code review. They identified three AI reviewer adoption practices and modeled pull request review discussions as reviewer interaction sequences to characterize collaboration patterns among human, LLM, and AI agent reviewers.

Result: Agent-involved collaboration patterns — particularly reviews initiated by AI agents or involving multiple AI agents — are associated with faster review decisions under Gradual AI Adoption and Rapid AI Agent Adoption. However, these efficiency gains do not translate into better review quality. Review activity and PR type remain important across all eras, but human-AI collaboration patterns become the strongest explanatory factor for review efficiency once LLM and AI agent reviewers participate.

Conclusion: The findings provide empirical guidance for designing AI-supported code review processes that can improve efficiency without compromising review quality. Practitioners should carefully consider adoption strategies and collaboration patterns rather than assuming AI participation automatically yields net benefits.

Abstract: Code review helps maintain software quality before code integration, but it also imposes a substantial workload on human reviewers. As generative artificial intelligence becomes part of software development, code review is shifting from a primarily human review process toward AI-supported review processes in which large language model (LLM) reviewers and AI agent reviewers participate alongside human reviewers. However, we still lack empirical evidence on how this transition affects review efficiency and review quality. In this paper, we study 1.02 million reviewed pull requests from 207 GitHub projects that transition across three code review eras: human-centric review, LLM-assisted review, and agentic code review. We identify three AI reviewer adoption practices: Gradual AI Adoption, Rapid LLM Adoption, and Rapid AI Agent Adoption. We further model pull request review discussions as reviewer interaction sequences to characterize how human, LLM, and AI agent reviewers collaborate during the review process. Our results show that agent-involved collaboration patterns, especially reviews initiated by AI agents or involving multiple AI agents, are associated with faster review decisions under Gradual AI Adoption and Rapid AI Agent Adoption. However, these efficiency gains do not translate into better review quality. We also find that review activity and pull request type remain important across eras, while human-AI collaboration patterns become the strongest explanatory factor for review efficiency once LLM and AI agent reviewers participate. These findings provide empirical guidance for designing AI-supported code review processes that improve efficiency without weakening review quality.

</details>


### [206] [Can LLMs Learn and Apply Multi-Level Modelling Semantics? A First Empirical Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13257)
*Yuhong Fu,Weixing Zhang,Bowen Jiang,Haowei Cheng,Karamjti Kaur,Markus Stumptner*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: First empirical study evaluating commercial LLMs (GPT-5.4, Claude Opus 4.6, Gemini 3.1 Pro) on multi-level modelling tasks. Syntactic correctness is achievable, but semantic correctness remains partial (52–79%), with LLMs struggling to infer implicit constraints. Claude shows the most balanced performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Industry 5.0 demands human-centric modelling tools, and multi-level modelling (MLM) offers richer abstraction but imposes complex semantic constraints. While LLMs have shown promise in two-level model-driven engineering, their applicability to MLM — which differs fundamentally in semantics — remains untested.

Method: Three commercial LLMs were tasked with generating multi-level models for the MULTI Warehouse Challenge using the SLICER language under six distinct prompting strategies, producing 90 models. These were evaluated against a manually validated reference across fourteen metrics spanning syntactic and semantic correctness.

Result: Syntactic correctness was largely achieved across models. However, semantic correctness — specifically Instantiation/Specialisation Correctness — ranged only from 52% to 79%. LLMs faithfully reproduced explicitly stated content but rarely completed implicit structural/constraint inferences. Self-checking strategies acted primarily as rule checkers rather than improving reference alignment. Prompting strategies exhibited a precision–completeness trade-off.

Conclusion: Current LLMs are not yet reliable for fully autonomous MLM generation due to semantic correctness gaps. The findings delineate the boundaries of LLM capability in MLM and inform the design of human-centred, AI-assisted modelling workflows for Industry 5.0, where LLMs may serve best as copilots rather than autonomous generators.

Abstract: Industry 5.0 emphasises human-centric industrial system design, placing additional demands on modelling tools. Multi-level modelling (MLM) can directly represent three or more abstraction levels, but this comes at the cost of more complex semantic constraints that model correctness depends on. Large Language Models (LLMs) have been increasingly studied in model-driven engineering, but this evidence rests entirely on two-level modelling tasks, and whether it generalises to MLM, whose semantics differ in kind, remains untested. This paper presents the first empirical study of this question. We have three commercial LLMs (GPT-5.4, Claude Opus 4.6, and Gemini 3.1 Pro) generate multi-level models for the MULTI Warehouse Challenge in the SLICER language under six prompting strategies, yielding 90 generated models compared against a manually validated reference using fourteen metrics. Syntactic correctness is within reach, but semantic correctness is only partially achieved, with Instantiation/Specialisation Correctness ranging from 52% to 79%. Models reproduce content stated explicitly in the task text, but rarely complete structure and constraints the text implies without stating. Prompting strategies trade off precision against completeness, and self-checking functions mainly as a rule checker rather than reliably improving alignment with the reference design. Among the three LLMs, Claude shows the most balanced profile. These results clarify the boundaries of current LLM capability for MLM and inform the design of human-centred, AI-assisted modelling workflows for Industry 5.0.

</details>


### [207] [Faithful Autoformalization of Natural Language Assertions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13303)
*Hongyi Liu,Madhusudan Parthasarathy,Adithya Murali*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Monty is an autoformalization framework that improves the translation of natural-language specifications into executable code assertions. It introduces a conformance score metric and validity scores from runtime testing to filter LLM-generated formalizations, achieving up to 20-point precision gains over naive LLM translation on 541 assertion-generation tasks from Java collection classes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Formal contracts (assertions) are critical for software testing and verification but are labor-intensive and error-prone to write manually. While LLMs can potentially autoformalize natural-language specs into assertions, two key challenges remain: (1) ensuring the validity of generated assertions, and (2) resolving ambiguity inherent in natural-language specifications. A naive LLM translation approach is insufficiently reliable.

Method: Monty frames autoformalization as a filtering problem. It generates multiple candidate formalizations using LLMs, then ranks and selects the best using two novel metrics: (1) a conformance score that measures alignment between the natural-language spec and the formalized assertion, and (2) validity scores derived from executing the candidate assertions against the actual codebase. The framework targets Java collection-like classes and evaluates on 541 assertion-generation tasks.

Result: On 541 assertion-generation tasks across 22 collection-like Java classes, Monty recovers the ground-truth assertions more reliably than naive LLM-based translation, improving precision by up to 20 points on average.

Conclusion: Autoformalization benefits significantly from a filtering-based approach that combines semantic conformance checking with runtime validity testing. Monty demonstrates that coupling LLM generation with execution-driven validation yields substantially more reliable assertion synthesis than direct translation alone.

Abstract: Formal contracts are essential for software testing and verification, yet writing them remains labor-intensive and error-prone. LLMs offer a promising path toward autoformalization: synthesizing executable assertions from natural-language specifications and thereby bridging the gap between informal developer intent and formal executable specifications. We present Monty: an autoformalization framework for assertions that tackles the challenges of expectations of validity of assertions and ambiguity in natural-language. Our techniques are based on filtering formalizations using a novel conformance score metric and validity scores obtained from testing the code against formalized assertions. We evaluate our approach on 541 assertion-generation tasks derived from 22 collection-like Java classes, and show that our technique produces the ground truth more reliably (improving upto 20 points in precision on average) than when using LLMs naively to translate assertions.

</details>


### [208] [A Telemetry-Driven Model for Quantifying Upgrade Risk in Durable Workflow Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13617)
*Luca Maraschi,Matteo Collina*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: The paper presents a probabilistic model to quantify the risk of upgrading in-flight workflow runs from one code version to another using static diffs and existing telemetry, without requiring dry-runs or sandboxing. It decomposes risk into protocol, interface, and state migration axes, combines exact backward rehydration analysis with a forward Markov model, and extends to coupling-aware fleet risk via minimum s-t cuts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Durable workflow engines replay immutable event logs deterministically, tying in-flight runs to their originating code version. Upgrading can silently corrupt state or halt progress. Existing mitigations (pinning, patch gates, side-by-side deployment) treat every change as maximally dangerous and require draining old versions, which is untenable for long-lived workflows that sleep for weeks. A principled, risk-based approach to version upgrades is needed.

Method: A closed-form probabilistic model called Workflow Upgrade Risk (WUR) is introduced. Risk decomposes along three axes: protocol, interface, and state migration. It combines (1) an exact backward (rehydration) term computed on recorded prefixes modulo trace equivalence of concurrent completions, and (2) a probabilistic forward term derived from hitting probabilities in an empirically estimated Markov model of control flow, using Bayesian estimation throughout to produce credible intervals. For coupling, an empirical coupling graph captures inter-run dependencies (hooks, hierarchy, shared resources), fleet risk is formulated as the least fixpoint of a failure-contagion operator, and the migrate/pin partition is computed as a minimum s-t cut.

Result: A zero backward-risk verdict certifies safe rehydration under the new version. The model yields a policy that partitions runs into migrate, review, and pin classes. The coupling-aware extension removes the inter-run independence assumption and computes exact migrate/pin partitions via minimum s-t cuts on the coupling graph.

Conclusion: The WUR framework provides a theoretically grounded, practical method for assessing workflow upgrade safety without disruptive draining of old versions, enabling fine-grained, risk-based migration decisions even for long-running workflows with complex inter-run coupling.

Abstract: Durable workflow engines reconstruct execution state by deterministically replaying an immutable event log, coupling every in-flight run to the code version that produced its history: a new deployment can invalidate the replay of runs started under the old version, silently corrupting state or halting progress. Existing mitigations -- pinning, patch gates, side-by-side deployment -- treat every change as maximally dangerous and drain old versions, untenable for workflows that sleep for weeks.
  We present a closed-form probabilistic model that quantifies the risk of upgrading in-flight runs from workflow version $V_1$ to $V_2$ using only a static structural diff and telemetry the protocol already persists -- event logs, step payloads, historical paths -- with no dry-run, sandbox, or shadow execution. Risk decomposes along three axes (protocol, interface, state migration) and combines an exact backward (rehydration) term, computed on recorded prefixes modulo trace equivalence of concurrent completions, with a probabilistic forward term from hitting probabilities in an empirically estimated Markov model of control flow. Estimation is Bayesian throughout, so the Workflow Upgrade Risk (WUR) score carries a credible interval and thin telemetry surfaces as uncertainty. We prove that a zero backward-risk verdict certifies safe rehydration under the new version, and derive a policy partitioning runs into migrate, review, and pin classes. Finally we drop the inter-run independence assumption: coupling through hooks, hierarchy, and shared resources is captured by an empirical coupling graph, fleet risk becomes the least fixpoint of a failure-contagion operator, and the coupling-aware migrate/pin partition is computed exactly as a minimum s-t cut.

</details>


### [209] [The Replication Assessment Problem in Software Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13815)
*Giuseppe Destefanis,Martin Shepperd,Leila Yousefi*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Replication studies in software engineering suffer from inconsistent and ad hoc assessment criteria. A systematic review of 10 recent replications (2021–2025) reveals substantial heterogeneity and contradictory practices. The authors propose a principled framework grounded in statistical, methodological, and measurement principles to improve consistency and evidence accumulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current assessment of replication study outcomes in empirical software engineering relies on loosely defined or ad hoc criteria, leading to uncertain and inconsistent interpretation. There is a need to document these inconsistencies and establish a principled, shared framework for meaningful evaluation.

Method: A systematic review of replication studies published between 2021 and 2025 in empirical software engineering. For each identified study (N=10), the authors extracted assessment criteria and analyzed them for heterogeneity, logical consistency, and alignment with established statistical principles. A principled assessment framework was then proposed and demonstrated through worked examples.

Result: Ten replication studies were located. Analysis revealed substantial heterogeneity in assessment practices, contradictory criteria applied to similar data, limited recognition of measurement uncertainty, and an absence of shared standards across studies.

Conclusion: Adopting consistent and transparent assessment principles would reduce ambiguity, improve comparability, and support more reliable evidence accumulation in software engineering replication research.

Abstract: Background: Replication studies in software engineering are increasingly common, yet their interpretation remains uncertain and inconsistent because assessments frequently rely on loosely defined or ad hoc criteria. Aim: This study aims to document how replication study outcomes are currently assessed in empirical software engineering, identify problems arising from inconsistent criteria and propose a principled framework for meaningful evaluation. Method: We conducted a systematic review of replication studies, with the search covering recent empirical software engineering replications (2021--2025). For each study, we extracted the criteria used to assess replication outcomes and analysed these for heterogeneity, logical consistency, and alignment with established statistical principles. Results: A total of 10 replication studies were located. The analysis reveals substantial heterogeneity in assessment practices, with contradictory criteria applied to similar data, limited acknowledgement of measurement uncertainty, and an absence of shared standards. We propose a principled framework grounded in statistical, methodological, and measurement considerations, and demonstrate its application through worked examples. Conclusions: Adopting consistent and transparent assessment principles would reduce ambiguity, improve comparability, and support more reliable evidence accumulation in software engineering replication research.

</details>


### [210] [PROBE: Benchmarking Code Generation in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13820)
*Rodrigo Pato Nogueira,Marco Vieira,João R. Campos*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: PROBE is an extensible benchmark framework for evaluating LLM code generation across three dimensions—functional correctness, solution proximity, and code quality—spanning five programming languages and multiple prompting strategies, revealing that LLMs struggle with harder problems and make fundamental, avoidable errors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing code generation benchmarks are limited: they focus on single programming languages, rely solely on unit test outcomes, and neglect critical dimensions like overall code quality and proximity to valid solutions. A systematic, multi-dimensional evaluation framework is needed to comprehensively assess LLM capabilities and limitations.

Method: The authors introduce PROBE, an extensible benchmark framework featuring diverse metrics across three complementary dimensions (functional correctness, proximity to valid solutions, code quality), representative workloads, varied prompt templates, and a robust experimental procedure. They evaluate four open-source and two proprietary LLMs under three prompting strategies across five programming languages, complemented by an analysis of common error patterns.

Result: LLMs achieve promising results overall but degrade significantly on harder problems. Smaller models particularly struggle with low-resource programming languages. Many failures stem from fundamental, easily avoidable errors, highlighting the unreliability of automatically generated code.

Conclusion: PROBE enables a more holistic assessment of LLM code generation than prior benchmarks. The findings underscore that despite progress, automatically generated code remains unreliable due to basic errors, and model performance is sensitive to problem difficulty and language resource availability.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly being used in everyday software engineering tasks, particularly in automated code generation. Despite their widespread adoption, these models remain far from perfect, making systematic and fair evaluation essential to understand their strengths and limitations. In the context of code generation, existing benchmarks are limited: they often target a single programming language and rely primarily on unit test outcomes, while overlooking other critical dimensions such as the overall quality of the generated code and its closeness to a valid solution. To address these gaps, we introduce PROBE, an extensible benchmark framework that, unlike prior work, establishes a systematic structure built on diverse and well-defined metrics, representative workloads, varied prompt templates, and a robust experimental procedure. In practice, the code generated by the LLMs is evaluated along three complementary dimensions: functional correctness, proximity to valid solutions, and code quality, enabling a comprehensive assessment of performance. We use PROBE to evaluate four open-source and two proprietary models under three prompting strategies across five programming languages. We further complement this analysis with a study of common errors in the code and provide concrete examples, offering clearer insight into where LLMs tend to struggle. Our findings show that, while LLMs achieve promising results, they struggle with harder problems and, in the case of smaller models, with programming languages that have fewer available resources for training, and they often fail due to fundamental and easily avoidable errors that underscore the unreliability of automatically generated code.

</details>


### [211] [ProfMalPlus: Agent-Coordinated Detection of Malicious NPM Packages via Static-Dynamic Analysis Synergy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13965)
*Yiheng Huang,Zhijia Zhao,Bihuan Chen,Susheng Wu,Zhuotong Zhou,Yiheng Cao,Kun Hu,Xin Hu,Xin Peng*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: ProfMalPlus is a malicious NPM package detector that combines object-sensitive behavior graphs with LLM-based reasoning over annotated code slices. It uses a multi-stage pipeline with local/global judges, optional static/dynamic enrichment, and achieves 98.1% F1-score while discovering 597 previously unknown malicious packages.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing malicious NPM package detectors suffer from four key weaknesses: (1) inadequate modeling of obfuscated behavior, (2) neglect of JavaScript's object-centric features, (3) poor coordination between static and dynamic analysis, and (4) semantic information loss during behavior abstraction. These gaps leave open source software vulnerable to supply-chain attacks via transitive dependencies.

Method: ProfMalPlus operates in several stages: (1) identifies installation commands and entry files; (2) constructs object-sensitive behavior graphs capturing sensitive APIs, third-party calls, and unresolved calls; (3) extracts security-relevant code slices with inline static analysis annotations; (4) employs local judge agents for independent per-slice assessment with self-consistency voting to reduce LLM variance; (5) a global judge synthesizes reports into an entry-level verdict; (6) for undetermined cases, a router selects between third-party enrichment (registry-derived module/method semantics) and dynamic augmentation (sandbox execution for runtime behavior); (7) enriched evidence is fed back for reassessment; (8) a localization agent outputs malicious code snippets with explanations.

Result: ProfMalPlus achieves a 98.1% F1-score, outperforming state-of-the-art detectors by margins ranging from 3.5% to 52.6%. It identified 597 previously unknown malicious packages, all of which were confirmed and subsequently removed from the NPM registry.

Conclusion: The integration of object-sensitive behavior graphs with coordinated LLM reasoning and hybrid static-dynamic enrichment effectively addresses the core limitations of prior NPM malware detectors. ProfMalPlus demonstrates both high detection accuracy and practical real-world impact through large-scale discovery of previously undetected malicious packages.

Abstract: Open source software is vulnerable to supply-chain attacks through transitive dependencies, especially malicious code injected into NPM packages. Existing detectors often inadequately model obfuscated behavior, overlook JavaScript's object-centric features, poorly coordinate static and dynamic analysis, and lose semantic information during behavior abstraction. We propose ProfMalPlus, a malicious NPM package detector combining object-sensitive behavior graphs with coordinated LLM reasoning over annotated code slices. It identifies installation commands and entry files, then constructs graphs capturing sensitive APIs, third-party calls, and unresolved calls. From these graphs, ProfMalPlus extracts security-relevant slices and adds inline static analysis evidence. Local judge agents independently assess each slice. Self-consistency consolidates repeated judgements to reduce LLM variance, while a global judge synthesizes their reports into an entry-level verdict. For undetermined cases, a router selects either third-party enrichment, which adds registry derived module and method semantics, or dynamic augmentation, which executes the package in a sandbox to resolve runtime dependent behavior. The enriched evidence is fed back for reassessment. Finally, a localization agent reports malicious code snippets with explanations. ProfMalPlus achieves a 98.1% F1-score, outperforming state-of-the-art detectors by 3.5% to 52.6%. It also identified 597 previously unknown malicious packages, all confirmed and removed from NPM.

</details>


### [212] [Early Adoption of Agentic Coding Tools by GitHub Projects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14037)
*Maliha Noushin Raida,Daqing Hou*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Analysis of 25,264 agentic PRs from 2,361 GitHub repositories reveals that intensive adoption of agentic coding tools is concentrated in few projects, small projects show higher participation ratios, most projects fall below industry productivity benchmarks, and human-agent collaboration is dominated by a single-human oversight model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work has focused on PR-level outcomes of agent-generated contributions but has not examined how agentic coding tools are adopted and managed at the project level. This study fills that gap by providing project-level empirical evidence on adoption patterns, productivity, and human-agent collaboration.

Method: The authors analyzed 25,264 agentic PRs from 2,361 popular GitHub repositories over a three-month observation period, examining three dimensions: (1) adoption patterns of agentic coding tools, (2) project-level agentic PR productivity compared to an industry-reported benchmark of 36 PRs per participant, and (3) patterns of human-agent collaboration (single-human vs. multi-human oversight).

Result: Key findings: (1) Median repository generates only 1–2 agentic PRs in three months — intensive adoption is concentrated in a small subset of projects; (2) Small projects (1–5 contributors) exhibit higher participation ratios and agentic PR activity than medium/large projects; (3) Only a few projects exceed 36 PRs/participant, with most below this threshold; (4) Human-agent collaboration is dominated by a single-human oversight model where one developer reviews/modifies agent contributions, while multi-human collaboration is rare.

Conclusion: Successful integration of agent-generated contributions depends on both agent capabilities and the human/organizational processes governing their use. The study captures an early snapshot of adoption, and future work should track how these patterns evolve.

Abstract: Agentic coding tools are increasingly capable of generating and submitting pull requests (PRs) to software projects, introducing new forms of human-agent collaboration in software development. While prior studies have examined PR-level outcomes of agent-generated contributions, less is known about how agentic coding tools are adopted and managed at the project level. In this paper, we analyze 25,264 agentic PRs from 2,361 popular GitHub repositories to investigate (1) the adoption of agentic coding tools, (2) project-level agentic PR productivity, and (3) human-agent collaboration patterns. Our results show that the median repository generates only one to two agentic PRs during a three-month period, indicating that intensive adoption remains concentrated in a small subset of projects. At the same time, small projects (1-5 contributors) exhibit higher participation ratios and average levels of agentic PR activity than medium-sized and large projects. We also observe substantial variation in project-level agentic PR productivity. While a small number of projects exceed an industry-reported estimate of 36 PRs per participant during the three-month observation period, most projects remain below this threshold. Finally, human-agent collaboration is dominated by a single-human oversight model, in which one developer reviews and/or modifies the agent's contributions, while multi-human collaboration patterns remain uncommon. These findings provide early empirical evidence on how open-source projects organize human oversight around agentic coding tools and suggest that successful integration of agent-generated contributions depends not only on advances in agent capabilities but also on the human and organizational processes that govern their use. Because this study captures an early snapshot of agent adoption, future work should continue to track how adoption patterns evolve over time.

</details>


### [213] [VisualRepair: Dynamic Tool Calling and Region Focusing for Visual Software Issue Repair](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14075)
*Jingyu Xiao,Zhongyi Zhang,Haoran Hou,Yuxuan Wan,Yuan Jiang,Yintong Huo,Michael R. Lyu*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: VisualRepair is an MLLM-based framework for automated program repair that leverages bug screenshots. It introduces Image Type-aware Tool Calling (ITTC) to classify and interpret heterogeneous visual attachments, and Dynamic Test-time Region Focusing (DTRF) to adaptively zoom in on bug-relevant regions, achieving state-of-the-art results on SWE-bench Multimodal with 196 and 25 resolved instances on test and dev sets respectively.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based APR approaches fail to effectively leverage visual information from bug screenshots (UI screenshots, IDE snapshots, GIFs, text-centric images). These heterogeneous attachments have distinct visual patterns and domain-specific semantics difficult for MLLMs. Additionally, screenshots contain large uninformative regions that distract models and reduce patch diversity.

Method: VisualRepair has two core modules: (1) Image Type-aware Tool Calling (ITTC) — classifies input images by type and dynamically invokes a tailored tool-calling chain for robust visual interpretation; (2) Dynamic Test-time Region Focusing (DTRF) — grounds multiple bug-related region candidates and refines them via an adaptive zoom-in/zoom-out strategy to improve fault localization and promote diverse patch generation.

Result: On SWE-bench Multimodal, VisualRepair resolves 196 instances on the test set and 25 on the dev set, surpassing the best baseline by 10 and 11 instances respectively.

Conclusion: Type-aware visual understanding and region-focused localization are highly effective for automated visual software issue repair. VisualRepair consistently outperforms state-of-the-art approaches, demonstrating the importance of tailored visual interpretation strategies in multimodal APR.

Abstract: Automated Program Repair (APR) has witnessed significant progress with the advent of Large Language Models (LLMs). However, as modern software systems increasingly expose rich graphical user interfaces, effectively leveraging visual information from bug screenshots has become essential for understanding bugs and generating accurate fixes in multimodal scenarios. Real-world issue reports frequently contain heterogeneous visual attachments including UI screenshots, IDE snapshots, GIFs, and text-centric images, each with distinct visual patterns and domain-specific semantics that impose substantial perceptual demands on MLLMs. Furthermore, bug screenshots often contain large expanses of uninformative and bug-irrelevant regions, distracting the model's attention and limiting patch diversity. To address these challenges, we propose VisualRepair, an MLLM-based framework for visual software issue repair comprising two core modules: Image Type-aware Tool Calling (ITTC), which classifies input images and dynamically invokes a tailored tool-calling chain for robust visual interpretation, and Dynamic Test-time Region Focusing (DTRF), which grounds multiple bug-related region candidates and refines them via an adaptive zoom-in and zoom-out strategy to improve fault localization and promote diverse patch generation. Extensive experiments on the SWE-bench Multimodal benchmark demonstrate that VisualRepair consistently outperforms state-of-the-art approaches. VisualRepair resolves 196 and 25 instances on the test and dev sets, respectively, surpassing the best baseline by 10 and 11 instances. These results highlight the effectiveness of type-aware visual understanding and region-focused localization for automated visual software issue repair.

</details>


<div id='cs.HC'></div>

# cs.HC [[Back]](#toc)

### [214] [SoftBoard: A Multi-Agent Tool for the Creation and Evaluation of Low-Fidelity Prototypes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13179)
*Gabriel R. S. Scapim,Gislaine C. L. Leal,Guilherme C. Guerino*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: SoftBoard is a web-based low-fidelity prototyping tool for MVP development that integrates a multi-agent LLM system to automate requirements elicitation, prototype generation, and heuristic-based usability evaluation, addressing gaps in existing tools which lack methodological guidance and AI integration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: UX is critical for software startup success, but startups face time constraints, limited resources, and low design maturity. Existing prototyping tools are predominantly general-purpose solutions repurposed for prototyping, lack integrated methodological guidance, and make only incipient use of AI. A systematic literature mapping confirmed these gaps, motivating the need for a dedicated tool that reduces reliance on UX expertise and standardizes the low-fidelity prototyping process.

Method: The authors developed SoftBoard, a web-based system comprising three integrated components: (1) a prototype editor for creating low-fidelity prototypes, (2) team-based project organization features, and (3) a multi-agent system powered by large language models. This multi-agent system supports requirements elicitation and refinement, automates prototype generation from requirements, and evaluates interface quality using established usability heuristics. The system is designed to embed methodological guidance directly into the tooling workflow.

Result: The paper presents the architecture and design of SoftBoard as a functional tool. No empirical results or user study data are reported in the abstract; a feasibility study with software professionals is stated to be currently underway as future work.

Conclusion: SoftBoard demonstrates a novel integration of multi-agent LLM systems into low-fidelity prototyping workflows for MVP development. By embedding AI-driven support for requirements, generation, and evaluation, the tool aims to lower the UX expertise barrier and standardize design practices in resource-constrained startup environments. Empirical validation through the ongoing feasibility study remains necessary.

Abstract: User Experience (UX) is recognized as a critical factor for the success of digital products, particularly in software startups, environments marked by time constraints, limited resources, and low maturity in design practices. Building Minimum Viable Products (MVPs) through low-fidelity prototyping represents a well-established strategy for rapid validation cycles at reduced cost. A systematic literature mapping, however, revealed gaps in the ecosystem of available tools: a predominance of general-purpose solutions adapted for prototyping, the absence of integrated methodological guidance, and the incipient use of Artificial Intelligence in the design process. This paper presents SoftBoard, a web-based tool for the creation and evaluation of low-fidelity prototypes in the context of MVP development. The tool integrates a prototype editor, team-based project organization, and a multi-agent system based on large language models that supports requirements elicitation and refinement, automates prototype generation, and evaluates interface quality based on usability heuristics. This integration aims to reduce reliance on prior UX expertise, standardize the prototyping process, and support teams in building MVPs aligned with user needs. As future work, a feasibility study with software professionals is currently underway.

</details>


### [215] [TANDE: Disentangling Verbal and Nonverbal Backchannels in Emotional AI-Avatar Conversations with Young Adults](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13357)
*Ann-Kareen Gedeus,Jack Good,Nadine Wagener,Angelique Taylor*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: TANDE is an LLM-powered embodied conversational agent designed for emotional conversations with young adults. A within-subjects study (N=36) compared nonverbal versus combined verbal-and-nonverbal backchanneling modalities, revealing a preference for nonverbal cues and significant implications for designing ECAs for emotional support.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Young adults face mental, personal, and social challenges with limited accessible tools. While ECAs increasingly leverage LLMs and multimodal interactions for emotional domains, there is insufficient understanding of how different backchanneling modalities (nonverbal vs. combined verbal-and-nonverbal) affect rapport, empathy, and engagement, particularly with regard to gender differences.

Method: The authors developed TANDE, an LLM-powered ECA, and conducted a within-subjects experimental study with N=36 young adult participants. The study manipulated backchanneling modality (nonverbal-only vs. combined verbal-and-nonverbal) and measured outcomes of rapport, empathy, and engagement, with gender analyzed as a moderating variable.

Result: Findings indicate that nuanced backchanneling cues matter significantly for emotional ECAs targeting young adults. Participants showed a preference for nonverbal backchanneling over combined verbal-and-nonverbal backchanneling in terms of rapport, empathy, and engagement. Gender differences were also identified.

Conclusion: Designers of emotional-support ECAs for young adults should prioritize nonverbal backchanneling cues over combined verbal-nonverbal approaches. The paper provides concrete design implications for building more effective ECAs aimed at emotional support and well-being, and releases the TANDE system as open-source.

Abstract: Embodied conversational agents (ECAs) need effective empathic grounding to foster social support and engagement. Expanding into emotional domains, ECAs now use Large Language Models (LLMs) and multimodal human-agent interactions to enhance their capabilities. Yet, understanding the impact of backchanneling modalities on young adults and their gender remains limited. We introduce TANDE, an LLM-powered ECA designed for emotional conversations with young adults, a population experiencing mental, personal, and social issues with limited tools to address them. In a within-subjects study with N=36 young adults, we explore nonverbal and combined verbal-and-nonverbal backchanneling modalities on rapport, empathy, and engagement and isolate for gender differences. Our research shows the importance of nuanced backchanneling cues with emotional ECAs with young adults, showing a preference for nonverbal cues. We derive design implications for more effective ECAs for emotional support and well-being in young adults. The code is available at https://github.com/Cornell-Tech-AIRLab/TANDE.

</details>


### [216] [VIP-MINGLE: A Corpus for Videoconference and In-Person Multimodal Interaction in Group Language Engagement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13614)
*Andrew Chang,Abhinay K Bodi,Wenxin Deng,Junrui Huang,Venu G Kadamba,Sumanth B H Karanam,Dhiwahar A Kennady,David Poeppel,Dustin Freeman*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: VIP-MINGLE is a multimodal dataset of group conversations with 59 hours of recordings from 105 participants across 32 groups, featuring paired within-subject sessions in both in-person and videoconferencing settings, revealing significant behavioral distribution shifts between the two modalities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing group conversation research is siloed within either in-person or videoconferencing settings due to the lack of datasets that span both. This limits the development of robust, generalizable models for understanding and facilitating group interactions across contexts.

Method: The authors collected a within-subject multimodal dataset (VIP-MINGLE) with 32 groups (105 participants) engaging in conversations in both in-person and videoconferencing settings. The dataset includes raw audio/video, psychometric data, processed multimodal features (diarized speech, facial expressions, transcriptions), and time-resolved human annotations. They then analyzed behavioral distribution shifts across modalities between settings.

Result: Analysis revealed significant behavioral distribution shifts across multiple modalities (e.g., speech patterns, facial expressions) between in-person and videoconferencing settings, confirming that conversational dynamics differ substantially between contexts.

Conclusion: VIP-MINGLE fills a critical gap as a cross-setting corpus, providing a vital resource for developing robust computational models of group conversations that generalize across both in-person and videoconferencing environments.

Abstract: Group conversations are a fundamental yet complex form of social interaction central to human cognition and telecommunication technology. While understanding and facilitating these interactions has been a long-standing goal, findings are often isolated within specific in-person or videoconferencing settings due to a scarcity of datasets that bridge the two. We introduce VIP-MINGLE, a multimodal dataset comprising 59 hours of recordings (32 groups, 105 participants), featuring paired within-subject sessions in both settings. The dataset includes raw audio/video, psychometric data, processed multimodal features (e.g., diarized speech, facial expressions, transcriptions), and time-resolved human annotations. Our analysis reveals significant behavioral distribution shifts across multiple modalities between settings, reinforcing the need for a cross-setting corpus. VIP-MINGLE serves as a critical resource for developing robust models of group conversations across settings.

</details>


### [217] [Interaction Density as a Behavioural Signature of Exhibit Type: A Minimal-Log Study from a Two-Venue Science Experience Centre](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13724)
*R A Udaya Rakshith,Inavamsi Enaganti,Umang J Gala*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: Using only start/end time and press count logs from 2,816 touch-exhibit sessions, the authors derive "interaction density" (presses/second) as a privacy-preserving behavioural signature that distinguishes game-type from quiz-type exhibits (AUC=0.778) and reveals counterintuitive engagement patterns where harder games yield both higher intensity and longer dwell times.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional visitor engagement analysis relies on manual observation or invasive multimodal sensing (eye-tracking, cameras, wearables), which is not scalable for most science centres. The authors explore how much behavioural insight can be extracted from minimal, default interaction logs already collected by touch-enabled exhibits — advocating for a scalable, privacy-preserving, and falsifiable methodology.

Method: Interaction density (presses per second) is introduced as a behavioural signature. The study analyses 2,816 visitor sessions across eight exhibits at two venues in Bengaluru. Density is tested for its ability to distinguish games from quizzes (Mann-Whitney, AUC), its relationship with dwell time (regression, R²), and is stress-tested via exhibit-level anomalies, cross-venue replication, and censoring analysis. Combined models using both press count and density are also evaluated.

Result: Density cleanly separates games from quizzes (Mann-Whitney r=0.556, AUC=0.778). Contrary to intuition, games exhibit both higher density and longer dwell times (r=0.172) — attributed to escalating difficulty creating open-ended re-engagement loops. Raw press count alone explains dwell time far better (R²=0.527) than density (R²=0.081), but combining both yields R²=0.667. Cross-venue replication and anomaly analysis support robustness rather than merely confirming results.

Conclusion: Minimal, privacy-preserving interaction logs already support rigorous behavioural research at scale in science centres. Interaction density serves as a useful but not universal metric; its real value lies in complementing existing measures. The broader methodological contribution demonstrates that sophisticated visitor analytics need not require additional sensors.

Abstract: Understanding how visitors engage with interactive exhibits usually calls for either labour-intensive manual observation or invasive multimodal sensing -- eye-tracking, cameras, wearables -- that few science centres can deploy at scale. We ask how much can be learned instead from the handful of fields that most touch-enabled exhibits already log by default: a session's start time, end time, and press count. Analysing 2,816 visitor sessions across eight exhibits at two venues of a science experience centre in Bengaluru, India, we derive interaction density -- presses per second -- as a simple behavioural signature, and use it to distinguish fast-paced games from slower, deliberate quizzes. Density does so cleanly (Mann-Whitney r=0.556) and predicts exhibit type on its own with a cross-validated AUC=0.778. But the data complicates the obvious story: games are not just more intense, visitors also dwell on them longer (r=0.172), reversing the intuitive trade-off between intensity and duration -- traced to exhibits whose escalating difficulty creates open-ended re-engagement loops rather than fixed endpoints. Density is not a universal replacement for existing metrics either: raw press count alone explains far more variance in dwell time (R^2=0.527) than density does (R^2=0.081), though combining both improves on either alone (R^2=0.667). Exhibit-level anomalies, a cross-venue replication check, and a session-length censoring artefact further stress-test rather than simply confirm these results. The broader case we make is methodological: minimal, privacy-preserving interaction logs -- not additional sensors -- can already support rigorous, falsifiable behavioural research at any science centre with touch-enabled exhibits.

</details>


### [218] [ZipLine: Visual Analysis of Multivariate Graphs with Predicate Logic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13767)
*Sjoerd Vink,Suyang Li,Brian Montambault,Michael Behrisch,Mingwei Li,Remco Chang*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: ZipLine is a visual analytics system for multivariate graphs that unifies topology and attribute analysis through a predicate language and an interaction-learning algorithm, enabling analysts to express, discover, and refine cross-space patterns across three demonstrated domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing graph analysis systems treat topological structure and node attributes in isolation, forcing analysts to switch between separate views and preventing effective exploration of patterns that depend on the interaction between these two complementary spaces. There is a need for an integrated approach that supports unified reasoning across both topology and attributes.

Method: ZipLine introduces two key components: (1) a unified predicate language that formally expresses patterns spanning topology, node attributes, and neighborhood relations; and (2) a predicate-learning algorithm that translates analyst interactions (e.g., subgraph selection in topology views, value brushing in attribute views) into the predicate language, enabling the system to learn and bridge cross-space patterns. This supports iterative refinement through coordinated reasoning.

Result: The system was demonstrated through three case studies in energy infrastructure, cybersecurity, and drug discovery analysis. Results show that ZipLine successfully enables expressive multivariate graph analysis by unifying reasoning across topology and attributes.

Conclusion: ZipLine demonstrates that a unified predicate-based formalism, combined with interaction-driven learning, can effectively bridge the gap between topological and attribute-based analysis of multivariate graphs, enabling richer pattern discovery and iterative exploration across diverse application domains.

Abstract: Multivariate graphs unite two distinct data perspectives: a topological structure defined by nodes and edges, and attribute data associated with each node. Analyzing such graphs therefore requires reasoning across two complementary spaces. However, existing systems typically emphasize the analysis of one space at a time, focusing either on topology or on attributes. As a result, exploration, analysis, and pattern discovery that depend on their interaction remain difficult. In this paper, we present ZipLine, a system designed to support integrative analysis of multivariate graphs by bridging both topology and attribute spaces. ZipLine introduces a predicate language that enables analysts to express patterns involving topology, node attributes, and neighborhood relations with a unified formalism. The system further provides a predicate-learning algorithm that maps analyst interactions across both topology (e.g., subgraph selection) and attribute views (e.g., value brushing), into the predicate language, enabling learned expressions that bridge the two spaces. This integrative approach supports iterative analysis by enabling analysts to refine patterns through coordinated reasoning over topology and attributes. We demonstrate ZipLine through three case studies in energy infrastructure, cybersecurity, and drug discovery analysis. The results show that ZipLine enables expressive multivariate graph analysis through unified reasoning across topology and attributes.

</details>


### [219] [ExpressionCueLens: A Cross-Cultural Analysis of Human-AI Companion Conversations on Social Media](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13924)
*Lynnette Hui Xian Ng,Yunze Xiao,Lionel Z. Wang,Weihao Xuan,Mona Diab*

Main category: cs.HC

TL;DR: The paper introduces ExpressionCueLens, a framework categorizing anthropomorphism in HAI interactions, and applies it to ~3500 Reddit and XiaoHongShu posts to reveal cross-platform/cultural differences in how users express agency toward AI companions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based AI companions are increasingly treated as social agents rather than mere tools, yet there is no systematic framework for profiling how users perceive and express agency in human-AI interactions across different cultural and platform contexts.

Method: The authors propose the ExpressionCueLens framework, organizing linguistic, cognitive, behavioral, and perceptual cues into ten anthropomorphism categories. They apply it to ~3500 posts from Reddit and XiaoHongShu using iterative expert annotation combined with LLM-assisted labeling for cross-platform comparative analysis.

Result: XiaoHongShu users express significantly more vulnerability, emotions, and non-perceptual cues. Reddit users employ more perceptual cues involving temporality and embodiment. These patterns support the hypothesis that cultural and platform norms shape how companion agents are anthropomorphized.

Conclusion: Cultural and platform norms meaningfully influence how users perceive and express agency in AI companions, carrying design implications for building culturally sensitive HAI companion agents.

Abstract: LLM-based AI companion agents are increasingly being perceived not only as tools but also as social companions. On social media, people recount conversations where these agents comfort, negotiate and assert boundaries, reflecting a growing attribution of human-like qualities. To profile how agency is perceived in human-AI (HAI) interactions, we introduce the ExpressionCueLens framework, which organizes linguistic, cognitive, behavioral and perceptual cues into ten categories of anthropomorphism expressions. We apply this framework to $\sim$3500 Reddit and XiaoHongShu posts that discuss HAI companionship. Through iterative expert annotation and LLM-assisted labeling, our cross-platform analysis indicates patterns consistent with the hypothesis that XiaoHongShu users use significantly more expressions of vulnerability and emotions, and more non-perceptual cues. Reddit users employ more perceptual cues with temporality and embodiment expressions. These findings suggest that cultural and platform norms shape the way that companion agents are treated as active, agentic partners, and provides design implications for culturally sensitive HAI companion agents.

</details>
